# Changelog

All notable changes to this repository should be documented in this file.

The format is loosely based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/), adapted for the current MVP stage of Cronsole.

> [!NOTE]
> **The product was called TaskHub until 2026-07-31**, and entries written before then still
> say so — deliberately. They record what happened under the name it happened under; rewriting
> them would make this file claim the product was always called Cronsole, which is the one
> thing a changelog must never do. Do not "clean these up".

## [Unreleased]

### Added
- **Export one task in two formats — and one of them is portable** (2026-08-17). The per-task **Export** button in the task modal is now a small menu, because "export this task" turns out to be two different requests.

  **Native** (what Export always did) puts *this exact task* back on *this platform*: Task Scheduler XML for a Windows task, Cronsole JSON for a native one. **Portable template** is new — a target-agnostic template that recreates the task on any install, on any platform Cronsole can compile it for.

  **You could already get the portable form, but it cost you a catalog row.** Save as template writes a template into your library, and only then could you download it. If you just wanted a file for another machine, you were left tidying up afterwards. This writes nothing.

  **The menu names each option by what it is for, not by its file extension** — "restores onto any Windows PC" against "recreate this task on any install" — because the extension is the half you can't act on. And it says what the portable one gives up **before** you click: it drops platform-specific settings like the account the task runs as. That matters, since a file that looks like a faithful backup and silently isn't would restore a task running as the wrong user, succeeding, with nothing to warn you.

  Portable also works where native can't: with the Windows agent offline, and for a **Claude routine**, whose definition lives at claude.ai and was previously not exportable at all. Available to AI tools as `export_task`'s new `format` parameter.

- **The Dashboard's Import button now asks which kind of import you mean** (2026-08-17). It opens on two options — **Tasks already on this machine** and **A task file (.json)** — instead of going straight to folder discovery.

  **They were never variants of one action.** Discovering *adopts* tasks that already exist: Cronsole creates nothing, and those tasks would run tomorrow whether or not you ever pressed the button. A task file *creates* a task that did not exist a moment ago and starts it running. So the chooser leads with that consequence rather than with the source — "nothing is created" against "this creates a task" is the part you can act on, and the file extension is the footnote.

  It also names where the third format goes: a Windows task's `.xml` is Tools → Restore, said up front rather than discovered as a refusal after you pick the file.

  **Discovery is not run until you choose it.** It is an agent round trip that can take its full timeout and fails outright when the agent is offline — so it is no longer spent while you are still reading two buttons, and someone importing a file never sees an agent error that has nothing to do with them. Either option can be backed out of without reopening the modal.

- **Import a task from a file, and bring back one you deleted** (2026-08-17). Cronsole has offered **Export** on a Cronsole-native task since it shipped, and there was nowhere to put the file back. There is now: **Tools → Import a task**, and an *Import a .json file* link in the New Task modal for people who are already trying to create the thing the file describes.

  **The same card restores deleted tasks.** Cronsole archives a Cronsole-native task's definition — and its last 20 runs — *before* deleting it, and refuses the delete if that archive cannot be written. Until today nothing could read one back, so the guarantee on offer was "we kept a copy" with no way to use it. Deleted tasks now appear in the card with a **Restore** button.

  **What a restore gives you is a new task**, and the app says so rather than implying the old one came back: new id, running on the schedule it had, and the archived run history is *not* reattached — those runs happened to a task that no longer exists. The archive is kept, so the record of the deletion survives.

  **Which file goes where is decided by the platform, not by preference.** A Cronsole-native task's database row *is* the task, so it round-trips through JSON. A Windows task's definition lives on the machine as Task Scheduler XML, so it goes through **Tools → Restore** as it always has. Hand Import a Windows bundle and it refuses by name and points at the other card — which is also why Restore's description now says which formats it actually reads.

  Also available to AI tools: `import_task`, `list_task_archives` and `restore_task_archive`. None of them is gated behind `CRONSOLE_MCP_ALLOW_DESTRUCTIVE`, and that asymmetry is deliberate — deleting is gated, undoing a delete is a create.

- **System diagnostics — "why isn't this working?", answered with evidence** (2026-08-17). A new panel that checks **Cronsole itself**: the backend, the database, the Windows agent, task-list freshness, the Cronsole-native scheduler, the template catalog, API-token expiry and the allowed browser origins. Open it from **Diagnose** at the end of the Dashboard health strip, or **Run checks** on the Tools tab. Also available to AI tools as the `get_diagnostics` MCP tool.

  **The gap it closes is that a status line throws its reasons away.** *"Windows offline — agent not connected"* is one sentence covering four different situations: a socket that never arrived, one that arrived and went, a request that timed out ninety seconds ago, and a request that timed out at 9pm yesterday and has been colouring the strip ever since. Those call for different actions, and Cronsole has had the facts to tell them apart all along — it just never showed them to anyone. Every check now renders the evidence behind its verdict, including the line that usually settles it: *when a request last timed out, and which one*.

  **It is a different question from Task health**, and it has to be answered first. Task health asks *"which of my tasks are failing?"*; that is only meaningful once Cronsole can see them at all, because a wedged agent makes every Windows task look unhealthy and the fix is in none of those tasks.

  **"Not measured" is not "OK".** A check that could not run says so, and the overall verdict ranks it *above* passing — a panel reporting "all clear" over something it never measured would be worse than one that admits the gap. A check with nothing to measure at all (no agent paired, no API tokens issued) is left out rather than shown as a pass.

  **The panel names the machine it measured.** On a Dockerized stack the backend measures the *container's* clock and filesystem, not yours.

  **Nothing here repairs anything, and that is a finding rather than caution.** Of the four agent-health problems in the troubleshooting log, three turned out to be the *readout* being wrong rather than the agent failing — one of them recorded a timeout fifteen seconds after every **successful** request. A "restart the agent" button shipped against any of those would have been restarting a perfectly healthy agent, on a schedule, forever, and looking like it worked. Diagnosis first.

  **What it cannot do is stated in the panel**: these checks are served *by* the backend, so they say nothing about a backend, database or Docker engine that is not running — which is exactly the case someone will open it for. That case belongs to the `Cronsole-Stack` startup tasks, which run from Windows Task Scheduler, outside the stack.

  Along the way, the backend now listens to the agent's `agent:hello` — which the agent has been sending since it was written, to nobody — so diagnostics can name the machine and OS your agent is running on. Its version string is deliberately **not** shown: the agent hardcodes `1.0.0`, so displaying it beside "Agent" would read as a freshness claim while carrying no information at all.

- **Collections — name a set of tasks you picked by hand** (2026-08-16). Put two Claude routines and two Windows tasks in one place, call it whatever you want, and click it in the sidebar. A collection appears in the source rail above your platforms, with its own count.

  **This is not a saved filter, and that is the point.** A view stores *conditions* — active, failing, due today — so its contents are whatever matches when you look. A collection stores *the tasks*. That is the only way to group four things that have nothing in common except that you care about them together: different platforms, different schedules, one of them disabled. No filter can describe that set, because there is nothing to filter on.

  Add a task from the **bookmark button** next to the star on any task — it opens a checklist of your collections, and you can create a new one from there with the task already in it. Collections compose with whatever view is lit, exactly like Favorites: *Failures* + your collection is the failing tasks in it. Manage, rename and delete them from the bottom of the sidebar.

  **Deleting a collection never deletes tasks** — it removes the grouping, and the confirmation says so with the count. Removing a task from Cronsole does remove it from any collection holding it, which is the same rule the star already follows: a membership describes a task Cronsole is tracking, so it goes when the task does.

- **Cronsole-native gained two job types: Scripts and Checks** (2026-08-15). Native could call a URL or run a program that already existed on the backend's machine. It can now run a **script you write in Cronsole itself**, and run a **check** that measures something and compares it to what you expect. Decision record: [ADR 0002](adr/0002-native-job-types.md).

  **Scripts store the body, which is the entire point.** Every native "script" until now was a command line pointing at a file that had to exist on the machine the backend runs on — a filesystem you cannot see on a Dockerized stack. So the most important half of the task, *what it actually does*, was the half Cronsole could not show you, include in an export, archive before a delete, or ship as a template. A script job holds the text: pick an interpreter (**PowerShell · PowerShell 7 · Bash · sh · Python · Node**), write the body, and Cronsole writes it to a temp file at run time, runs it, and deletes it — including when it times out and is killed.

  This is the one job type where **a shell is expected**, and it does not bend the no-shell rule: that rule bans an *implicit* shell wrapped around a command you typed, where a value can become a second process. Here you name the interpreter and write the body, so nothing is re-parsed — and **`interpreter` is a fixed list, never a path**, which is what keeps this from being "run a program" with an extra step. `-NoProfile` is always passed to PowerShell, or your profile silently becomes part of the task. Whitespace is preserved exactly, because indentation is significant in Python and a trailing newline is what makes a shell script's last line run.

  **Checks are the only job type whose failure means something.** A failed script is usually a bug in your script; a failed check is a fact about your system — which is what the failure notifications, health tiers and the *Failures* view were built for, and were previously pointed at job types whose failures are mostly self-inflicted. Four probes behind one type: **Endpoint** (status range, body must contain, or a dotted JSON path must equal), **Port**, **File freshness**, **Disk space**.

  A check is separate from an HTTP job by **intent, not capability**. An HTTP job *does* something — fires a webhook — so "the endpoint accepted it" is the honest success test. A check *verifies*, so **a 200 serving an error page has to be able to fail**. Every probe's log states the measurement rather than a verdict (`D:\backups\nightly.zip last modified 3.0h ago (limit 60m)`), because the number is the reason you ran it. A **missing file fails** the freshness check deliberately: it exists to notice a backup that stopped, and one that was never written is that problem in its worst form.

  **File and disk probes measure the *backend's* filesystem** — the container's on a Dockerized stack, not yours. The form names the execution host before you save, on those probes only; the other two reach out over the network and carry no such warning, because a caution shown everywhere is one nobody reads where it matters.

  **Six new templates**, so the types are usable the day they ship rather than being an empty form: a script starter, a PowerShell disk report, and checks for endpoint health, backup freshness, disk space and port reachability. Three are **core** (auto-synced into every install) — and a script template is the first in the catalog *guaranteed* to work on a fresh machine, since it needs nothing on disk. Catalog is now **72 templates, 10 core**.

### Changed
- **`export_task` now tells an AI assistant that it has two formats** (2026-08-17). The tool's own description — the text an assistant actually reads before choosing — described only the native export, and mentioned the portable template solely in the `format` parameter's fine print. So the likeliest outcome of "set this task up on my other machine" was the format that cannot do it. The description now leads with both, says which one is not a backup, and names where portable is the only option that works: agent offline, or a Claude routine.

  The file's own tool inventory was stale in the same way, listing 17 tools while the server registered 33 — regenerated, with a note to rebuild it from the file rather than append to it by hand. Neither is a behavior change; both are the kind of drift that surfaces later as an assistant confidently picking the wrong thing.

  **The Connect Pack had the same number wrong** and it mattered more there, because a downloaded copy sits on someone else's machine and can never be corrected in place: its surface table had said *"15 tools"* since July while the server grew to 33, so an assistant reading it could conclude a verb it needed did not exist. Removed rather than corrected — a count in prose is right only until the next tool ships — and the pack is **v1.11**, since two copies must never carry the same version and different words.

- **The two test files that spawn real processes no longer fail at random** (2026-08-17). `NativeTaskExecutor.test.ts` and `NativeJobTypes.test.ts` run real interpreters rather than mocking `spawn` — on purpose, since the whole point of a script job is that a real interpreter accepts the file — so their wall clock is the machine's scheduler, not anything the code controls. Under a full parallel run on a 2-core CI runner, `spawn` had been exceeding the 5-second default about one run in three since 2026-08-15, most recently reddening a commit that changed only documentation and CI config.

  Both files now allow 30 seconds, set once per file rather than per test: the flake had already moved between tests, so a constant each test must remember is one the next spawning test forgets. 30s still catches a genuine hang — it just stops reporting a busy runner as a defect. A suite that reddens at random teaches people to re-run instead of read, which costs far more than the seconds it gives back.

- **Documentation links are checked by CI** (2026-08-17). `scripts/check-doc-links.mjs` resolves every relative markdown link in the repo — **1307 across 147 files** — to a real file and a real heading, and fails the `repo-hygiene` job on any that does not. It found three dead links on its first clean run, on top of the 13 fixed by hand the same day.

  **Extended the same day to repo-doc paths written as string literals in source** — the set the diagnostics panel turns into "Read more" links. Those were checked by nothing: the app's own link test only sees `help.ts` and `onboarding.ts`, and a markdown sweep cannot see a `.ts` file. It is the worst place for a dead link, too — a panel someone opens *because something is broken*, offering a link that silently lands on a table of contents. (On its first run it duly reported the illustrative example inside its own doc comment, which is at least a working demonstration.)

  This exists because a broken doc link **does not 404**. GitHub serves the page scrolled to the top, so a renamed heading quietly starts delivering the table of contents instead of the section that was promised, and nothing anywhere goes red. The TaskHub → Cronsole rename renamed three troubleshooting headings on 2026-07-31 and left 13 links pointing at the old slugs; they survived every green CI run until 2026-08-17, because nothing checked links *between* docs. The app's own links have been checked since they existed (`docsLinks.test.ts`), which is precisely why the gap was easy to miss.

  Fixed with it: the ROADMAP's P0 section is now linked by a stable `#p0-security` anchor rather than by a heading slug that carries dates — that heading has been edited twice, and each edit silently broke every link to it.

- **You can add a task to a collection from anywhere a task appears** (2026-08-16). The bookmark button now sits next to the star on every card in Grid, every row in List, every Kanban and Schedule entry — not only inside a task's detail modal, which is where it had been. Collections were a first-class place in the sidebar that you could navigate to and, in practice, barely fill: reaching the only control that added a task meant opening it first and finding an unlabelled 19px glyph. The star — the same kind of per-user label, on the same tasks — was already on all five surfaces.

  Two changes come with it. **The button carries a number** once a task is in something, so you can see which tasks are filed without opening anything. And **in the detail modal it now says "Add to collection" in words**, since that is the surface with room for them; a star needs no caption because everyone knows what one does, but a bookmark beside it read as decoration. The help text already described the control as sitting "next to the star on any task" — that is now true rather than aspirational.

- **The native row labelled "Scripts" is now "Programs"** (2026-08-15), because it never took a script — it runs a program that must already exist on the backend host. The name now belongs to the type that actually carries a script. **Presentation only:** no stored job, source key, saved view or link changed. The MCP tool was renamed to match — **`create_native_script_task` → `create_native_program_task`** — since leaving the wrapper calling it a script while the app called it a program is exactly the drift the rename was meant to remove. A call to the old name now fails loudly rather than doing the wrong thing quietly.
- **Every source now explains itself in the dashboard** (2026-08-15). Selecting a source (or one of its job types) shows a one-line description of what it *is* under the heading, with a `?` to the full help topic. The heading and breadcrumb named the scope; nothing said what it meant — and the gap was widest exactly where it mattered, since the rail lists every native job type whether or not you have one, so a new user's first sight of *Checks* was a lit row over an empty list with no explanation of why anything would go there.
- **The New Task and Edit forms are one job form** (2026-08-15). The create modal held its own copy of the native fields, covering only HTTP and EXEC. With four job types that would have meant two field sets, two validations and two payload builders per type — the shape that lets a job be submittable in one form and refused by the other. Both now render `NativeJobFields` and serialize through `nativeJobPayload`.

### Fixed
- **The Platforms screen could report your agent offline while it was answering** (2026-08-17). The health verdict on the Platforms tab — and on `list_platforms`, the tool an AI assistant is told to check *before* planning work — was read back from a stored column instead of being asked for. That column has exactly one writer: the dashboard's own 45-second poll. So the verdict was current whenever a browser tab happened to be open, and arbitrarily old whenever one was not.

  **Found by two Cronsole surfaces disagreeing in the same second.** The system report said the Windows agent was *"connected and answering"* while the Platforms row said *`OFFLINE — Agent not connected`*, and the row contradicted itself as well: its own **List folders** cell carried a success from a minute earlier. Nothing was wrong with the agent. One call to the dashboard's health route flipped the row to Healthy with nothing on the machine changing.

  **It reads worse over an AI tool than in the browser**, which is why it went unnoticed: a person sees a stale strip and presses Sync, while an assistant told the agent is offline reports that back and declines Windows work that would have succeeded. Health is now derived from the connector at the moment you ask, the same way the system report and the dashboard strip already did — so the three cannot disagree. A platform that cannot describe itself reports **Not checked**, never a healthy-looking gap.

- **A Claude routine can now be exported as a portable template** (2026-08-17). The two-format export shipped earlier today promised that the portable format works where the native one cannot — *"with the agent offline, and for a **Claude routine**, whose definition lives at claude.ai"*. It did not: every Claude routine was refused with *"this task has no command Cronsole can capture yet"*.

  **The routine's command is its prompt**, and Cronsole already had it — it stores the saved prompt whenever a routine is read through your Claude Code session. The exporter simply had no case for it, so it looked past the prompt, found no program to run, and refused. A routine now exports as an `ai-prompt` template carrying its prompt, ready to recreate on another install.

  A routine you connected *by declaration* — a pasted id and token — is still refused, because Cronsole genuinely never had its prompt. It now says that, instead of advising a sync that cannot help.

- **A plain `npm run build` no longer breaks remote access** (2026-08-17). If you reach Cronsole from your phone through the reverse proxy, the dashboard used to depend on which of two near-identical build commands was run last: `npm run build:remote` produced a working bundle, and `npm run build` produced one that renders perfectly and cannot reach the backend from any device but the one running the stack.

  **The reason it was so easy to get wrong is the reason it is now fixed in code rather than documented.** Both commands write the same `dist/`, both report success, both pass every check, and `npm run build` is the obvious one — so nothing anywhere could tell you which bundle you had. And it is invisible from the desktop, where the baked-in `http://localhost:3000` genuinely is the backend; the failure only appears on the phone, where that address is the phone. It caught people twice.

  Every production build now resolves the API against whatever address it is served from. The dev server is unchanged — it still points at `localhost:3000`, because there the API really is on another port. `npm run build` and `npm run build:remote` now produce the same correct bundle, and setting `VITE_API_URL` still overrides both for a deployment on a different host.

- **Deleting a task from the UI now archives it, the way deleting one through an AI tool always did** (2026-08-17). Whether a deleted Cronsole-native task could be recovered depended on *which door you deleted through*: the MCP route archived the definition first and refused the delete if it could not, while the Delete button in the app did not. Neither screen said so, and nobody would have guessed it. Both archive now — and with the restore verb shipping in the same change, the archive is finally worth something. (Windows tasks are unchanged: their definition lives on the machine as XML, and reaching it needs the agent online, which cannot be a precondition of a delete. Back those up with **Tools → Back up tasks**.)

- **The README described a product two job types and seventeen templates out of date** (2026-08-17). Four claims on the repo's front page were false, all of them the same shape CLAUDE.md warns about — a first-ship description left standing in the present tense after the thing changed:

  - **Cronsole-native was described as "HTTP jobs"**, in both the platform table and the feature list. It has had four job types since 2026-08-15 (HTTP, an existing program, a script you write here, and checks), and the two rows that would tell a stranger so were the two that still said otherwise.
  - **"55 templates in 6 packs"** — the registry holds **72 in 8** (verified against `registry/index.json`, not against another doc, which is the only way that number is ever right).
  - **"Star the tasks you watch, and the dashboard opens on them"** — it stopped opening on them on 2026-08-12, deliberately, and the sentence after it described a disclosure banner that was removed in the same change. So the front page documented a behaviour *and* apologised for it, months after both were gone.
  - **Collections were missing entirely**, having shipped 2026-08-16.

  Added a row for the diagnostics panel at the same time. None of this breaks a test — a README is the purest form of the surface that "never fails loudly when it drifts", and it is the one a stranger reads first.

- **The Connect Pack now tells an assistant how to check whether Cronsole is working** (2026-08-17, pack **v1.8**). The pack's route tables are read as complete — its own version notes say an omission from them is a claim rather than a gap — so leaving out `GET /api/tools/diagnostics` left an assistant to diagnose "nothing is running" from `task-health`, which reports **every** Windows task as unhealthy whenever the agent is wedged. That is a confident list of wrong answers produced exactly when the real fault is somewhere else.

- **A passing check now says which assertions it made** (2026-08-17). A check's run log ended `| all assertions passed`, which named none of them — so an endpoint check requiring the body to contain `ok` logged **character-for-character identically** to one that only looked at the status code. The failing path had always named the specific assertion; the passing path, which is the one you read most, did not.

  It now lists what each assertion observed: `GET https://… → 200 (expected 200–299) | body contains "ok" | status.db is "up"`. A check with no assertions beyond the status range prints just the status line, so **an assertion that went missing between the tool that created the task and the job that was stored is now visible in the log** rather than looking exactly like one that ran and held. This is the rule checks were built on — every probe's log states the *measurement*, not a verdict — finally met in the success case.

- **The template gallery renders a script as a script and a check as an assertion** (2026-08-17). On [the public catalog](https://mikesailab.com/cronsole-registry), a template with no single command line fell back to printing its raw JSON — so the one template family whose *content is the whole point* was displayed as `"body": "// Runs on...\n// Anything printed..."`, escapes and all. A script now shows its body as code under the interpreter that runs it (**Script — PowerShell**), and a check reads as the assertion it makes:

  ```
  GET {{url}}
    status must be 200–299
    body must contain "{{expectText}}"
  ```

  Placeholders are highlighted as they are everywhere else on the page, and Copy takes what you can see. A template shape the page does not recognise still falls back to JSON on purpose: the gallery is a static page and cannot ask anything what a new shape means, so inventing a reading for one it does not know is the wrong failure.

  **A partial reading is worse than the JSON**, and review caught three ways to produce one. A check that sends **headers** — an authenticated endpoint — read as a bare `GET {{url}}`, hiding both the header and the parameter you have to fill in; headers now get a line each. A malformed `expectStatus` (the registry holds a probe as a passthrough, so `{}` really can be published) printed `undefined–undefined`; it now declines the whole reading rather than substituting the documented default, because **absent and malformed are different facts and only the first has a default**. And a script missing its interpreter titled itself *SCRIPT — UNDEFINED*; it now just says *Script*. Also fixed: the Copy button overlapped the first line of code in a phone-width column — invisible until now, because the old JSON fallback opened with a lone `{`.

- **Windows no longer reports "not responding" fifteen seconds after every request it answered** (2026-08-16). The dashboard sat at **"Windows Task Scheduler degraded — Agent connected but not responding (task:list timed out)"** almost permanently, over an agent that was answering everything: syncs returned, folders listed, tasks ran. Pressing Sync cleared it for a moment, then it came back on its own with nothing having happened in between.

  Nothing had timed out. Every agent verb started a 15-second deadline and **never cancelled it**, so a request answered in 200ms still recorded a timeout a quarter of a minute later. Two of that stale timer's three effects were genuinely harmless — removing an already-removed listener, resolving an already-resolved promise — and the third wrote to the health record. That is why it lasted: the only visible symptom was a status line, on the one surface with no other way to check its own work. Windows could not stay healthy for longer than 15 seconds after its last request, and the 45-second health poll almost always landed inside the poisoned window. All ten verbs had it.

  **The cost was not a wrong colour.** Platform health exists to report evidence rather than assumptions — it was rebuilt for that in [#40](troubleshooting/README.md#40-the-sidebar-says-windows-is-online-and-synced-just-now-while-every-agent-request-times-out), where it claimed health it had not observed. This was the same dishonesty pointed the other way, reporting **a failure that never happened**, and it is the more expensive direction: a dashboard that cries wolf over a working agent teaches you to ignore the one line whose whole job is to mean something is wrong. Pinned by a test over all ten verbs that fails 10/10 against the old code. [Troubleshooting #62](troubleshooting/README.md#62-windows-reports-not-responding-15-seconds-after-every-successful-request).

- **The job-type buttons are a proper tap target on a phone** (2026-08-16). The four native job types (Call a URL / Run a program / Write a script / Check something) rendered **34px** tall at 375px, against the **42px** platform picker directly above them — the same "pick one of N" control, one field up, noticeably shorter for no reason. They now match. Found by rendering them at that width for the first time; the layout itself (two rows of two) was already right.

- **A schedule Cronsole can't convert between zones now says so, instead of claiming the zone doesn't matter** (2026-08-15). Typing `0 9-17 * * 1-5` — a working day — in a Pacific-zone install stored it **verbatim as UTC**, so the task ran 01:00–09:00 Pacific. Cronsole declines to shift a schedule whose hour field isn't a single number, which is the right call; what it did not do was **say so**. And the message it printed instead was not silence: the hint under the field read *"This schedule has no fixed clock time, so it reads the same in PDT and UTC."* Both halves were false — the expression names seven clock times, and it reads seven hours earlier.

  The field now explains the refusal and tells you what to do about it (write the hours in UTC, or use a single hour and have it converted). This covers hour **lists** (`1,13`), **ranges** (`9-17`) and **steps** (`*/6` — four fixed times, four different ones in Pacific), a fixed hour with several minutes (`0,30 9 * * *`), and the partial-hour-zone case where a multi-value *minute* field would have moved (`0,15 * * * *` in Kolkata). Genuinely zone-invariant schedules — `*/15 * * * *`, `20 * * * *`, `* * * * *` — are unchanged and still say nothing, because warning about those is what trains people to ignore warnings.

  **Nothing about storage or conversion changed** — the same expressions shift, and the same ones don't. What changed is that declining to convert is now stated rather than reported as "no conversion needed". [Troubleshooting #60](troubleshooting/README.md#60-a-schedule-is-stored-78-hours-off-and-the-ui-says-the-timezone-doesnt-matter).

- **A check that finds a problem is no longer reported as a check that could not run** (2026-08-15). A Cronsole-native job that ran and **failed** came back as an HTTP **502**, the same answer given when the job could not be started at all. Over MCP that meant `run_task` *threw*, so an agent asked to verify something reported *"I couldn't run the check"* on top of a message saying the check had run and the endpoint was broken — **"your disk is full" and "monitoring is broken" were indistinguishable**, and they call for opposite actions. In the dashboard the same run raised a red *Failed to run* toast instead of showing a failing result.

  The old behaviour was correct for the platforms it was written for. On Windows or Claude a failed run *is* a failure to dispatch — an offline agent, an ACL denial, a paused routine — so `502` ("the gateway had a problem, retry") is honest. **Checks broke that premise**: a failing check is the check *working*, reporting a fact about your system, which is the entire reason the job type exists. Same for a script exiting non-zero — the run happened, and its exit code is the answer, not a transport error.

  A run result now says **which question `success` is answering**: a job that executed and failed returns **200 with `success: false`**, and only a genuine failure-to-start is a 502. A spec rejected before anything runs still returns 502, because nothing was measured and there is no verdict to report. Cronsole-native is the only platform this can affect — it is the only one where dispatching and executing are the same act. `run_task` now returns such a run as a **result** reading `Task ran and FAILED: …` rather than as an error, and the dashboard shows it as a failed run rather than a request error. What Cronsole *records* is unchanged: the run was already stored as `FAILURE` and already triggered failure notifications. [Troubleshooting #59](troubleshooting/README.md#59-a-check-that-correctly-finds-a-problem-is-reported-as-could-not-run-the-check).

- **One unreadable template no longer empties the whole hosted catalog** (2026-08-15). `RegistryCatalogSource` threw on the first template it could not parse, and the catch falls back to the *entire* bundled snapshot — so a single file discarded every other template in the registry, silently, with the app looking like it was working. The realistic trigger is a **version gap**: `action` is a discriminated union, so a member added later is an unknown discriminator to every older install, and publishing this release's `script`/`check` kinds would have blanked the catalog for anyone who had not updated. Unreadable templates are now skipped and logged individually; **a checksum mismatch still throws**, because that is an integrity failure on executable content rather than a version gap. [Troubleshooting #58](troubleshooting/README.md#58-one-unreadable-template-silently-empties-the-whole-hosted-catalog).

### Changed
- **The app navigates by a top toolbar and a source rail — pass 1 of the dashboard redesign** (2026-08-15). The five section links (Dashboard · Templates · Platforms · Tools · Settings) moved from a 256px left column into a **top toolbar**, and the left column now belongs to the dashboard: a **source rail**, two levels deep, listing each system you are connected to and opening to that system's own grouping.

  **The problem was peer controls, not missing features.** The dashboard stacked five full-width things above the first task — title + five action buttons, the health strip, a source chip row, a saved-views chip row, and the search/filter/view-mode bar. Three of those were horizontal chip rows doing three different jobs at near-identical visual weight, and the one axis you actually navigate by on a real machine — *which Task Scheduler folder is this in?* — was not among them. It was a facet buried inside the Filters popover. There are now **one rail and one chip row**.

  **Level 2 is whatever the platform groups by, and that is a property of the platform.** Windows opens to its real Task Scheduler folders (`\AI-Maintenance\`, `\AI-Tools\`, …), which Cronsole already carried as `category`, derived from the folder path at import. Cronsole-native opens to its **job type** — *HTTP jobs* and *Scripts*, which used to be two **top-level** sources; a platform sitting beside a job type was two levels of concept in one list. So a rail node carries **the filter patch it applies** rather than one dimension's value, because a Windows folder sets `category` and a native job type sets `source`. Existing `?source=TASKHUB_NATIVE:EXEC` links still resolve — source matching was already prefix-aware.

  **Navigating stopped invalidating your view, and that is the substantive behaviour change.** `filtersEqual` has ignored `source` since the source bar shipped, on the rule that the disqualifying thing is a **hidden** constraint. `category` now joins it. Picking *Failures* and walking from Windows into `AI-Tools` and on to Claude keeps *Failures* lit the whole way, where before a folder click dropped the bar to **Custom** and a source click did not — two halves of one control behaving oppositely. The dimension did not change; **where it is displayed did, and that is what the comparison was always about.** Saved views no longer store either dimension (`viewFiltersFrom` strips both), and both ride alongside `?view=` in the URL so a bookmark still reproduces what you see.

  **The rail is navigation, which inverts one rule the chip row followed.** The old bar refused to show a source with no tasks — right for a filter chip, wrong here: a connected Claude account with nothing imported must be reachable, because its empty state is the only place that can tell you to import from it. **Existence** now comes from tasks-you-have ∪ platforms-you-are-connected-to; the **count** stays faceted, so a row can legitimately read `0` and that zero predicts the click.

  **`\Microsoft\` is one collapsed group at the bottom of Windows**, counted out loud (~257 tasks), rather than twenty system folders mixed in with your dozen. It has **no filter patch on purpose** — selecting it would mean writing `system: 'only'` from the rail, making it a third controller of a lens that already has two. Its children set `system: 'include'` instead, which is enough: a `\Microsoft\` category contains nothing else.

  **Per-platform health moved onto the rail and the sidebar's "System Status" panel is deleted.** That panel listed exactly these platforms with exactly these dots; a status readout beside the thing it describes needs no panel of its own. The dashboard health strip stays — it reports last sync and last command, which the dots do not.

  **The mobile drawer got simpler, not harder.** The old sidebar needed an off-canvas panel, a backdrop, an Escape handler and an `inert` dance to keep a hidden menu out of the tab order. Five toolbar icons fit across a 375px screen, so that drawer is gone; the one that remains belongs to the **rail**, which genuinely cannot fit, and it opens from beside the list it re-scopes rather than from the app toolbar. *(Verified in-browser on desktop against 363 real tasks; the phone layout is by construction — the window would not resize in the automation session.)*

  Pass 2 — source-scoped views, actions and empty states — is still open. `buildSourceTree` is pure and carries 16 tests; the rail component carries 8.

- **A visual pass over the new chrome** (2026-08-15), because the first cut was structurally right and read as unfinished. Both surfaces had the same underlying fault: **everything at one weight, and no zoning.**

  **The toolbar now has three zones** — brand · sections · account — separated by hairlines rather than by gaps, which had let the wordmark and the first tab read as one run of text. The active section is no longer a rounded fill (which says "a button here is highlighted"); it is a **2px accent on the bottom edge sitting on the header's own border**, so the active tab is visually joined to the page under it — which is the question a nav actually answers. The header became a translucent blurred plate (`bg-surface/80` + `backdrop-blur-xl`) so chrome reads as chrome. And the theme toggle lost its border: as a bordered box it was **the heaviest element in the entire bar**, a three-way preference outweighing the navigation.

  **The rail got hierarchy it did not have.** Depth was expressed only as left padding, so fifteen folders under Windows read as a flat list that happened to start further right — you could not see where a branch began or ended. Each nested level now draws a **hairline guide rail**. Selection gained a **3px accent bar on the leading edge** (the toolbar's grammar, rotated for a vertical list) because a `bg-primary/12` tint at depth 2 is barely a shade. The health dot moved to a **fixed leading slot** after the platform icon, having previously floated between the label and the count and landed somewhere different depending on whether a dot existed at all. Counts became a **right-aligned tabular column** instead of a ragged row of pills — fifteen numbers now scan vertically instead of competing with the folder names beside them. Rows are a fixed 30px, so nothing shifts on the 45-second poll.

  **The `\Microsoft\` group's disclosure moved from its label to a badge, and the label got shorter.** "System — the OS's own (hidden by default)" wrapped onto two lines, leaving one ragged row among single-line siblings; and it was redundant — *System* and *OS* say the same thing twice. It is now **System tasks** plus a `HIDDEN` badge, which matches the wording of the toolbar chip reporting the same set (`213 system hidden`) — two surfaces describing one thing should use one word for it. The badge appears **only while the lens is actually hiding them**, pinned by a test in both directions, because that badge is now the sole on-screen statement that 213 tasks are held back.

  Role tokens only throughout — no raw palette utilities — and verified in **both themes** in the browser.

- **Favorites moved into the source rail, and the rail's top level got its own weight** (2026-08-15).

  **Favorites is a row, second from the top, under *All sources*** — and it is gone from the views bar. Starred-is-a-place is where people look for it, and as a rail row it **composes with whichever view is lit** rather than replacing one: *Failures* + *Favorites* is your failing starred tasks, and both controls say so together. It deliberately does **not** rewrite your status and ownership lenses the way the old Favorites *view* did; that would have made it the single rail entry behaving unlike every other, which is the inconsistency the rail was built to remove. The rule that shaped the old view — *"a star is an explicit choice, so no **default** may overrule it"* — still holds exactly where it was written: a bare URL opens on **All**, so clicking Favorites from a fresh load shows every starred task including disabled, missing and OS-owned ones. It narrows only under a view you deliberately picked, and that view is lit while it does. `favorites` therefore joins `source` and `category` as a rail dimension: `filtersEqual` ignores it, `viewFiltersFrom` strips it, the Filters badge stops counting it, and it rides the URL beside a matched view id (`?view=failures&fav=only`).

  **Cronsole-native now always lists both job types**, so *Scripts* appears with a `0` instead of vanishing when you have not written a script task yet. This is the existence-vs-count split one level down, and the two levels genuinely differ: a Windows folder is **data** — Cronsole knows `\AI-Tools\` exists because a task is in it, so an empty one is unknowable — while native's two job types are **structure**, always present. A structural destination disappearing reads as a missing feature rather than an empty bucket, and the rail is navigation, so an empty destination still needs a route to it.

  **The rail's top level is now visibly a level.** Platform rows are taller, carry their icon in a tile with the health dot docked to its corner, and set their label larger; folders stay quiet underneath. Previously a Task Scheduler folder and the entire Windows platform were the same 30px of the same weight, which is what made the tree read flat even after the guide rails landed.

- **The source rail is a panel, and it collapses** (2026-08-15). It had been a column of links sitting on the page background with a gap beside the task list, which made the two read as halves of one scrolling document. It now has **its own surface, a right border, and the full height of the viewport under the toolbar** — chrome you navigate with, visibly distinct from the content you navigated to. That required moving the page padding off `<main>` and onto each screen: a padded main inset the panel, leaving a strip of page background down its left edge.

  **Collapsing** (the button at the top of the rail, remembered between visits via a new `railCollapsed` preference) narrows it to a 72px column of icon tiles — each system with its health dot and its count stacked beneath, names on hover. The folder level is **dropped rather than shrunk**: at that width a Task Scheduler folder name has nowhere to go, and a second level rendered as a column of tooltips is worse than one that admits it needs room. The counts survive the collapse deliberately — they are what says whether a source is worth clicking, and without them the collapsed rail is a row of anonymous glyphs. The mobile drawer is never collapsed; it opens at full width, since an icons-only tree inside a panel you had to tap open would be two gestures to reach one folder.

  Kept out of the URL, like the per-branch expansion state: a bookmark reproduces *which tasks you are looking at*, and a link that also folded someone else's sidebar would make two URLs mean the same thing.

### Fixed
- **A rail count promised 363 and delivered 6 — for the length of one commit** (2026-08-15). With Favorites selected, `All sources` read `6`; clicking it showed all 363. The rail population neutralized source, category and the system lens, but not `favorites`, so every rail count was being taken *inside* the starred scope — including the counts for the rows that exist to leave it. Caught in the browser immediately after the feature landed, and it is the same rule as the two before it: **a population narrowed by a dimension the control *offers* can never count the alternatives.** Third instance now (the status chip's `Showing All 269`, the system-hidden chip's `257`, this) — the shape is stable enough that the rail's population comment names it directly.

### Fixed
- **The "N system hidden" chip could name a number nothing would reveal** (2026-08-15). It printed `applySystemLens`'s count, which is how many OS-owned tasks **exist** (257 here) — while the chip's own label claims what *this list* is withholding. Under the *Failures* view, clicking it revealed 111. The two were the same value until the source rail landed and put a faceted count of the same set two inches away, so nothing had ever printed them side by side. The chip is now faceted (every other lens applied, so it predicts the click); the existence count survives under its own name and keeps its one legitimate job — gating whether the Ownership control appears at all, which must not vanish merely because the current view contains no system tasks. Same defect the status chip had when it printed `Showing All 269` above two rows: **a count beside a filter control is a promise about what clicking it does.**

### Added
- **API tokens with selectable expiry, and revocation** (2026-08-15). **Settings → Account → API tokens**: name a token, pick **30 / 60 / 90 days or never**, confirm with your password, copy it once. The list shows each token's last-used date and revokes any one of them individually. Backed by `ApiToken` and `POST`/`GET`/`DELETE /api/auth/tokens`.

  **`never` is only on the menu because these are revocable.** A permanent credential you can withdraw is a convenience; one you cannot is a liability — and until now nothing could be withdrawn at all, since the only way to kill a token was rotating `JWT_SECRET`, which signs out every client at once. That is also why this shipped as a *manager* rather than a "copy your token" button.

  **The database stores the `jti` and nothing else.** The signature already proves authenticity, so the only question a row has to answer is *"has this been withdrawn?"* — storing the token, or a hash of it, would be a second copy of a credential with no use for it. The token is shown once and there is no reveal endpoint, because there is nothing to reveal.

  **Revocation covers every door.** `checkToken` is a single definition shared by the REST middleware and the Socket.IO handshake: a revoked token that the API refuses but the live-update channel still accepts would keep streaming task updates, which is the half nobody would think to test. It **fails closed** — if the database cannot be asked, the answer is `503`, never "assume valid".

  **And it cost the hot path nothing.** Only tokens carrying a `jti` are looked up; a browser session has none, so the dashboard's poll still verifies a signature and stops. `lastUsedAt` is throttled to roughly a minute so an active client does not turn every read into a write.

  **Browser sessions stay at 24h**, deliberately decoupled — that coupling was the known limit of the `JWT_EXPIRES_IN` change below, and this closes it. Issuing requires your password even though you are already signed in: this mints a credential that can outlive every session, so a borrowed open tab must not be enough to create one. Covered by 14 integration tests against real Postgres, including IDOR (one owner cannot revoke another's token, and the refusal is indistinguishable from not-found).

### Fixed
- **A drifted migration checksum made `prisma migrate dev` demand a database reset** (2026-08-15). `20260728203202_add_task_exclusions` had explanatory comments added to it *after* it was applied, which changes its checksum without changing a line of SQL. Prisma reads that as history it cannot trust and offers to drop the schema — on a dev machine holding 365 real tasks. `migrate status` reported "up to date" throughout, so nothing surfaced it until someone tried to add a migration. Reconciled by verifying the live `TaskExclusion` table against the file's DDL column-by-column (identical, including both indexes and the FK's `ON DELETE RESTRICT`) and then updating the stored checksum. **Never edit an applied migration** — even a comment; put the explanation in the schema or the docs.

### Security
- **Cronsole can issue the credential its own MCP integration requires** (2026-08-15). Token lifetime was hardcoded to `24h` — right for a browser tab, useless for a long-lived stdio client that cannot re-authenticate. So [`MCP_Server_Guide.md`](user-guides/guides/MCP_Server_Guide.md#getting-a-token) told users to run `jsonwebtoken.sign(..., {expiresIn:'30d'})` by hand, with the backend's `JWT_SECRET` in scope and a `userId` read out of the database. **That was not a workaround someone invented; it was the documented path** — a stranger following our own guide had to forge a credential to use a feature we ship.

  **`JWT_EXPIRES_IN`** (default `24h`, so nothing changes for the browser) makes the long-lived token supported. The guide now says *log in and read `token` off the response* — no signing secret, no database lookup, no Node. Login and setup also return **`expiresIn`**, because the failure being fixed is a credential lapsing *silently*: an expired token makes the MCP server refuse to start, so its tools go **missing** rather than erroring, which reads as "the integration is broken" instead of "my token expired" ([#8](troubleshooting/README.md#8-every-mcp-tool-returns-403-invalid-or-expired-token)).

  **Two details that are the difference between working and subtly broken.** The value is **probe-signed at boot** rather than pattern-matched — `ms` accepts a wide, undocumented range of spellings (`2 days`, `1y`, `10h`), so any regex would reject valid values or admit invalid ones; the probe also rejects values that parse but mint an already-expired token (`0`, negatives). And an all-digits value is converted to a **number** so it means *seconds*: `jsonwebtoken` passes a string to `ms`, which reads a unitless string as **milliseconds**, so `JWT_EXPIRES_IN=3600` would have issued **3.6-second** tokens — every login appearing to succeed and every request after it 403ing. Pinned by a test, and mutation-tested (removing the conversion yields a 3-second token).

  **This is part (a) of the reopened P0, not the whole of it.** `JWT_EXPIRES_IN` is deliberately one value for *all* tokens, so raising it for the MCP server also lengthens browser sessions — a 30-day token in `localStorage`, including on a phone now that remote access ships. Separating them, and adding **revocation** (today a leaked token can only be killed by rotating `JWT_SECRET`, which signs out everything), needs the named-token surface still open on the [roadmap](ROADMAP.md).
- **A real owner token was compiled into the production frontend bundle** (found and fixed 2026-08-15). `api.ts` read an optional dev/E2E fallback, `import.meta.env.VITE_DEV_TOKEN`, used whenever nobody is logged in. Vite loads `.env.local` in **every** mode — not just `dev` — and inlines each `VITE_*` reference as a literal, so `npm run build` baked a valid JWT for the owner account (`exp` 2036) straight into `dist/assets/*.js`. Any browser that loaded the built dashboard was **already authenticated**: the login screen rendered only when the token was absent, so on a built copy it effectively never did.

  **The blast radius was local until now, which is exactly why this had to be fixed first.** `dist/` had never been served anywhere but localhost. The single-origin proxy below serves that same bundle through a public Cloudflare tunnel — so shipping the remote-access work without this fix would have published a permanent credential for an app whose purpose is running commands on your machine.

  **The fix is structural, not a rule to remember.** The reference is now gated on `import.meta.env.DEV`, which is statically `false` in any build, so the branch folds and the minifier drops the string — the secret cannot reach a bundle even when `VITE_DEV_TOKEN` is set in the building environment. Dev and the Playwright E2E suite are unaffected.

  **And it is verified on every build.** `frontend/scripts/check-bundle-secrets.mjs` scans the build output for credential-shaped literals (JWTs, AWS/GitHub/Anthropic/OpenAI keys, private-key blocks) and fails with the offending file; it is wired into both `npm run build` and `npm run build:remote`, and exposed as `npm run check:bundle`. It deliberately reads **`dist/`, not the source** — the source never contained the secret, the compiler added it, so a linter or a source grep would have reported the code as clean. Mutation-tested: reverting the gate makes it fail.

  **The general rule, now written down:** a `VITE_*` variable is *public by construction*. The prefix means "safe to publish", not "available to the frontend". See [troubleshooting #55](troubleshooting/README.md#55-the-dashboard-is-already-signed-in-on-a-browser-that-never-logged-in).
- **MCP `delete_task` is now Cronsole-native only, and archives before it destroys** (2026-08-13). The one MCP verb that could not be undone previously had the *widest* reach on the surface: it wrapped the same `DELETE /api/tasks/:id` the UI uses, so with the gate open an agent could remove a real Windows Task Scheduler entry through the elevated local agent. It now wraps **`DELETE /api/tasks/:id/native`**, which refuses every other platform with a `400`.

  **The resulting property is whole-surface: no MCP tool can destroy an artifact on your machine.** Removing a Windows task over MCP means `untrack_task` — Cronsole's row goes, the scheduled task keeps running — and genuinely destroying one needs a human in the Cronsole UI. The refusal is worded as a boundary rather than a "not yet", so an assistant reports it instead of hunting for a flag.

  **The restriction lives in the backend route, not in the wrapper.** A platform check inside `mcp-server/` would be a client-side check the REST API still ignores, so the guarantee would hold only for callers who went through the wrapper — which is not a guarantee. `mcp-server/` still owns no logic.

  **The env gate stays shut by default.** The blast radius is now bounded and recoverable, which weakens the case for `CRONSOLE_MCP_ALLOW_DESTRUCTIVE` — but loosening two safety dimensions in one change would mean a later failure could not be attributed to either.

### Added
- **Remote access: a single-origin reverse proxy and a Cloudflare Tunnel profile** (2026-08-15). Reaching your own instance from a phone previously meant exposing **two** origins (`:7373` and `:3000`) and typing the **Settings → About → API origin** override into every device — invisible `localStorage` state, easy to get wrong, lost when site data is cleared. Two opt-in Compose profiles replace that:

  ```bash
  docker compose --profile proxy  up -d   # Caddy only — verify at 127.0.0.1:8080
  docker compose --profile remote up -d   # proxy + cloudflared
  ```

  **One origin.** `proxy/Caddyfile` serves the built dashboard at `/` and forwards `/api/*` and `/socket.io/*` to the backend. Socket.IO's `/ui` namespace rides inside the protocol rather than the URL, so the one socket rule covers live updates. A `try_files` fallback keeps React Router deep links (`/tasks/:id`) working on refresh — the exact link someone sends to their phone.

  **`npm run build:remote`** builds with `VITE_API_URL=same-origin`, a new sentinel that resolves the API against `window.location` instead of a baked-in address. One build is now correct at every address it is served from — the tunnel hostname and `localhost:8080` — which is what removes the per-device override. It is a **build-time** value only: `setApiOrigin` refuses to *store* it, because a saved value that resolves against the page would freeze whichever address it was saved at.

  **`TRUST_PROXY`** (backend, off by default) makes Express read the client address from `X-Forwarded-For`. Behind the proxy without it, every request appears to come from the proxy and the login rate-limiter collapses to one global bucket — ten wrong passwords from anywhere lock the owner out. Off by default because trusting the header is the widening choice: on a directly-reachable `:3000` it lets a caller forge their address and evade the limiter.

  **The proxy binds `127.0.0.1:8080`, not `0.0.0.0`.** cloudflared reaches it over the compose network, and Tailscale reaches it with `tailscale serve --bg --http=8080 http://127.0.0.1:8080`, which re-serves loopback **on the tailnet only** — so both paths get one origin without anything binding a public interface. Binding `0.0.0.0` would publish the dashboard to whatever Wi-Fi the machine is on. Note the explicit `--http=`: the shorter `tailscale serve --bg 8080` implies TLS and **hangs indefinitely** provisioning a certificate when the tailnet has HTTPS disabled, rather than failing with a message. Plain HTTP is not a hole here — a tailnet is WireGuard-encrypted device to device — but HTTPS is a one-toggle upgrade, at the cost of publishing machine names to public Certificate Transparency logs.

  **`TRUST_PROXY` is a per-deployment decision, not a consequence of the proxy.** Set it behind Cloudflare Tunnel (only `proxy:80` is routed, so the proxy is genuinely the only way in); leave it unset on Tailscale, where the tailnet exposes the whole machine and `:3000` stays directly addressable, making a trusted `X-Forwarded-For` forgeable. The same proxy yields opposite answers. Guide: [Remote Access](user-guides/guides/Remote_Access_Guide.md). Verified end to end on this machine — dashboard, API, SPA deep link, Socket.IO handshake, and a `401` on an unauthenticated read, all through the single origin.

### Fixed
- **A `${VAR:?}` in an unused Compose profile broke every `docker compose` command** (2026-08-15). The `tunnel` service declared its token as required, and Compose interpolates the **whole file before it selects a profile** — so `docker compose up -d`, which starts only Postgres and Redis, failed on a missing Cloudflare credential, as did `docker compose config`. A tunnel token had become a precondition for starting the database. Now `${CLOUDFLARE_TUNNEL_TOKEN:-}`; an unset token surfaces in `docker compose logs tunnel` instead. It cannot be caught earlier in-container — `cloudflare/cloudflared` is distroless, with no shell to wrap the command in. ([#54](troubleshooting/README.md#54-a-compose-profile-you-never-start-breaks-every-compose-command))

### Changed
- **The app icon gains an emerald ring, and the toolbar stops showing a `T`** (2026-08-13). The favicon is a near-black plate, which dissolves into a dark browser tab strip — at 16px only the grid read, floating with no edge. A solid `#10b981` ring now sits at the plate edge (inset by half its 14px stroke, so the stroke's outer edge lands on the edge), matching the Agent-Chat icon set.

  **It replaces the gradient outline that sat 20px in rather than joining it.** Two concentric rings 20px apart are about a pixel each with a pixel of gap at 16px, which is the size a favicon is actually judged at, and they turn to mush. The purple half of that gradient survives in the bars and dots. The light variant takes the same ring in `#059669` — a near-white plate dissolving into a light tab strip is the same failure, not a different one.

  **The app's own toolbar mark was a purple tile with the letter `T` — TaskHub's initial, still there through the whole rename.** Both instances (the desktop sidebar and the mobile top bar) now render the real icon. Applied to `frontend/public/favicon.svg`, both canonical assets under `images/Cronsole-Images/icons/`, and the inline data-URI favicon in `registry-site/index.html` — which reaches two hosts, so it needs **both** publish scripts. The `02-schedule-orbit` and `03-agent-pulse` concepts are unused alternates and were left alone.

- **The Templates and Tools tabs are tightened, and the template filters collapse into one control** (2026-08-13). The Templates tab stacked four permanently-open facet rows — Target, OS, Category, Tags — under the search box. With the catalog at 66 templates the Tags row alone ran to two lines, so the filters were taller than the first template card and the page opened on its own controls. They now live behind a single **Filters** button (`components/TemplateFilterBar.tsx`).

  **Collapsing the controls does not collapse what they withhold** — the rule inherited from the dashboard's `TaskFilterMenu`, which paid for it twice. The trigger carries the active-filter count, every active facet stays outside the drawer as a removable pill (`Target: Cronsole ×`), and a `2 of 7` line sits beside them. This tab gets off lighter than the dashboard did: every facet here defaults to *All*, so a shut drawer over an unfiltered list is hiding nothing. If a facet ever gains a non-All default it belongs outside the drawer with its count, the way the system and status lenses are.

  **Template cards are now the size of dashboard task cards** — same `rounded-2xl`, same padding, and the same `md:2 / xl:3` grid at `gap-4` instead of `lg:2` at `gap-6`. They were visibly larger, which made the two card grids read as two different products. Descriptions are clamped to two lines and the tag list caps at three plus a `+N`, so one template with nine tags no longer sets the row height for its neighbours.

  **The Tools tab is one card per row, and the cards are as tall as their content.** All eight repeated the same shell literal — `p-6 space-y-5 flex flex-col h-full min-h-[26rem]` — so the size lived in eight places and could only drift; it is now one `ToolCard` component at `p-4 space-y-3`, with the header icon and copy scaled to match.

  **Both the height floor and `h-full` are gone, because the two-column grid that needed them is gone.** They existed to tidy a side-by-side layout: `h-full` levelled the pair in each row, `min-h` stopped a short card looking stubby beside a tall one. What that actually did was hand the shorter card the taller one's slack as dead space — and with a chart in one card and four numbers in another, there was always a shorter one. Task health showed it worst, centring its summary in the middle of a hole. A stack has no pairs to balance, so each card is its own height and the padding is the only spacing left. Everything that can run long (the health list, a restore plan, the idle report) already carried its own `max-h-* overflow-y-auto`, which is what made removing the card height safe rather than a way to produce a 3,000px card.

  **Tools and Platforms now share one centred column at `max-w-6xl`.** Both screens are a single column of full-width panels, so they have to agree on where that column ends — otherwise two tabs of the same app read as two layouts. On Platforms the width moved from the individual `<section>`s onto the page wrapper, so the heading is centred with the cards it introduces instead of starting at a different left edge.

  **Form controls are capped too.** An uncapped `w-full` turned a 5-field cron into a 1,000px input with the value sitting in the first inch. The cron field and target select cap at `max-w-md`, the CSV period at `max-w-xs`, and the export folder select at `max-w-xl` — wider on purpose, since those are full Task Scheduler paths.

- **One Edit button per task, instead of four scattered ones** (2026-08-13). The task modal carried four separate edit affordances in four places, with three different shapes: a **pencil** beside the title (inline rename form), a **"Change"** link on the Local Category card (inline input), an **"Edit"** in the Action panel header (opening one of two modals depending on platform), and **"Edit Schedule"** in the footer (a third modal). They are now a single **Edit** button opening one `EditTaskModal` with name, category, schedule and what-it-runs in one form. `EditScheduleModal`, `EditActionModal` and `EditNativeJobModal` are deleted; their field groups live on as `components/edit/*Fields.tsx`, and the prefill + gating rules moved to `utils/taskEditing.ts` so the same rule decides both what a field is prefilled with and whether its section renders at all.

  **One gesture, still three routes — and the save reports each one separately.** `PATCH /tasks/:id` (labels), `PATCH /tasks/:id/schedule`, and `PATCH /tasks/:id/actions` or `/job` remain three routes, deliberately: a label write is a DB row this process owns, while a Windows schedule or command change is an elevated agent round trip Windows can refuse. So only changed sections are sent, sequentially, and each records its own outcome — a section that succeeds is re-baselined and goes clean, a section that fails keeps its values and its error, and pressing Save again retries **only what is outstanding**. Same per-item rule the bulk verbs follow, for the same reason: at this scale partial success is the normal case.

  **All-or-nothing was considered and rejected**: rolling back the successful half runs through the same offline agent that just failed, so the guarantee would break exactly when it was needed and leave the half-edited task it promised to prevent.

  **A part this platform cannot change is now a sentence, not a greyed-out button.** The old controls disabled themselves with the reason in a `title=` tooltip — unreadable on the phone this app is required to work on, and the reason was the useful half. The Edit button itself is never disabled, because name and category are editable on every source, so "nothing here can be changed" is never true.

  Closing with unsaved changes now asks first. Row-level category editing on the task **card** is unchanged — it is a one-word shortcut on a different surface, not a second way to edit a task.

### Fixed
- **An open edit modal no longer closes by itself, discarding what you typed** ([#52](troubleshooting/README.md#52-an-open-edit-modal-closes-by-itself-discarding-what-you-typed), 2026-08-13). `TaskModal`'s reset effect depended on the **task object**, and the dashboard passes `tasks.find(t => t.id === routeTaskId)` — a new object on every refetch. So the 45-second poll, any `task:updated` socket event, and the editor's own post-save invalidation each re-ran it and slammed any open editor shut, silently. Now keyed on `task?.id`, which is the identity the effect was always asking about. Pre-existing — it hit `EditScheduleModal` too — but merging four short-lived single-field modals into one longer form widened the window enough to make it a real loss: **the defect did not change, the exposure did.**

### Added
- **The prompt library is reorganized by what you're trying to do, and covers every source** (2026-08-13). [`docs/prompts/`](prompts/README.md) had one page of MCP prompts written against a 16-tool surface; the surface is now **27 tools across four platforms**, and the page had no prompts for headless coding-agent runs, Cronsole-native jobs, or Claude routines — three of the four things a user is most likely to want.

  The MCP section is now a folder of eight task-shaped sets rather than one list: [**Windows tasks**](prompts/mcp-server/windows-tasks.md), [**AI agent jobs**](prompts/mcp-server/ai-agent-jobs.md) (headless Claude Code / Codex against a local repo, with the four fences that make an unattended coding agent safe to schedule), [**Cronsole-native**](prompts/mcp-server/native-tasks.md) (HTTP and script jobs, and the execution-host question that decides whether a path even exists), [**Claude routines**](prompts/mcp-server/claude-routines.md), [**templates**](prompts/mcp-server/templates.md), [**inspect & audit**](prompts/mcp-server/inspect-and-audit.md), [**manage**](prompts/mcp-server/manage-tasks.md), and [**cleanup & removal**](prompts/mcp-server/cleanup-and-removal.md). The hub gains an "I want to…" table so the entry point is the goal rather than the surface.

  **One prompt per CLI, and an honest line between verified and not.** The AI-agent set covers
  Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, opencode, Cursor, Antigravity and Aider — with a table of where
  each one's fence actually sits, because they differ: Claude Code fences per tool, Codex by
  sandbox, and three of them have no allowlist at all, so their only headless mode is blanket
  auto-approve and the fence has to move to the checkout. Only the Claude Code and Codex
  invocations are template-backed and verified; **every other prompt instructs the assistant to
  read `--help` before writing the command line** rather than reprinting flags this repo hasn't
  checked. Antigravity gets the sharper warning it needs: a GUI-first tool scheduled with no
  desktop session hangs silently, so "does it even have a headless mode" is the first question,
  not the command line.

  **The prompts carry the refusals, not just the happy path.** Each set states what Cronsole will decline and why — folder-must-exist, `\Microsoft\` denied, untrack refused on native and Claude, delete native-only and archived first — because a prompt library that only shows the successes teaches an assistant to route around them.

  The REST set picks up the surfaces that landed since it was written (bulk verbs, analytics, CSV history, deleted-task archives, bulk export/restore, Claude routine declarations), and the skill set gains connector and capability-matrix prompts. Linked from the root README, the docs hub, the User Guides index, the MCP Server Guide, the `mcp-server` package README, `skills/README.md`, the templates catalog, and the Sources Guide. **Adding or removing an MCP tool now obligates this folder** — recorded in the mirror-surface tables in `CLAUDE.md` §11a and `SKILL.md`.

- **The template library covers the two newest sources: 11 new templates, and the Apply path that makes them real** (2026-08-13). The catalog goes from **55 to 66 templates (7 core + 59 extended) in 8 packs**, adding a **Cronsole Native** pack (6) and a **Claude Routines** pack (5).

  **The gap this closes is bigger than a content update.** Cronsole-native had been a first-class source for six weeks — its own place on the dashboard's source bar, its own capability row, its own MCP create tools — and **not one of the 55 templates targeted it**. A user with no agent installed opened the Templates tab and found nothing they could use, from the one source that needs nothing installed.

  **Cronsole Native** — `native-run-program` and `native-http-uptime-check` are **core** (they ship built in, because native works on a fresh install with no agent and no credential), plus Node and Python script starters, a `git pull --ff-only` repo refresher, and a dead-man's-switch heartbeat ping. The script entries say **where they run** in their own parameter help: a native job runs wherever the *backend* runs, which is inside the container on a Dockerized stack — a path you can see in Explorer is simply not there.

  **Claude Routines** — issue triage, dependency review, CI failure digest, docs drift, and a standup digest. These are **not** the existing `ai-claude-*` templates, which register a Windows task that shells out to the Claude Code CLI on your machine; these create a real routine that Anthropic runs in the cloud against the repositories you attach. None is `core`: creating one needs a readable Claude Code session on the machine running the backend, and a template that ships to every install should be applicable by every install.

  **Applying a native template only works because the connector was fixed in the same change.** `CronsoleNativeConnector.createTask` refused every command that was not a URL — true when native could only ping a URL, false from the day it gained script jobs — so a native script template would have applied to an error message. `buildNativeJob` moved out of `routes/tasks.ts` into `services/nativeJob.ts` and is now shared by the create route, the edit route and the connector: **one definition of what a native task runs**, or a template stores a spec creation itself would have refused. The command decides the job type — a URL is an HTTP job, anything else is a program — which is safe in the only direction that matters, since `https://…` is not a runnable executable.

  **Two latent defects fell out of it.** `POST /api/tasks` upserted a task row for *every* platform after the connector create, which for native would have overwritten `metadata.job` with the `{schedule, command}` shape other platforms carry — a task the executor refuses to run, after a create that reported success. It was unreachable only because nothing had created a native task through the connector. And applying a **Claude** template created a real routine at Anthropic while tracking nothing until the next sync; the apply route now tracks every platform except native (which writes its own row), and records `create` capability evidence like `POST /api/tasks` already did.

  Applying a Claude template also takes **repositories** now — one URL per line, never defaulted or guessed, because a routine with no sources still runs (it just has no checkout) while attaching the wrong repository to an agent that can commit is not a mistake you can see before it happens.

- **The Templates tab asks the server what it can create, instead of holding a list** (2026-08-13). "Compatible with" badges, the platform buttons in Apply, and the new **Target** facet all read `GET /api/tools/platforms` — the same per-install capability matrix the Platforms tab renders, with the evidence behind each claim.

  **The list it replaces was wrong in both directions inside two days.** `CREATABLE_PLATFORMS = {WINDOWS_TASK_SCHEDULER, TASKHUB_NATIVE}` marked Claude uncreatable while `create_claude_routine` shipped, and marked native creatable while its connector refused every non-URL command. It could not have been right anyway: **for Claude the answer is a property of your install** — with a Claude Code session `create` works, without one it is a boundary — so no constant compiled into the bundle can be true in both cases.

  **Three answers, not two.** Verified and declared both mean "you can apply this" (refusing *declared* would wait for evidence only applying can produce). While the matrix is still loading the tab **asserts nothing**: the target stays selectable, no "can't create here" marker appears, and only the **Create Task** button waits — an optimistic selection is recoverable, an optimistic submit is a request the platform may refuse.

  A **Target** facet joins OS, Category and Tags in the tab (and in the public gallery), because three of the four target families are `cross-platform` and the OS chips can no longer answer "what can I actually create this on".

- **Claude Code routines can now be listed, created, rescheduled and paused from Cronsole** (2026-08-13). Previously Cronsole could only *fire* a routine you had already created elsewhere and pasted a token for. It can now read the real routines on your account — their names, schedules, enabled state and next run times — create new ones, change when they run, and pause and resume them.

  **Why this was thought impossible.** Anthropic documents exactly one routines endpoint (`POST /v1/claude_code/routines/{id}/fire`, scoped *"one routine only; no read access"*), and Cronsole's connector was built around that as a fact about the platform. It was a fact about the *documentation*: Claude Code itself has always created and listed routines through a second, undocumented API (`/v1/code/triggers`) authenticated with your Claude Code account session rather than a per-routine token. That is what `/schedule` in the CLI uses.

  **It turns on by itself, on a host-run stack, if you are signed into the Claude Code CLI.** Cronsole reads the session from `~/.claude/.credentials.json` at request time. There is nothing to configure and nothing new to paste. Sync, and your routines appear with real schedules.

  **What you no longer need:** a per-routine API token. Firing works with the account session, so the paste-a-token-per-routine flow becomes optional — useful given claude.ai shows each token exactly once. The existing token registry still works and is still the fallback.

  **Limits, stated plainly.** The underlying API is undocumented and beta-gated, so it can change without notice — Cronsole falls back to the per-routine token path if it does, rather than breaking.
