It is unavailable inside the Dockerized backend (the credentials file is on your machine, not in the container); set `CLAUDE_OAUTH_TOKEN` there if you want it. Cronsole **never stores or refreshes** the credential — refreshing would rotate the CLI's own token and could sign you out of Claude Code. And **routines still cannot be deleted from Cronsole**: neither Claude API exposes a delete, so Cronsole can disable a routine but removing it happens at claude.ai.

- **MCP: `create_claude_routine`** (2026-08-13). Create a real Claude Code routine from an assistant — name, prompt, UTC cron, optional repositories and tool allowlist. `list_claude_routines` now also reports `session.mode`, so an assistant can tell whether this install can create routines before offering to. Routines created this way arrive with **no MCP connectors attached** deliberately: on a real account the server would otherwise attach all of them, and a routine made from one sentence must not silently arrive holding your mailbox.

- **Deleted tasks are archived before they are destroyed** (2026-08-13). `DELETE /api/tasks/:id/native` writes the task's full definition and its **last 20 run records** to a new `DeletedTaskArchive` table *before* the delete transaction, and **refuses the delete if that write fails** — leaving the task completely unchanged. A backup that only succeeds when you did not need it is worse than none, so the archive is a precondition of the destruction rather than a best-effort side effect.

  **The archive deliberately has no relation to `Task`, and therefore no cascade.** It has to outlive the row it describes — this is the `TaskExclusion` shape, not the `TaskFavorite` one. Wiring it to `Task` with a cascade would delete the backup in the same transaction that made it necessary, and every other test would still have passed.

  **It captures run history because for a native task that history is real evidence** — exit code, duration, captured output. (On a Windows task a `SUCCESS` only records the agent accepting a start, which is why that judgement lives in Windows.) Without it the archive cannot answer "was this working before I deleted it?".

  **Read it back at `GET /api/tools/task-archives`** (list) and `GET /api/tools/task-archives/:id` (full bundle + captured runs), both scoped to the owner — an archive holds a complete job spec, headers included. A write-only backup is most of the way back to having no backup, since one nobody can enumerate is one nobody restores from.

  The archived bundle is built by the **same function** that serves `GET /api/tasks/:id/export`, so the download and the archive cannot drift into two formats — a drift that would only surface on the day someone tried to restore.

- **In-app help, per control** (2026-08-12): a **?** button beside the things that most often surprise people, each opening one topic — what the control is, the two or three non-obvious facts about it, and a link to the section of the docs that covers it in full. Fourteen placements: the Dashboard title, the Source bar, the Views row, the Filters drawer, four in the New Task modal (platform · schedule · job type · command), the task modal header, the schedule and native-job editors, the Import modal, the Templates and Platforms headers, each Platforms row, Mass actions and Task health.

  **The Help Center and the `?` are one modal, not two.** Opened from the header it is the hub; opened from a `?` it is a single topic. They share a component because the interesting part is the seam: every topic ends in **Browse all help**, and the hub carries an index of every topic. The two entry points fail in opposite directions — a `?` is only findable once you are already looking at the control it explains, and a hub is only useful if it can reach what those buttons say — so each covers the other.

  **Source help follows the source you are on.** The `?` on the Source bar opens Windows, Cronsole (HTTP), Cronsole (Scripts) or Claude specifically, and the overview on *All sources*; the same resolution puts per-platform help on each Platforms row and on the New Task platform selector, where it changes as you switch. An unrecognized source degrades to its platform and then to the overview, the same way `sourceLabel` does, so a connector landing before its help copy still opens something true.

  **A topic must link to a doc.** `HelpTopic.doc` is required, and a topic with nothing to point at is one explaining something undocumented — the fix for which is a doc, not a longer popover that quietly becomes the only place a rule is written down.

- **A new [Sources Guide](user-guides/guides/Sources_Guide.md)** (2026-08-12): one section per source — Windows Task Scheduler, Cronsole (HTTP), Cronsole (Scripts), Claude Code routines, plus quick links and what's next — covering what Cronsole can and cannot do with each, and the things that catch people out. Written because the UI guide is organised by *screen* and the question "what is this system, and what can Cronsole do with it?" is organised by *source*; it is also the deep-link target for every source help topic.

- **Edit what a Cronsole-native task runs** (2026-08-12): an Edit pencil on the task modal's Action section, `PATCH /api/tasks/:id/job`, and an `update_native_job` MCP tool (25 tools). Change an HTTP job's URL, method, headers or body; change a script job's command or working directory; or convert between the two. **A native HTTP task's URL previously could not be changed at all** — the only route to a different URL was delete and recreate, losing the run history.

  **Deliberately a separate route from `PATCH /:id/actions`**, which is the Windows path, because the two are different operations rather than different shapes. Editing a Windows action asks the elevated agent to rewrite a task on the machine, can be refused by the platform, and needs the agent online. Here the DB row *is* the task: the write is the change, nothing can refuse it, and it works with the agent offline.

  **It replaces the job rather than patching it.** The two job types share no fields, so a merge would leave a stored `url` sitting inside an EXEC job — something the executor never reads and a reader cannot explain. Switching type is allowed and never accidental: the form names what will be discarded *before* the click, and the task keeps its name, schedule, category and history either way.

  Normalization and validation are the **same two functions the create route uses**, so an edit cannot produce a spec that creation would have refused — the kind of thing that is accepted at edit time and fails at 3am. Commands are tokenized server-side into `{executable, args[]}` with **no shell**, and the editor states which host a script actually runs on, since a native job executes wherever the backend does.

- **Rename any task** (2026-08-12): a pencil beside the title in the task modal, `name` accepted by `PATCH /api/tasks/:id`, and a `rename_task` MCP tool (24 tools). Works on every platform.

  **A name is a Cronsole label, exactly like a category — not the machine.** Nothing is renamed on the platform: a Windows task keeps its Task Scheduler path, so the modal keeps showing the real `externalId` and, once the two diverge, says *"Renamed in Cronsole — Task Scheduler still calls it X"*. Silence there would send someone searching Task Scheduler for a name that was never in it.

  **Why this was blocked, and why the fix costs nothing.** `TaskService.upsertTasks` wrote `name` back from the platform on every sync, so a rename would have silently reverted minutes later. That line turns out to be structurally a no-op: **no platform can supply a new name for an existing row.** A Windows task's name is the last segment of its path, and the path *is* `externalId` — the key the row is matched on — so renaming a task in Task Scheduler produces a *different* task (old path MISSING, new path imported), never a new name on this one. Measured on a real machine before shipping: 354 Windows tasks, **zero** whose stored name differed from their path leaf. Claude's name comes from the registry the user declared; a native row *is* the task. So the line could only ever fire to undo a rename, and removing it puts `name` under the same protection `category` has always had.

  A rename **cannot collide**: the Windows duplicate-name guard exists because a *created* task's name becomes part of its path, and a rename never touches the path.

- **Fix a connected Claude routine's id or name without re-entering its token** (2026-08-12): a pencil on the Platforms routines panel, `PATCH /api/tools/platforms/claude/routines/:id`, and the `edit_claude_routine` MCP tool.

  Pasting a routine's **name** into the id field is the easy mistake — the connect form warns about it and saves anyway, because the id format is experimental and not promised. Until now the only fix was disconnect-and-reconnect, which **discards the stored token**; claude.ai shows a token once, so a typo cost a regeneration at Anthropic — which also revokes that token anywhere else it was used. A typo should not cost a credential.

  **The tracked task moves with the id.** A Claude task's `externalId` *is* the routine id, so re-pointing the config alone would strand the row: the old task would go MISSING at the next sync and a fresh one appear, losing its run history, star and category. The edit renames the row in the same operation and reports how many moved.

  There is deliberately **no token field on the edit form** — its absence is the feature. To rotate a token, add the routine again with the same id; re-connecting replaces it.

- **The MCP server grew from 16 tools to 22, and finally reaches `/api/tools`** (2026-08-12): every tool it had wrapped `/api/tasks` or `/api/templates`, so the entire cross-task surface — where most recent work landed — was invisible to an agent. Added:

  | Tool | What it answers |
  |---|---|
  | `list_platforms` | "What can Cronsole actually do with Windows?" — the capability matrix, with `verified` / `declared` / `unsupported` and the evidence behind each |
  | `get_task_health` | "What's broken?" — every task scored worst-first, each signal naming the field it came from |
  | `list_run_history` | "What failed this month?" — across tasks, unlike the per-task history |
  | `sync_tasks` | Import a category, or refresh what you already track |
  | `list_claude_routines` · `connect_claude_routine` · `disconnect_claude_routine` | Manage the Claude routines Cronsole can fire |

  **There is deliberately no `create_claude_routine`.** Anthropic exposes one routines endpoint (`fire`) and no create, so a tool by that name could only ever fail — and an agent reading `tools/list` would plan around a capability that does not exist. The tool is named `connect_claude_routine` for the verb it performs, and a test pins the absence of the other name.

  Two omissions that are decisions, not gaps. **The three bulk verbs stay REST-only**: friction has to scale with blast radius, and the UI earns that with a typed confirmation past 25 tasks — an MCP tool has no equivalent gesture, so a bulk untrack of 254 tasks would arrive with *less* deliberation than the single-task path. And `connect_claude_routine` takes a live credential as a parameter, so it lands in the host's transcript; the tool description says so and points at the UI when a human is present.

- **Connect a Claude routine straight from the Dashboard** (2026-08-12): **New Task → Claude** now takes a routine id and token, so you no longer have to go to Platforms and then Import. The routine appears on the dashboard in one step.

  **The modal renames itself for this option, and that is the point.** Cronsole *creates* a Cronsole-native or Windows task; it can only *connect* a Claude routine, because Anthropic exposes no create endpoint. So picking Claude changes the title to **"Connect a routine"**, the button to **"Connect Routine"**, and says outright that Cronsole cannot create, schedule or pause one. A "New Task" flow that quietly meant something else for one option is how someone ends up believing Cronsole made a routine it cannot make.

  The schedule field is **replaced by a sentence** rather than left in place: a routine's cadence lives at claude.ai and is unreadable through the single endpoint Anthropic exposes, so a cron box could only ever set a value Cronsole has nowhere to send — and the card would then advertise a schedule the routine does not follow. Category is gone too (routines file under `Claude` server-side), as are Job type and Command.

- **A Claude routine files under "Claude", not "Uncategorized"** (2026-08-12): Import is where you pick which categories to track, and a routine you typed into the Platforms tab by hand needs to be findable — `Uncategorized` also mixed it in with unrelated tasks. Windows keeps its folder-derived category; Claude has no hierarchy to reflect, so it is a constant.

- **You can actually add a Claude routine now** (2026-08-12): the Platforms tab gained a **Routines** panel on the Claude card — paste a routine's id (or its whole fire URL) and API token, name it, and it appears on the dashboard with a working Run button. Backed by `GET`/`POST`/`DELETE /api/tools/platforms/claude/routines`.

  This is the first connection config a user *composes* — every other platform gets credentials another way (the Windows agent pairs, Cronsole-native needs none), because Anthropic mints a **bearer token per routine** and exposes no API to list or manage them. Three things follow from that and are visible on screen: **the panel says Cronsole can't discover your routines**, since the absence of a Sync button isn't self-explanatory on a tab where everything else has one; **the token is write-only** — stored encrypted, never sent back, shown as *Token stored* rather than a row of dots, because dots imply something to reveal and claude.ai can't re-display it either; and the button says **Remove, not Delete**, because Cronsole cannot stop a Claude routine and a "Delete" label would let you believe a nightly job had been turned off while it kept firing. Removing states where the routine keeps running and counts the tasks it strands *before* the click.

  Pasting the whole fire URL works — the modal shows the URL next to the token, so that's the expected input, not an edge case. An id or token that doesn't match the shape claude.ai issues is **saved with a warning rather than refused**: `/fire` is experimental behind a dated beta header, so a hard format check would break the day Anthropic changes it.

- **Claude Code routines are a real source now — for the one thing Anthropic lets anyone do to them** (2026-08-12): a routine you declare in the Claude connection gets a dashboard row and a **Run** button that genuinely fires it, with the session URL in the result so you can watch it. It has been an untouched scaffold since the MVP.

  **Checking the API before scoping changed what the work was.** Anthropic exposes exactly one routines endpoint — `POST /v1/claude_code/routines/{trig_id}/fire` — and describes its token as *"One routine only; no read access."* There is no list, create, or enable/disable endpoint. So three of the four capabilities this was scoped for are **not unfinished, they are boundaries**, and the Platforms matrix now says `unsupported` rather than `declared` for `create` and `setStatus` — `declared` reads as *"reachable, just unproven"* and would leave you waiting for evidence that cannot arrive. Routines are still created and paused at claude.ai; Cronsole now says so in the place you'd look.

  Two consequences are worth knowing because they are unlike every other platform here. **Cronsole cannot see whether your routine is paused** — a paused routine is a `400` on fire, which is the *only* signal it ever gets, so that status now says "most often the routine is paused" instead of "Bad Request". And **listing is your declaration, not a read**: a routine deleted in claude.ai still appears until its next run returns `404`.

- **The dashboard opens on All, not on Favorites** (2026-08-12): a bare URL now resolves to the widest view. It opened on Favorites for about a day.

  The reasoning for reverting it is the machinery the old default needed. Opening on a subset the user chose by a gesture they may have made weeks ago is a constraint they did not ask for *today*, so it required a banner to stay honest — naming the filter, counting the tasks it withheld, and offering the way out. **That apparatus was itself the evidence the default wanted apologising for.** Opening on everything withholds nothing, so there is nothing to disclose, and the banner is deleted rather than left as dead code describing behaviour that no longer exists.

  The rule the banner encoded still stands and is kept in `CLAUDE.md` §9 for the next self-applied default: *a constraint the user did not choose must be named in the page, not in a tooltip.* Two of the four saved defaults still apply on open — `category` and `source` narrow along axes All says nothing about — while `status` and `system` are superseded, because they are exactly the lenses All *is*.

  The **Favorites** chip now carries the same amber star its tasks do, so the set is recognisable at a glance rather than by reading. It is the `warning` role token, not a raw `text-yellow-…` utility: one literal cannot stay legible in both themes.

- **The source bar splits Cronsole-native, and the views gain an All tab** (2026-08-12): the bar now reads *All sources · Cronsole (Scripts) · Cronsole (HTTP) · Windows Task Scheduler*. One bucket called "Cronsole" made the bar's most granular entry its least informative the moment native could hold two genuinely different kinds of task.

  **A source is now finer than a platform, and the two are kept apart deliberately.** A platform is what Cronsole talks to — one connector, one capability row, one `PlatformConnection`; a source is what you navigate by. The key is `PLATFORM` or `PLATFORM:SUBTYPE`, derived **server-side** (`services/taskSource.ts`) and sent per task like `isSystem`, because deciding it means reading the native job spec and a browser-side copy of that judgement is the drift that once took a folder out of every sync. Three consequences: **the Platforms tab does not split** (native HTTP and native scripts are the same connector with identical capabilities); **matching is prefix-aware**, so selecting the bare `TASKHUB_NATIVE` still matches both subtypes — which is what let this ship without silently emptying every stored default and every existing link; and **the selected source stays listed even when no task derives it**, or a pre-split link would filter the list while leaving the whole bar unlit, which is the lit-chip problem inverted.

  **All** joins the views as the widest lens — every task, including the ~257 Windows owns and anything disabled or missing. Every other view *withholds* something and there was no single click meaning "stop withholding"; reaching the full list took opening the drawer and changing two separate lenses, which made the most honest question the hardest to ask. Its count states the scale before you click.

  Fixed while adding it: the Filters badge read `2` on the All view — the widest possible state, withholding nothing — because it counted deviations from the dashboard *default*. It now counts what you have added on top of *the view you are on*, so it reads 0 there. A badge that fires when nothing is constrained is one people learn to ignore, which costs exactly the case it exists for.

- **Cronsole-native runs programs, not just HTTP** (2026-08-12): a native task can now be a **script or executable** — `New Task › Cronsole › Run a program`, or `create_native_script_task` over MCP. Until now native could only call a URL, which made the one source Cronsole fully owns useless for the most ordinary scheduled job there is.

  It reuses **`StructuredAction {executable, args[]}`** — the same no-shell primitive that already backs Windows task creation — so this is the existing P0 injection guarantee applied to a second executor rather than a new attack surface. A typed command line is tokenized **server-side** by the same parser the Windows path uses, so the browser never needs a copy of a security-relevant parse. And unlike a Windows run, where `SUCCESS` only means the agent accepted a start, the run history gets a **real verdict**: exit code, duration, and captured stdout/stderr.

  **Two things make it safe rather than merely working, and both are the point:**
  - **The child process inherits none of Cronsole's environment.** This backend holds `DATABASE_URL`, `JWT_SECRET` and the AES key that encrypts every `PlatformConnection.config`. A scheduled script inheriting `process.env` could read out the key protecting every stored platform credential — defeating encryption-at-rest through the same UI that created the task. `childEnv()` passes an allowlist of OS essentials plus whatever the job sets explicitly. Pinned by tests and mutation-tested; verified live, where a task asking for `$env:JWT_SECRET` printed `JWT_SECRET absent`.
  - **The task says where it will run.** A native job executes wherever the *backend* does — the user's machine on a host-run stack, and **inside the container** on the Dockerized one, against a filesystem that is not theirs, failing as "executable not found" for a file visible in Explorer. Same task, same UI, two meanings. The server detects it at boot (`/.dockerenv`, cgroups, `KUBERNETES_SERVICE_HOST`) and the New Task modal states which — with the evidence, not just the verdict.

  *Corollary worth keeping:* a script that must run **as the user** or survive Cronsole being down still belongs on the **agent**. The agent is the thing that unambiguously means "your machine".

- **A source bar — the dashboard's first-level axis** (2026-08-12): a row above the saved views, one button per system a task comes from: *All sources*, *Windows Task Scheduler*, *Cronsole (Native)*, and whatever lands next. Cronsole is growing past one scheduler, and "which system is this even from?" is the question you ask before "is it failing?", so it gets the outermost control. Platform moved **out** of the Filters popover in the same change — two controls for one dimension is the duplication that cost the dashboard its row selection.

  **Source is an outer lens, not a peer filter**, and that is the design decision the rest follows from. Picking a source does *not* drop the view bar to "Custom": `filtersEqual` now ignores `platform`, so *Failures* stays lit while you move between sources and the two chips read as one sentence — failing tasks, Windows only. That is allowed precisely because both are on screen at once; the rule it bends is about **hidden** constraints, and a selected button one line above is not hidden. Consequences, each of which had to be built: source rides *alongside* the view id in the URL (`?view=my-jobs&platform=TASKHUB_NATIVE`) so a bookmark reproduces what you see; saved views no longer store a platform (`viewFiltersFrom` strips it, because `matchView` would never read it); the Filters trigger stops counting it, since a badge may only describe what its own drawer can clear; and **view counts are taken inside the selected source** — reading `My jobs 88` above a list of one is the same broken promise as `Showing All 269` above two rows.

  Two things the build got wrong first, both caught by using it: the button list was derived from the *faceted* population, so on the Favorites view — where both starred tasks were Windows — the bar saw one source, decided there was nothing to choose between, and removed the only control that could switch away. **An outer lens may not disappear because an inner filter narrowed the list.** Existence now comes from the whole task list and only the *counts* stay faceted, which is why a source can legitimately read `0`: that zero predicts the empty list instead of hiding the way to it.

- **The Platforms tab is a capability matrix** (2026-08-12): it now leads with the platforms Cronsole can actually operate — **Windows Task Scheduler**, **Cronsole-native**, and the experimental **Claude Code** scaffold — each with its connection state, tracked task count, last real sync, and a row of ten capability chips. It previously did not mention Windows or Cronsole-native **at all**: the whole tab was three `localStorage` bookmarks to Claude, ChatGPT and Gemini, so the two platforms that work were visible only as a sidebar chip. The bookmarks survive as a *Quick links* section, which is the honest home for a platform with no connector.

  **Every cell is evidence, and the middle state is the point.** A verb reads **Verified** only when it has actually succeeded on this machine — and the cell carries the timestamp that earned it. A verb the route would accept but has never been observed to work reads **Declared**, never *yes*. A verb the route would refuse reads **Unsupported**. Expanding a row shows the per-verb table: state, last success, last failure and the platform's own reason for it.

  This required a new store, because none of it could be derived. The tempting shortcut — `typeof connector.deleteTask === 'function'` — is wrong twice: it is a claim about the code rather than about this install (the same shape [#40](troubleshooting/README.md#40-the-sidebar-says-windows-is-online-and-synced-just-now-while-every-agent-request-times-out) was fixed for), and **for Cronsole-native it is factually false** — the DB row *is* the task, so `routes/tasks.ts` deletes, reschedules and exports native tasks itself without touching a connector method. A connector-derived matrix would report that platform as unable to do three things it does every day. So reachability is a property of the **route**, stated in `services/platformCapabilities.ts` and pinned by a test that checks it against both the connector objects and the route source; evidence is a new `PlatformCapability` table written by the routes as they run.

- **A health strip on the dashboard** (2026-08-12): connection state, last real sync, and **the last thing Cronsole asked a platform to do and how it went**, in the page header. Deciding what to do next previously meant reading the sidebar, and even that could not tell you whether the last command worked. The strip reports the newest recorded outcome, **success or failure** — a status line that hid failures would go quiet exactly when something was wrong. With nothing recorded it says *"No commands run yet"* rather than showing a tick it has not earned.

- **Layout and visual-regression coverage** (2026-08-12): a Playwright suite over the dense surfaces — dashboard at 1280px and 375px, Templates, Tools, Platforms, and the New Task and Import modals. It is deliberately split: **pixels for chrome that does not move** (with live regions masked), **structural assertions for everything else**. A full-page baseline over 269 live tasks with ticking relative times fails on the next minute, and a suite that cries wolf gets switched off. The Platforms matrix and the Import modal get no pixel baseline at all — both are almost entirely live evidence, and masking them would leave a baseline of an empty frame that still breaks whenever a row's height moves.

  **This is also the 375px verification the roadmap recorded as never having been done.** The browser-resize route could not reach the tab, so the check kept being reasoned about rather than run; it now runs, and it caught real problems (below).

### Removed
- **Row selection is gone from the dashboard** (2026-08-12): the per-row checkboxes, the select-all, the bulk action bar and the bulk-category modal are all removed, along with `utils/taskSelection.ts` and the dashboard's four bulk mutations. **Bulk work is now only the Mass Actions console on the Tools tab.**

  Two ways to say "these tasks" was one too many, and selection was the weaker one: `254 selected` cannot survive into a confirmation as anything a person can check, and it capped out at what a single request would accept — on a real machine `Select all 269` built a batch every button then 400'd on. A scope survives (*"Disable 47 tasks in Backups"*) and it batches.

  **The objection raised against this turned out not to apply.** Keeping selection had been argued for on §9's rule that a gate making the safe path harder than the unsafe one is worse than no gate — the concern being that "Remove from Cronsole" would end up further from the Delete it exists as the alternative to. Checked before removing: **that pairing lives in the task modal, per task** (`TaskModal.tsx`, the two buttons adjacent), which is where it always actually was. The bulk bar was never what kept them together. Nothing about the dashboard's four bulk mutations was a second capability either — they called the same `/api/tools/tasks/*` routes the console calls, so this removed a second caller, not a feature.

### Changed
- **The mobile dashboard is one screen instead of a scroll** (2026-08-12) — the half of the thin-first-viewport pass that was left open. At 375px the saved-views bar was four stacked rows of chips, the header action row was three rows of buttons, and the filter toolbar was two rows that then scrolled away. Now: the **views bar is one horizontally-scrolling row** bled to the screen edge (so the cut-off chip is visibly cut off, and gets swiped); **Help Center and Import drop to icons**, keeping their names as `aria-label`/`title`; **New Task becomes a floating action button** — the *same* control, not a second one, since the header button is hidden at that width; and the **filter toolbar is one scrolling row and sticks to the top**, because on a phone it is the only way back out of a filtered list and one flick used to put it off-screen. Desktop is unchanged.

- **Icon-only controls are named** (2026-08-12): all **8 modal close buttons** (Apply, Clone, Create, Edit Action, Edit Schedule, Help, Import, Task detail) carried neither `title` nor `aria-label`, so every modal's dismiss was unnamed to a screen reader. The task card's category control was an unnamed `div` with an `onClick`, and is now a button.

  **The card itself was the bigger defect**: a clickable `<div>` is unreachable by keyboard and has no accessible name, so the dashboard's primary action was mouse-only. The title is now the real control — a button named *"Open details for &lt;task&gt;"* — which is where it had to go, since the card contains the row-action buttons and a button may not nest inside a button. A hover/focus *"Open details ›"* hint in the card footer names the card's default click, which nothing had said out loud.

- **The Mass Actions console is action-first** (2026-08-12): it now opens as a vertical list of what you can do — *Enable tasks*, *Disable tasks*, *Move to a category*, *Remove from Cronsole* — each with a one-line description of what it does and does not touch. Picking one opens its own scope step, the plan, and the run button; **All actions** goes back. **The scope opens on *By category*, not *All tasks***: starting pointed at every task would make the widest possible operation the path of least resistance, so you would have to actively narrow it to do the ordinary thing. Category is also how these tasks are already organised — a Windows task's category comes from its Task Scheduler folder — so it is both the common case and a scope that reads back. The starting value comes from `defaultScopeValue`, shared by the open and the kind-switch, because a category scope with an empty value resolves to nothing and would greet you with "no tasks here".

  The order was inverted deliberately. A list of verbs answers *"what can this do?"* on sight, where a scope picker with four buttons under it answers that only after a choice you had no basis for making. It also lets each action ask for only what it needs — Categorize wants a target category, the others don't — so the confirmation is the last thing you meet rather than where you discover a required field. Scope belongs to the chosen action rather than the card, so backing out cannot leave a half-built scope aimed at whatever you open next.

  One value, `willChange`, feeds the plan list, the button count and the confirmation, so those three cannot drift apart. It is the eligible set and never the scope size: 80 tasks in scope of which 70 run is a 10-task Enable.

- **The dashboard's first viewport is thinner, and the filters live behind one control** (2026-08-12): status, ownership, platform and category moved into a **Filters** popover (`TaskFilterMenu`) with an active-filter count on the trigger. Two full rows of category chips and the platform toggle are gone from the top level; on a real 269-task machine the first task now sits roughly a row-and-a-half higher.

  **The rule that shaped it: collapsing the controls must not collapse what they are withholding.** A closed drawer over a filtered list is the invisible fence with a nicer lid, which this project has paid for twice. So the split is by whether a lens speaks for itself. **Category and platform** became dismissible pills — you can read "Backups" and know the rest is elsewhere, and a count would add nothing. **System and status** print what they hold back *outside* the drawer, always (`189 system hidden`, `10 inactive hidden`), because they are **defaults nobody chose today** that withhold rows while looking like a neutral starting state — the one case where the user cannot be assumed to know. Each of those chips is also the control that undoes it. Verified live: opening the status lens made `10 inactive hidden` disappear while `189 system hidden` correctly stayed.

  `withheldBy` and `activeFilterCount` are pure and pinned by tests, so the rule survives edits to the component rather than living in JSX. The filter zone is now a `bg-raised` panel — the neutral step added earlier finally spent on the thing it was added for, separating the controls from the results they describe.

- **`TaskCard` is memoised** (2026-08-12): the dashboard re-renders the whole list on every search keystroke, and a full teardown of 255 cards measured ~63ms — a few dropped frames per character. The default shallow compare suffices only because the handlers are stable dashboard mutations rather than inline closures; if that stops being true the memo silently becomes a no-op, which is noted at the call site as the thing to watch.

  *Context worth recording: this was investigated because a browser pass appeared to show the dashboard freezing for 45s at 269 tasks. **It did not.** The tab was hidden, so `requestAnimationFrame` never fired and my probe awaited a frame that could not come; Chrome's intensive throttling of long-hidden tabs explains the rest. Measured properly, a theme toggle is 0.8ms at 13,228 DOM nodes. **No virtualization was added, because none is warranted** — the fix that isn't needed is worth naming as loudly as the one that is.*

### Fixed
- **"Every hour, 9–5, on weekdays" is no longer reported as a perfect conversion of a task that runs once a day** (2026-08-13). A list or a range in a cron's **hour or minute** field — `0 9-17 * * 1-5`, `0 9,17 * * *`, `0,30 9 * * *` — was accepted at **confidence 1.0 with no warnings** and built a malformed Windows start time (`"9-17:00"`, `"09:0,30"`).

  This is the `parseInt('1-5')` day-of-week bug fixed on 2026-07-14, one field over, where it was missed: `parseInt('9-17')` is `9`, so both fields passed the `!isNaN()` guard, and the pad-to-two-digits step then left the raw text untouched. A Windows trigger starts at exactly **one** time, so a multi-value time has no representation at all — it was never a rounding error.

  **Nothing wrong ever reached Task Scheduler**: the agent rejects a malformed start time, which is why this survived so long. It surfaced instead as a `500`/`502` — *"the platform failed, try again"* — for a schedule Cronsole simply cannot express, sending callers to retry something that could never succeed. These now land on the documented replaced-with-hourly fallback, which says out loud that the expression was discarded, and adds the one workaround that exists: **several start times means several tasks**. Out-of-range values (`0 25 * * *`) are fixed by the same guard.

  **A lossy conversion now also rides the *success* of a schedule edit**, not only the refusal. `PATCH /api/tasks/:id/schedule` and MCP's `update_task_schedule` return the same `conversion` block create has always returned. That gap was unreachable before — these expressions used to die at the agent — and fixing the converter without it would have traded a noisy `502` for a silent `200` over a task quietly rescheduled to ~24 runs a day, which is the worse of the two.

- **A step schedule carrying a list or range no longer runs on half of what you asked for** (2026-08-13). `*/10,45 * * * *` (every 10 minutes **and** at :45) registered as a plain `PT10M` repetition, and `0 */6,13 * * *` as `PT6H` — each at **confidence 1.0 with no warnings**, the extra time discarded in silence.

  Found by grepping for the defect above rather than fixing the one instance, and it was **the more dangerous half**: a multi-value *time* produced a malformed value the agent refused, so it failed loudly. A mis-parsed *step* produces a perfectly well-formed repetition that Windows accepts — nothing errors, and the task simply runs on the wrong schedule indefinitely. Both now fall to the replaced-with-hourly fallback. Plain steps (`*/15`, `0 */4`) are unaffected.

- **Reading a task's schedule back off the machine no longer invents a time it does not have** (2026-08-13). `convertWindowsTriggerToCron` parsed a trigger's start boundary with an unguarded `parseInt`, so a malformed `"9-17:00"` read as `0 9 * * *` at **confidence 1.0** — and `"25:00"` as hour 25. This path reads real machines, where a task may be hand-written, made by another tool, or left by an older Cronsole that emitted exactly those boundaries. An unreadable boundary is now declined rather than guessed at.

- **A disabled task is no longer scored for the runs it was disabled to skip** (2026-08-13). Windows keeps counting `numberOfMissedRuns` while a task is parked, and the health scan was charging them — so on a real machine the **worst-ranked task was a disabled one**, sitting above every task that was still running and still failing.

  Disabling is the recommended way to stop a task safely; it is why `set_task_status` ships ungated over MCP. A health model that deducts points for the safe fix pushes people toward the unsafe ones. The `disabled` signal itself was already weightless — this was `missed-runs` being applied on top of it.

  It was an inconsistency rather than a new rule: `overdue` and `never-run` already skipped disabled tasks. The count is suppressed, not forgotten — re-enable the task and the signal comes back.

  Also corrected: the evidence line read *"Windows reported 1 missed runs"* under a summary that correctly said *"1 scheduled start"*.

- **The task-health scan no longer summarizes one set of tasks while listing another** (2026-08-13). Asking an assistant "what's failing?" produced *"Across 358 task(s): 25 critical"* directly above a list of **13** — the summary counted every tracked task, including the ~257 Windows owns, while the list showed only yours. Both numbers were true about different populations, and nothing in the response said so.

  **The filters moved onto the route, beside the counting.** `GET /api/tools/task-health` now accepts `tier`, `includeSystem` and `limit`; the MCP server forwards them and filters nothing. Previously it filtered client-side and printed the server's unfiltered counts — and a filter applied anywhere other than where the counting happens cannot be reconciled afterwards, because neither side knows what the other did.

  **`counts` is taken after the system lens but before the tier filter**, which is the rule the dashboard's filter chips already follow: a count beside a control describes the population that control governs. Counted after `tier` it would report the tier you asked for and four zeros.

  **The response now names its own population** — `scope: { includeSystem, tier, systemExcluded }` — so "25 across everything" is distinguishable from "13 among yours", and the hidden system tasks are counted out loud rather than silently dropped. A malformed `?includeSystem=fasle` is a `400` rather than a silent widening.

  Nothing changes for the dashboard: every route default is "everything", so an unparameterized request behaves exactly as before.

- **A platform no longer reports "not responding" from a failure that has long since expired** (2026-08-13): platform health gained a fourth state, **`UNKNOWN`**, shown as **"Not checked"**, and a request timeout now ages out of the verdict after 15 minutes.

  Found by looking at a live dashboard: Windows read **Degraded — "Agent connected but not responding (task:folders timed out)"** and had done for **ten and a half hours**, over an agent that was running the whole time and answered a sync immediately when finally asked. The tell was in the API before any debugging: every capability row showed a recent success and **zero** failures, and the newest timestamp anywhere on the platform was hours old. Nothing was failing, because nothing was asking.

  **The verdict was true when written and had no way to get old.** Health is the newest of `lastResponseAt` against `lastFailureAt` — the fix for [troubleshooting #40](troubleshooting/README.md#40-the-sidebar-says-windows-is-online-and-synced-just-now-while-every-agent-request-times-out), where a wedged agent read as healthy because a live socket was mistaken for a working one. That rule is right; it simply had no notion of an observation expiring. One timeout at 21:05, nobody using Cronsole overnight, and it was still being asserted in the present tense at breakfast.

  **The asymmetry that makes the fix coherent: evidence of a connection renews itself and evidence of a failure does not.** The transport heartbeat re-proves every few seconds that the agent process is up and reachable, so *Offline* and *Online* are continuously re-earned. A timeout happens once, and nothing after it says the agent is **still** wedged — because nothing after it asked. So the socket may stand indefinitely and the timeout must age out. An agent someone is actually trying to use stays **Degraded**, since every attempt renews the evidence.

  **"Not checked" is deliberately neutral, and deliberately not green.** It is not a fourth severity — it is the absence of a verdict. Amber would make an absence of information look like a problem to go and solve; the health strip saying *"All 3 platforms online"* over a platform nothing had heard from since yesterday would be the confident lie the strip exists to prevent, and strictly worse than the amber it replaced — amber over-warns, green tells you to stop looking. So it ranks below the two observed problems and above healthy.

  **No probe was added.** `getHealth` runs on a 45-second dashboard poll; probing there would put a synthetic request on the agent for every open browser tab. **Sync** is the user's probe and it is one click — and if you are wondering whether a platform is really alive, that is the button. ([troubleshooting #48](troubleshooting/README.md#48-windows-sits-at-degraded-for-hours-while-the-agent-is-perfectly-healthy))

- **A platform connection is no longer born "Online"** (2026-08-13): `PlatformConnection.healthState` defaulted to `HEALTHY`, and two places also wrote that literal on create — so a Platforms card read *Online* from the instant the row existed, having contacted the platform exactly never. It now defaults to `UNKNOWN` and both literals are gone; the real verdict is derived a few lines later in the same request. Existing rows are **not** backfilled: each was written by a real poll, and overwriting a true verdict to make the column uniform would be the same class of mistake.

  Same change, same reason: the Claude connector's four *no-evidence* branches — no routines configured, no user scope, an unreadable capability table, and no run yet — returned `DEGRADED` for want of anywhere else to go. A routine that has never fired has not failed. One of those tests was named *"does not blame the platform when the evidence store is unreadable"* while asserting `DEGRADED`, which is precisely blaming the platform for our own database hiccuping; the name is now true.

- **The in-app doc links are now checked** (2026-08-12): `frontend/src/data/__tests__/docsLinks.test.ts` resolves every help-topic, walkthrough and Help Center URL back to a real file and a real heading in this repo — 43 assertions.

  This is the mirror-surface problem (CLAUDE.md §11a) in its purest form: **a stale doc anchor does not fail.** Rename a heading and GitHub still serves the page, scrolled to the top, with no error anywhere — so a button promising *"Removing a task"* silently starts delivering the table of contents, and the only person who finds out is the user who needed the answer. A repo path the app links to but the test does not import **fails** rather than being skipped, because skipping unknown paths is how a checker ends up checking nothing.

  Two traps found while writing it, either of which would have made it pass while proving nothing: splitting the markdown on `\n` leaves a trailing `\r` on every heading (CRLF files, and `.` does not match `\r`), yielding **zero** anchors; and collapsing runs of whitespace in the slug is wrong, because GitHub does not — dropping the em dash from *"Removing a task — two buttons"* leaves two spaces and therefore two hyphens. Mutation-tested by renaming a heading and confirming the failure.

  The docs are read with Vite's `?raw`, not `node:fs` (the frontend project has no node types, deliberately), and `server.fs.allow` is widened in **`vitest.config.ts` only** — so the dev server keeps its default sandbox and gains no ability to serve anything outside `frontend/`.

- **A Claude routine's task kept coming back after "Remove from Cronsole"** (2026-08-12): reported as *"I keep deleting it but it keeps coming back"* — and `TaskExclusion` was empty, which made it look like untrack had never run at all.

  It had run. **Untrack is built for the Windows shape** — the task lives on the machine, sync enumerates the machine, so Cronsole records an exclusion meaning *"don't re-import this"*. Claude inverts that: Anthropic exposes no list API, so `syncTasks` returns **the routines the user declared**, read back out of the connection config. **The registry is the platform.** The exclusion therefore fenced the user's own configuration off from itself, while the declaration sat untouched — and importing the Claude category clears exclusions inside it *by design* (it is the documented way back for a Windows task removed by mistake), which un-hid a routine that was never gone.

  The mirror half was just as broken: removing the routine under **Platforms → Claude** left the task row behind, un-runnable, flipping to MISSING on the next sync.

  Now one fact has one mechanism — **a Claude task is its declaration**. `POST /api/tasks/:id/untrack` 400s for `CLAUDE_CODE` (the refusal `TASKHUB_NATIVE` already had) and names the control that works; bulk untrack refuses it per item without halting the batch; `DELETE /api/tools/platforms/claude/routines/:id` removes the tracked tasks and their history with the declaration and reports `tasksRemoved`; and the task modal shows **Disconnect routine** instead of *Remove from Cronsole*.

  **Why refuse rather than make untrack remove the routine too** — the one-button version everyone wants: it would silently discard the stored API token, which claude.ai shows once, under a label that says nothing about credentials. **A control may not spend something its label does not mention.** See [troubleshooting #47](troubleshooting/README.md#47-a-claude-task-keeps-coming-back-after-remove-from-cronsole).

- **The Claude platform reported itself Online without ever having contacted Anthropic** (2026-08-12): `getHealth` returned `HEALTHY` whenever the config held a routine — a verdict derived from a **precondition**, which is troubleshooting [#40](troubleshooting/README.md#40-the-sidebar-says-windows-is-online-and-synced-just-now-while-every-agent-request-times-out)'s exact shape, one connector over. Health now comes from whether a run actually succeeded, read back from the evidence the run route already records.

  **It deliberately does not probe**, and that is the interesting part: the only endpoint Claude exposes has a side effect, so *"check whether this works"* and *"run the user's routine"* are the same HTTP request. A probing health check would have fired someone's nightly job on every poll and burned their daily run cap. Where there is no read-only probe, the honest health signal is the record of real runs — there is nothing else it can be. See [troubleshooting #45](troubleshooting/README.md#45-cronsole-cant-list-pause-or-create-claude-code-routines).

- **A failed run reported HTTP 500, blaming Cronsole for the platform's answer** (2026-08-12): `POST /api/tasks/:id/run` returned `500` for every failure, so a paused Claude routine, an offline agent and an ACL denial all read as *"the server broke"* — sending you to debug the wrong side. Now `502`, matching what the enable/disable route already returned for a platform refusal. A live paused routine now answers `502 — Refused (400): Routine is paused.`

- **A failed Claude run buried Anthropic's actual reason under a guess** (2026-08-12): the 400 handler always appended *"Most often the routine is paused"*, so a live run reported `Refused (400): invalid routine ID: Refresh sidebar links. Most often the routine is paused — resume it at claude.ai/code/routines.` The API had named the exact cause and the hint talked over it, sending you to unpause a routine that was fine. The hint now appears **only when Anthropic gave no reason** — it is for filling a silence, not for talking over one.

- **Asking a platform to do something it has no API for returned "502 Bad Gateway"** (2026-08-12): disabling a Claude routine, or creating one, failed with a status that means *the gateway had a problem — retry*. It will never work, however many times you click. Both now return **400** with the reason, matching the convention every optional connector verb already followed ("an honest 400 from the route"). A verb that failed because the agent is offline still returns 5xx — that is the genuinely retryable case, and keeping the two apart is the point.

- **Firing a Claude routine sent filler text that displaced the routine's real input** (2026-08-12): every run posted `text: "Triggered from Cronsole"`. That field arrives inside the routine's `<routine-fire-payload>` block, so for the routines built to read it — alert triage, deploy verification, anything whose prompt says *"investigate the alert in the payload"* — Cronsole's filler string arrived **where the alert should have been**. The body is optional; it now sends none.

- **`create_native_task` over MCP had never worked** (2026-08-12): every call returned `400 Unsupported jobType: undefined` — the tool built its payload without the discriminator the route requires, from the day it shipped. See [troubleshooting #44](troubleshooting/README.md#44-an-mcp-tool-400s-on-every-call-and-the-whole-suite-is-green).

  **The MCP suite passed 142/142 throughout**, and one of those tests was named *"nests the HTTP job the way the route expects"* while asserting the exact payload the route rejects. The suite stubs the HTTP client — a correct, deliberate choice that makes it fast and hermetic, and one that means every assertion is about what the wrapper *sends* and none about whether the API *accepts* it. The test did not miss the bug; **it pinned it.** This is the gap `docs/testing/README.md` already named by name, found sitting in the surface it was written about.

- **"Synced 7m ago" over a task list from the previous day** (2026-08-12): `GET /api/tasks/health` reported the Windows agent's **last inbound event of any kind** as `lastSync`. The dashboard renders that as *"Synced N ago"*, so a folder listing seven minutes earlier made a **nineteen-hour-old** task list read as fresh. See [troubleshooting #42](troubleshooting/README.md#42-the-dashboard-says-synced-7m-ago-over-a-task-list-from-yesterday).

  **This survived the fix for [#40](troubleshooting/README.md#40-the-sidebar-says-windows-is-online-and-synced-just-now-while-every-agent-request-times-out), which is what makes it worth recording.** That fix stopped `getHealth` inventing a timestamp with `new Date()`. What replaced it was a *real* timestamp of the wrong event — first-hand, absent when there is no evidence, correctly stale when the agent goes quiet, and still not a sync. The comment three lines above the bug already read **"Connected is not synced."**

  Fixed structurally rather than locally: `ConnectorHealth.lastSync` is **deleted**, because no connector has an honest way to fill it — a sync happens in exactly one place, `POST /api/tasks/sync`, which writes the column itself. Connectors now report `lastContactAt`, named for what it holds, and the health strip shows both when they differ: *"Synced 19h ago · agent replied 2m ago"* — which is precisely the fact that was hidden while one was reported as the other.

- **The health strip changed its own height, shoving the dashboard down mid-read** (2026-08-12): it was `flex-wrap`, so when a segment appeared or a relative time grew a digit it went from one row to two and moved everything below it by ~22px — a layout shift on a 45-second poll, with no interaction to explain it. It is now one line that scrolls. Found by the new screenshot suite, which is the sort of thing it is for.

- **Every status colour in the light theme failed WCAG AA, and could not be fixed where it was written** (2026-08-12): status colour lived as **263 raw Tailwind palette utilities across 8 hues**, and a raw utility has one value for both themes. So light mode was wearing dark mode's colours. Measured against white, before → after:

  | role | before | after |
  |---|---|---|
  | `warning` | **1.67** | 5.05 |
  | `success` | **1.92** | 5.65 |
  | `info` | **2.14** | 5.80 |
  | `system` | **2.72** | 8.65 |
  | `danger` | **2.77** | 6.42 |

  AA for small text is 4.5, so all five were failing and `text-amber-400` at 1.67:1 was very nearly invisible. Dark mode was already fine and stayed fine (7.15–11.70, i.e. AAA). **This is why the fix had to be tokens rather than better values**: one literal cannot be light-on-black and dark-on-white at once, so no amount of tuning at the call site could have fixed light mode without breaking dark.

  Each role now carries a pair — `--x` (the accent, for fills and borders at low opacity) and `--x-text` (the role as text on the page background, which inverts between themes). Zero raw palette utilities remain in `frontend/src`.

- **The chip distinguishing a Cronsole-native task from a Windows one drew no distinction** (2026-08-12): found while tokenising. `bg-violet-600` and `bg-primary` are both `262 83% 58%` — the same colour — so `p === 'TASKHUB_NATIVE' ? 'bg-violet-600 …' : 'bg-primary …'` rendered identically in both branches. Native now has its own `--native` token, deliberately offset from `--primary`.

### Changed
- **Status colour is a semantic role, not a hue** (2026-08-12): `--success` · `--warning` · `--danger` · `--isolate` · `--info` · `--system` · `--neutral-text`, plus platform identity (`--native`, `--claude`, `--chatgpt`) and a `--danger-surface` pair for the backend-down alarm. A restrained theme pass is now an edit to one file instead of a 263-site sweep.

  **Three "duplicate hue pairs" were logged from a frequency count; checking each before merging, only one survived — and the check caught a real bug.** green + emerald genuinely were one role (*good*) and merged into `success` — **except in `platform.ts`, where emerald is ChatGPT's brand colour**, so a blind merge would have recoloured a live badge. violet vs purple are not duplicates at all: violet is the system/personal lens, purple is Claude's identity. red vs rose are not either: red is failure, rose marks a lens that is *isolating* — which is also why it has to stay distinct from `--warning`, the lens that shows you more. The roadmap item asserting three pairs has been corrected rather than quietly dropped.

  Also added the missing neutral step, `--raised`, so a panel can sit *above* `surface` — `background → surface → muted` had no raised level, which is the mechanical reason controls all read at one weight. It is applied to the Mass Actions plan panel (which was painting an inner panel with the page colour, reading as a recessed input well rather than as the thing the card is about); using it across the app is the *thin the first viewport* work, still open.

### Added
- **Mass Actions console on the Tools tab — scope-first bulk operations with graduated confirmation** (2026-08-12): pick *what* (all tasks / a category / a platform / a status / a health tier) rather than ticking each row, see exactly what it resolved to, then run. Enable, Disable, Categorize and Remove from Cronsole, each stating what it would **actually change** rather than the scope size.

  **Why a scope and not a bigger checkbox.** A selection answers "these ones", which is right for three tasks on screen and useless at 250: `254 selected` cannot survive into a confirmation as anything a person can check. A scope can — *"Disable 47 tasks in Backups"* names the set in the terms it was chosen by, so the dialog is re-readable instead of a number to be trusted. Everything else follows: the plan is visible before a platform is asked anything, and the result is per task because at this scale partial success **is** the normal case.

  **The safety mechanism is the graduated confirmation, not the relocation.** Bulk status and untrack already confirmed and already named their real counts — what was missing is that the dialog for 254 tasks was the *same dialog* as for 3, so nothing hardened as the blast radius grew and habit built on small batches carried straight through. At or above **25 tasks** the confirmation now requires typing the count. Moving buttons to another tab is friction by obscurity and wears off in a week; a dialog that hardens with scale is friction by design. **The dashboard keeps its selection** for small contextual batches — §9's rule cuts both ways, and exiling "Remove from Cronsole" from beside Delete would push people toward the destructive one.

  **It adds no backend and no new authority.** Every verb is an existing `/api/tools/tasks/*` route under the five-outcome contract. The console **chunks at the server's own 100-task ceiling** rather than arguing with it — that bound is a correctness one (each Windows task can cost its full ~15s agent timeout), and chunking also buys progress reporting and a stopping point that one twenty-minute request could never have had. A halt in any batch stops the run and names every remaining task as `skipped` rather than letting it vanish from a report that returns fewer items than it was asked for.

  **Undo covers enable/disable and says so.** The report already names exactly which ids changed and the inverse is well-defined, so undo is one click. It deliberately does **not** generalize — untrack's undo is a re-import, categorize's would need prior categories stored — and the card states that rather than offering a button that would not work. Export and import are likewise absent by choice: both already have tools that own their real logic (the directory-picker/ZIP paths, and discovery), so a button here would either duplicate them or be a permanently-disabled control that reads as broken.

  Verified live against a real 269-task machine at **zero mutation**, by recategorizing tasks into the category they were already in — a full round trip through request, planning, five-outcome report and rendering in which the server writes nothing. That exercised both a single batch (8 tasks) and **chunking across two** (189 tasks, `0 done · 189 already so`, all 189 rows reported), plus the typed gate at 189 and its absence at 8. Confirmed from the database rather than from the reporting layer: 13 categories intact, 269 tasks, nothing collapsed.

### Fixed
- **The dashboard offered a select-all it could not act on** (2026-08-12): every bulk route refuses above 100 tasks, so on a real machine `Select all 269` built a selection whose every button then failed with a 400 — found while capping select-all for the Mass Actions work, not by anything reporting it. Select-all is now offered only while the resulting selection is one the routes would accept; above the ceiling it hands off to the Mass Actions console, which is scope-based and batches. Per-task selection is untouched: the point is to stop *one click* becoming a machine-wide operation, not to make small batches harder.

- **The typed-confirmation field used the required number as its placeholder** (2026-08-12): caught in the browser during the same pass — a grey `70` in an empty box reads as already filled in, so the dialog looked satisfied when it was not. Now `Type 70 here`. Stating the number in the sentence above it is deliberate and stays: typing is not a secret, it is a deliberate act that muscle memory cannot produce.

- **The dashboard's status chip counted every task while the list showed the filtered ones** (2026-08-12): opening on Favorites with two starred tasks printed **`Showing All 269`** directly above two rows. Found by the 2026-08-12 UX review, which read it as a wording conflict with the favorites banner; it was a counts-vs-list bug, and the label was the smaller half. `DashboardScreen` took the chip's number from `tasks.length` and derived `hiddenByActiveFilter` from `tasks` as well — both across the **whole** set, never through the pipeline the list uses. The `Active Only` state had the same defect in the other direction: on Favorites it would offer to reveal rows that were being withheld by the star, not by the status filter.

  **The rule that settles what the number should be: a count beside a filter control is a promise about what clicking it will reveal.** Not the dashboard total, and not the filtered list either — the population that control *governs*, which is every task passing every **other** lens. New `applyTaskFiltersExcept(tasks, filters, dimension, opts)` in `utils/taskFilters.ts` computes exactly that, with `applyTaskFilters` delegating to it so there is one pipeline rather than two. `hiddenByActiveFilter` is now derived as `governed.length - filteredTasks.length`, so it cannot disagree with the list it sits above.

  **This was already the established pattern one function away** — the category and platform facet chips were doing leave-one-out by hand (`{ ...viewFilters, category: 'All' }`), and now route through the same helper; two spellings of "leave this one out" is how one of them ends up leaving out a dimension the other doesn't. The status chip's count was deliberately scoped to the system lens only, which was defensible with four dimensions and stopped being so once saved views could pin favorites, due and outcome — **the previous reasoning is preserved in the code comment as what changed and why**, since it was a decision rather than an oversight.

  The chip now names the dimension it owns — **`All statuses` / `Active only`**, not `Showing All`, which claimed the whole list on behalf of a control that governs one lens. Verified live in the browser against the DOM rather than against its own computation: on Favorites the chip reads `2` above 2 rendered rows; on `My jobs` it reads `70 shown · 10 hidden` against a personal total of exactly 80. Six new tests, mutation-checked — dropping the favorites lens from the helper kills two of them.


- **Platform health reported HEALTHY, and "synced just now", from a socket that was answering nothing** (2026-08-11): found by clicking through the Tools tab and the Import modal — the roadmap item that exists because the previous click-through found a real defect. On arrival the Windows agent was wedged: `GET /api/tasks/folders` returned 502, `GET /api/tasks/discover` omitted the Windows platform entirely, and the backend log repeated `[Discovery] Error for WINDOWS_TASK_SCHEDULER: Agent sync timeout` — while `/api/tasks/health` said `HEALTHY` and the dashboard chip said **synced just now**. Two fabrications in one four-line function: `getHealth` returned `HEALTHY` whenever a socket *object* existed, and stamped `lastSync: new Date()`.

  **Neither precondition can be falsified by the thing actually breaking.** Socket.IO's heartbeat is answered by the transport, so an agent whose command loop has stopped keeps a perfectly healthy socket; and a timestamp the health check generates itself can never be stale, which is exactly why it can never be true. The Import modal was the only surface telling the truth — *"No tasks discovered — the Windows agent may be offline"* — two inches under a sidebar saying Online.

  Health is now reported from evidence the agent produced. `AgentManager` records `lastResponseAt` (set centrally by `socket.onAny`, so no verb can forget) against `lastFailureAt` (set at each of the connector's ten request timeouts, naming the verb), newest wins: no socket → `OFFLINE`; newest evidence a timeout → **`DEGRADED`**, *"Agent connected but not responding (task:list timed out)"*; otherwise `HEALTHY`, justified by the authenticated handshake. `lastSync` is now only ever real — the agent's last inbound event, else the column written by `POST /api/tasks/sync` where a sync actually happened, else **absent**. **Connected is not synced**, and the chip now disappears rather than lying.

  **The same fabrication was in all three connectors**, and fixing only Windows would have achieved nothing: the dashboard chip takes the newest `lastSync` across *all* platforms, so Cronsole-native — which never syncs, because this database is its source of truth — would have kept it pinned to "just now" on its own. Native and Claude now report no `lastSync` at all, and a sync clears native's column so the fabricated values already written are retired rather than left sitting there. One neighbouring bug fixed in passing: `healthReason` was written as bare `health.reason`, and Prisma reads `undefined` as *"leave this column alone"* ([#22](troubleshooting/README.md#22-deleted-a-windows-task-synced-and-cronsole-still-shows-it--while-reporting-missing-n)), so a recovered platform kept the explanation from the last time it was unhealthy.

  **No test caught it because the tests pinned the bug as the specification** — every `getHealth` case asserted the socket-present branch returned `HEALTHY`, and there was no case for "socket present, agent not answering" because the code could not represent it. Six new cases, mutation-checked: reverting `getHealth` to the old two lines kills exactly three of them. New trap: [#40](troubleshooting/README.md#40-the-sidebar-says-windows-is-online-and-synced-just-now-while-every-agent-request-times-out) — note it is the **inverse** of [#5](troubleshooting/README.md#5-windows-offline-after-running-a-transient-test-agent)/[#37](troubleshooting/README.md#37-running-the-e2e-suite-knocks-your-real-windows-agent-offline--and-it-stays-that-way), where a stranded socket reports OFFLINE while the agent runs fine; the same surface was already capable of lying in both directions.

- **The idle report claimed the all-clear while hiding idle tasks** (2026-08-11): when every idle task happened to be one of Windows' own, the system lens emptied the list and Execution analytics printed the emerald *"Every scheduled task has run recently."* directly beside *"1 system hidden"* — a fact and its own contradiction in one breath. The message is now scoped to the population it describes (*"No idle tasks outside Windows' own — 1 system task is idle"*, with the count as the control that reveals them), and the plain all-clear is kept for when nothing at all is idle.

  **The existing test constructed exactly that state and passed**, because it only asserted what *should* appear — the hidden task's absence and the "1 system hidden" label — never that the contradicting claim was absent. Proved before fixing, by adding one assertion to that test and watching it fail. Same family as [#38](troubleshooting/README.md#38-a-row-of-summary-numbers-doesnt-add-up--one-of-them-counts-a-different-population): *never let a filtered list narrate an unfiltered claim* — and the reusable lesson for the suite, **a test that only checks what should appear cannot catch a screen that also says the opposite.**

- **The mirror surfaces caught up with favorites — and two of them were false, not merely silent** (2026-08-11): found by running `/sync-surfaces` over the two shipping commits rather than by anything failing. **Four artifacts carried a list of what is "still REST-only"** (`CLAUDE.md` §8, `SKILL.md` in two places, `references/workflows.md`), and favorites was missing from all four. A list like that is read as *complete* — so an omission is a claim, and an agent asked to "star my nightly jobs" would have read the tool surface, found nothing, and concluded Cronsole has no such feature. Each now names the routes **with the reason**: a star is a per-user display preference, and `compactTask` does not forward `isFavorite`, so `list_tasks` cannot read one either. **Connect Pack v1.6** carries the same row, stamped across all five downloadable artifacts.

  **`docs/reports/examples/tasks.json` was wrong about three routes, none of them new.** It documented `GET /tasks` as returning `{ "tasks": [...], "total": 25 }` — an envelope that has never existed; the route returns a bare array. It named the run-history route `/logs` (it is `/executions`) and wrapped those rows in an envelope too, and gave `POST /:id/run` a `202` with an `executionId` (it answers `200` with `{ message, ...result }`). Rewritten from the routes themselves, with the favorite routes added and the `lastRun*` fields labelled for what they actually record. The public README was stale in the same quiet way: **40 templates** where the registry holds **55 in 6 packs**.

  **The pack's own test caught the artifact the sweep missed.** A `sed` over the four markdown files left `mcp-config.json` still saying v1.5, and *"stamps the version into every artifact a user can download"* failed on exactly that — the one check here that isn't documentation checking documentation.

- **Docs caught up with the two surfaces the fix changed** (2026-08-11): the [UI User Guide](user-guides/guides/UI_User_Guide.md) §6 now says what the three platform states actually mean — **Degraded** is *connected but not answering*, which is the state that used to read as Online — and that *"synced N ago"* is about syncing rather than being connected, so it is **absent** until a sync has happened and Cronsole-native never shows one at all. It also notes that **Check now** re-reads status without syncing, so it will not move that indicator. The testing known-gaps row claiming *"the Tools tab has never been driven in a browser"* was **narrowed rather than deleted**: the cards have now been driven, but export's `showDirectoryPicker` and restore's file input are native dialogs and remain unexercised — and a new row records the pattern that every browser pass so far has out-found the suites, because three of these four defects were **claims**, not computations. UAT **U5.4b** ticked with its numbers and one honest residual (this machine had zero idle tasks, so the `\Microsoft\`-domination question it exists to answer is still open), plus a new **U5.4c** for the Import modal.

- **The Import modal gave a heading to a platform that discovered nothing, and said "1 tasks"** (2026-08-11): Cronsole-native reports zero categories on any machine with no native tasks, so a bare **CRONSOLE** heading sat above empty space — and in the agent-offline case it landed directly under *"No tasks discovered"*, denying and promising a list at once. A section header is a claim that there is a section. The per-category count badge also hardcoded the plural, while the preview line one inch below it pluralized correctly.

### Added
- **Favorites: star a task, and the dashboard opens on your stars** (2026-08-11): a star on every task surface — grid card, list row, both kanban columns, the Schedule timeline, and the detail modal header — backed by `POST` / `DELETE /api/tasks/:id/favorite` and an `isFavorite` flag on `GET /api/tasks`. A bare dashboard URL now resolves to your starred tasks when you have any, and to your normal saved defaults when you don't.

  **A `TaskFavorite` join model, keyed on the task row — the deliberate opposite of `TaskExclusion`.** An exclusion is keyed on `(platform, externalId)` precisely *because* it must outlive the row it describes: without that, the next sync re-imports what you untracked. A favorite is a preference about a task Cronsole is *tracking*, so it cascades with the row — a star that survived a delete would come back attached to nothing, or worse, to a re-imported task nobody starred. Sync `upsert`s on `(platform, externalId)`, so ids are stable and a MISSING task keeps both its row and its star. Per-user, never a column on `Task`, for the reason `TemplateFavorite` isn't one either.

  **The Favorites view ignores every other lens** (`status: any`, `system: include`). A star is an explicit per-task choice, so no *default* may overrule it: starring a task, parking it, and then finding it absent from the view named after your stars would make the star mean "shown, conditions apply". The one task you starred out of `\Microsoft\` shows for the same reason.

  **The dimension is a field on `TaskFilters`, not a seventh piece of component state** — so it lives in the URL (`fav=only`), the view bar can name it, and the counts beside the list know about it. The opening rule is a pure function (`openingFilters`), not a branch inside the screen, so it is pinned by a test rather than only by rendering a dashboard; and it is *derived* from a bare URL rather than an effect that rewrites one, which is what makes it impossible for the default to fight you — touch any filter and the URL stops being bare.

  **Found by clicking through, not by the suite: a dashboard that filters itself has to say so.** The lit "Favorites" chip is enough for a view you clicked — you know what you asked for. This one arrives on its own, and landing on 1 of 269 tasks with the reason available only as a hover tooltip is `Active Only · 110 hidden` again, worse for being unrequested. There is now a banner that names the filter, **counts the tasks it is holding back**, and puts "Show the full dashboard" one click away — shown only while the view was *defaulted to*, never when you selected it yourself. Verified live end to end: opened on Favorites with the banner, the escape hatch landed on `?view=my-jobs` with 70 tasks, starring from a card lit the star and moved the chip 1 → 2 without opening the task, and with the last star removed a bare URL returned to the normal dashboard. Tests: backend 502 unit + 160 integration (+8, incl. the IDOR refusal and the cascade), frontend 347 (+11).

- **Task cards say when the task runs** (2026-08-11): the dashboard's grid cards, the kanban cards and the Schedule timeline now carry a one-line schedule — *"Daily at 8:00 AM PDT"* — read in the Settings zone, the same reading the details modal gives. The card is the surface people actually read, and answering "when does this run?" cost a click on every task; the Schedule view, ironically, printed the raw UTC cron.

  **Three outcomes, not one, because two of them are facts rather than gaps.** A recognizable cron is described; a shape `describeCron` won't guess at (a date-pinned `0 4 1 * *`) is shown **raw** rather than approximated; and a task with no cron at all says **"No cron schedule"**. Rendering nothing for the last two would make *"this runs at 8 AM"* and *"Cronsole has no idea when this runs"* look identical across a wall of cards — the same failure the card is being added to fix.

  A card is a **preview, not an input**, so it does not print the stored UTC form beside the shifted reading the way a schedule *field* must (CLAUDE.md §9): the UTC cron rides in the tooltip, and the modal one click away prints it in full. "Where the cron lives" is now one function (`taskCron` in `utils/schedule.ts`, covering the column and both metadata spellings), used by the cards **and** by the modal that previously had its own copy — so the two can never disagree about whether a task has a schedule. Tests: frontend 336 (+8). bulk enable/disable shipped 2026-07-31 with the selection model and the five-outcome reporting convention; the three remaining verbs now ride on both. `POST /api/tools/tasks/category` (`{taskIds, category}`), `POST /api/tools/tasks/untrack` (`{taskIds}`), and a third scope on the existing export — `POST /api/tools/export/tasks` with `scope: 'selection'` and `taskIds`. In the dashboard they are four buttons on the selection bar, each **stating the count it will actually change** rather than the selection size.

  **The convention got extracted rather than copied.** The five outcomes, the item shape, the tally and the agent-down predicate now live in one place (`backend/src/services/bulkOutcome.ts`), with `bulkStatus` refactored onto it and its exports unchanged. The cheap alternative was three response bodies with three ideas of what "it didn't work" means, so a caller who handled one had learned nothing about the others. **The halt rule stayed separate on purpose** — it belongs only to verbs that talk to the agent. Category and untrack never ask a platform, so they *cannot* halt, and their `skipped` is structurally `0`; it stays in the response anyway, because four zeros tell a caller something and four different shapes tell it nothing.

  **Recategorize is DB-only, and says the thing that makes it confusing.** A Windows task's category is *derived from its real Task Scheduler folder* at import, so on the dashboard the label and the folder look like one fact — and after a bulk relabel they are not. It is not refused (it is a legitimate thing to want, and single-task `PATCH /api/tasks/:id` always allowed it), but the response carries **`detachedFromFolder`** and the modal states the number **before** the click: a warning that arrives with the result arrives too late to change the decision. The folder verdict comes from `TaskService.extractCategory` and is passed *into* the planner rather than re-derived — a second copy of a path-derived rule is exactly [#20a](troubleshooting/README.md#20a-and-a-renamed-category-silently-stops-syncing-its-folder). Renaming stays safe for sync, which resolves its include-set from native paths (`scope: 'tracked'`), never from these labels.

  **Bulk untrack exists because over-import is a bulk mistake.** The Import modal makes a whole folder one click away, so the thing it undoes is never one task — and without it, tidying 40 accidentally-imported rows meant 40 modals or reaching for **Delete**, which destroys the real scheduled tasks. *A destructive path that is easier than the safe one is how the safe one stops being used* — the same argument that keeps `set_task_status` ungated over MCP. Each row's delete and its `TaskExclusion` land in **one transaction**: a row removed without its exclusion returns on the next sync, so the user does the work twice and trusts it less the second time.
