That atomicity is affordable precisely *because* there is no platform round-trip inside it, which is also why it works with the agent offline — when someone is most likely to be tidying up. A `TASKHUB_NATIVE` task is **`refused` per item, without halting the batch**; the UI says "Remove from Cronsole", never "Remove".

  **Export-selected names what it could not export.** The scope takes **Cronsole task ids, not native paths** — the server resolves them, so an authenticated caller cannot name an arbitrary task on the machine. `all` and `folder` describe a *region*, so whatever is there is the answer; a selection names specific tasks, and a named task the machine does not report is a fact about the request. Those come back as `requestedMissing` (and are written into the archive manifest, since a downloaded backup has to be able to say a year later that it is missing three tasks on purpose), with `unsupported` for selected non-Windows tasks, which have no Task Scheduler XML at all. An explicit selection also overrides the `\Microsoft\` default exclusion: that fence exists so a machine-wide export isn't buried under ~257 system tasks, not to override a row the user deliberately ticked — the same rule as `filterExcluded`, that a fence must never swallow a direct request.

  Tests: backend **496** unit (+21) and **147** integration (+13, real Postgres — the planners are pure, so only Postgres can show the decision was actually *written*, owner-scoped, and transactional), frontend **323** (+16). Mutation-checked. Connect Pack **v1.5** — the pack gained the two new routes and a new claim a reader can get wrong in either direction: *a category is a label, so recategorizing a Windows task does not move it on the machine.*

  **Verified live in the browser against three disposable tasks, reading Windows rather than Cronsole's own report.** Recategorize moved the labels and the toast said `3 moved to "ProbeBucket" · 3 now labelled differently from its Windows folder` — with `Get-ScheduledTask` confirming all three still sat in `\Cronsole\` and **no `\ProbeBucket\` folder had been created**, which is precisely the claim the modal's pre-click warning makes. Export-selected round-tripped through the real agent: 358 tasks enumerated, exactly 3 selected, 3 exported, first bytes `fffe` (UTF-16 LE BOM), and the files landed under `Cronsole/` — the **machine's** folder, not the new label. Untrack reported `3 removed from Cronsole`, dropped the dashboard 83 → 80, and left all three tasks `Ready` on the machine with their three `TaskExclusion` rows written. The way back works too, and unplanned: the Import modal already showed the `Cronsole` category unticked with an amber **"+3 removed"** badge and *"Includes 3 you had removed"* in the footer — the bulk verb fed the existing exclusion-surfacing machinery with no change to it. Everything was cleaned up afterwards (0 probe tasks on the machine, 0 rows, 0 exclusions).

  **The click-through earned its keep: it found a defect no suite could.** The untrack confirmation rendered a literal `**Nothing on your machine is touched**` — `useConfirm` puts its message in a text node, so the markdown asterisks reached the screen. Invisible to every test, because a test asserts the string that was passed in, and **the string that was passed in was the wrong one**. Fixed to plain prose and re-verified in the browser; a sweep confirmed no other `confirm()` call passes markdown. The general form is worth keeping: *a suite can only check that the value you chose arrived — never that it was the right value to choose.*

  **One near-miss worth recording, because it was caught by a rule rather than by a failure.** The shared helper first resolved each verb's rows generically, which can only be typed with a **cast on a query result** — [#30](troubleshooting/README.md#30-a-route-500s-on-real-data-while-tsc-is-green--a-cast-on-a-query-result) precisely, where the compiler stops checking at the boundary most likely to drift and the failure reappears as a 500 on real data. Split into two helpers that take rows a route has *already* fetched with a literal `select`, so Prisma's inference does the checking. `tsc` was green either way; the trap log is what said it was wrong.

- **A task can now create the Task Scheduler folder it's filed into — opt-in, signed, and it says what it made** (2026-08-04): `create_task`'s `folder` could only name a folder that already existed, so an agent asked to file a task somewhere new had to give up or dump it in `\Cronsole`. `POST /api/tasks` and MCP `create_task` take **`createFolder`** (default `false`), which creates the missing chain instead of refusing, reusing the agent's existing `CreateFolderChain` — the same machinery restore has used since 2026-07-28.

  **This is the second carve-out to "Cronsole creates only `\Cronsole`", and the shape is the whole design.** That invariant exists because folder deletion needs elevation, and [#28](troubleshooting/README.md#28-a-restored-task-or-the-folder-it-landed-in-cant-be-deleted-access-is-denied) sharpened it past its original wording: the agent is **elevated**, so a folder it creates carries an administrator ACE — a door only an *administrator* can close, not merely "only the user". So the flag is `false` by default, rides **inside the HMAC signature** (an unsigned flag would let an on-path attacker turn an honest refusal into a folder nobody asked for), and every folder created is **named in the response** — including on failure, since the chain can be built and `RegisterTaskDefinition` then throw, which leaves a real folder behind. It never widens *where* a task may land: `\Microsoft\` is refused in the backend and independently in the elevated agent, with or without it.

  **Deliberately not a free-standing `create_folder` tool**, which was the more obvious design. A folder stays *incidental to a task the caller actually asked for*, so the blast radius is tied to intent. A tool that makes folders on its own is one an agent feeling its way uses to leave `\AI-Tools`, `\ai_tools` and `\AITools` behind — each permanent without elevation, at machine speed. That is precisely the dogfood that produced the invariant (three empty folders needing hand-deletion, 2026-07-14), with a faster clock. The MCP tool description says so, and says the cost out loud rather than only the capability.

  **Breaking:** the `task:create` signature gained a field, so backend and agent must deploy together — an older agent verifies the old message shape and refuses, which is the honest failure. Golden cross-language HMAC vectors regenerated, and per the established practice the generator was first validated by **reproducing all ten previously-committed vectors from the old format** — a generator that can't reproduce what's already in the repo emits new values that are confidently wrong and then get pinned by both suites. A **second** create vector is now pinned with the flag `true`: with only a false case, an implementation that hard-coded `0` would pass, and two vectors differing in exactly one bit are what prove the bit is signed. Mutation-checked — unsigning the flag kills exactly the pass-through and tamper tests. Tests: backend 475 unit (+4) + 134 integration (+2, incl. `\Microsoft\` still refused *with* the flag set), agent 154 (+3), mcp-server 142 (+3). New trap: [#39](troubleshooting/README.md#39-a-mutation-test-still-fails-after-you-restore-the-file--dotnet-test-ran-a-stale-build).

  **Verified live after an agent republish, reading Windows rather than Cronsole's own report.** Three probes against the real machine: a create into a missing folder **without** the flag was refused with a message that now names the opt-in *and* its cost; the same create **with** the flag returned `foldersCreated: ["\\CronsoleFolderProbe"]`, and the folder was confirmed to exist through the **`Schedule.Service` COM API independently of the task**, with the task `Ready`, registered as a direct `cmd.exe` exec, at the correct local time; and `\Microsoft\Windows\Defrag` **with** the flag set was refused `400`, with Defrag intact and no `SneakyProbe` anywhere on the machine. **The cost is confirmed real rather than theoretical:** after deleting the probe task, the folder remained (Cronsole prunes only its own `\Cronsole`), and an unelevated attempt to remove it returned **`Access is denied (0x80070005)`** — exactly [#28](troubleshooting/README.md#28-a-restored-task-or-the-folder-it-landed-in-cant-be-deleted-access-is-denied), which is the whole reason this is opt-in and warned about at every layer rather than being a convenience.

### Security
- **The last open advisory is closed: `react-router-dom` 7.18.1 → `react-router` 8.3.0** (2026-07-31): the deferral recorded on 2026-07-28 (below) assumed the only remedy was npm's suggested **downgrade** to 7.11.0. It wasn't — it was just the only one npm offers. The advisory range is `7.12.0 – 8.2.0`, so **8.3.0 is the forward fix**; what hid it is that `react-router-dom` **stopped at 7.18.2 and has no 8.x**, because v8 folded the DOM entry point back into `react-router` itself. `npm audit fix` can only suggest a version of the package you already have, so a fix that lives under a *different package name* is invisible to it — and the honest deferral note quietly became a permanent one. Swapped the dependency and rewrote the seven `from 'react-router-dom'` imports to `from 'react-router'`; the whole API surface is four names (`BrowserRouter`, `MemoryRouter`, `useNavigate`, `useLocation`), all unchanged in v8. **Production `npm audit --omit=dev` is 0 again across backend, frontend and mcp-server.** Validated as a dependency major deserves (regression row R5.7): lint clean, 195 frontend tests, build clean, and the **full 9-test Playwright E2E suite green** — routing and the `/tasks/:id` deep links are exactly what a router bump can break, and no unit test would have caught it.
- **Login rate limiting is implemented — and the docs that called it a gap are corrected** (2026-07-31): `makeAuthLimiter()` (`backend/src/middleware/authLimiter.ts`) puts a per-IP limit of **10 attempts / 15 min** on `POST /auth/login` **and** `POST /auth/setup`, returning `429` with a plain-language message. Setup is limited too on purpose: it is a credential-*creation* surface, so limiting only login moves the target rather than removing it. Skipped under `NODE_ENV=test` and via `DISABLE_AUTH_RATE_LIMIT=true` so the suites and local load testing aren't throttled — a factory rather than a singleton precisely so a test can build one with a tiny `max` and prove the 429 actually fires. **The reason this appears in a changelog as a *correction*:** three testing docs (functional F4.6, the testing-home gaps table, the Go-public checklist) still described it as *not implemented*, which is the worse direction for a security control to drift — a real mitigation that everyone believes is missing gets re-planned, and meanwhile nothing verifies it still works.
- **`POST /api/auth/register` is gone — account creation is `/setup` only** (2026-07-31, Go-public blocker): the single-user login shipped with `/setup` as the real first-run path, but the raw registration primitive was left mounted, unauthenticated, "for the tests and a future multi-user path". On a local-first install that is a **public account-creation endpoint on a service whose job is creating and running commands on your machine** — anyone who could reach port 3000 could mint themselves a real account and a valid token, and the P3 remote-access work is specifically about making that service reachable from other devices. Removed rather than flag-gated: an env flag is one misconfiguration away from open, and the product already has the two account paths it needs (`/setup` creates the one owner and 409s afterwards; `/login` authenticates). **The integration suite drove `/register` for its own convenience**, which is exactly how a route nobody wants stays alive — those tests now run through `/setup`, and one of them **pins the 404** so it can't drift back in. Verified live against the running backend: `POST /api/auth/register` → 404, and `needsSetup` unchanged (it created nothing on the way out). If multi-user is ever built, registration returns as a designed flow with an invite or owner approval.
- **REST CORS is restricted to `ALLOWED_ORIGINS`, the same list Socket.IO already enforced** (2026-07-31, Go-public blocker): `createApp()` ran a bare `app.use(cors())` — reflect **any** origin — while the socket channel was restricted, so the two halves of one API disagreed about who may call it, and any page the user had open while TaskHub was running could read the REST API cross-origin. Both now read one parser (`backend/src/config/origins.ts`), so they cannot drift apart again. Two deliberate carve-outs: a request with **no `Origin` header is always allowed** (every non-browser caller — the MCP server, `curl`, the integration suite — and CORS was never their gate; authentication is), and an **empty `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` stays permissive** so a dev who never set the variable is unaffected — but no longer silently: the backend now **warns at boot** that any origin can read the API, because "unset" and "deliberately open" look identical from inside the process and only one of them is a decision. Both `.env.example` and `docker-compose.yml` ship the variable set, so the normal path is the restricted one. 11 tests asserting the **response headers** rather than the status code (a refused origin still gets a 200 body — what it does not get is `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`), mutation-checked. Verified live: `http://localhost:7373` allowed, `http://evil.test` refused, no-`Origin` authed call still 200.
  - **A refusal now explains itself, once per distinct origin** (2026-07-31, follow-up): the refusal was correct and silent, which left the expensive half — the diagnosis. The failure appears in the **browser** as a generic CORS error while `curl` keeps working and confirms the wrong theory, and the server was the only party that knew *both* the rejected origin and the configured list. It now says so: `CORS: refused origin "…" — not in ALLOWED_ORIGINS (allowed: …). If that is your dashboard, add it and restart the backend.` **Once per origin, not per request** — a dashboard that can't reach the API retries, and a warning that repeats forever is one you learn to scroll past — capped at 20 distinct origins so a sprayed header can't bury the log or grow memory. The origin is attacker-controlled header content on its way into a log line, so it is stripped of control characters and truncated: without that, a crafted `Origin` could forge log entries around itself (same instinct as the CSV export's formula neutralization — the sink decides what its own dangerous characters are). Verified against the running backend: three requests from one origin produced **one** line, a second origin produced its own.
- **Production dependency advisories back to zero across all three JS packages** (2026-07-28): the P1 claim below ("production `npm audit` is 0") had **drifted** — new advisories landed against dependencies that were clean when it was written, and nothing re-checks it, so it sat as a stale-but-plausible statement. Re-audited and remediated: backend `axios` 1.16.1 → **1.18.1** and `body-parser` 2.2.2 → **2.3.0** (10 axios advisories incl. prototype pollution and `maxBodyLength` bypasses, plus a body-parser DoS where an invalid `limit` silently disables size enforcement); frontend `axios` likewise; `mcp-server` `fast-uri` and one moderate transitive. **Production `npm audit` is now 0 for backend, frontend, and mcp-server.** All 561 tests green after the bumps (backend 319, frontend 115, mcp-server 127) and all three builds pass.
  - **One advisory was deliberately left open, with reasoning rather than a version bump:** `react-router` 7.12.0–8.2.0 *"RSC Mode CSRF Bypass Allows Action Execution Before 400 Response"* (high). npm's only offered remedy was `--force`, which **downgrades** `react-router-dom` to 7.11.0 and is flagged breaking. The vulnerable path is **React Server Components mode**, and the frontend is a static Vite SPA that uses only `BrowserRouter` / `useNavigate` / `useLocation` — no `createBrowserRouter`, no `RouterProvider`, no loaders or actions, and no server at all. The code path is unreachable, so downgrading a core router (risking the routing + deep-link work) bought nothing. **Recorded here rather than left as a mysterious non-zero audit** — an unexplained advisory is how the next person learns to ignore the whole report. **Closed 2026-07-31 by the forward fix — see below.**
- **Security audit + production dependency remediation** (P1 QA final slice): audited REST auth/session/CORS/error handling, agent Socket.IO auth and command signing, structured command execution, stale-pruning safety, frontend storage/XSS-sensitive patterns, native HTTP jobs, and dependency advisories. Remediated high production advisories by updating lockfile transitive packages: backend `engine.io` 6.6.8 -> 6.6.9, `socket.io-adapter` 2.5.7 -> 2.5.8, `ws` 8.20.1 -> 8.21.0, `form-data` 4.0.5 -> 4.0.6; frontend `form-data` 4.0.5 -> 4.0.6. Post-fix production `npm audit` is 0 for backend and frontend; NuGet vulnerability checks are clean for the agent projects. Remaining public-hosting security work is tracked in the Go-public checklist: REST CORS restriction, auth/API rate limiting, Helmet/security headers, real account/session flow, per-user agent pairing, and SSRF guardrails for hosted native HTTP jobs.
- **Agent-auth hardening follow-ups** (P1, closes the three residuals from the 2026-07-09 WebSocket-auth review): (1) **`task:create`'s trigger is now signed** — the per-command HMAC message gained a canonical trigger field (`canonicalizeTrigger` in `backend/src/utils/scheduler-conversion.ts`, mirrored by `AgentAuthenticator.CanonicalizeTrigger` in C#; fixed-order pipe-joined, null → `"none"`), so an on-path attacker on a plaintext link can't rewrite a task's schedule in flight. The agent parses the trigger before verifying and the shared golden vector gained null-trigger + Weekly-trigger create cases. (2) **Per-command replay guard** — the agent rejects a `(ts, sig)` already accepted within the freshness window (bounded, self-pruning cache). (3) **Session-key reconnect overlap** — the agent keeps the current session key (non-expiring) plus any superseded key for a grace window and verifies against either, so a command signed under the old connection isn't false-rejected when the socket rekeys. `emitSignedCommand`'s `extra` param was removed (trigger is a first-class signed field now). **Breaking:** the create signature changed — agent and backend must deploy together. Verified live end-to-end (Windows task with a real Daily trigger created + a signed run >120s after handshake, which caught and fixed a latent "all keys expire after 120s" bug). Backend 121 tests (+3), agent 56 tests (+10).
- **Server-side per-token template substitution** (P1, closes the structured-command residual): the Apply flow used to substitute `{{placeholders}}` into the command string in the browser and send the finished string; the backend then tokenized it, so a parameter value containing an unbalanced quote could split into extra literal arguments. The client now sends **raw parameter values** (`parameters` in `POST /api/templates/:id/apply`) and the backend owns substitution (`backend/src/utils/templateCommand.ts`): the template is tokenized first, then values are substituted per-token — a quoted or composite placeholder (`-File "{{scriptPath}}"`, `--flag={{v}}`) is always exactly **one argument** regardless of quotes/spaces in its value, while a bare placeholder token (`{{args}}`) keeps its intended multi-argument semantics (contained to that position). Parameters are validated server-side against the template's declared definitions (required non-blank, `select` values must match an option, undeclared keys and non-string values rejected with specific 400s). The resolved `{ executable, args[] }` action is passed straight to the agent connector (no re-tokenization of the display string); non-argv platforms (native HTTP jobs) get whole-string substitution. The legacy pre-substituted `command` body field still works (deprecated). Verified end-to-end: a `scriptPath` of `C:\nonexistent\x.ps1" & calc.exe` registers in Windows Task Scheduler as a single backslash-escaped argument to `powershell.exe`. Tests: backend 116 (+23), frontend 45.
- **Structured command handling — no `cmd.exe /c` shell wrap** (P0, completes P0 security): the agent registered every created task as `cmd.exe /c "<command>"`, so a template parameter substituted into the command string was interpreted by the shell — command injection / RCE on the user's machine. The backend now structures the resolved command into `{ executable, args[] }` (`backend/src/utils/commandParser.ts`, a quote-aware tokenizer) and the agent registers a **direct ExecAction with no shell** (`agent/TaskHub.Agent/ArgumentQuoting.cs`, Windows `CommandLineToArgvW` per-arg quoting). With no shell parsing the line, a malicious parameter can at most become a malformed argument to the intended program — it can't launch a second process. The structured action is covered by the `task:create` HMAC signature (a new action-canonical field, executable + args joined by 0x1F, pinned by the shared cross-language golden vector). `ITaskScheduler.CreateTask` now takes an `AgentExecAction`; two Tier-B seed patterns that need a shell (`pg_dump … > backup.sql`, `del /q %temp%\\*`) now name `cmd.exe /c` explicitly. **Breaking:** agent and backend must be deployed together (the create signature changed). Tests: backend 93 (+10), agent 46 (+7).
- **Multi-tenancy route scoping + JWT fail-fast + dev-JWT rotation** (P0): the task API ran as a single hardcoded `cli_user_placeholder`, and its by-id routes looked tasks up by `id` alone — so any authenticated caller could list every user's tasks (`GET /api/tasks` had no owner filter) and read/patch/delete/**run** anyone's task by id (IDOR; the run path is remote command execution on another user's agent). Every task route now derives `userId` from the verified JWT (`req.user.id`) and scopes its queries: `GET /` filters by `userId`; `PATCH`/`DELETE`/`POST /:id/run` use `findFirst({id, userId})` and 404 on a miss; `GET /:id/executions` confirms task ownership before returning logs; create/native/health/sync/discover and the template `apply` route attribute to the token user. The backend now **fails fast on a missing/weak `JWT_SECRET`** (`>= 16` chars; the `|| 'fallback-secret'` default that let anyone forge a token is gone). The **committed dev JWT was removed** from `frontend/src/api.ts` and replaced with an env-sourced `VITE_DEV_TOKEN` (gitignored `.env.local`, no committed fallback), and **`JWT_SECRET` was rotated** — invalidating the leaked token. Added `.env.example` for backend and frontend. Templates remain a shared seeded catalog by design.
- **`PlatformConnection.config` encrypted at rest** (P0): the connection config column — which holds API keys, agent IDs, and pairing secrets — was stored as plaintext JSON, so any DB dump or backup leaked every credential. It's now encrypted with the existing AES-256-GCM helpers, routed through a single new module (`backend/src/auth/connectionConfig.ts`): `serializeConfig()` encrypts on write, `deserializeConfig()` decrypts on read. The Prisma column stays `Json`; the ciphertext is stored as a JSON string (`iv:tag:ciphertext`), and since encrypted configs are strings while legacy configs were objects, the stored type disambiguates the two — **legacy plaintext rows keep working with no migration**. Every config read (create/apply-template/health/sync×2/run across `routes/tasks.ts` + `routes/templates.ts`) and both writes (the native + Windows connection auto-provision) go through the helpers; no route reads `connection.config` directly anymore. Tampered/wrong-key payloads throw (GCM auth failure is surfaced). Importing the module also makes the backend fail fast on a missing/short `ENCRYPTION_KEY` at boot. Tests: +5 `connectionConfig` cases (round-trip, empty, legacy passthrough, null/undefined, tamper rejection) → 83 backend tests.
- **Agent WebSocket authentication** (P0): the agent↔backend Socket.IO channel is no longer unauthenticated. The backend now runs an `io.use()` handshake middleware (`backend/src/ws/agentAuth.ts`) that verifies an HMAC-SHA256 proof over `(agentId, nonce, ts)` against a shared `AGENT_PAIRING_SECRET` (with a 120s freshness window and constant-time compare) and **rejects any socket that can't authenticate** — closing the "any process reaching port 3000 gets remote command execution" gap. The handshake is single-use within its window (server-side nonce replay cache) so a captured handshake can't be replayed to displace the live agent. State-changing commands (`task:run` / `task:create` / `task:set_status`) are signed server-side with a **per-session key** derived from the handshake nonce, and the .NET agent (`AgentAuthenticator.cs`) **refuses to execute any command it can't verify** (bad signature or stale timestamp). Socket.IO CORS is restricted to `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` (empty by default; only the non-browser agent connects today). The agent reads its server URL + pairing secret from env (`TASKHUB_SERVER_URL`, `TASKHUB_PAIRING_SECRET`, `TASKHUB_AGENT_ID`; WSS-capable) **or** a gitignored `agent/TaskHub.Agent/appsettings.json` (env overrides the file; `appsettings.example.json` is the committed template, copied beside the published exe so the Windows auto-start task picks up the secret without re-exporting it), and the backend fails fast if `AGENT_PAIRING_SECRET` is unset/weak. The auto-start / self-heal scheduled task was re-published and validated end-to-end (published agent authenticates from its bundled config; Windows connector reports `HEALTHY`). A shared cross-language HMAC golden vector is asserted by both `backend/src/ws/__tests__/agentAuth.test.ts` and `agent/TaskHub.Agent.Tests/AgentAuthenticatorTests.cs` so the two implementations can't silently diverge. **Breaking:** existing agents must be rebuilt with the pairing secret configured.

### Fixed
- **The Connect Pack still told AI tools that a folder "must already exist"** (2026-08-04): three of the pack's five artifacts — `SKILL.md`, `AGENTS.md` and the task-authoring reference — carried that invariant one day after **`createFolder` shipped** (2026-08-04, above). Nothing failed: the pack *describes* Cronsole, so a false claim in it is invisible to every suite, and the drift was caught by `/sync-surfaces` while stamping v1.5 for an unrelated feature. A pack copy lives on someone else's machine and **can never be corrected**, so shipping a version bump over a known-false invariant is the one thing that version number must not do. All three now describe the flag *and its cost*: the agent is elevated, so a folder it creates carries an administrator ACE and the user cannot delete it themselves — the guidance is to prefer an existing folder from `list_folders`, name the folder before creating it, and never reach for the flag merely to clear an error.
- **The Task health card mixed two populations in one row of numbers** (2026-07-31): on the real machine it read **12 critical · 25 attention · 1 unmeasured · 218 healthy**, under the label *"across 352 tasks"* — four figures summing to 256, describing no population at all. Three tiers were counted over the **personal** list (Windows' own tasks excluded, as the card intends) while `Healthy` was taken straight from `data.counts.ok`, the server's summary of **every** task on the machine. The correct personal row is `12 · 25 · 1 · 57`, across **95** tasks.

  Found by cross-checking a number the new *Failures* view produces (37 = 12 critical + 25 attention) against the panel that ought to agree with it — the panel's own "38 needing attention" is a *different and correctly labelled* question, since it also counts the 1 unmeasured task, but chasing the difference surfaced the real one beside it. The card now derives every tier, the `across N` label, and the system-hidden count from **one scoped list**, and deliberately stops reading the server's `counts` at all: they summarize the whole machine, and keeping them as a shortcut for a single tile is exactly how the two drifted apart. The system-hidden figure now reports **all** system tasks rather than only the unhealthy ones, so it finally matches the dashboard's own `257 system hidden` instead of quietly saying 96.

  **Why no test saw it:** the fixture built `data.tasks` from the *unhealthy* tasks only and faked the healthy ones by bumping `counts.ok` — but the route returns **every** scored task. So the suite modelled a payload the server never sends, and the one tile reading the unscoped field was the only thing that could tell the difference. A fixture that disagrees with the server is a suite that agrees with itself; same family as the stubbed Prisma client in [#22](troubleshooting/README.md#22-deleted-a-windows-task-synced-and-cronsole-still-shows-it--while-reporting-missing-n) and the stubbed serializer in [#9](troubleshooting/README.md#9-agent-payload-arrives-with-every-field-empty). The fixture now materialises healthy tasks, and a new test pins that every tier is counted over the same population as its label. **That test did not work on the first try and mutation testing is what said so**: `toHaveTextContent('0')` passes against `"10"` by substring, so the unscoped value it exists to reject sailed straight through — anchored to `/^0$/`, the mutation fails as it should. Verified live: the card now reads `12 · 25 · 1 · 57` across 95 tasks with 257 system hidden, and 95 + 257 = 352.
- **The login screen still said "TaskHub"** (2026-07-31): the first thing a logged-out user sees carried the old brand through the entire rename, because the markup split it as `Task<span className="text-primary">Hub</span>`. **A brand broken across an element is invisible to a grep for the brand** — `grep TaskHub frontend/src` returned two code *comments* and looked clean. Two independent safety nets both had a blind spot exactly here: a rename sweep works on text, and the E2E suite authenticates past this screen with the dev token, so `AuthScreen` has never once rendered in a browser under test. Now a single contiguous `<h1>Cronsole</h1>`, matching the Sidebar — the two-tone accent isn't worth a string no tool can find. Guarded by two cases in `AuthFlow.test.tsx` asserting the **rendered** heading in both login and first-run-setup modes, mutation-checked by restoring the old markup and watching them fail. Same family as [#33](troubleshooting/README.md#33-a-rename-pass-silently-disables-the-back-compat-it-just-added--and-rewrites-the-tests-too) and [#35a](troubleshooting/README.md#35a-and-the-holder-was-the-agent-itself-running-under-its-pre-rename-name): **a rename is only as complete as the search that verifies it, and every one of these hid from that search a different way.**
- **The E2E suite could not run at all, and failed as if the app were broken** (2026-07-31): `frontend/.env.local` held `VITE_DEV_TOKEN=""`. The browser has no login step in the suite by design, so all nine specs landed on `AuthScreen` and died on a missing dashboard heading — a failure that reads like a broken frontend, with the only real evidence buried in a page snapshot in `test-results/*/error-context.md` showing a **Sign in** form. Because E2E is the one suite **not in CI**, an empty credential disables the entire full-stack gate while every other suite stays green. Documented as a preflight in [testing/README](testing/README.md), with the command to mint a token and the warning that its `id` must be a real `User.id`. Suite is 9/9 with a valid token.
- **Every "stop the agent" now looks for the pre-rename process name too — three checks had gone blind at once** (2026-07-31): the published exe was renamed `TaskHub.Agent` → `Cronsole.Agent`, but **a process keeps the name it was launched with**, and the agent on this machine had been running since 2026-07-28. All four call sites had been updated to the new name *and to nothing else* — `Get-AgentProbe` reported **DOWN**, `Invoke-Down` printed **"agent already stopped"**, `Republish-Agent.ps1` logged **"no Cronsole.Agent process running"** and then published over a locked exe, and `Migrate-RepoFolder.ps1` renamed the checkout into a live handle. So one rename made three independent shutdown checks wrong simultaneously, in the single way that leaves each of them still **reporting success**; the migration then failed all ten attempts and printed `pid 47312 TaskHub.Agent.exe` two lines under `agent already stopped`. `Stop-ScheduledTask` was no help either — it stops what the task *launched*, and this process had outlived its launcher.

  All four now match **both** names, as a find-only alias: we start only `Cronsole.Agent`, and the legacy entry exists solely to be found and stopped. A legacy-named agent probes **`WARN`, not `UP`** — it is genuinely running and doing real work, but it is also running code older than the checkout, and a bare `UP` would hide the staler of the two facts; `cronsole up` branches on the **process** rather than the probe state, so a `WARN` agent does not get a second agent started beside it (two agents on one machine both answer `task:run`). `Invoke-Down` also **verifies the kill** instead of trusting `-ErrorAction SilentlyContinue`, which was swallowing the `Access is denied` an unelevated stop gets from a `RunLevel Highest` process — the same "reported success" failure one layer down. `Republish-Agent.ps1` additionally **deletes pre-rename leftovers from `agent\publish\`**: `dotnet publish` writes into the output folder without cleaning it, so `TaskHub.Agent.exe`/`.dll` had survived next to the new binaries, launchable by a double-click into a process nothing would find — and unable to start at all, since its `runtimeconfig`/`deps` were already replaced. **Generalizable, and not about agents: renaming a binary does not rename the processes already running it. Keep the old name in every lookup that *stops* something** — the blind spot opens the instant you rename and closes only when every pre-rename process has been restarted, which is exactly what the broken lookup can no longer do. Logged as [#35a](troubleshooting/README.md#35a-and-the-holder-was-the-agent-itself-running-under-its-pre-rename-name); sibling of [#33](troubleshooting/README.md#33-a-rename-pass-silently-disables-the-back-compat-it-just-added--and-rewrites-the-tests-too).

  **The migration's abort path now re-enables the launcher tasks itself** rather than printing the command to do it. Step 3 disables `\Cronsole-Stack\` so nothing fires into a half-renamed tree; exiting with them still off means a failed migration has silently turned **logon start** off too, and you find out at the next reboot — the precise silent-breakage shape the script exists to prevent. It restores what it disabled, reports any it could not, and names the stack as stopped-and-not-restarted.
- **`cronsole down` now stops the dev-server *tree*, not just whatever owns the port** (2026-07-31): `Stop-Port` killed the process holding the listening socket, which under `npm run dev` is the innermost node — `tsx watch` and its `cmd.exe` wrapper are its **ancestors** and survived, still holding a working-directory handle inside `backend\`. Nothing local noticed, and nothing should have: the port really was free and the service really was stopped, so the shutdown report was **true but not the claim that mattered**. The cost landed somewhere else entirely — stage 4's checkout-folder migration (`Migrate-RepoFolder.ps1`) failed all ten rename attempts with `Access to the path … is denied`, because Windows will not rename a directory that any process has as its CWD, and the surviving watcher had been up for four days. `Invoke-Down` now calls `Stop-DevServerTree`, which sweeps `node.exe`/`cmd.exe` whose **command line** names `backend\` or `frontend\` under the repo. Matching the command line rather than the CWD is the deliberate part: it reaches every process the script itself started and **cannot** reach an editor, terminal, or AI session that merely happens to be sitting in the folder — those hold an identical handle and are not ours to kill.

  **The migration's failure message now names the holders** instead of listing "common culprits". The old text suggested an editor or Explorer window, which sends you closing windows while the actual holder is a headless watcher no window will ever reveal. It also now says the thing a process scan structurally cannot: a holder whose only claim on the folder is its **current directory** does not name the path on its command line, so an **empty list is not evidence that nothing is holding it** — close the session you are reading the error in and re-run from elsewhere.

  **The migration also repoints the skill junction, which it was silently going to break.** `.claude\skills\cronsole` is a junction, and a junction stores its target as an **absolute path** — so renaming the checkout would have left it aimed at a folder that no longer exists, and Claude Code would have loaded no `cronsole` skill at all without reporting anything, because **a dangling junction is not an error, it is an absence**. That is the same silent-breakage shape the script already guards for the three `\Cronsole-Stack\` tasks, and the script's stated job is repointing whatever names this folder by absolute path — a scheduled task was just never the only such thing. It now re-runs `setup-skill-links.ps1` from the new root and **verifies by resolving the link and testing its target**, not by `Test-Path` on the link itself, since a dangling junction is still a directory entry and would pass the naive check. Logged as [#35](troubleshooting/README.md#35-the-checkout-rename-fails-with-access-to-the-path-is-denied--right-after-down-reported-everything-stopped); same family as [#23](troubleshooting/README.md#23-network-error-after-a-reboot--the-database-system-is-starting-up), where a surviving `tsx watch` is exactly what kept a crashed container alive and stopped `restart: unless-stopped` from firing.
- **The Import modal showed the raw platform enum — "TASKHUB NATIVE", months after the product became Cronsole** (2026-07-31): the section heading was `platform.replace(/_/g, ' ')`, so it printed a database identifier instead of a name. `frontend/src/platform.ts` opens with the words *"Single source of truth for platform display"* and maps `TASKHUB_NATIVE → 'Cronsole'`; every other surface — task cards, sidebar, settings, apply, clone — calls its `platformLabel()`. The Import modal was the one caller re-deriving it, which also meant it printed "WINDOWS TASK SCHEDULER" where the rest of the app says "Windows". **The same shape as the CORS drift and the `isSystem` lens: a second mechanism answering a question that already had one definition.** Fixed to call `platformLabel()`, pinned by a mutation-checked test asserting the raw enum reaches the screen in no form.

  Worth recording *how* it was found: **by opening the modal.** The three rename passes swept 1,513 + 712 + 95 occurrences and every suite stayed green, because nothing in the tree contains the string "TASKHUB NATIVE" — it is **computed at render time** from an enum the rename deliberately kept. CLAUDE.md justified keeping `PlatformType.TASKHUB_NATIVE` on the grounds that it is "an identifier no user ever sees"; that was true of the database and false of the screen. **A stored identifier is only invisible while every display path agrees to translate it** — so "no user ever sees it" is a claim about *code*, not about the value, and it expires the moment one caller formats the raw thing.
- **`taskhub status` asks each service whether it is serving, instead of asking whether a port is bound** (2026-07-28, P1): the script reported Postgres, Redis, the backend and the frontend **all DOWN while `/api/health` returned 200 and the frontend returned 200**. That is the *inverse* of [#23](troubleshooting/README.md#23-network-error-after-a-reboot--the-database-system-is-starting-up), where `Test-Port 3000` said *"backend already up"* about a dead container's held port and refused to start the real one — **both directions are the same root cause: a port is not a service**, and "the probe could not run" was being rendered as "the service is down". The generalization was already in the traps table; the script had never been changed to follow it.

  Every service is now probed by asking the service — `GET /api/health` (backend), an HTTP `GET /` (frontend), the docker healthcheck / `pg_isready` (Postgres), and a **RESP `PING` spoken directly over the socket** (Redis, so the probe survives a broken docker CLI). The port is corroboration only, and `Test-PortBound` now attempts a real TCP connect rather than relying solely on `Get-NetTCPConnection`, whose CIM provider is unavailable in some shells — which is what produced the false negative.

  **A third state was the actual fix.** Where something is clearly present but cannot be confirmed to be serving, `status` reports **`[WARN]`** rather than a confident UP or DOWN, and every row prints **the signal it used** (`GET /api/health -> 200`, `docker healthcheck: healthy`), because a claim without its evidence is useless exactly when the claim is wrong. The `up` liveness gate reads the same probe: a held-but-dead `:3000` now names the holder and tells you to clear it, instead of silently declaring the backend up. `Stop-Port` likewise no longer reports "already stopped" for a port a container proxy still answers on. The agent row is honest about its own limit — the agent binds nothing, so the process is the only local signal, and whether it is *connected* is only visible in the app.

  **Verified in both directions**: in a simulated broken environment (bogus docker path + a `Get-NetTCPConnection` that throws — the reviewer's conditions) the four services that were previously called DOWN now report UP with evidence and the one genuinely unverifiable service reports WARN naming why; and against a raw TCP listener that accepts connections and never speaks HTTP — the corpse-port case — `Test-PortBound` is true while `Get-HttpStatus` is 0, which is precisely the WARN branch. Parse-checked under both pwsh 7 and Windows PowerShell 5.1, and confirmed free of non-ASCII bytes (the 5.1 em-dash trap).
- **The closed mobile sidebar is no longer reachable by keyboard or screen reader** (2026-07-28, P1): the drawer was hidden with `-translate-x-full`, which is a **visual** transform only — its nav buttons stayed in the DOM, in the tab order, and in role queries, so a keyboard user tabbing through the dashboard walked an invisible menu before reaching the page, and a screen reader announced navigation that wasn't on screen. The `<aside>` is now `inert` + `aria-hidden` while closed, and **only below `md`** — at and above the breakpoint the same element is the real, always-visible navigation and `open` is meaningless, so inerting on that signal alone would have hidden the entire nav. The viewport is read through a new `useMediaQuery` hook, since a Tailwind class can only change how something *looks*. This is filed as a fix rather than polish because the drawer shipped specifically to meet the `<375px` first-class-mobile requirement, and a drawer that leaves focus behind itself does not meet it. Tests assert the behavior (`byRole` only sees accessible elements) rather than the attribute, and the real browser was used to confirm the one thing jsdom cannot: with `inert` applied, Chrome refuses `focus()` and focus falls to `<body>`.
- **CI green again — a literal NUL in `bulkExport.ts` made it binary to git** (2026-07-28): the `repo-hygiene` job had been failing since the Tools tab landed, across six commits, because a filename-sanitizing regex was written with **actual control bytes** (`[…|<NUL>-<0x1F>]`) instead of escapes (`[…|\x00-\x1f]`). Runtime behavior was correct, but git classified the file as **binary**, so its diff was unreviewable in the PR — which is the whole harm, and precisely what the 2026-07-16 sweep and its CI guard exist to prevent. The guard did its job; **nobody looked at Actions**, because every local suite was green. Fixed to escapes (identical at runtime), verified by the hygiene check, by `git grep` now treating the file as text, and by the 319 backend tests still passing. Recorded in [#17](troubleshooting/README.md#17-a-source-file-is-binary-to-git-because-of-a-stray-control-byte) along with the trap *inside the fix*: a `\x00` typed into a shell/tool pipeline can collapse to a real NUL, so three "successful" replacements swapped the control bytes for identical control bytes and looked like failed writes — build the backslash from its code point instead.

### Changed
- **`ROADMAP.md` is a checklist again; its narrative moved here** (2026-08-04): the roadmap had grown to 337 KB — every item carried its own design record, dated addenda, verification notes and "what the build settled" writeup, so the two questions it exists to answer (*what is done* and *what is still open*) were buried inside prose that was really a changelog under another name. It is now a plain list: state, item, date, one line each, grouped P0 → P3 → Go-public → Open decisions, with completed work in collapsed sections and the template/scheduler taxonomy kept as a reference appendix. **Nothing was deleted** — the entire pre-2026-08-04 narrative is preserved verbatim in the *Roadmap narrative archive* appendix at the bottom of this file. **The split is by tense, not by importance:** the roadmap now says what is true now, and this file says what happened and why. The one thing that made the old shape self-perpetuating is worth naming — an item that records its own reasoning in place has nowhere to be *finished*, so it stays on the plan forever, and a reader cannot tell a live commitment from a completed one at a glance.
- **Schedules are authored and displayed in your own timezone, defaulting to Pacific — storage is still UTC** (2026-07-31): every cron field in the app was labelled `cron · UTC` and took a UTC expression, so applying a template whose schedule reads `0 8 * * *` created a task that ran at **1 AM Pacific**. Nothing was broken — the value was stored honestly, converted to a Windows trigger correctly, and registered by the agent at the right local time — which is exactly why it was easy to miss: the only defect was that the number on screen was in a zone nobody thinks in, and the arithmetic was left to the user. The new **Settings › Schedule timezone** holds a real IANA zone (default `America/Los_Angeles`, with Machine local, UTC and ten common zones offered), and `frontend/src/utils/timezone.ts` converts **at the edge**: the field shows and accepts your zone, `zoneCronToUtc` runs once on submit, and everything below the browser keeps reading UTC. Applies to the Apply-template, New-task, Edit-schedule and Schedule-tester surfaces, the preset chips (`Daily 8am PDT`, not `Daily 8am UTC`), the template cards — which used to print a UTC cron the Apply modal would then silently change — the human schedule line, the dashboard clock readings, and the Windows trigger's start boundary (genuinely UTC on the wire; `TriggerReader.ToUtcHhmm`).

  **The zone deliberately does not reach storage, the API, or the MCP tools.** A cron whose meaning depended on who wrote it would fork the database, and `create_task` has no access to a browser preference — so the machine surface stays unambiguously UTC and only the human surface converts. Every field that shifts prints the stored UTC form beside it, so the dashboard and `list_tasks` never appear to disagree without explanation.

  Two honest refusals rather than plausible guesses: a **date-pinned** schedule whose conversion crosses midnight (`0 4 1 1 *` — the once-a-year expression from [#14](troubleshooting/README.md#14-a-rare-cron-becomes-an-hourly-trigger)) becomes 31 December in Pacific, which cron cannot express, so it stays UTC and says why; and **DST** is stated instead of hidden — a Windows task holds its local wall-clock across the transition, a Cronsole-native task does not, because `NativeScheduler` evaluates the stored UTC cron forever. Day ranges are expanded before rolling (`0 22 * * 1-5` → `0 6 * * 2,3,4,5,6`), the same `parseInt('1-5')` trap that once made every weekday schedule Monday-only in three layers — and only when the shift actually crosses midnight, so a same-day conversion doesn't churn the stored expression. 33 new unit tests covering both halves of the DST year plus two authoring tests on the Apply modal; full frontend suite 219 green, build clean.
- **Cronsole rename, stage 4 — the local checkout folder** (2026-07-31): `…\Repos\Live_Apps\taskhub` → `…\Repos\Live_Apps\cronsole`, performed by `scripts/startup-task/Migrate-RepoFolder.ps1`. Stages 1–3 each ruled this out and the standing note in `CLAUDE.md` called the path permanent, on the grounds that every scheduled task embeds it absolutely — **but that is an argument for migrating the holders, not for never moving**, and stages 1 and 2 had already built the machinery to do exactly that. Three things held the path: the three `\Cronsole-Stack\` launcher tasks, the published agent, and the `.claude\skills\cronsole` junction.

  **Anyone with a clone must repoint it through that script, not by hand** — a stale `-File` path, working directory, or junction target fails *silently* at the next fire rather than erroring. Absolute paths in a personal `.mcp.json` or editor config are outside the script's reach and need updating by hand; the committed `.mcp.json.example` uses a relative path and is unaffected. Verified after the move from Windows and the backend rather than from the migration's own report: all three tasks resolve, the junction target exists, `cronsole.ps1 up` → ALL UP, and both platforms report `HEALTHY` with a fresh `lastSync` from an agent running out of the new path. See ROADMAP › Rename stage 4 for the three "reported success" failures the migration surfaced.
- **Cronsole rename, stage 3 — the public surface** (2026-07-31): the three GitHub repos are renamed — `taskhub` → **`cronsole`**, `taskhub-registry` → **`cronsole-registry`**, `taskhub-site` → **`cronsole-site`** — along with their descriptions, which still advertised TaskHub (and, for the site, still called itself a "landing page" retired on 2026-07-28).

  **The template registry moved to `https://mikesailab.com/cronsole-registry/`.** This is the one with real consequences: it is the documented `TEMPLATE_REGISTRY_URL` installed apps sync their catalog from, and **GitHub redirects a renamed repo's git URLs indefinitely but not its Pages paths** — the old path 404s immediately, as verified. It was safe to move *exactly once*: `TEMPLATE_REGISTRY_URL` is commented out in both `backend/.env` and `.env.example`, so **zero installs were fetching it**, including this machine. The identical move after the first real install would be a silent outage on someone else's machine, on a schedule nobody watches. Both `CLAUDE.md` §9 and `publish-frontdoor.ps1` previously argued the URL was frozen *because* moving it would break installs; that argument was correct and is now spent, so both were rewritten to say what actually happened and that there is no second free move.

  95 occurrences across 25 files updated by a scripted pass with an explicit protection list — the same reviewable method stages 1 and 2 used. **`scripts/rename-stage2.mjs` was protected**, because the strings it holds are stage 2's own *protection list*, annotated `// Stage 3 (public surface)`: rewriting them would have made the script misreport its own scope and erased the record of what it deliberately held back — [#32](troubleshooting/README.md#33-a-rename-pass-silently-disables-the-back-compat-it-just-added--and-rewrites-the-tests-too)'s exact shape, a rename pass helpfully rewriting the thing that guards it. `CHANGELOG.md` and `ROADMAP.md` history stays under the name it happened under.

  **The local clone directory stays `taskhub`, permanently** — every scheduled task embeds it as an absolute path and it is not user-visible branding. `CLAUDE.md` said it "moves (if ever) with the stage-3 repo rename"; that is now decided and says so.

  **The front door moved to `https://cronsole.mikesailab.com`** once the DNS record resolved (checked against two independent resolvers before flipping, since flipping first takes the site down for however long propagation takes). The 25 held references went with it, including the **Connect Pack**, whose version bumped **1.1 → 1.2**: `CONNECT_PACK_HOME` is literally "where a reader should look for a newer copy than the one in their hand", so changing it is a content change, and the stamp is the only way a downloaded copy can say it predates the move. The pack's guard test caught the bump mid-flight — the constant said 1.2 while the authored markdown still said v1.1 — which is exactly its job.

  **`taskhub.mikesailab.com` is retired** — its DNS record was deleted, so it now `NXDOMAIN`s (confirmed on both `8.8.8.8` and `1.1.1.1`). It was briefly kept on the expectation that GitHub would forward it, and it returned a flat **404** instead — logged as [#34](troubleshooting/README.md#34-the-old-custom-domain-404s-after-moving-a-pages-site-to-a-new-one). **Pages redirects only `<user>.github.io/<repo>` to the configured custom domain, never a second custom domain pointed at the same IPs**, because serving is keyed on the `Host` header matching the repo's `CNAME`. The DNS looked healthy throughout, which is what makes it a trap: the record resolved to the right GitHub IPs and the failure lived one layer above, in vhost routing no resolver can see. Retired rather than redirected because the URL was never advertised — and because **a name that doesn't resolve reads as "gone" while one that 404s reads as "broken"**, which makes deletion the more honest failure mode as well as the cheaper one.

  Verified live: both hosts 200, registry serving all **55** templates and 6 packs (corrected 2026-07-31 — this and two ROADMAP lines said 61, which matches no build; the hosted `index.json` and the local artifact both report 55/6), content-addressed template and pack paths resolve, `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *` still permits the front door's cross-origin fetch, HTTPS enforcement re-enabled after the new cert provisioned, and both pages serve **zero** stale references. Backend 434 unit + typecheck green.
- **Import says what it will do before you click, and stops re-selecting everything** (2026-07-31): the Import modal offers **None / Non-system / All** presets, remembers the categories from your **last import**, and states the outcome up front — *"This will import 214 tasks across 12 folders"* — with the primary button reading `Import 214 tasks` instead of `Sync 12 Categories`. The count was always free: `/discover` already returns every category's total; the modal just never showed it, so "12 categories" quietly meant 214 rows.

  **First run and every run after are now different questions.** A fresh install still defaults to everything non-system, because a user who imports nothing sees an empty product — but that generous default fires **once**, and afterwards the modal restores your own last answer. Inheriting the first run's answer forever is how a 352-row dashboard happens. A remembered category whose folder has since gone is dropped rather than shown ticked (the count must not promise tasks that aren't there), and the selection is saved **only when you actually import** — ticking boxes and closing changes nothing. Both mutation-tested.

  Also removed: two `console.log`s that printed the entire discovery payload — every task name and native path on the machine — into a console that gets screen-shared and screenshotted.
- **Cronsole rename, stage 2 — the names this machine points at** (2026-07-31): the agent project and its published exe (`Cronsole.Agent.exe`), the launcher scripts (`cronsole.ps1`, `Start-Cronsole.ps1`), the three scheduled tasks, and the Task Scheduler folder `\Task-Hub\` → **`\Cronsole-Stack\`** — kept deliberately separate from the app's own `\Cronsole`, so the app's prune-its-own-folder logic can never reach the launcher tasks. Browser state migrates forward on first load (`migrateLegacyStorageKeys`), so the rename does **not** log you out, reset your theme, or drop a runtime API-origin override; `index.html`'s pre-paint script reads the old theme key too, so there isn't even a one-frame flash. A one-time elevated migration (`scripts/startup-task/Migrate-ToCronsole.ps1`) registers the new tasks **before** removing the old ones and keeps any old task whose replacement didn't appear — a half-migrated machine that still starts beats a tidy one that doesn't.

  **Still not renamed, and now for the last time:** `PlatformType.TASKHUB_NATIVE` (a Postgres enum on every task row), the Docker database/role/volume names (renaming them orphans the volume — the one thing here that can't be rebuilt from the repo), and the local clone's directory name, which every scheduled task embeds as an absolute path.

  **The sharpest find:** the stage-2 pass rewrote stage 1's `CRONSOLE_* ?? TASKHUB_*` env fallbacks into `CRONSOLE_* ?? CRONSOLE_*` — a tautology that compiles and reads correctly while silently dropping the compatibility it exists to provide — **and rewrote the tests guarding them, so they still passed.** Legacy prefixes are now assembled from parts so no future pass can rewrite them, and each fallback is mutation-checked. Also fixed in passing: a script that ends with `Stop-Process -Id $PID` kills its caller, so the migration runs each registrar in its own process and verifies the outcome via Task Scheduler rather than an exit code.
- **TaskHub is now Cronsole** (2026-07-31, stage 1 of 3): the name was crowded — two App Store apps, a hospitality SaaS, a crypto micro-task platform, `task-hub.app`, and Azure's own "Task Hubs" in the automation space — and both the 2026-07-13 research and the independent 2026-07-28 review recommended renaming before the first public link. `Cronsole` has an unregistered npm name, effectively no GitHub presence, and is legible on sight to the audience it is for; cron is also literally the storage model (§9: every schedule is 5-field UTC cron). This stage renames the **in-repo brand and code identity only** — 183 files, 1,513 occurrences, applied by a scripted pass with an explicit protection list so it is reviewable and repeatable rather than 183 hand edits.

  **An existing setup keeps working.** `CRONSOLE_TOKEN`, `CRONSOLE_API_URL`, `CRONSOLE_TIMEOUT_MS`, `CRONSOLE_MCP_ALLOW_DESTRUCTIVE` and `CRONSOLE_FAILURE_WEBHOOK_*` all **fall back to their `TASKHUB_*` predecessors**, warning once. Those variables live in the user's environment, not in this repo, and on Windows need a fresh terminal to even re-read ([#8a](troubleshooting/README.md#8a-and-restart-from-a-fresh-terminal-does-nothing-under-vs-code)) — renaming them in code alone would have taken the MCP tools away from a working host and silently stopped failure notifications, the one feature whose entire job is to speak up when something breaks.

  **Deliberately NOT renamed yet:** the `\TaskHub\` Task Scheduler folder and the agent's `\Task-Hub\` launcher, the agent project and its published exe, the Docker database and container names, and the `taskhub.*` `localStorage` keys — every one of those is state *the machine points at*, so renaming it in code without migrating the machine would break logon start, the self-heal, or your session. That is stage 2, where existing tasks get **moved** to `\Cronsole\` rather than orphaned. **Also excluded, for longer:** `PlatformType.TASKHUB_NATIVE`, a Postgres enum stored on every task row (a migration plus a coordinated four-component change, for an identifier no user ever sees), and the historical CHANGELOG/ROADMAP entries, which record what happened under the name it happened under — rewriting those would make the records claim the product was always called Cronsole.
- **Package manifests say Apache-2.0, and the stray `check_db` pokes became a real script** (2026-07-31, Go-public repo hygiene): `backend/package.json` and `mcp-server/package.json` both carried `"license": "ISC"` — the `npm init` default, never updated when Apache-2.0 was chosen on 2026-07-13 — so **two machine-readable license claims disagreed with the `LICENSE` file and the README badge**. Both corrected, plus a real `description` on the backend; `frontend` stays `private: true` with no license field, which is correct. Separately, `backend/check_db.js` and `backend/check_db.ts` sat in the package root — outside `src/`, outside every test suite, and outside `tsconfig`'s `include`, which is precisely why the `.ts` one could sit there **malformed** (`prisma.\()`) without anything noticing. They are replaced by one working, documented script: **`npm run db:check`** (`backend/scripts/check-db.ts`), read-only, printing the generated-Prisma-client currency **first** — a client missing an enum member silently drops writes to it ([#22](troubleshooting/README.md#22-deleted-a-windows-task-synced-and-cronsole-still-shows-it--while-reporting-missing-n)), which makes every count under it a claim rather than a fact — then tasks by platform and status, exclusions, template provenance (managed vs. imported), and the run log. Kept rather than deleted because *"what does the DB actually contain?"* is the question that diagnosed #22. The scripts README also lost a row for the deleted `publish-landing.ps1` and gained the two it was missing. *(A third manifest, `agent/test-server/package.json`, also said ISC — now Apache-2.0 and marked `private: true`, since a mock backend for driving the agent in tests is never published.)*
- **Docs honesty sweep alongside the Go-public work** (2026-07-31) — four statements that had quietly stopped being true, found by mapping the change onto the surfaces it obligates rather than by anything failing: the **Remote Access Guide** still told users TaskHub "authenticates with a development token, not a real login screen" and called Cloudflare Access a stand-in for "TaskHub's not-yet-built login screen" — the single-user login shipped 2026-07-16, and a security guide that is visibly out of date is a security guide people stop believing. It now names what exists **and** what still doesn't (no reset, no refresh, no second account, no per-user pairing) and explains why the network gate is still the real boundary, with TaskHub's login the layer behind it. The manual **CORS check** (Security Checks §10) printed only `%{http_code}` — but a refused origin still returns 200, so **the check could not fail**; it now reads the response headers, and names the two non-failures (a no-`Origin` request is admitted on purpose; an empty `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` is permissive by design). The integration-testing matrix had **I6.3 CORS as ⬜** when it is now automated, and **I6.4 still said "all 5 MCP tools"** when there are 15. CLAUDE.md §3's production-hosting row and the skill's "JWT access + refresh; rotation supported" claim (there is no refresh flow) are corrected too.
- **Dark is now actually the default theme, not just documented as one** (2026-07-28, P1): `useTheme`'s `readStored()` fell back to **`'system'`** both when nothing was stored and when the stored value was unrecognized, so a fresh install followed the OS — and on a machine set to light, TaskHub opened light. Meanwhile `CLAUDE.md` §9, the `index.css` token comments, the README badge and feature table, the skill's invariants, and the functional-test checklist all stated dark was the default. Nothing was broken; **the records were lying**, which is §11a's failure mode aimed at a user this time: someone reads "dark by default", sees light, and concludes their install is wrong. The code now agrees with all six. `system` is unchanged as a mode the user picks on purpose, and the pre-paint script in `index.html` — which had *always* claimed in its comment to default to dark while passing `'system'` — was corrected to match, with a test that fails if the two defaults ever drift again. Resolves the *"Default theme: dark, or follow the OS?"* open decision in favor of dark: the token set, the design system, and the public site are all built dark-first, so following the OS shipped a first paint the product was never designed in.
- **One public page instead of two — the landing page is retired and the gallery is the front door** (2026-07-28): TaskHub had two public pages saying overlapping things: a marketing landing page at `taskhub.mikesailab.com` and the template gallery at `mikesailab.com/taskhub-registry/`. A visitor had to guess which was the real front door, and the landing page's links were the only route to the source. The landing page is **decommissioned**; `taskhub.mikesailab.com` now serves the **gallery**.

  Merged into the gallery's Home so nothing was orphaned: the **product pitch** (it now opens with *"Your automations, finally in one place"* rather than a templates-only hero), the **Why TaskHub** value strip, a **Run TaskHub locally** quick start with a copy button, and **Built in the open** cards linking the app repo, the registry repo, and template requests. A **Get TaskHub** nav link and an updated footer, title and meta description round it out. `landing-site/` and `scripts/publish-landing.ps1` are deleted; `scripts/publish-frontdoor.ps1` replaces the latter and publishes the same `registry-site/index.html` to the second host.

  **The registry base URL deliberately did NOT move.** `https://mikesailab.com/taskhub-registry/` is the documented `TEMPLATE_REGISTRY_URL` that installed TaskHubs already fetch their catalog from — moving it to the new domain would have broken catalog sync for every existing install, and every content-addressed template and pack path with it. Only the *page* moved. The single `index.html` works at both hosts by trying `./index.json` first and falling back to the canonical registry origin when it isn't co-located; GitHub Pages serves `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *`, which was verified before relying on it.

  **A related trap is now guarded.** `CNAME` decides which domain a Pages repo answers on, so both publish scripts explicitly exclude it from the mirror and it is owned by the target repos. Without that, a stray `CNAME` in `registry-site/` would hand `taskhub.mikesailab.com` to the **registry** repo — silently relocating the registry JSON and breaking exactly what the frozen URL protects. `publish-frontdoor.ps1` also warns if the target's CNAME has gone missing, rather than publishing a site the custom domain no longer serves.

  **Verified** by serving the page in the two shapes it ships in: co-located with the registry (relative fetch), and standalone with **no** local `index.json` — where it correctly fell back cross-origin, loaded all 55 templates, and rendered template detail pages and pack bundles from the canonical origin, with zero console errors.

### Added
- **Saved views — name a filter combination, bookmark it, come back to it** (2026-07-31): at 350+ tasks the question is never "show me everything", and the filters that answer the real questions were rebuilt by hand on every visit because nothing persisted them. Five built-in views ship — **My jobs**, **Failures**, **Due today**, **Disabled**, **System** — alongside any number the user saves, each a chip across the top of the dashboard with its own count.

  **The roadmap item said these "compose filters that all already exist". They did not, and finding that out reshaped the work.** The dashboard had category, platform, search, and two **binary** toggles: active-only-vs-everything, and personal-vs-include-system. So only *My jobs* was expressible. "Showing everything" is not "showing only the disabled ones"; "including system tasks" is not "only system tasks"; and there was no run-outcome or next-run filter of any kind. Four of the five named views needed dimensions that did not exist. So the filter state became one `TaskFilters` object (`frontend/src/utils/taskFilters.ts`) with **status** widened to four values (isolation, not just inclusion), the **system lens** to three, plus new **run outcome** and **due window** dimensions.

  **Failures reads the server's verdict rather than forming its own.** The obvious implementation — filter on `lastRunStatus === 'FAILURE'` — would be confidently wrong on the platform that dominates a real dashboard: `ExecutionLog` records runs **Cronsole performed**, so a Windows task's `SUCCESS` there means *the agent accepted the start*, not that the job worked. The real evidence is Windows' own `lastTaskResult`, plus knowing that Task Scheduler's informational codes (`267009` running, `267014` terminated) are not exit codes — all of which already lives in `backend/src/services/taskHealth.ts`. So the view consumes `GET /api/tools/task-health` (`useTaskHealthTiers`, sharing the `['task-health']` cache key with the Tools panel so the two can never show different verdicts for one task) and **no failure rule was written in the browser**. A second copy of that judgement is the drift that once took a whole folder out of every sync ([#20a](troubleshooting/README.md#20a-and-a-renamed-category-silently-stops-syncing-its-folder)).

  **Absence of evidence is carried through to the chip.** A task the server has not scored is `unknown` — never `ok`, never `failing`. With no health data at all every task is unknown, so a "Failures" view would render an **empty list**, and an empty list reads as *"nothing is failing"* — a far more comforting claim than *"I have not looked yet"*. The chip therefore shows **`–`, not `0`**, until the scan lands, and the dashboard says so in a banner above the list. The count is fetched only when a view actually asks about outcome; the default dashboard does not pay for it.

  **A view is a URL.** State lives in the query string (`?view=failures`, or the fields spelled out for an ad-hoc combination), so a view survives a paste into another window. History is written with `replace` rather than `push` — filters are a state of the page, not a sequence of pages, and search-as-you-type would otherwise turn Back into an undo-one-character button. A **matched view always writes its id**, including the one whose filters equal the defaults: the first attempt suppressed it to keep the address bar clean, which was wrong, because a bare URL already means *"this user's saved dashboard defaults"* and those need not be the same thing — someone with `defaultPlatform: WINDOWS` would click "My jobs" and land back on a Windows-only list. Two states cannot share one encoding. A `view=` id that does not exist (shared from another machine, or since deleted) degrades to the explicit fields and then to the defaults, never to an empty dashboard.

  Three smaller decisions worth keeping: the **layout is not part of a view** (grid/list/kanban/schedule persists separately — a view answers *which tasks*, not *how they are drawn*, and picking "Failures" should not silently rearrange the page); **built-in views are code, not data**, so a later fix to what "Failures" means reaches everyone instead of only users who had not opened the dashboard yet; and **deleting a saved view does not change what you are looking at** — the filters stay exactly as they were and simply stop having a name. The two pre-existing toggles still work and now render a **third state** each rather than mislabelling one they cannot express (`Disabled only` in rose, `System only` likewise), and clicking the system lens still writes the standing preference while a *view* setting it does not — a view is a lens you look through, not a new default. The empty state now names the constraints in force, because with six dimensions "you have none" and "your filters excluded them all" look identical.

  Tests: frontend **305** (+59: 27 `taskFilters`, 21 `savedViews`, 10 `ViewBar`, 1 regression below), six mutation checks — reading an absent tier as `ok`, comparing "today" in UTC, letting kanban relax a deliberate isolation, treating an unparseable next-run as epoch zero, rendering an unknown count as `0`, and restoring the bare-URL encoding — each failed exactly the tests it should. Verified live against the real 352-task machine: `My jobs 85 · Failures – · Due today 30 · Disabled 10 · System 257`, Failures resolving to **37** once the scan landed with facets re-faceting to match, saving a view collapsing the URL to `?view=v-…`, and deleting one leaving the list untouched. No console errors.
- **Bulk enable/disable — select tasks in any view and change them together** (2026-07-31): every dashboard action was a per-task modal round-trip, which does not survive 350 tasks — the same asymmetry that forced *Bulk-clear MISSING* (one sync flags 56 at once; clearing them was 56 open→confirm→close cycles). Checkboxes on all four views, a toolbar `Select all N shown`, shift-click ranges, and a bar carrying the actions. **One id-keyed `Set` covers every view** because all four already render from one `filteredTasks` array — grid and list map it, schedule re-sorts it, kanban partitions it by status — so "what is on screen" already had one definition and selection needed no per-view concept.

  **The new route reports per task, not per batch.** `POST /api/tools/tasks/status` (on `/api/tools`, because cross-task routes compete with `/:id` on `tasks.ts`) drives the **same signed `task:set_status`** the single toggle uses — **no new agent verb and no new authority**. What it adds is the reporting a partially-successful batch needs, since at this scale partial is the *normal* case: five outcomes, not a boolean. `unchanged` is separate from `updated` (a task already in the target state is not a success to count and not an error to invent — re-sending a request reads `0 disabled · 2 already disabled`), `refused` covers a `MISSING` task the server declines *before* asking the platform, and `failed` is a real platform refusal such as an elevation/ACL denial. **The run stops the moment the agent looks gone**, marking the remainder `skipped` with the reason: a Windows call against an offline agent costs its full ~15s timeout, so carrying on would turn a 50-task selection into a twelve-minute request ending in fifty copies of one error. A *per-task* refusal deliberately does **not** halt — it says nothing about the next task.

  Three details that are load-bearing rather than polish: the bar names **how many selected tasks the current view isn't showing** (kanban shows the disabled tasks the other three hide, so switching views can strand part of a selection — it is kept, keyed by id, and the gap is stated, exactly as `Active Only · 110 hidden` does); each button names **what it will actually change** (`Enable 3`, not `Enable 12`, when nine are already enabled); and a failed task **stays selected** afterwards, because the next move is a retry and re-finding three rows among two hundred is the problem this feature exists to solve. Duplicate ids are de-duplicated before the 100-task cap so a repeat can't push a selection past it, and an id matching nothing comes back in `notFound` rather than vanishing.

  Shipped with the refactor the roadmap asked for while the area was open: the clone/toggle/run cluster was written out **five times** at four icon sizes and three background shades nobody chose, which is why adding one affordance to "every task" looked like a five-file change — now `components/TaskRowActions.tsx`, with the selection rules extracted to `utils/taskSelection.ts` as pure functions so the awkward parts (shift-range order, what happens to a selection when the view changes under it) are testable without standing up the dashboard. Tests: backend 471 (+14, mutation-checked — disabling the agent-down detector fails four of them), frontend 246 (+27). **Verified live against real Windows tasks and confirmed from Windows itself** (`Get-ScheduledTask` → `Disabled`), not from Cronsole's own report.
- **Execution analytics on the Tools tab — failure trend, duration trend, and idle tasks** (2026-07-31): the three questions `ExecutionLog` could not answer while it was readable only 20 rows at a time, per task — *"what failed this month?"*, *"which tasks are getting slower?"*, and *"what hasn't run lately?"*. One new route (`GET /api/tools/analytics`) over one new service (`services/runAnalytics.ts`), no agent work, and no new data: the bulk read shipped with the health score and this is the analysis on top of it.

  **Each of the three reads a different source, and that is the whole feature rather than an implementation detail.** `ExecutionLog` records runs **Cronsole performed**, so: the **failure trend** reads it and is labelled as such — an empty week means Cronsole triggered nothing, not that nothing ran; the **duration trend** reads it but **only `native-execution` rows**, because a Windows row times the round trip of *asking the agent to start something*, so ranking those would compare handshakes to jobs (the excluded count is stated, since on a Windows-only machine that list is legitimately empty and an empty list with no explanation reads as broken); and the **idle report** cannot use it at all for Windows — "no rows in 30 days" would flag every task on the machine — so Windows tasks are judged from **Windows' own `lastRunTime`** in the sync snapshot. That is `taskHealth.ts`'s two-sources rule applied across tasks instead of within one.

  **Four outcomes, not two.** *Idle* (evidence of a last run, older than the threshold), *never run* (a different fact and a different fix), *no run evidence* (the agent has never reported a last-run time — reporting this as idle would flag the whole machine the moment an agent is one build behind), and *disabled / on-demand*, which are **not** failures: parking a task is the recommended safe action and an on-demand task has no schedule to be late for. Everything not assessed is counted out loud, so "nothing is idle" can't be misread as "everything is fine". Every idle row carries the evidence sentence naming its source.

  **Totals are exact; the chart is honestly partial.** The counts come from an aggregate over the whole window and never depend on the row ceiling. If the ceiling is hit the trend reports a **narrower covered span** — a complete chart of a shorter period rather than a period-shaped chart with holes in it — and says so. Empty days are drawn as empty columns, because a trend plotted only from days that have rows draws a straight line across an outage.

  **Days are cut in the reader's timezone**, sent with the request and echoed back in the response; an unknown zone is a 400 rather than a silent fall back to UTC, since a chart bucketed in the wrong zone looks exactly like one bucketed in the right one. The chart's identity never rests on hue — emerald and red separate by only ΔE 8.1 under deuteranopia, so every stacked segment carries a surface gap, the legend is always present, and hovering **or tab-focusing** a bar prints that day's numbers into a live region. Tests: backend 23 unit + 12 integration against real Postgres, frontend 12 — three mutation checks on each side.
- **Schedule tester on the Tools tab** (2026-07-31): type a cron and see what the platform will actually do with it — the trigger it registers as, the warnings, and the next five run times in **local and UTC** — before any task exists.
