It creates nothing. `POST /api/tasks/preview` now returns `requestedRuns`, `effectiveRuns` and a `diverges` flag alongside the existing `score`/`warnings`/`trigger`/`lossy`, all from one new service (`services/schedulePreview.ts`) so the New Task modal and the tester cannot answer the same question differently.

  **It shows two lists, and that is the feature.** The trap it exists for isn't that cron is hard to read — it's that on Windows the cron can be **discarded**: any expression the converter doesn't recognize is replaced with a fixed hourly trigger, so `0 4 1 1 *` — the schedule you pick *because* it can't fire during a test — becomes ~8,760 runs a year ([#14](troubleshooting/README.md#14-a-rare-cron-becomes-an-hourly-trigger)). The card renders that as one run next January under *"what you asked for"*, beside an hourly list under *"what will actually run"*, headed **"This is not the schedule you typed."** The trap was documented in three places and visible in none.

  **What it refuses to do is equally deliberate.** An `approximated` step (an uneven `*/N`) gets **no** effective dates. The reverse converter maps a `PT7M` repetition straight back to the same step expression, so a round-trip reports the two schedules as identical when the drift is precisely the point — Windows repeats continuously from a start boundary while cron restarts each hour. Simulating that properly means writing a second scheduler, which is the confident-lie risk this feature exists to remove, so run times are shown only where they can be **derived**, never inferred, and the drift stays stated in words. A failing test found this, not review. Also folded in: `isValidCron` had been copy-pasted into two route files and is now one definition. Backend 434 unit + 120 integration, frontend 167.
- **Run history export (CSV) + an automation health score** (P2/P3, 2026-07-28): shipped together because they need the same thing first — **one bulk `ExecutionLog` query**. Until now run history was readable only 20 rows at a time, *per task*, so *"which tasks failed this month?"* had no answer anywhere in the product. New `GET /api/tools/history` (JSON or CSV) and `GET /api/tools/task-health`, and **Task health** + **Export run history** cards on the Tools tab.

  **The finding that reshaped the score, found before writing it:** `ExecutionLog` rows are created in exactly **two** places — `POST /api/tasks/:id/run` and `NativeScheduler`. It records runs **TaskHub performed**, not runs that *happened*. A Windows task firing on its own schedule writes nothing, and a manual Windows run logs `SUCCESS` meaning *"the agent started it"* (fire-and-forget; `durationMs` times the trigger, not the work). Scoring Windows tasks from it would have reported nearly every task on a real machine as never-run — **a confident lie at dashboard scale**, and the exact hazard the roadmap item was already warned about.

  So the evidence for a Windows task comes from **Windows**. The agent now reports `lastTaskResult` (the exit code) and `numberOfMissedRuns` on the read-only `task:list` — two extra properties on a `Task` object it already had open, no new verb and no signature change. **Needs an agent republish.** This also delivers most of the P2 *Task-detail trust indicators* item, which asked for exactly this field.

  **Three rules the scorer is built on.** *Score only what can be evidenced* — every signal carries the field it came from, and the UI renders it beside the verdict. *Absence of evidence is `unknown`, never `ok`* — a task on an un-republished agent reports `unknown` and says which agent to republish, because reading an absent field as "never ran" would flag every Windows task at once. *The number ranks, it does not grade* — fixed weights, `100` minus their sum, whose only job is sorting worst-first; it never appears alone. Disabled is deliberately **not** unhealthy: parking a task is the recommended safe action, which is why `set_task_status` ships ungated.

  **The CSV is honest about what it holds.** Every row carries a `runKind` (`native-execution` vs `manual-trigger`) so `status` is readable, and the UI says an empty period means TaskHub triggered nothing — not that nothing ran. It ships **UTF-8 with a BOM** (Excel otherwise mangles non-ASCII task names) and **neutralizes formula injection**: TaskHub stores command lines, so a task named `=cmd|'/c calc'!A1` would otherwise make the report a live payload the moment someone opened it. A new index on `ExecutionLog.triggeredAt` backs the cross-task range — the existing index leads with `taskId` and cannot serve it.

  **Two design bugs were found by running it against the real 354-task machine, not by the tests.** (1) Four of the five worst-scoring tasks were `\Microsoft\` system tasks — re-creating precisely the burial the dashboard's Personal lens was built to stop; the panel now applies the same server-owned `isSystem` verdict, hides them by default and **names how many** (37 personal vs 99 system on this machine). (2) `1073807364` (`DBG_TERMINATE_PROCESS`) was being read as an exit code and reported as *"the last run failed"* for tasks that were merely stopped at shutdown; it is now *"stopped before it finished"*, which moved three tasks out of critical. Both are now pinned by tests.

  **The Tools tab is a uniform grid, and health leads with its summary.** Every card is an equal-height flex column with a `min-h` floor and its action row pinned to the bottom, so rows line up instead of ending ragged; anything that can grow (a restore plan, the health list) scrolls inside its own card rather than stretching the whole row. Task health shows **only the counts** by default — a card on a utility tab should answer its question at a glance — with the per-task list behind an explicit disclosure and a **Collapse** control at the bottom once it is fully expanded, so closing it doesn't mean scrolling back up to the control you scrolled past.

  **Health lives on the Tools tab, not the Dashboard.** It shipped first as a panel above the task list and took over the main content area on the screen people actually work in. Tools is where it belongs by that tab's own rule — it reads across every task and acts on none. The card is self-contained (deep-links to the task rather than taking Dashboard state) and **always renders**, including an explicit *"nothing needs attention"*: a banner you didn't ask for should stay quiet, but a **tool** that disappears when all is well reads as a missing feature.

  Tests: backend 406 unit (+45) and 120 integration (+13) against real Postgres, frontend 158 (+13), agent 151; the two highest-consequence behaviors mutation-checked (treating "no evidence" as healthy, and dropping formula neutralization — both caught). New traps logged as [#29](troubleshooting/README.md#29-prisma-migrate-refuses-to-run-migration-was-modified-after-it-was-applied--and-offers-to-drop-your-database) (an applied migration file is immutable, comments included — and Prisma's only suggested remedy drops your database) and [#30](troubleshooting/README.md#30-a-route-500s-on-real-data-while-tsc-is-green--a-cast-on-a-query-result) (a cast on a query result is a promise the query cannot keep).
- **Restore tasks from a backup — the other half of the export** (P2, 2026-07-28): bulk export shipped on 2026-07-28 and restore did not, which made the Tools tab a filing cabinet: TaskHub could tell you 56 tasks had gone MISSING and hand you a folder of XML, and could not put a single one back. New **Restore** card on the Tools tab, backed by `POST /api/tools/restore/tasks` and a new **signed** agent verb, `task:import`. Takes a `.zip` an export produced, a picked folder, or individual `.xml` files.

  **It plans before it writes, and the plan costs no writes.** Every request first works out one of four verbs for every file — `create` / `overwrite` / `skip` / `refuse` — from the machine's *real* tasks and folders, both readable through the two **read-only** agent verbs that already shipped. `dryRun: true` returns that plan and touches nothing, so the UI shows *"this will change 3 tasks on this machine"* **before** offering the button that does it. `RegisterTaskDefinition` overwrites silently and the agent runs elevated, so restore must never be the first moment you learn what an archive contains. An offline agent is a **502, not a blind restore** — a plan built without knowing what already exists would confidently promise to create tasks that are already there.

  **Overwrite is refused by default.** A task already on the machine is reported *"already exists — left exactly as it is"*: not a failure (nothing is wrong and the user's task is intact) and emphatically not a success (nothing was restored). That third state survives all the way to the wire and the screen rather than being collapsed into a boolean. Replacing is an explicit checkbox, and Windows enforces it independently via `TaskCreation.Create` so a task created between the plan and the write still cannot be clobbered.

  **The XML is inside the signature, by hash.** The XML *is* the task — its action, its trigger, and the account it runs as — so an unsigned definition would leave the path and flags protected while the thing that actually executes could be swapped in flight. It enters the canonical message as a **sha256 of its UTF-8 bytes** (a fixed-width digest, unambiguous across the language boundary, rather than arbitrary `|` bytes inside a pipe-delimited message), pinned by a golden vector both suites assert. Both flags are signed too, for the reason `folder` is: each widens what one command may destroy or create.

  **One standing invariant was deliberately amended.** "TaskHub creates exactly one folder: its own `\TaskHub`" exists because folder deletion needs elevation, so a folder TaskHub creates is a door only the user can close — it was written after a template apply left three empty folders nobody asked for. A restore is the opposite consent situation: the user is asking for *their own* tree back, by name, from a file they chose, and refusing would mean a restore onto a reinstalled machine refuses every task in the archive. So **Recreate missing folders** is an explicit opt-in and the result **names every folder it created**. `\TaskHub` remains the only folder TaskHub ever *removes*.

  Smaller decisions worth knowing: the destination path comes from the export **manifest**, else the **`<URI>` Windows wrote into the XML**, else the **filename** — in that order, because `safeSegment` made filenames lossy on the way out, and the UI warns when a destination was guessed. Two files targeting one path refuses the second (both would "succeed" and one would survive). `\Microsoft\` is refused in the plan *and* independently in the elevated agent. Files travel as **base64 bytes**, never as JSON strings, so the UTF-16 LE + BOM encoding Windows requires survives the round trip. And restoring a task puts it on the machine — it does **not** make TaskHub track it; that still takes an explicit Import.

  **Needs an agent republish** to take effect ([#7](troubleshooting/README.md#7-new-agent-command-502-times-out-until-the-agent-is-republished)) — a running agent has no `task:import` handler. The **preview works without one**, since it only uses read-only verbs. Scope is Windows XML only; a TaskHub-native JSON restore is not in this slice, stated rather than silently absent.

  **Verified live against real Task Scheduler**, reading Windows rather than TaskHub — the reporting layer cannot be its own witness. A probe task was registered by hand in its own folder and exported; the plan **skipped** it while it existed; task and folder were deleted; the plan then **refused with the folder named** (checkbox off) and **planned exactly one folder** (checkbox on); the commit reported `created` with `foldersCreated: ["\TaskHubRestoreProbe"]`, and `Get-ScheduledTask` confirmed the round trip — `Ready`, with action, trigger, description and, critically, **principal intact** (`UserId=mikes LogonType=Interactive RunLevel=Limited`). That settles the one thing no unit test could: `TaskLogonType.None` really does preserve the XML's own principal rather than re-registering the task as somebody else. Restoring again with overwrite off returned `exists` and left the **registration date unchanged**; with overwrite on it returned `replaced`. A genuine `\Microsoft\Windows\Defrag\ScheduledDefrag` export was **refused**, and Defrag was untouched.

  **The live pass found the real cost of the folder carve-out, and it is sharper than the invariant assumed** — logged as [#28](troubleshooting/README.md#28-a-restored-task-or-the-folder-it-landed-in-cant-be-deleted-access-is-denied). Because the agent runs **elevated**, a task it restores — and any folder it creates — get an administrator ACE. So the user who created the original *unelevated* could no longer delete the restored copy: cleanup had to go back through TaskHub (import → Delete from Windows, which routes through the same elevated agent), and the leftover empty folder needed an elevated prompt, since TaskHub only ever prunes its own `\TaskHub`. The original rationale called a TaskHub-created folder "a one-way door only the user can close"; in practice it is a door **only an administrator** can close. The design stands — refusing would fail every task in an archive on a reinstalled machine — but it is now stated **on the checkbox and again in the plan**, so it is a decision rather than a surprise.

  Tests: backend 361 unit (+33) and 8 new integration cases against real Postgres, agent 151 (+25), frontend 145 (+7), with four load-bearing behaviors mutation-checked. New traps logged as [#27](troubleshooting/README.md#27-a-route-that-takes-an-upload-413s--and-raising-the-global-body-limit-is-the-wrong-fix) and [#28](troubleshooting/README.md#28-a-restored-task-or-the-folder-it-landed-in-cant-be-deleted-access-is-denied).
- **Untrack — remove a task from TaskHub without deleting it from Windows** (P2, 2026-07-28): `DELETE /api/tasks/:id` removes the **real Task Scheduler entry** via a signed agent command, and it was the only removal a user had. So an over-import had **no undo that didn't destroy someone's actual scheduled tasks** — and the Import modal makes an over-import one click away (the live dashboard: 352 tracked tasks, **257 of them `\Microsoft\`**). New `POST /api/tasks/:id/untrack` drops TaskHub's row and its run history, makes **no platform call at all**, and leaves the scheduled task running.

  **The design is in the labeling, not the code.** Two removals that differ only in blast radius, one irreversible, must not look alike — so they are separate verbs (**Remove from TaskHub** / **Delete from Windows**), separate mutations, separate icons and colours, and confirm copy that names **what survives** rather than what goes. A shared "Delete" with a checkbox is the version of this that eventually erases a backup job.

  **Untrack had to not fight the next sync.** A task removed while still on the machine would be re-imported by the next sync that includes its category — correct by the sync's own logic and indistinguishable from "untrack didn't work". A new `TaskExclusion` table remembers it, keyed on the platform-native `externalId` (never the user-renameable `category` — [#20a](troubleshooting/README.md#20a-and-a-renamed-category-silently-stops-syncing-its-folder)) and **subtractive only**: an exclusion can remove a task from a sync, never add one, so "which categories do we sync" still has exactly one definition.

  **The way back is explicit re-import**, and it announces itself in both directions: `/discover` reports `excludedCount` per category **before** you commit (an amber `+N removed` chip in the Import modal), and the sync toast says *"Re-imported N tasks you had removed from TaskHub."* afterwards. Sync Now (`scope: 'tracked'`) deliberately never clears exclusions — a routine refresh undoing a deliberate removal is the invisible fence again. Untracked tasks are counted separately in the un-imported signal (`excludedCount`, like `systemCount`), because nagging someone about a choice they made on purpose is the fastest way to teach them to ignore the banner.

  Refuses `TASKHUB_NATIVE` tasks with a 400: they exist only inside TaskHub, so "remove but keep it" cannot be true, and doing a delete under a reversible label is the exact mistake this feature prevents.

  **Verified live end to end, from outside TaskHub:** a throwaway Windows task was untracked — the row vanished, and **Windows' own `Get-ScheduledTask` still reported it `Ready`** — then a tracked sync left it out (`excludedCount: 1`, task did not return), then an explicit category import cleared the exclusion and brought it back (`exclusionsCleared: 1`), then it was deleted properly and the exclusion table left empty. Tests: backend 328 unit (+9) and 6 new integration cases against real Postgres, frontend 138 (+10), MCP 135 (+8).
- **`untrack_task` on the MCP surface — 14 → 15 tools, ungated** (P3, 2026-07-28): before this the only removal an agent had was the gated, irreversible `delete_task`. So an assistant asked to "clean up my dashboard" had exactly one tool for the job and it destroyed real scheduled tasks — **the same gate-makes-the-safe-path-harder failure that keeps `set_task_status` ungated**. Gating deletion is only honest once a safe removal exists without the gate. Wraps the new route with no new logic; `delete_task`'s description now names it as the tidy-up alternative. Mirror surfaces updated in the same change per §11a: both README tool tables, `SKILL.md`, `task-authoring.md` (with a new *three verbs, three blast radii* table), the Connect Pack (rebuilt), and CLAUDE.md.
- **A persistent system/personal split on the dashboard** (P2, 2026-07-28): Import excludes `\Microsoft\` by default, but that guard fires **once**; afterwards nothing distinguished an OS-owned task from one the user wrote, so every headline number, category chip, view mode and un-imported signal counted 257 rows nobody will ever act on — **the 95 tasks that matter were a rounding error in their own dashboard**. `GET /api/tasks` now returns a server-owned `isSystem` verdict (`TaskService.isSystemTask` — the predicate the untracked-tasks summary already used, never re-derived in the browser), and the dashboard gained a **Personal / Incl. System** toggle, persisted in settings.

  It is a **separate control** from Active Only rather than a shared mode: they answer different questions ("whose task is this?" vs "will it ever fire?") and the useful default is both at once. Per the lesson from the active/all toggle, it **says what it is hiding** (`Personal · 257 system hidden`) with three redundant signals — colour, icon, count — and it only renders when the machine actually has system tasks. The two rules that make it correct (outermost lens; count taken over *all* tasks, not the filtered set) live in `applySystemLens` so tests pin them rather than the next reader of a 900-line component.
- **Gallery search is global, and packs are now a filter** (registry, 2026-07-28): three changes to the template gallery, plus a latent routing bug they exposed.

  **1. Search moved into the header, on every view.** It previously lived *inside the filter rail* — which is a collapsed drawer on mobile and doesn't exist at all on Home, Collections, or a pack page. So the catalog had search, but only if you were already on Browse and only if you opened the filters. It now sits in the nav on every page, with a **`/` shortcut** to focus it and **Escape** to clear, and typing from any view jumps straight into filtered Browse results. The input deliberately lives **outside `#app`**, because `route()` replaces `#app.innerHTML` on every render — an input inside it loses focus and the caret mid-keystroke.

  **2. Pack is a filter dimension.** Packs shipped earlier the same day but Browse couldn't see them, so the only way to view one was the separate Collections page. There is now a **Pack facet** with cross-faceted counts, active pills, a `#/browse?pack=<id>` deep link, and a **"Filter & search this pack"** button on each pack page that hands off to a filtered Browse. Membership is read from the registry, so there is no second definition of what a pack contains. Selecting two packs is OR within the dimension (in *either*), matching how availability and category already behave.

  **3. The filter rail is itself searchable.** With 40+ tags truncated to 14 behind a "+more", finding the *filter* was its own search problem. A **"Search filters…"** box narrows the options across every facet, hides facets with no match, and reveals all matching tags instead of the truncated set. It is a **separate state field** from the template search — the two do different jobs and neither resets the other.

  **The bug this surfaced:** `#/browse?pack=monitoring` landed on **Home**. `parseHash()` split the raw hash on `/` *before* stripping the query string, so `parts[0]` was `"browse?pack=monitoring"` — matching no view and falling through to the landing page. This was **pre-existing**: the older `?tag=` deep link had the identical defect and nobody noticed, because tag chips navigate to a bare `#/browse` and apply the filter in JS rather than through the URL. Every query-string deep link had been silently 404-ing to Home. Fixed by splitting the path only.

  **Verified in a real browser** on the live registry data: typing in the header search filters live while keeping focus through every keystroke (the failure mode that made the header placement necessary); pack facet counts cross-facet correctly (`docker` → Developer 2, Backup & Cleanup 1, Monitoring 1, rest dimmed); search and pack filter compose (`codex` + AI & Agents = exactly 3); the rail search collapses to just `#git`/`#github` while leaving template results untouched, proving the two searches are independent; the `?pack=` deep link and the pack-page hand-off both land on a correctly filtered Browse; zero console errors. **Honest gap: the mobile layout was not visually verified** — the browser window would not actually resize in this environment (it reported success while `innerWidth` stayed 2124). The narrow-screen rule is reasoned from measured widths (brand + icon buttons consume ~241px of a 375px row, leaving the search ~118px), so below 620px the nav wraps and the search takes its own full-width row. Worth a real device check.
- **Template packs are downloadable — one file, one import** (registry, 2026-07-28): the gallery could only hand you templates **one at a time**. Now every collection has **Download all N**, which serves a pre-built bundle containing the whole pack.

  **Packs became a first-class registry concept rather than a gallery trick.** They previously existed only as JavaScript predicates inside `registry-site/index.html` — `tags.includes('dev')`, `category === 'monitoring'`. That is invisible to the registry, to the app, and to any other consumer, and worse, membership was **derived**: tagging an unrelated template `dev` silently changed what the "Developer Pack" contained. Tolerable while a collection was just a filtered view; **not** tolerable once a pack is a file that lands in someone's catalog.

  So membership is now **declared** in `backend/src/catalog/packs.ts`, and `buildRegistry` **throws** on an unknown or duplicated template id — the failure moves from silent drift to a broken build. `index.json` gained an optional `packs[]`, and the artifact gained `registry/packs/<id>.json`, content-addressed by sha256 exactly like templates.

  Decisions worth keeping:
  - **The bundle is the shape import already accepts.** `{ taskhubCatalogVersion, pack, templates[] }` — `POST /api/templates/import` reads `.templates` and ignores the rest, so downloading a pack is one file and one import, not N downloads and N imports. No backend change was needed at all.
  - **`packs` is optional in both directions**, so it shipped without a coordinated release: a registry published before packs has no key and still parses, and an app built before packs parses a registry that has one (Zod's `z.object()` strips unknown keys rather than rejecting). With no packs declared the key is **omitted entirely**, keeping a pre-packs registry byte-identical.
  - **No timestamp inside a bundle.** Registry files are hashed over exact bytes; a clock in the payload would change the hash every build and make the drift test meaningless.
  - **The browser never assembles the bundle.** It fetches the published file, so what you download is byte-for-byte what the registry published — verifiable against its `sha256`.
  - **A new sixth pack, "System & Utilities"** (8 templates: service restart, DNS flush, update scan, battery + event-log reports, rclone sync, two heartbeats). Without it those eight belonged to **no** pack, so "download every pack" would have been quietly less than the catalog. Coverage is now 55/55, asserted by a test.
  - **The registry owns the data, the gallery owns presentation** — icons/colors are keyed by pack id with a default, so adding a pack needs no site change.

  **Verified end to end against the running stack**, which is the one thing the unit suite structurally cannot do (it can't prove the wrapper and the real route still agree): the file downloaded from the gallery in a real browser is **byte-identical** to the published bundle (12,170 bytes), and posting it **untouched** to `POST /api/templates/import` returned *11 total, 10 created, 1 updated, 0 errors*. The test import was then reverted, restoring the catalog to its original 5 rows. All six collections render from the registry with correct counts (20 / 11 / 11 / 8 / 6 / 8) and zero console errors.

  **Tests:** +9 backend (sha256 matches bytes, bundles importable as-is via the real `extractTemplateCandidates`, every member is a real template, full 55/55 coverage, unknown-id and duplicate-id build failures, packs key omitted when empty, declared order preserved, bundles deterministic across clocks). Mutation-tested — dropping either guard, always emitting the key, or scrambling declared order each fails exactly the assertion that pins it. `publish-registry.ps1` now mirrors `packs/` with the same delete-then-copy semantics, so a removed pack's URL stops working instead of silently serving a stale bundle.
- **A "Tools" tab: bulk task backup, and downloads that connect other AI tools** (P2, 2026-07-28): a fifth tab beside Dashboard / Templates / Platforms / Settings, at `/tools`, holding two utilities that act across tasks rather than on one.

  **1. Back up scheduled tasks.** Export **all** Windows Task Scheduler tasks, or all tasks in **one folder**, as native XML. This closes a real hole: the *Clear all MISSING* feature above exists because one deleted Task Scheduler folder can flag dozens of tasks missing at once — and until now that was an unrecoverable loss. TaskHub could tell you the tasks were gone and clear the rows; it could not give them back. New `POST /api/tools/export/tasks` (`backend/src/services/bulkExport.ts` + `backend/src/routes/tools.ts`).

  **The finding that shaped the work: it needed no agent protocol change.** `task:export` takes a raw `taskPath` and never checked that the path belongs to a *tracked* task — it is read-only and unsigned, like `task:list` — so the agent could already export anything on the machine; the restriction to imported tasks lived entirely in the old route, which is keyed on a DB row. Combined with the existing full enumeration, "export every folder" became backend + frontend work only, with **no agent republish**.

  Decisions worth keeping:
  - **It exports the machine, not the imported subset — and says so.** The tasks most at risk are the ones nothing else is tracking. An "Export all" that quietly meant "all the ones we happen to know about" would be the same invisible fence that made un-imported tasks impossible to notice ([#20](troubleshooting/README.md#20-sync-now-never-brings-in-a-task-you-just-created-in-task-scheduler)).
  - **`\Microsoft\` is excluded by default, and the count is shown rather than hidden.** On the real machine the split is 95 user tasks vs **257** system ones; including them by default would bury the 95 that matter, and dropping them silently would be the opposite failure. A checkbox opts in.
  - **The archive is built server-side, and the base64 path exists for the same reason.** Task Scheduler XML is UTF-16 LE + BOM — the only encoding Windows re-imports — so the bytes never travel as JSON text, which is exactly how the MCP `export_task` tool once decoded UTF-16 as UTF-8 into mojibake.
  - **Destination:** the browser's File System Access API gives a real folder picker (Chromium; `localhost` is a secure context, so it works for a local-first app), with a single-ZIP download fallback elsewhere. The picker opens **before** the export request because it needs transient user activation ([#24](troubleshooting/README.md#24-showdirectorypicker-throws-must-be-handling-a-user-gesture-after-an-await)).
  - **The agent is deliberately NOT given a file-write verb.** It is already on the machine and already elevated, so "let it write where told" is the tempting shortcut — and it would turn a task-scheduler agent into a general elevated arbitrary-file-write primitive reachable from the backend, in exchange for a directory picker.
  - Every export carries a `_taskhub-export.json` manifest naming what was saved, what was skipped, and what failed; one unreadable task is recorded and the run continues, because a backup that saves 94 of 95 and says which one it missed beats one that aborts.

  **2. Connect another AI tool.** Downloads that teach Claude Code, Cursor, Codex, or a custom agent to drive *this* TaskHub: a skill (`SKILL.md` + a task-authoring reference), an `AGENTS.md` system prompt for hosts with no skill concept, an MCP config snippet, and a full pack. New `GET /api/tools/downloads` + `/:id`. **These are newly authored, not the repo's own skill** — `skills/taskhub/` teaches an agent to work *on* the TaskHub codebase, so shipping it to an end user would hand them `catalogSync`, `normalize.ts`, and registry-publishing internals instead of usage instructions. Every artifact is version-stamped and points at a canonical URL, because a downloaded copy on someone else's machine is a mirror surface that can never be updated — it cannot be kept current, so it must at least be able to say how old it is.

  **Verified live against the real agent, with evidence from outside TaskHub.** Full-machine export: 352 enumerated → 95 exported → 0 failed, in 1.7s, 257 system tasks skipped. Every one of the 95 files carries the `FF FE` BOM; the archive mirrors the folder tree across 14 folders with no `Microsoft/` present; a bulk-exported XML is **byte-identical** to the same task from `GET /api/tasks/:id/export` (3,908 bytes, zero differing bytes) on both the ZIP and base64 paths. Decisively, an exported file was fed back through **Windows' own `Schedule.Service` COM parser**, which accepted it and read back the action and trigger — the reporting layer cannot be its own witness, so the proof came from Task Scheduler itself. The tab was then driven in a real browser: live counts (95 / 257), the folder picker populated with real folders and counts, scope switching, and zero console errors.

  **Tests:** 51 backend (bulk-export selection, system exclusion, folder scoping, filename collisions, BOM encoding, bounded concurrency, partial failure, manifest; plus connect-pack assembly, version stamping, and an audience guard asserting no repo internals or repo-relative links leak into the shipped pack) and 27 frontend (byte-fidelity of the directory-write path against a fake handle, count arithmetic, scope switching, ZIP-vs-picker branching, partial-failure reporting). All mutation-tested — dropping the `\Microsoft\` exclusion, the BOM, the collision counter, the folder scoping, or the frontend system filter each fails exactly the assertions that pin them. The connect pack's markdown compiles into a bundled module (`npm run connectpack:build`) guarded by a drift test, because `tsc` emits only `.js` and loose `.md` files would never reach `dist/`. Honest residual: the **native folder picker dialog itself** cannot be driven by automation, so the OS dialog is a manual check — the writing logic behind it is unit-tested against a fake directory handle.
- **The dashboard now says what a sync *didn't* import** (2026-07-27): Sync Now can only refresh folders you already track — it **cannot discover a new one** — and it never said so. A user created tasks in `\IAM\` and `\Edge-Radar-MikesAILab\`, pressed Sync Now repeatedly, and got a cheerful `Tasks synced.` every time while 26 tasks sat one fence away; `/discover` had been reporting them the whole time. The fence is correct (selective import is the design); its **invisibility** was the defect, and establishing that nothing was broken cost a full debugging session ([#20](troubleshooting/README.md#20-sync-now-never-brings-in-a-task-you-just-created-in-task-scheduler)). `POST /api/tasks/sync` now returns `untracked: { count, folders, systemCount }` per platform via a new `TaskService.summarizeUntracked`, and the Sync Now toast names the remainder: *"Synced. 26 tasks in 2 folders aren't imported — use Import to add them."* **No new agent round-trip** — it reads the same full pre-filter enumeration reconciliation already computes. Two decisions worth keeping: **(1) OS-owned tasks are counted separately, not folded in.** Verified live on a real machine, the split is 95 user tasks across 13 folders vs **257** `\Microsoft\` tasks — so counting them would pin the message at "352" forever, and **a warning that never changes is one you stop reading**. Excluding them *silently* would be the opposite failure, so `systemCount` is reported, just not counted. **(2) The message is deliberately not gated behind `toastOnSuccess`** — that setting suppresses routine "it worked" noise, and this is its inverse: the one thing the sync did **not** do, and the only prompt a user gets that Import exists. **Verified end-to-end against the real thing, twice.** First arithmetically, by forcing an empty include-set on the real 352-task machine: 95 user tasks / 13 folders / 257 system, and 95 + 257 = 352 exactly accounts for the enumeration — nothing silently dropped — with the probe mutating nothing (`missing: 0`, DB unchanged). Then **for real**: a new `\Testing-Import\` folder holding one task was created natively in Task Scheduler, reproducing [#20](troubleshooting/README.md#20-sync-now-never-brings-in-a-task-you-just-created-in-task-scheduler) exactly. `/discover` saw it immediately, Sync Now correctly still refused to import it (`count: 352`, unchanged), and the response carried `untracked: {count: 1, folders: ["Testing-Import"]}` — where before it would have returned only `{count: 352, missing: 0}` and the UI would have said `Tasks synced.` The toast was then **observed rendering in a real browser**: *"Synced. 1 task in 1 folder isn't imported — use Import to add it."* — the singular grammar path, character-for-character identical to the unit test that pins it. Tests: 6 backend + 7 frontend (the toast copy, including its plural forms, pinned by extracting `describeUntracked` into a pure helper rather than leaving it inside a mutation callback), all mutation-tested — dropping the system-category split, treating "no filter" as "everything is untracked", emitting the message at zero, and double-counting a folder each fail exactly the assertion that pins them.
- **Clear all MISSING tasks in one action** (2026-07-25): a single sync can flag dozens of tasks `MISSING` at once — deleting a Task Scheduler folder takes every task under it, which is the normal case rather than an edge case — but clearing them was per-task only: 55 modal open → confirm → close cycles. The asymmetry was the defect: the operation that creates the mess in bulk offered no way out in bulk. New `DELETE /api/tasks/missing` plus a **Clear N Missing** button that renders only when N > 0 (a permanent "Clear 0" would be noise on a healthy dashboard), with a danger-toned confirm stating exactly what happens: TaskHub's records and their run history go, **nothing on your machine is touched**, and if a task still exists the next sync re-imports it. Three implementation notes worth carrying: the route is registered **above `DELETE /:id`**, because Express matches in declaration order and the parameterised route would otherwise capture `missing` as a task id and answer 404 — a route that silently never runs; it deliberately makes **no platform call**, unlike the single delete, since `MISSING` means the platform already reported the task gone and there is nothing left to remove or confirm; and the query is **guarded against a stale generated client**, because `TaskStatus.MISSING === undefined` inside a **`where`** clause is *ignored* by Prisma and would silently widen to every task the user owns — the exact inverse of [#22](troubleshooting/README.md#22-deleted-a-windows-task-synced-and-cronsole-still-shows-it--while-reporting-missing-n), which under-wrote a field where this would over-delete rows. 5 integration tests against real Postgres (only-MISSING, owner scoping, log cascade, 0 no-op, and route-order), mutation-tested against both a dropped status filter and a dropped owner scope; the confirm/cancel path verified in a real browser, including that Cancel leaves all 55 rows intact.
- **Prompt library** (docs, 2026-07-20): a new [`docs/prompts/`](prompts/README.md) section with copy-paste example prompts for driving TaskHub in natural language, organized by *how* you talk to it. A hub README routes to three method subfolders: [**mcp-server/**](prompts/mcp-server/README.md) (drive a running TaskHub through the MCP server's 15 tools — inspect/convert/create/run/manage/delete, grouped by tool tier, including the real "clean up runaway Python processes" example), [**skills/**](prompts/skills/README.md) (work on the codebase with the `taskhub` skill — understand the system, catalog/MCP work, and trap debugging), and [**rest-api/**](prompts/rest-api/README.md) (drive the REST API directly, the only supported path for the MCP-inaccessible operations: template import/export, save-as-template, sync, pairing). Linked from the docs hub Guides table + footer nav. Documentation only — no code or endpoints changed.
- **Enable/disable a task from the dashboard tiles, and Run is grayed out when a task can't run** (P2 product value, 2026-07-16): enabling/disabling a task previously lived only inside the detail modal's Status card, so it read as missing — and the Run button stayed fully clickable on a **disabled** task, firing a click that could only fail (since [#15](troubleshooting/README.md#15-run_task-times-out-instead-of-saying-the-task-is-disabled), with an honest error rather than a silent hang, but a wasted click all the same). Now every task surface — grid card, list row, both kanban columns, schedule row — carries a **power toggle** (green when active) that enables/disables in place, and the **Run button is disabled with an explanatory tooltip** whenever the task isn't `ACTIVE` (*"This task is disabled — enable it first to run it"*, or a MISSING-specific reason). A `MISSING` task shows no toggle (re-sync or delete it instead), matching the modal. The toggle is wired to the **existing** `PATCH /api/tasks/:id/status` route — for Windows tasks that drives the agent's signed `task:set_status`; no new backend logic. One shared mutation + pending state lives in `Dashboard.tsx` so all views agree; the runnable/tooltip logic is centralized in `frontend/src/utils/taskActions.ts`. Also fixed a pre-existing flaky assertion in `ImportModal.test.tsx` (it sampled the "Sync N Categories" button before the default-selection effect settled) surfaced by the heavier suite. Frontend tests: +4 TaskCard (toggle shown/called, Run disabled when disabled, toggle hidden for MISSING); suite 81, green across repeated runs.
- **Single-user local login — the dev token is no longer the only way in** (Go-public *Account/login system*, 2026-07-16): TaskHub launches local-first and single-user, but the dashboard authenticated with a hand-minted `VITE_DEV_TOKEN` bearer and had **no login UI at all**. Now there's a real first-run + login flow. **Backend** (all on the existing `/api/auth`, JWT + bcrypt that already shipped): `GET /auth/status` → `{ needsSetup }`, `POST /auth/setup` (first-run account creation, 409 once an owner exists), and `PATCH /auth/password` (change password — requires the current one; no email reset on a local install). **Frontend**: an `AuthProvider` state machine (`loading → needsSetup → unauthed → authed`) + an `AuthGate` that renders the first-run **Create account** screen, the **Sign in** screen, or the app; a dynamic token store (a real login token in `localStorage`, falling back to the dev/E2E token) that the axios interceptor **and** the `/ui` socket both read; a **401/403 → auto-logout** response interceptor that bounces an expired session to login (skipping `/auth/*` so a bad password isn't mistaken for an expired session); **logout** in the sidebar + Settings; and a **change-password** form in Settings.
  - **Setup CLAIMS the password-less placeholder rather than creating a second user** — found by live testing, not reasoning. The boot seed always upserts `cli_user_placeholder` (password `''`) to own the shared catalog, so "is setup needed?" is *"is there a user with a password?"*, not *"are there any users?"* — otherwise the setup screen would never appear. Setup sets the owner's email + password **on that same row**, so the owner inherits its task ownership and the existing dev/MCP tokens (minted for that id) stay valid. Verified end-to-end in a browser: fresh DB → Create account → dashboard → logout → login, with the real axios wiring and zero console errors.
  - **Login rate-limiting** (`express-rate-limit`) on `/login` + `/setup` (per-IP; the known 429 gap), skipped under `NODE_ENV=test` and via `DISABLE_AUTH_RATE_LIMIT=true`.
  - **`VITE_DEV_TOKEN` is now a fallback, not the mechanism** — a real login token always wins; the dev token only keeps the E2E suite and local dev working without logging in, and is removed at the real public cut. **The MCP server's `TASKHUB_TOKEN` is unaffected** (separate surface). **Still deferred** (multi-tenant): open registration is closed in the UI but the raw `/register` primitive remains for tests; per-user agent pairing and password reset stay out until/if a hosted product is pursued.
  - Tests: backend +8 auth integration (status / setup / claim-placeholder / change-password, real Postgres) +3 rate-limiter unit (mutation-checked); frontend +7 auth-flow (the full state machine + both screens + error + token-trust). Backend 244 unit + 14 auth integration, frontend 77 — all green.
- **MCP task management — the surface goes 7 → 14 tools** (P3 *MCP surface expansion*, the management verbs, 2026-07-15): an assistant could create and run tasks but could not **manage** them — it couldn't answer *"did last night's job work?"*, couldn't park a task, couldn't fix a wrong command, and couldn't say what was actually registered. Seven new tools, each wrapping a route that **already existed**, with **no new backend logic**: **`create_native_task`** (`POST /api/tasks/native` — a full HTTP job spec with method/headers/body, distinct from `create_task`'s URL-only native path), **`get_task_history`** (`GET /api/tasks/:id/executions`), **`export_task`** (`GET /api/tasks/:id/export`), **`set_task_status`** (`PATCH …/status`), **`update_task_schedule`** (`PATCH …/schedule`), **`update_task_action`** (`PATCH …/actions`), and **`delete_task`** (`DELETE /api/tasks/:id`, gated — below). The wrappers were the mechanical part; **the gating decision was the actual work**, and it's now settled and recorded in the roadmap.
  - **The gate is tiered, not blanket.** Read-only and *reversible* verbs ship ungated; only the *irreversible* one is gated. **`set_task_status` ungated is a deliberate safety choice**: it is the honest way to park a task, and gating it would push an assistant toward the unsafe workaround of cronning a task into silence — which is [#14](troubleshooting/README.md#14-a-rare-cron-becomes-an-hourly-trigger) exactly. **A gate that makes the safe path harder than the unsafe one is worse than no gate.**
  - **`delete_task` requires `TASKHUB_MCP_ALLOW_DESTRUCTIVE=true`**, and when off it is **absent from `tools/list`** rather than present-and-erroring — a tool the model can see is a tool it will plan around, and a capability that announces itself then refuses burns turns on a door that doesn't exist. Only an exact `true`/`1` opens it; a typo, an empty value, or an unexpanded `${…}` literal all fail **closed** (a false negative costs one missing tool; a false positive hands out a deletion verb nobody granted).
  - **Why an env var and not a `confirm: true` parameter:** the model fills a parameter in itself, so it is the caller assuring *itself* it is sure — precisely the deliberation that's missing when there's no confirm dialog. An env var is **out of band**: the human sets it, and no amount of agent reasoning reaches it. That mirrors the posture the elevated agent already takes toward *its* caller (`\Microsoft\` is refused in the agent as well as the backend, because the agent holds the elevation and cannot trust who's asking).
  - **One tool per verb, not mode-flagged mega-tools.** Collapsing the three `update_*` would save two tools and cost the thing that makes them safe: a mode-flagged tool needs one description covering every mode, diluting the per-verb warnings exactly where they matter. **The descriptions are the safety mechanism** — an assistant reads those, not the roadmap.
  - **Honesty carried into the tool text, not just the code.** `get_task_history` says an empty history means *"TaskHub has no record"*, **not** *"it never ran"* — a Windows task firing on its own trigger is recorded by Windows — and warns that a `SUCCESS` is exactly what a hung task reports ([#12](troubleshooting/README.md#12-a-template-passes-every-test-and-still-hangs-on-the-target)). `export_task` decodes the route's **UTF-16 LE + BOM** XML to real text (a plain read would hand back mojibake) and tells the caller it must be *saved back* as UTF-16 or Windows rejects it. `update_task_action` says it **replaces** rather than patches the action.
  - Tests: **49 new (124 total)**, mutation-tested — removing the delete gate, decoding the export as UTF-8, putting undefined optionals on the wire, and dropping either honesty warning each fail a test. The mutation pass **found a weak test of my own** (an alternation `/a|b|c/` that still passed with two-thirds of the guidance deleted) and it was tightened. **Verified live over stdio** against the real backend + agent: the tool list changes with the gate, a task was created → disabled → re-scheduled to `0 18 * * 1-5` → re-pointed at a new command → exported as readable XML → run → deleted, and `\TaskHub` auto-pruned itself afterwards.
- **MCP `list_folders` — find a folder you can actually create in** (P3 *MCP surface expansion*, 2026-07-15): `create_task` shipped with a `folder` parameter an agent had **no way to fill honestly** — TaskHub creates only its own `\TaskHub` and refuses a create into a missing folder (*never create what you cannot remove*), so any other folder must already exist, and nothing over MCP could discover which do. The choice was guess-and-get-refused, or don't use folders. New **`list_folders`** wraps the existing `GET /api/tasks/folders` (read-only, no new backend logic), returning each folder's path, task count, and whether a task can be created there, plus the default folder; optional `search` / `writableOnly` / `limit` (a real machine here has folders in the hundreds — the same route once returned **161**). Sequenced first among the surface-expansion tools **because it's read-only**: the mutating verbs (delete, re-schedule, edit) still wait on the destructive-op gating decision, and "the route already exists" was never the argument for exposing them. Two honesty behaviors carried through from the route rather than re-decided in the wrapper: **an unwritable folder is listed, not hidden** (`\Microsoft\…` shows as *NOT writable*) — the route made that choice so the UI could say *why*, and an omission would read as "that folder doesn't exist" and send the caller hunting for something it can already see; and **the default `\TaskHub` is reported separately from the listing**, because it's created lazily and **pruned when its last task is deleted**, so it is routinely *absent* from a real machine's folder list — an agent reading absence as "unusable" would go invent a folder that then gets refused. That second case was **found by driving the tool live**, not reasoned about: the listing came back without `\TaskHub` moments after the day's test tasks were deleted. **Verified live end-to-end** against the real backend + agent (all fields populated — the [#9](troubleshooting/README.md#9-agent-payload-arrives-with-every-field-empty) camelCase fix confirmed on the very route that produced it, which is exactly what the stubbed suite cannot prove). Tests: 12 new (75 total), mutation-checked — hiding unwritable folders fails four of them. Surface is now **7 tools**.
- **MCP server test suite** (2026-07-15): the MCP server shipped with **no tests** — its tools were covered only by the REST endpoints beneath them, and CI ran build-only, so a broken tool surface reached a host before anything complained. **63 tests** across two files, wired into `npm test` and the CI `mcp-server` job (renamed *MCP server build* → *MCP server tests*). Scope follows the wrapper's contract: it owns **no logic**, so the suite pins exactly what it *does* own — tool registration, input-schema validation, client-side filtering/limits, request-body shaping, error normalization, and honest rendering — and stays out of the backend's business, which has its own suites. **`tools.test.ts`** drives the **real registered tools** through a **real MCP client** over `InMemoryTransport` with a stubbed `TaskHubClient`, so a tool that fails to register or a schema that rejects valid input actually fails a test (calling handlers directly would catch neither). **`client.test.ts`** runs `TaskHubClient` against a **real local HTTP server** instead of a mocked axios — the thing under test *is* how axios reports failures, so a mock would only assert our belief about it. Notable guards: the surface test pins **the exact tool list** (adding/renaming one obligates both README tool tables + the skill per §11a, and those never break loudly on their own — so the test is a deliberate tripwire, not a chore); the unexpanded-`${TASKHUB_TOKEN}` guard is pinned in both directions (refuses the literal, but must **not** reject a real token containing braces — a false positive there is a refusal to start); `list_tasks` "Showing N of M" honesty; `create_task` omitting undefined optionals rather than putting them on the wire; a rejected call **never reaching the API**; lossy conversion surfaced next to a create rather than reported as a clean success; and `convert_schedule` spelling out all five weekday names (the Monday-only `1-5` bug scored 1.0 with no warnings — the rendering is the only thing that made it visible). **Verified the suite actually bites** by mutation: dropping the weekday rendering, swallowing the lossy warnings, and putting undefined optionals on the wire each failed exactly one test, and all 63 passed again on restore — a green suite that can't fail is decoration. **Honest residual, now logged as a coverage gap:** the suite stubs the client, so it proves what the wrapper does, **not** that the wrapper and the API still agree — a route whose response shape moves keeps the stub green while the real tool breaks ([#9](troubleshooting/README.md#9-agent-payload-arrives-with-every-field-empty) one layer up). Hand-driving a wrapped route after changing it remains required.
- **MCP `create_task` — create a task from a command, no template** (P3 *MCP surface expansion*, part a, 2026-07-15): the MCP surface could only create **from a template**, which made the catalog a gate on a capability the app itself never gated — the dashboard has had a free-form New Task since the MVP. An assistant asked to "run this script nightly" had to find a template that happened to fit, or bend the request to one. Found by doing exactly that: asked over MCP for a task that "just launches a PowerShell app", the only path was `tpl_starter_powershell_script`, whose command is `-File "{{scriptPath}}"` — so a `.ps1` had to be *invented for the template to point at*. **The template is the wrong unit when you already know the command.** New **`create_task`** tool wraps the existing `POST /api/tasks` (`name`, `command`, `schedule`, optional `platform`/`category`/`folder`) — **no new backend logic**, so no-shell `{executable, args[]}` structuring, the `\Microsoft\` refusal, the folder-must-exist rule, the per-folder 409 duplicate guard, signed agent commands, and cron→trigger conversion all stay server-side where they're tested. Gated to the creatable platforms (Windows + TaskHub-native; native takes a URL → HTTP GET job and honestly refuses anything else). Lossy schedule conversions are printed **next to the result**, since the backend *accepts* a fallback trigger rather than refusing it — a task can be created on a schedule that isn't the one asked for. The wrapper deliberately does **not** re-derive the trigger to compensate for the backend's mild warning text: that fix belongs in the route, and a wrapper that patches over a backend message has started owning logic. **Verified live end-to-end** over stdio against the real backend + agent: a task registered as a **direct exec** (`Execute: powershell.exe`, no `cmd.exe /c` — the P0 guarantee on a real registration), the trigger converted UTC→local correctly (`0 9 * * *` → `02:00 -07:00`), and it ran to completion — confirmed by Windows' own `LastTaskResult: 0` **plus a real side effect** (a log line), because TaskHub's own `SUCCESS` is exactly what a hung task also reports. Cleaned up after. Surface is now **6 tools**.
- **Skill: task authoring & management guide** (P3 *MCP surface expansion*, part b, 2026-07-15): new [`skills/cronsole/references/task-authoring.md`](../skills/cronsole/references/task-authoring.md), shipped **with** the tool rather than after it. A `create_task` tool without the invariants is a machine for creating *wrong* tasks confidently: schedules are 5-field cron in **UTC**, Windows commands are structured **no-shell** so a shell must be opted into explicitly, `\Microsoft\` is refused, a non-`\TaskHub` folder must **already exist**, and a `0.7` confidence means the schedule was **replaced**, not nudged. Covers the six creation paths and when each is right, command recipes per kind of work (PowerShell script / direct exe / explicit shell opt-in / HTTP / npm / Python / Docker / unattended CLI agent), quoting rules, and a pre-flight checklist. Documents honestly that **enable/disable, re-schedule, edit, delete, history, and export are REST-only** today and tells an agent to say so rather than improvise — specifically, never to "disable" a task by rewriting its cron, which backfires into an hourly trigger. Closes with how to verify: a green suite proves a command *tokenizes*, not that it *runs*, and **when you're testing the reporting layer's honesty, the reporting layer cannot be your witness** — get evidence from outside TaskHub.
- **On-demand elevated agent republish** (dev tooling, 2026-07-14): the .NET agent never hot-reloads and runs at RunLevel Highest, so every agent change needed a hand-opened Administrator prompt — which in practice means changes get tested against a **stale agent**, the exact bug class [troubleshooting #7](troubleshooting/README.md#7-new-agent-command-502-times-out-until-the-agent-is-republished) exists to describe. New [`scripts/Republish-Agent.ps1`](../scripts/Republish-Agent.ps1) (stop → publish → relaunch, refuses honestly when unelevated, logs to `%TEMP%\taskhub-republish.log`, and warns if the dll timestamp didn't advance) plus [`scripts/startup-task/Register-RepublishTask.ps1`](../scripts/startup-task/Register-RepublishTask.ps1), which registers a **no-trigger** `\Task-Hub\TaskHubRepublish` task at RunLevel Highest. Register once elevated; afterwards `Start-ScheduledTask -TaskPath '\Task-Hub\' -TaskName 'TaskHubRepublish'` works from any prompt. **Dev tool** — not registered by `Register-TaskHubStack.ps1` or any installer. Honest about the tradeoff: it is by construction a way to run code elevated without a UAC prompt, but it runs one fixed script from this repo and `\Task-Hub\TaskHubStack` already runs `taskhub.ps1` elevated on a recurring trigger, so it adds an entry point rather than a capability.
- **Windows folder selector on apply** (P2, 2026-07-14): a created Windows task always landed in `\TaskHub\` — the agent hardcoded the folder and both create paths built `externalId` as `\TaskHub\<name>`. That contradicted the rest of the code: `TaskService.extractCategory` already derives a Windows task's category from its **real root folder**, so an *imported* task categorized as `Microsoft`/`Work`/whatever it actually lived under, while every *created* task categorized as `TaskHub` forever. For Windows, **category is a projection of the folder** — so the Apply modal now selects a real one (sourced from a new read-only `task:folders` agent enumeration), and non-Windows platforms keep the local category concept. **Only folders that already exist are offered**, and the agent refuses a create into a missing one: TaskHub creates exactly one folder — its own `\TaskHub`, the same one it prunes when emptied — because deleting a folder needs elevation, so anything else it created would be permanent litter only the user could clear. Never create what you cannot remove. New `folder` param on `POST /api/tasks` and `POST /api/templates/:id/apply`, a new `GET /api/tasks/folders` route, and a `folder` argument on the MCP `create_task_from_template` tool.
  - **`\Microsoft\` and its descendants are refused** (case-insensitively, by root *segment* not prefix — `\MicrosoftEdgeBackups` and `\Work\Microsoft` stay allowed), as is `..` traversal. This is not cosmetic: `RegisterTaskDefinition` **silently overwrites** a same-named task in the same folder and the agent runs **elevated**, so a free-text folder would let the dashboard — or an AI through the MCP tool — destroy a real system task with no error.
  - **The refusal is duplicated in the agent** (`TaskFolderPath.cs`), not only the backend (`windowsTaskFolder.ts`). The agent holds the elevation and calls the overwriting API; a signed command proves *origin*, not *correctness*, so it must not trust its caller.
  - **`folder` is part of the `task:create` HMAC signature**, matching the existing invariant that every field the agent acts on is signed — an unsigned folder would let an on-path attacker redirect a create onto an existing task. Both golden-vector suites were regenerated in lockstep. **Requires an agent republish**; an old agent verifies the old message shape and refuses the create, which fails honestly rather than misplacing a task.
  - Prune-on-delete stays scoped to `\TaskHub\` — empty non-TaskHub folders are the user's to keep.
- **`native-agent-engineer` + `template-curator` subagents, `/doctor` + `/sync-surfaces` commands, `release-engineering` skill** (2026-07-14): `.claude/` gained the coverage the roadmap exposes. `native-agent-engineer` fills a real gap — nothing covered C#/.NET, Task Scheduler COM, HMAC canonicalization, or WiX, which is where the next two P3 items (macOS/launchd agent, installer packages) live. `/doctor` encodes the **three processes that run stale** (Dockerized backend, published agent, `mcp-server/dist/`) plus agent health and token expansion. `/sync-surfaces` mechanizes the new mirror-surface rule. `release-engineering` covers four open roadmap items (installers, distribution & trust, versioning, legal) that `senior-devops` does not.
- **Template gallery site** (P3 — *Template gallery site + selective-import distribution*, part 1, 2026-07-14): a public, rich **browse-and-import** website over the `taskhub-registry` artifact, live at **`https://mikesailab.com/taskhub-registry/`**. Source is a new self-contained `registry-site/index.html` in this repo (inline CSS + vanilla JS, no build/CDN, hash-routed) with four views: **Home** (hero + live stats + curated collections + featured + how-to-import), **Browse** (a real facet rail — checkbox Category/Platform/Runtime/Availability with cross-faceted counts, tag cloud, search, sort, active pills, mobile drawer), **Collections**, and shareable **per-template pages** (`#/t/<id>`: humanized schedule + placeholder-highlighted command + parameter table + honest creatable-vs-manual targets + related templates). **Download** / **Copy JSON** feed the shipped **Templates → Import** path. Published in lockstep with the registry JSON by an extended `scripts/publish-registry.ps1` (mirrors `registry-site/*` into the public repo root). The one-click "Add to my TaskHub" protocol handoff (part 3) remains open. Browser-verified, 0 console errors.
- **TaskHub landing page** (Go-public *Launch surface*, 2026-07-14): a rich, self-contained landing page (`landing-site/index.html`, same dark aesthetic as the gallery) pitching TaskHub and linking the app repo, the template registry repo, and the template gallery — plus a **Get started** section (Docker-Compose quick-start with copy buttons + honest private-beta note) and a **Questions & feedback** section. Deployed to a **new public repo `michaelschecht/taskhub-site`** with GitHub Pages, **live at `https://taskhub.mikesailab.com`** (the `taskhub` DNS record was repointed off a stale Vercel target to `michaelschecht.github.io`; TLS enforced). Source-of-truth in `landing-site/`, mirrored by a new `scripts/publish-landing.ps1`. Hero later set to the brand name **TaskHub** (Task in violet, Hub in cyan). README now links the website, gallery, and registry repo.
- **Core/extended catalog split, Extended Pack, and prune-on-sync** (P3 — *Template gallery site + selective-import distribution*, part 2, 2026-07-14): templates carry an optional **`core`** flag; **`catalogSync` auto-syncs only `core: true`** into a DB, so a fresh install gets a small curated default and the long tail is import-only from the gallery. The built-in core is a deliberately tiny sampler — **5 templates** (PowerShell Script, Daily Database Backup, Weekly System Cleanup, Git Fetch & Prune, Webhook Ping) — and the Templates tab shows a banner linking to the full gallery. **Grew the catalog** with a 15-template **Extended Pack** (`bkp-`/`cln-`/`sys-`/`mon-`/`data-`/`ntf-` — folder zip & robocopy mirror, old-file & recycle-bin cleanup, service restart, DNS flush, update scan, battery report, disk/ping/service monitors, event-log export, rclone cloud sync, Discord + log heartbeats), all Windows-creatable and resolvability-swept; catalog is now **55 = 5 core + 50 extended**. The gallery reads `core` from `index.json` and surfaces an **Availability** facet (Built-in / Import on demand) + a "Built-in" badge. **Prune-on-sync:** auto-synced rows are marked `Template.managed = true` (migration `20260714200000_add_template_managed` backfills existing rows), and after upserting core, `catalogSync` deletes managed rows outside the core set — so **existing** installs converge to the curated default, not just fresh ones. Import-safe: imported / saved-as-template rows are `managed: false` and are **never pruned**; guarded against an empty core so a bad sync can't wipe the catalog. No behavior-breaking migration to the catalog vocabulary — `normalize.ts` whitelists Prisma fields, so `core` never reaches the DB. Applied live: the running instance's catalog dropped 55 → 5 on the next sync (`pruned 50 stale managed`). Tests: backend 195 unit (+ prune scope / empty-core guard / managed-on-upsert), frontend 69, tsc + lint clean.
- **Launch prep — licensing + community scaffolding + audit** (Go-public, 2026-07-13): first pass at the Go-public checklist. **License:** the repo `LICENSE` is now **Apache-2.0** (permissive OSS + patent grant), replacing the proprietary all-rights-reserved notice; README gains a license badge + CI status badge, and the footer reflects Apache-2.0. **Community files:** added `SECURITY.md` (private vulnerability reporting via GitHub advisories + the security model + the "don't expose publicly until real login lands" limitation — non-negotiable for a command-executing tool), `CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md` (Contributor Covenant 2.1), issue templates + `config.yml` (security routed to private reporting, blank issues disabled), and a PR template. **Secret & history audit:** manually scanned all 155 commits + the working tree — **clean on the critical items** (no `.env` ever committed; the real `JWT_SECRET`/`ENCRYPTION_KEY`/pairing-secret values never entered history; personal email not tracked). The committed **dev JWT** is already removed + rotated (invalidated) and only lingers in history → decided to **squash to a fresh public root** at publish time. **Hygiene:** removed the stray `fix_connection.sql` and generalized a hardcoded personal path in the Agent Setup Guide. **Name research:** "TaskHub" is heavily used (multiple app-store apps, a hospitality SaaS, a crypto micro-task platform, `task-hub.app`, Azure "Task Hubs") — rebrand recommended before going public (decision pending). **Auth scope decided:** the local-first launch gets a simple single-user local login (replacing the dev token); full multi-tenant accounts are deferred to an if-hosted future. See ROADMAP › Go-public.
- **CI: MCP server build job** (2026-07-13): added an `mcp-server` job to `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (npm install + `tsc` build) so the new package is guarded in CI, mirroring the frontend/backend jobs. *(Note: Actions runs are currently blocked by the repo's Actions spending budget — an account setting, not a code issue — so checks won't execute until the limit is raised or the monthly minutes reset.)*
- **Frontend refactor** (P3, 2026-07-13): a five-slice quality/UX pass over the frontend. **(1) Accessible modal foundation + custom confirm dialog** — new `frontend/src/components/ui/Modal.tsx` (focus trap, Escape, `role=dialog/alertdialog` + `aria-modal`/labelling, body scroll lock, focus restoration, portal) and a promise-based `hooks/useConfirm.ts` + `ConfirmProvider.tsx` replace the two native `confirm()` gates (task run + task delete; delete gets a red danger tone). **(2) Modal shell adopted across all 8 modals** (Help, Import, CreateTask, CloneTask, ApplyTemplate, EditSchedule, EditAction, TaskModal) so Escape/focus-trap/ARIA/scroll-lock are universal; form modals keep `closeOnBackdrop=false`. A module-level **modal stack** makes only the top-most modal handle Escape/focus, so a confirm/edit dialog over the task modal closes top-first, not all at once. **(3) Mobile sidebar drawer** — below `md` the sidebar is an off-canvas drawer (hamburger toggle, dimmed backdrop, mobile close button; closes on nav-select + Escape) and `main` padding is `p-4 md:p-10`, meeting the `<375px` requirement. **(4) Router + deep links** — added `react-router-dom` + `BrowserRouter`; the active section and open detail modals live in the URL (bookmarkable, back/forward), with `/tasks/:id` opening the task detail and `/templates/:id` the apply modal (`activeTab`/`selectedTask`/`applyTarget` are now URL-derived; deep-links load via SPA fallback). **(5) `Dashboard.tsx` split** 1,830 → **248** lines (the shell) plus `src/screens/DashboardScreen.tsx` (698), `TemplatesScreen.tsx` (746), `PlatformsScreen.tsx` (167) — pure structural move, no behavior change. Verified: 69 frontend unit tests (+ ConfirmProvider a11y suite, Sidebar drawer tests, updated TaskModal/Import portal-query tests), build, lint, and live browser checks per slice (custom confirm + cancel, nested-Escape, the 360px drawer, all deep-links, all three extracted screens with 0 console errors). Follow-up logged: route-level code-splitting (the single bundle now trips Vite's 500 kB hint).
- **MCP server** (P3, 2026-07-13): a new [`mcp-server/`](../mcp-server/README.md) package exposes TaskHub's REST API to any MCP host over **stdio**, so Claude Code / Claude Desktop / Codex / Cursor can list, run, and create scheduled tasks in natural language. **5 tools**, each a thin call through a small axios client (`src/client.ts`) into an existing route — no new business logic, so per-user scoping, no-shell `{executable,args[]}` command structuring, signed agent commands, and cron→trigger conversion all stay server-side: **`list_tasks`** (`GET /api/tasks`, with client-side `platform`/`status`/`category`/`search` filters, bounded to 50 by default with a "showing N of M" note), **`run_task`** (`POST /api/tasks/:id/run`), **`list_templates`** (`GET /api/templates` — surfaces each template's id, tags, target platforms, default schedule, and declared `{{placeholder}}` params so an assistant can discover what to fill), **`create_task_from_template`** (`POST /api/templates/:id/apply` — fills placeholders from raw `parameters`, converts the cron, registers via the signed agent; gated to the creatable platforms Windows + TaskHub-native), and **`convert_schedule`** (`POST /api/tasks/preview` — validate/convert a 5-field UTC cron to a native trigger with a 0–1 confidence score + lossy-conversion warnings). Auth is a single user JWT via `TASKHUB_TOKEN` (base URL via `TASKHUB_API_URL`); the server acts entirely as that one user. Honest failures: the client normalizes an API error into the backend's own message + status (`Task not found`, `HTTP 409 …`) and every tool returns `isError: true` rather than throwing; all logging goes to stderr so stdout stays a clean JSON-RPC stream. Built on the stable `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk` 1.29 (`McpServer` + `StdioServerTransport`, zod input schemas), TypeScript strict/ESM matching the backend. **Deliberately not wired into the repo's root `.mcp.json`** (that's Claude Code's dev tooling; this is a product component users point their own host at — documented via `claude mcp add` + a Claude Desktop JSON snippet). New docs: [`docs/user-guides/guides/MCP_Server_Guide.md`](user-guides/guides/MCP_Server_Guide.md) + [`mcp-server/README.md`](../mcp-server/README.md). Verified live end-to-end against the running backend: MCP handshake + `tools/list` shows all 5; `list_tasks` returned 285 real tasks (capped to 50), `list_templates` surfaced git templates with correct per-param names, `convert_schedule` handled a valid cron (confidence 1 + trigger) and an invalid one (score 0 + warning), and a dummy token surfaced the real `HTTP 403 Invalid or expired token`. `tsc` build clean. Side-effecting tools (`run_task`, `create_task_from_template`) were not fired live to avoid running/registering real Windows tasks — identical plumbing to the verified read/preview paths.
- **TaskHub-native schedule editing** (P2 native follow-up, 2026-07-13): TaskHub-native tasks with cron schedules now use the existing **Edit Schedule** modal and `PATCH /api/tasks/:id/schedule` path. Native updates are applied directly by the backend-owned scheduler: the route recomputes `nextRunTime`, preserves `metadata.job`, mirrors the cron into metadata for export/template parity, emits live task updates, and rejects semantically invalid 5-field cron expressions without changing the task. The modal copy/preview now distinguishes TaskHub-native edits from Windows Task Scheduler edits. Tests: targeted backend integration + `TaskModal` frontend regression.
- **Settings runtime backend URL override** (P2 Settings follow-up, 2026-07-13): Settings → About → API origin is now editable instead of read-only. `VITE_API_URL` remains the default, but a browser-local override (`localStorage` `taskhub.apiOrigin`) can point the dashboard at another backend without rebuilding the frontend. The runtime store updates Axios `baseURL`, the backend outage banner, and the Socket.IO `/ui` live-update connection; Reset returns to the env default, and Reset all preferences clears the override too. Added focused frontend unit coverage for default/override/reset validation. The agent-pairing panel remains gated on the future account/pairing flow.
- **AI Pack templates — Codex** (P2, 2026-07-13): the AI/CLI pack's Codex slice shipped as **3** real, creatable Windows templates using the Codex CLI's documented non-interactive mode. The safe unattended path was validated against the current Codex manual and installed CLI (`codex exec --help`, v0.144.1), then smoke-tested live with `codex --ask-for-approval never exec --sandbox read-only --ephemeral ...` returning a sentinel response without opening the TUI. Templates use `codex --ask-for-approval never exec`, explicit `--sandbox` (`read-only` by default, `workspace-write` only on the higher-trust autofix template), `--ephemeral`, `--color never`, `-C` for the repo root, `-o` for final-message capture, and PowerShell stream redirection for full logs. Added **Codex Headless Run** (`ai-codex-headless-run`), **Codex Repo Digest** (`ai-codex-repo-digest`), and **Codex Auto-Fix Workspace** (`ai-codex-autofix-workspace`), all tagged `ai`/`llm`/`cli`/`agents`/`codex` plus `report` or `git` where relevant. Registry rebuilt and published **37 -> 40**; hosted index verified serving all 40 with the 3 new `ai-codex-*` entries.
- **AI Pack templates — Claude Code** (P2, 2026-07-13): the AI/CLI pack's first slice — **4** `ai-agent` use-case templates that schedule the **Claude Code CLI to run unattended** as real, *creatable* Windows tasks (`compatibleTargets: ['windows']`), distinct from the honest-manual `ai-prompt` link starters. Registry rebuilt **33 → 37** and **published** to the hosted registry (live at `https://mikesailab.com/taskhub-registry`, verified serving all 37). This clears the roadmap's **"safe non-interactive execution path"** gate for Claude Code, with the invocation verified against the current CLI docs (cli-reference / headless / permission-modes): **headless print mode** (`claude -p '{{prompt}}'`), permissions fenced by **`--permission-mode dontAsk`** (auto-denies anything not pre-approved, so a scheduled run never hangs on an approval prompt — the safe choice over `--dangerously-skip-permissions`), an explicit **user-scoped `--allowedTools`** allowlist, **`--bare`**, `--max-turns` + `--model`, and **captured output**. `claude` is a native Windows binary but redirection needs a shell, so — like the npm pack — a shell is opted into explicitly; here **PowerShell** (`-NoProfile -Command "…"`) so the multi-word prompt and tool list ride as **single-quoted strings** inside the one `-Command` arg (the same double-quote-outer/single-quote-inner nesting the webhook starter uses; "avoid single quotes in the prompt" caveat matches git-autocommit). The four: **Claude Code Headless Run** (`ai-claude-headless-run` — flexible primitive, read-only `Read,Grep,Glob` default), **Claude Code Repo Digest** (`ai-claude-repo-digest` — read-only, printed digest → dated `.md` report), **Claude Code Auto-Fix & Commit** (`ai-claude-autofix-commit` — higher-trust edit + scoped-git, labeled), and **Claude Code Log Cleanup** (`ai-claude-log-cleanup` — the roadmap's "agent log cleanup"). Every entry tagged `ai` + specifics (`llm`, `cli`, `agents`, `claude-code`, `report`, `git`, …) via the tags model; plain-kebab `ai-*` ids. The whole-catalog resolvability sweep now covers all four (each PowerShell command tokenizes to a single structured action). Codex slice shipped later the same day; see the Codex entry above. Verified: backend **192 unit** (+1 AI-tag guard; counts 33→37 across catalog + registry tests) + `tsc` clean, `npm run registry:build` regenerated `registry/` (37 files), drift test green; hosted index verified serving all 37 templates post-publish.
- **Developer Pack templates** (P2, 2026-07-13): 9 development-focused use-case templates added to the bundled catalog and published to the hosted registry (24 → **33** templates, live at `https://mikesailab.com/taskhub-registry`). **Git hygiene ×3** — Git Fetch & Prune, Git Repo Maintenance (`git maintenance run`), Git Auto-Commit & Push (PowerShell `add; commit; push` — spaced paths/messages ride as PS single-quoted strings inside one `-Command` argument). **Node toolchain ×3** — Dependency Update Check (`npm outdated > report`; honest help text: npm exits non-zero exactly when updates exist, so a failed run *is* the signal), Nightly Build (`npm run {{script}}`), Scheduled Test Run (`npm test`) — all via explicit `cmd.exe /c` opt-in (npm is a `.cmd`; redirection needs a shell). **Build/infra ×3** — .NET Build (`dotnet build`, direct no-shell exec), Docker Cleanup (`docker system prune -f`), Docker Compose Self-Heal (`docker compose up -d` on an interval — the pattern TaskHub's own stack uses). Every entry carries the `dev` tag plus specifics (`git`, `npm`, `build`, `test`, `ci`, `docker`, …) via the tags model; new ids use the plain-kebab registry form (`dev-*`) per the v1 spec, with the legacy-id test pinning exactly the 24 pre-registry `tpl_*` rows. Also added a **whole-catalog resolvability test**: every bundled `commandTemplate` must resolve through the real Apply pipeline with synthesized params, so a typo'd `{{placeholder}}` can never ship in catalog content. Verified: backend 191 unit (+2) + 74 integration, `tsc` clean, registry drift test green, hosted index serving all 33 templates post-publish.
- **Template tags model** (P2, 2026-07-13): templates now carry **free-form `tags`** (`String[]`), distinct from the single `category` enum — the shared prerequisite for the Developer Pack + AI/CLI template packs. New `tags String[] @default([])` column (migration `20260713193924_add_template_tags`); tags flow through `normalize` (registry→DB) + `denormalize` (DB→registry, export), round-trip verified. In the Templates tab, the previously mislabeled "Tags" chip row is renamed to **Category** (it was the enum), and a real **Tags facet** is added — multi-valued, cross-faceted with OS + Category, plus per-card tag chips and tags folded into search. Verified: backend 189 unit + 73 integration (+1), frontend 55 + build + lint; live end-to-end (all 24 templates surface tags in the facet + card chips).
- **Export existing tasks** (P2, 2026-07-13): per-task **Export** in the task modal via `GET /api/tasks/:id/export` (owner-scoped). A **Windows** task exports as its native Task Scheduler **XML** through a new read-only `task:export` agent round-trip (`Win32TaskScheduler.ExportTaskXml` returns `Task.Xml`, identical to `Export-ScheduledTask`), delivered **UTF-16 LE + BOM** with the native `encoding="UTF-16"` declaration so it re-imports through every Windows path — a UTF-8 declaration was tried first and rejected by the COM/string import ("unable to switch the encoding"), verified against real Task Scheduler. A TaskHub-**native** task exports as TaskHub **JSON** (`{ taskhubTaskVersion, exportedAt, task }`) built from the DB row. Honest errors: agent-offline 502, unsupported platform 400, IDOR 404. Per-task granularity; a whole-account/multi-select bulk export is deferred. Tests: backend 189 unit (+2) + 73 integration (+5), agent 71 (+2), frontend 55 + build + lint.
