- **User resources & onboarding** (P2, 2026-07-13): the in-app **Help Center** was reworked to lead with a 4-step **Getting Started** walkthrough (connect the Windows agent → import & categorize → run & manage → use & grow templates) and a **Guides & Docs** section linking the real guides (UI User Guide, Windows Agent Setup Guide), the template catalog spec, and the public template registry. A dismissible **first-run banner** on the dashboard ("New to TaskHub? Take the tour") opens the Help Center and is remembered via a new `onboardingSeen` preference. Onboarding content is data (`frontend/src/data/onboarding.ts`). Existing pro tips + external resources kept. Tests: frontend 55 (+3 HelpModal) + build + lint.
- **Save task as template** (P2, 2026-07-13): the task detail modal gained a **Save as template** button (Windows + TaskHub-native tasks) that turns a real tracked task into a catalog template. `POST /api/tasks/:id/save-as-template` derives a Registry v1 template from the task via `backend/src/catalog/templateFromTask.ts` (native HTTP job → its URL; a single Windows exec action → exe + args with the exe quoted when it has whitespace; a plain stored command verbatim — mirroring the modal's Action panel), infers the runtime from the executable, maps the free-text category to a v1 category, and stamps a fresh `tpl_saved_<12hex>` id, then routes it through the **same import pipeline** (schema + `{{placeholder}}` resolvability + upsert-by-id) so a saved template is validated like any catalog content. A task with no cron-expressible schedule or no capturable command is an honest 400; the button is disabled with a reason when there's no schedule. The frontend invalidates `['templates']` so the new template appears immediately. The second non-reseed authoring path alongside import. Tests: backend 187 unit (+8) + 68 integration (+5), frontend 52 + build + lint.
- **Template import/export + agent authoring** (P2, 2026-07-13): the non-reseed way the catalog grows. `GET /api/templates/export` lowers live DB rows to portable **Registry v1 JSON** via a new `backend/src/catalog/denormalize.ts` (the inverse of `normalize.ts`, with the enum inverse maps derived from the forward maps so they can't drift) — the whole catalog / a `?ids=a,b` subset as an `{ taskhubCatalogVersion, exportedAt, templates[] }` bundle, or `?id=x` as a single bare v1 object, both as `Content-Disposition` downloads; export reads the **DB** (what the user actually sees, incl. imported rows) and emits only round-trippable entries. `POST /api/templates/import` accepts a bundle, a bare array, or a single object and, per entry, (1) validates against `registryTemplateSchema`, (2) runs the **same `{{placeholder}}` resolvability check Apply enforces** (`importCatalog.ts` synthesizes a value per declared param and drives the real `resolveTemplateParams` + `substituteStructuredCommand` — an undeclared placeholder or empty command is rejected), then (3) normalizes + **upserts-by-id** into the shared catalog (favorites/applied tasks untouched, mirroring `catalogSync`). Bad entries are reported per-item in `errors` without failing the batch; the route returns 400 only when nothing imported. Because import targets the v1 schema, an agent can be pointed at it to draft/bulk-edit templates. Frontend: **Export**/**Import** buttons in the Templates header — Export downloads through the api client (auth header included), Import reads a picked file, POSTs, shows an honest per-item toast (`N added, M updated, K skipped`), and invalidates `['templates']`. Tests: backend 179 unit (+12: denormalize round-trip over all 24 bundled templates, import validation/partial-batch/shape parsing) + 63 integration (+8: export bundle/single/404, export→wipe→import round-trip, updated-vs-created, partial-success 200 vs all-invalid 400, unfillable-placeholder 400, 401s), frontend 52 + build + lint. Code-review pass hardened two edges pre-ship: export skips an un-mappable/degenerate row instead of 500-ing the whole download, and import does one batched existence query instead of a per-entry `findUnique`.
- **Template registry — decoupled, versioned, hosted catalog** (P2): the template catalog is no longer inlined in `backend/src/seed.ts` — it's data behind a `TemplateCatalogSource` interface (`backend/src/catalog/`) in a target-agnostic **Registry v1 JSON schema** (Trigger → Action → Execution Target; structured no-shell `exec` / `http` / `prompt` actions; honest `compatibleTargets` — real only where a compiler exists, else the manual-copy path). It ships as a **static registry** (`registry/index.json` + `templates/*.json`, each with a `sha256` in the index; `npm run registry:build`), fetched at runtime by `RegistryCatalogSource` — integrity-checked, cached, and **falling back to a compiled-in bundled snapshot** on any failure. The artifact is mirrored to a **separate public repo** [`michaelschecht/taskhub-registry`](https://github.com/michaelschecht/taskhub-registry) served via GitHub Pages at `https://mikesailab.com/taskhub-registry` (`scripts/publish-registry.ps1`); the backend **syncs the catalog into the DB on boot + on an interval** (`catalog/catalogSync.ts`, `TEMPLATE_REGISTRY_URL` / `TEMPLATE_REGISTRY_SYNC_INTERVAL_MS`), so catalog updates land with **no reseed/redeploy** — upsert-by-id, favorites/applied-tasks untouched. Registry files are content-addressed and LF-pinned (`.gitattributes`); a drift test guards the committed artifact. Schema + rationale: `docs/reports/templates/Registry_Schema_v1.md`, `docs/adr/0001-template-registry-schema.md`. No behavior change to the existing 24 templates (byte-for-byte identical DB rows). Verified live end-to-end (seed + runtime refresh source from the hosted registry with checksums matching over the CDN; falls back to bundled when unreachable/tampered; a modified registry propagated a template rename into the DB). Tests: backend 167 unit (+14 across schema/normalize/registry/sync).
- **Favorite templates** (P2): a signed-in user can **star/unstar** any template (grid card, list row, kanban card) and filter the Templates tab by a **Favorites** toggle with a live count; favorites float to the top of each group. Stored per-user in a new `TemplateFavorite` join (`@@unique([userId, templateId])`, cascade-delete) so favoriting never mutates the shared catalog. `GET /api/templates` enriches each row with `isFavorite`; new idempotent `POST` / `DELETE /api/templates/:id/favorite`; optimistic UI. This is the honest personalization signal that replaced the removed fake `upvotes`. (See ROADMAP for full detail.)
- **Edit Windows tasks from TaskHub — enable/disable, schedule, action/settings** (P2, the three-slice Windows management pass): signed agent round-trips now change a Windows task's **enabled** state (`task:set_status`), **schedule** (`task:update_schedule` — rebuilds only the trigger), and **action + settings** (`task:update` — command/args/working-dir/description/run-level via the no-shell `{executable,args[]}` model), preserving triggers / principal / other settings. The DB updates only after the platform confirms, with honest offline (502) and elevation (`E_ACCESSDENIED`) errors; each command is covered by the per-session HMAC and pinned by cross-language golden vectors. Live-dogfooded against real Task Scheduler. (See ROADMAP for full detail.)
- **Templates tab — view selector, Resources menu, honesty pass** (P2): a **Grid / List / Kanban** view switch (persisted to `settings.templateView`), a **Resources** dropdown linking curated docs / scheduler references / repos, and an honesty pass — removed the fake `upvotes`, reframed cross-platform targets under a **"Compatible with"** label (Apply gates to creatable platforms, with an honest manual-copy note for the rest), and parameterized the four Tier-B patterns like the starters. (See ROADMAP for full detail.)
- **Apply-modal upgrades + Windows task-name guard** (P2): the Apply Template modal gained an editable **task-name field** (prefilled with the template name), **cron preset chips** (shared with the New Task modal via `frontend/src/utils/cronPresets.ts`), and a **human-readable schedule preview** honoring the Settings Local/UTC mode. Server-side, a new Windows task-name guard (`backend/src/utils/windowsTaskName.ts`, used by both `POST /tasks` and `POST /templates/:id/apply`) rejects invalid names (400) and names colliding with a tracked `\TaskHub\` task (409) — closing the silent same-name overwrite (`RegisterTaskDefinition` replaces an existing task without warning). The apply route now upserts the created task immediately (like `POST /tasks`), so applied tasks show on the dashboard without waiting for a sync and back-to-back duplicate applies 409. Implementation note: the collision check matches the name column case-insensitively and compares the `\TaskHub\` path in JS, because Prisma's `mode: 'insensitive'` compiles to ILIKE where `\` escapes — a backslashed path never matches (caught by the new integration tests). Also fixed the stale New Task modal hint claiming commands run "via cmd.exe /c" (direct no-shell ExecAction since the P0 command-handling work), and the E2E mock agent learned `task:delete` so the apply spec cleans up through the real signed delete path. Review pass caught pre-ship: the immediate-upsert is now Windows-only (it would have wiped `metadata.job` on a native apply, breaking the task — regression-tested), the `POST /tasks` fallback externalId now matches `\TaskHub\<name>` (the old `\<name>` guess would have blinded the duplicate guard), trailing-dot names are rejected (Windows strips them on disk), and time-bearing preset labels say UTC. Verified live end-to-end (custom-name apply → real `\TaskHub\` task; duplicate → 409; bad name → 400; API delete → gone from schtasks + DB). Tests: backend 139 unit (+6) + 33 integration (+6), frontend 48 (+2) + 6 E2E, lint/build green.
- **Agent `task:delete` — delete Windows tasks from TaskHub** (P2, closes the missing CRUD op): `DELETE /api/tasks/:id` now removes real Windows Task Scheduler entries through the agent instead of rejecting synced tasks. The backend emits a signed `task:delete` command (new `SignableCommand` variant; message `task:delete|taskPath|ts`, covered by the per-session HMAC and pinned by a new cross-language golden-vector case) via a new `WindowsAgentConnector.deleteTask`, and the DB row is only removed after the platform confirms — TaskHub never claims a task is gone while it still exists on the machine. The agent verifies signature + freshness + replay guard, deletes via `Task.Folder.DeleteTask` (no shell), and acks `task:deleted`; deleting a task already gone natively is an **idempotent success** so stale rows can be cleaned up. Platforms without connector delete support (Claude, quick-links) keep the honest 400; agent offline → 502 and the row survives. `PlatformConnector` gained an optional `deleteTask`; the task modal now shows the Delete button for Windows tasks with scheduler-specific confirm copy. Verified live end-to-end: created a probe task via API → deleted via API → gone from `schtasks` + DB; second probe deleted natively first → API delete still cleaned the row. Not breaking (create/run signatures unchanged; an old agent simply times out the new command). Dogfood follow-up (same day): tasks registered by an elevated process carry read-only user ACLs, so the unelevated agent gets `E_ACCESSDENIED` — the agent now maps that to an actionable message ("requires administrator rights — delete from an elevated Task Scheduler, or run the agent elevated") instead of surfacing the raw HRESULT; the row still survives the refusal. Tests: backend 133 unit (+4) + 27 integration (+3), agent 60 (+4), frontend 46 (+1).
- **Failure notifications** (P2 first slice): backend now sends server-side notifications for failed manual task runs and failed scheduled TaskHub-native runs. New `FailureNotificationService` supports `generic`, `discord`, and `ntfy` webhooks configured by `TASKHUB_FAILURE_WEBHOOK_URL`, `TASKHUB_FAILURE_WEBHOOK_TYPE`, and optional `TASKHUB_FAILURE_WEBHOOK_HEADERS_JSON`. Delivery is queued fire-and-forget and delivery errors are swallowed/logged so webhook outages never fail the task run path. Added `backend/.env.example` entries and `artifacts/taskhub_failure_notifications_2026-07-10.md`. Tests: backend 129 unit, 24 integration; production audit remains 0.
- **Security audit and performance baseline artifacts** (P1 QA final slice): added `artifacts/taskhub_security_audit_2026-07-10.md` and `artifacts/taskhub_performance_2026-07-10.md`. The performance baseline records local API timing over the 285-task dataset (`GET /api/tasks` p50 74.1 ms / p95 96.9 ms), full 284-task Windows sync timing (1674.7 ms), frontend production bundle size (436.62 kB JS raw / 128.91 kB gzip), and the post-remediation verification matrix: backend 123 unit + 22 integration, frontend 45 unit + 6 E2E + build/lint, agent 56.
- **Mock-agent Playwright E2E suite** (P1 QA — third slice): added a deterministic test-only Socket.IO agent under `frontend/tests/e2e/helpers/` that authenticates with the real agent HMAC handshake, answers `task:list`, records/responds to signed `task:run`, and handles `task:create` with structured actions. New E2E coverage drives the browser through Import discovery, sync + Run Now, template apply through the real create socket command, offline status after disconnect/reload, and a `<375px` dashboard smoke. The helper preserves current Windows external IDs during sync so stale-pruning does not wipe local rows. Full Playwright suite is now 6 tests; frontend lint and Vitest remain green.
- **Playwright E2E scaffold + dashboard smoke test** (P1 QA — second slice): `@playwright/test` + Chromium, `frontend/playwright.config.ts` (baseURL overridable via `PLAYWRIGHT_BASE_URL`, trace/screenshot on failure), and `npm run test:e2e`. `tests/e2e/**` is excluded from the Vitest config so the runners don't collide; Playwright artifacts are gitignored. The smoke suite drives real Chromium against the running dev stack — loads the dashboard, asserts the live task-count subtitle (covers frontend → backend → Postgres end to end), and navigates to the template library. Plumbing slice; the full scenario set (Run Now, template apply, offline guard) needs a mock agent next.
- **Backend integration test suite against real Postgres** (P1 QA — first slice, carries the IDOR regressions deferred from P0): a harness that runs the real Express routes (supertest) and services against a real Postgres. Extracted `backend/src/app.ts` (`createApp()`) so tests drive the app without the agent socket or native scheduler. New `vitest.integration.config.ts` (serial, single fork) with a `globalSetup` that creates + migrates a dedicated `taskhub_test` DB and a `beforeEach` TRUNCATE; `*.integration.test.ts` is excluded from the unit config, and `npm run test:integration` runs it (added a CI job with a Postgres service). 21 tests: IDOR/multi-tenancy (user B 404s on another user's task across GET/PATCH/DELETE/run/executions; list is owner-scoped), auth flow (register/login/401/duplicate-400/Zod-400), config encryption-at-rest (ciphertext in the raw column, round-trip, tamper-reject, legacy-plaintext read), and data flow (native-create nextRunTime, 250-task batched `$transaction` upsert, stale pruning). Known gap noted during the audit: the Test_Plan's `429` login rate-limit isn't implemented yet (tracked in Go-public).

### Changed
- **The frontend moved off Vite's default port: `5173` → `7373`** (2026-07-27). `5173` is the default every Vite project on a machine wants, so whichever starts first wins and the loser either fails or silently lands on `5174` — leaving `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` pointing at an origin nobody is serving. `7373` is distinctive, and `server.strictPort: true` now makes a collision **fail loudly instead of drifting to the next free port**, because a silently-shifted port is exactly what breaks the live-update socket in a way that looks like a backend problem. Updated in lockstep across `frontend/vite.config.ts`, `frontend/Dockerfile`, `frontend/playwright.config.ts` (default `baseURL`), the compose port mapping and `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` default, `backend/.env.example`, `scripts/taskhub.ps1` (status/up/down), `scripts/startup-task/`, the install/setup/testing/user docs, the landing site, and the `taskhub` skill's architecture diagram. **Verified the part that actually matters and isn't visible in a diff:** REST CORS answers `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *` regardless, so it proves nothing — the Socket.IO handshake is the real check, and it now returns the ACAO header for `http://localhost:7373` while returning **none** for `:5173` or an unrelated origin, i.e. the browser live-update channel is correctly scoped to the new port. *(If you run the landing site publicly, it needs a republish to pick up the new URL.)*
- **`docker compose up -d` no longer starts a second, competing copy of the app** (2026-07-27): the `backend` and `frontend` services are now `profiles: ["docker"]`, so a plain `up -d` starts **db + redis only** — which is what `scripts/taskhub.ps1` has always done and what the stack is actually designed for (Postgres/Redis in Docker; backend, frontend, and agent as host processes, since the agent needs Task Scheduler COM and can't be containerized). The containerized variant is still available, just explicit: `docker compose --profile docker up -d`. Running both at once is what turned a recoverable startup race into a silent 12-minute outage — see the Fixed entry below.

### Fixed
- **The backend no longer dies permanently when it boots faster than Postgres** (2026-07-27): reported as "TaskHub is giving me a network error". The stack looked healthy — all four containers `Up`, port 3000 mapped — but every request returned `HTTP 000` and the log ended with `prisma.user.upsert()` → `FATAL: the database system is starting up`. **Cause: a boot-order race, not a code bug.** `depends_on: [db]` waits only for the DB *container to start*, never for Postgres to *accept connections*; after an unclean shutdown Postgres spends several seconds in crash recovery (`database system was not properly shut down; automatic recovery in progress`) refusing every query, and the boot seed runs straight into it, so `main()` throws before the HTTP server binds. **What made it permanent rather than transient is the supervisor: `tsx watch` survives the crash**, so the container never exits, `restart: unless-stopped` sees a healthy container and never fires, and Docker's port proxy holds `:3000` with nothing behind it indefinitely ([#3](troubleshooting/README.md#3-port-listening-but-http-000--eaddrinuse)). Fixed in `docker-compose.yml` by gating on *readiness* instead of *existence*: a `pg_isready -U taskhub -d taskhub` healthcheck on `db` (5s interval, 12 retries, 10s start period) plus `depends_on: db: {condition: service_healthy}` on `backend`. Verified by reproducing the race deliberately — `docker compose kill db backend` forces an unclean stop, and the subsequent `up -d` now prints `db Waiting → db Healthy → backend Starting` and comes up serving. **The same race bites the host backend too, with a different error string** (`Can't reach database server at localhost:5432` in `logs/backend.err.log`) because `taskhub.ps1` ran `compose up -d db redis` and launched the host backend on the very next line — fixed there by a new `Wait-Db` that polls the db container's health first, so `taskhub up` now prints `db ready (accepting connections)` before `started backend`. **What turned a recoverable race into a silent outage was two stacks running at once:** the container grabbed `:3000`, the host backend couldn't bind it and died, then the container died too — leaving the port held by a dead process while `taskhub.ps1`'s `Test-Port 3000` liveness check reported **`backend already up`** and refused to start the real one. Every layer reported something true and the sum was a lie. Addressed by the compose-profile change above, plus a `taskhub status` warning for the exact state that hid it (`:3000` bound while `/api/health` fails). **Two general traps: a dev container whose command is a watcher stays `Up` after the app inside it dies — "container is `Up`" is not "app is serving", and no restart policy will save you; and never probe your own service with a bare port check, because a bound port proves something holds it, not that it's yours.** See [troubleshooting #23](troubleshooting/README.md#23-network-error-after-a-reboot--the-database-system-is-starting-up).
- **A natively-deleted Windows task is now correctly marked `MISSING` — the feature had never once worked in Docker** (2026-07-25). *(Note what this does and doesn't do: `MISSING` **keeps the row on the dashboard**, flagged amber, because absence isn't proof a task is gone — an offline agent looks identical. It does **not** remove the task from the dashboard; that's an explicit delete, which works fine on a MISSING row.)* Deleting tasks (or a whole folder) natively and syncing left them listed as ordinary `ACTIVE`/`DISABLED` forever, while the sync response reported `missing: 56` — claiming it had marked them. The database was byte-identical before and after. Cause: the container's **generated Prisma client** predated the `MISSING` enum (shipped 2026-07-16), so `TaskStatus.MISSING` was `undefined` — and **Prisma treats `undefined` in a `data` payload as "leave this field alone"**, so `{ status: MISSING, nextRunTime: null }` silently degraded to `{ nextRunTime: null }` while `updateMany` still returned a non-zero matched count. A *wrong* enum value would throw; a **missing** one no-ops. The client was stale via the same mechanism as troubleshooting #18 — compose shadows `node_modules` with an anonymous volume, so a host `prisma generate` never reaches the container, while `prisma migrate` updates the *database* — so the DB enum and the client drifted apart with nothing to notice. Fixed by regenerating in-container, and **guarded** so it cannot lie again: `missingTaskStatusMembers()` + `warnOnStaleGeneratedClient()` (`backend/src/db.ts`) print a loud boot banner naming the absent members and the fix (non-fatal — a stale enum breaks the features that write it, not the whole app), and `TaskService.reconcileMissingTasks` now **throws instead of performing a partial write**, so the route surfaces an honest per-platform error rather than a false count. Tests: 5 new (including one asserting the *real* generated client is current — the one check a mocked suite can never make), mutation-tested; verified live by deleting a real folder and confirming 56 rows flipped in Postgres. **Why nine days of green tests missed it: the suites `vi.mock('@prisma/client')`, so the mock asserted the code's intent while the container disagreed with it** — when a bug lives in the generated client, a suite that mocks that client is structurally blind to it. See [troubleshooting #22](troubleshooting/README.md#22-deleted-a-windows-task-synced-and-cronsole-still-shows-it--while-reporting-missing-n).
- **"Sync Now" no longer goes blind when you rename a category** (2026-07-25): categories are renameable from a task card and `upsertTasks` deliberately preserves that override — but the sync filter matches on `TaskService.extractCategory(externalId)`, re-derived from the **folder path**, which knows nothing about the rename. Sync Now sent the *stored* category names, so after a rename it sent a name matching no folder and that folder dropped out of the sync entirely: it stopped picking up new tasks **and** stopped refreshing, silently. A folder holding a single renamed task went dark completely. `POST /api/tasks/sync` now accepts `{ scope: 'tracked' }` (mutually exclusive with `categories`; 400 if both) and resolves the include-set server-side from the tracked tasks' native paths via the new `TaskService.trackedCategories` — immune to renames by construction, and keeping the one definition of "category" on the server instead of duplicating it in the client. Sync Now sends that instead of echoing labels back; Import still sends the explicit `categories` the user ticked. Unit-tested (rename immunity, dedup, root→`Uncategorized`, and an empty tracked set meaning "sync nothing" rather than "sync everything") and mutation-tested; live-verified against the real agent by renaming a single-task folder's category and confirming the folder still synced. **Root cause class: never filter or look up by a user-editable label** — the same shape as the mutable-email upsert key below. See [troubleshooting #20a](troubleshooting/README.md#20a-and-a-renamed-category-silently-stops-syncing-its-folder).
- **The template catalog silently stopped syncing the moment you logged in** (2026-07-25): `ensureCatalogOwner()` keyed its upsert on the **mutable** `CATALOG_OWNER_EMAIL` while creating the **fixed** `CATALOG_OWNER_ID` — the exact bug fixed in `src/index.ts` on 2026-07-16, surviving in a second call site that fix didn't touch. Once the single-user login flow changed that row's email to a real address, the lookup missed, the upsert fell through to re-create an existing id, and `P2002` threw on every boot and every refresh interval. Unlike the `index.ts` instance this one is **caught** by the caller (`[catalog] sync failed:`), so the backend booted clean, the app looked perfectly healthy, and the only symptom was a Templates tab frozen forever — registry rebuilds and republishes had no effect. Now keyed on `id`; verified by the success line (`[catalog] synced 5 core templates from "bundled"`) rather than by absence of the error. **A swallowed error on a background refresh is invisible in exactly the way a crash isn't** — and when fixing a bug of this shape, grep for the pattern rather than fixing the one instance. See [troubleshooting #21](troubleshooting/README.md#21-templates-never-update-catalog-sync-failed--p2002-on-every-boot).
- **Backend no longer crash-loops on boot after you log in** (2026-07-16): once the single-user login flow (`2975fd3`) let the placeholder user's email change to a real address, every backend boot threw `PrismaClientKnownRequestError P2002` from the seed's `prisma.user.upsert()` and the HTTP server never came up — the dashboard at `localhost:5173` couldn't reach the API even though the container showed `Up` (requests returned `Connection refused` from inside the container). **Cause:** the seed keyed the upsert on the **mutable** `email` (`where: { email: 'mike@example.com' }`) while always creating the **fixed** `id: 'cli_user_placeholder'`; after the email drifted, the lookup missed → the upsert fell through to *create* the already-existing id → `P2002` → `main()` threw. **Fix:** key the seed upsert on the stable primary key (`where: { id: 'cli_user_placeholder' }`) so it stays idempotent regardless of the row's current email — the general rule being *seed on the immutable identity, never on a field the app lets the user edit*. Verified: backend serves `GET /api/health` → `200` on a single clean boot, agent reconnects. Logged as troubleshooting [#19](troubleshooting/README.md#19-backend-crash-loops-on-boot-with-p2002-after-you-log-in--frontend-cant-reach-it).
- **Removed literal control bytes that made two source files binary-to-git, and added a CI guard so they can't come back** (P3, 2026-07-16): a stray control byte in a text file makes git classify it **binary** — and a binary file's diff is unreviewable, a silent failure that only bites the *reviewer* (the code compiles, the tests pass, a security-relevant change slips through unread). It had already bitten `backend/src/ws/agentAuth.ts` (a NUL separator, fixed earlier). A repo-wide sweep of all 550 tracked files found two more, both raw bytes pasted into a spot that describes them: `mcp-server/src/__tests__/tools.test.ts` held **NUL bytes** — one in a comment about UTF-16-as-UTF-8 mojibake, and one *inside a live regex* (`.not.toMatch(/␀/)`, asserting the decoded export contains no NUL); and `agent/TaskHub.Agent.Tests/AgentAuthenticatorTests.cs` held two **0x1f (Unit Separator)** bytes in a comment showing the canonical-action format. Fixed by writing each as an **escape** (`\x00`, `\x1f`) — the runtime string is byte-identical, and the file stays plain text with a readable diff. New `scripts/check-control-bytes.mjs` scans every tracked text file (skipping genuinely-binary extensions + an explicit allowlist for the two UTF-16 Task Scheduler XML exports, which legitimately carry NULs) and fails on any control byte other than TAB/LF/CR; wired into CI as a new `repo-hygiene` job. Mutation-checked: the guard passes clean on the repo and fails on an injected NUL. [ROADMAP › "Sweep for literal control bytes in source".]
- **Running the same task twice quickly no longer hangs for 15 seconds** (agent protocol, 2026-07-15): a second **Run Now** within one second of the first hung ~15s and failed with `Agent trigger timeout` — while the agent was healthy and the first run had just worked. Wait a second and the identical click succeeded. **Cause:** signed agent commands carry a **second-granular** `ts`, and it was the only field that varied between two otherwise-identical commands — so both produced a byte-identical message and therefore an identical signature. The agent's replay guard keys on `(ts, sig)` and, entirely correctly, **cannot distinguish that from a captured frame being replayed**, so it dropped the second one — silently, as a forgery deserves. With no reply, the backend timed out and reported the only thing it knew: a message naming the **transport** for what was really a clock-resolution problem. **Fix:** every signed command now carries a fresh 16-byte **nonce inside the signed message**, immediately before `ts`, restoring the property the guard always assumed — *a legitimate re-send is never byte-identical, a replayed frame still is*. It sits **inside** the signature rather than beside it because an unsigned nonce could be rewritten in flight to turn a captured frame into a "fresh" command, defeating the guard outright (the same reasoning that already covers `folder` and `trigger`). The replay cache itself needed **no change** — the signature is now unique per instance, so its existing key stops colliding on its own. **Requires the backend and agent to ship together** (a message-string change on one side alone rejects every command — by design; there is deliberately no back-compat shim, since an optional nonce would mean two valid message forms). Cross-language golden HMAC vectors regenerated, with the generator first validated by reproducing the **previously committed** vectors from the old format — a generator that can't reproduce what's already in the repo would emit new values that are confidently wrong and get pinned by both suites. `agent/test-server/index.js` updated too (it signs commands to a real agent, and the agent's rejection is silent — a stub that forgot the nonce would just look like the agent had died). Tests: +7 backend (240), +2 agent (126), mutation-tested on both sides. **Verified live**: five back-to-back runs at 27–60ms each. Logged as [#16](troubleshooting/README.md#16-a-second-identical-agent-command-within-one-second-is-dropped).
- **Running a disabled task now says it's disabled, instead of blaming the agent for 15 seconds** (agent, 2026-07-15, found by driving the new `set_task_status` + `run_task` MCP tools live): `run_task` on a **disabled** Windows task hung for ~15s and failed with `Agent trigger timeout` — a message that names the **transport** while the agent is connected, healthy, and handling every other command. It sent you to debug connectivity for a task state with a one-click fix. **Cause:** the agent's `task:run` handler only replied on **success**; `RunTask` returning false (no such task) just wrote to the console, and a thrown COM error (a disabled task makes `task.Run()` throw `0x80041326 "The task is disabled."` in ~7ms) was swallowed by the catch. The agent is launched hidden, so that console has no reader — the information existed the entire time and was dropped on the floor, leaving the backend's 15s timeout as the only thing it could report. Every other verb (`task:delete`, `task:set_status`, `task:create`) already had an explicit ack/error; `task:run` was the outlier. Now every **accepted** command answers, and a failure answer carries the reason: `(23ms) The task is disabled, so Windows refused to run it. Enable the task first, then run it again.` The **one** case that stays silent is a command failing signature verification — a forger should learn nothing, and the server's timeout is correct there. Windows' own message leads for unrecognized failures rather than a guessed classification; only the disabled case adds the fix, since that's the one the raw error doesn't state. 3 new xUnit tests (124 total), mutation-tested — restoring either silent path fails them. **Needs an agent republish** ([#7](troubleshooting/README.md#7-new-agent-command-502-times-out-until-the-agent-is-republished)); verified live after one. Logged as troubleshooting **#15**, alongside **#16** — a *second* pre-existing bug with the same `Agent trigger timeout` symptom, where the replay guard silently drops a legitimate second identical command inside one second (second-granular `ts` ⇒ byte-identical signature). Two bugs, one message: which is why that message now deserves suspicion rather than belief.
- **The hourly-fallback warning now names the cost instead of describing drift** (P3, 2026-07-15): an unrecognized cron is replaced with a **hard-coded hourly** trigger that is not derived from the input at all — yet the warning read *"execution times might not align 100%"*, which describes **drift** (the right schedule, slightly off). So `0 4 1 1 *` — once a year — silently becoming **~8,760 runs a year** read as a rounding error, at a mild-sounding `confidence: 0.7`. By TaskHub's own honesty rule that's the quiet register of a confident lie, and the asymmetry sharpens it: **the fallback only ever runs *more* often than asked, never less** — and a deliberately *rare* cron is the input most likely to miss the pattern list, so *being careful* is what triggers it. The warning now states plainly that the schedule is **REPLACED — not approximated**, names the resulting frequency (*"every hour from 00:00, about 24 runs a day (~8,760 a year)"*), states the asymmetry, and points at the honest alternative (**disable the task; don't encode "rarely" in the cron**). Fixed **once in the backend** so it protects every caller — UI, Apply, and MCP alike — rather than being special-cased per surface; the MCP wrapper deliberately gained no logic. 3 new backend tests (233 total), mutation-tested against the old wording, including a guard that the genuinely-approximate `*/7` step **keeps** its drift language: the two share a `0.7` score, so the wording is the only thing distinguishing "approximated" from "discarded". **Known residual, recorded not papered over:** the score is still `0.7` for both, so a caller thresholding on `>= 0.7` still accepts a replacement — read the trigger, not the score ([#14](troubleshooting/README.md#14-a-rare-cron-becomes-an-hourly-trigger)).
- **The core webhook template no longer strands a hung `powershell.exe` every 15 minutes** (P2 catalog, 2026-07-15, found by the first live end-to-end run of the MCP `create_task_from_template` + `run_task` tools): `tpl_starter_webhook_windows` — **one of the 5 `core` templates every fresh install gets** — registered `powershell.exe -Command "Invoke-WebRequest -Uri '{{url}}' -Method {{method}}"`. Windows PowerShell 5.1 parses `Invoke-WebRequest` responses with the **Internet Explorer engine, which Windows 11 no longer ships**, so the call dies on `Object reference not set to an instance of an object` (`System.NullReferenceException`). Worse: interactively that error prints and the process exits, but **under Task Scheduler there is no console to write it to and the process blocks forever** — the task sits `Running` with flat CPU indefinitely. On the template's `*/15 * * * *` default that is a **new stuck process every 15 minutes, permanently**, on a brand-new user's machine — while the dashboard reports `lastRunStatus: SUCCESS`, because the agent only observes that the task *started*. Now `powershell.exe -NoProfile -Command "… -UseBasicParsing"` (verified: the old form NullRefs, the new returns HTTP 200). The only other HTTP template (the Discord notifier) is **unaffected** — it uses `Invoke-RestMethod`, which parses JSON directly and never touches IE, and already passed `-NoProfile` (both verified on 5.1). **Why no test caught it:** the whole-catalog resolvability sweep proves a `commandTemplate` *tokenizes and substitutes* — **not that the command runs on the target**; 230 backend tests were green with the broken command in place. That limitation is now written down where template authors will hit it ([`references/templates.md`](../skills/cronsole/references/templates.md) + troubleshooting **#12**). **Published** to the hosted registry the same day (`scripts/publish-registry.ps1`) and verified live: `https://mikesailab.com/taskhub-registry` now serves the fixed command across all 55 templates, with the `index.json` sha256 matching the served file byte-for-byte — so existing installs pick it up on their next sync, with no app redeploy. (The source fix alone would have changed nothing for users: the catalog syncs from the registry, not from `bundled.ts`, wherever `TEMPLATE_REGISTRY_URL` is set.)
- **Multi-day weekly schedules no longer silently collapse to one day** (P1, 2026-07-14, found while validating the MCP server): `convertCronToWindowsTrigger` ran `parseInt` over the whole day-of-week field, and `parseInt('1-5')` is `1` — so `0 9 * * 1-5` ("every weekday") became a **Monday-only** trigger reported at **confidence 1.0 with no warnings**, as did any list (`1,3,5`) or range. A confident lie in the layer whose entire job is honest schedules. The same truncation ran in **all three layers**: the reverse path (`convertWindowsTriggerToCron`) kept `daysOfWeek[0]`, so a real Mon/Wed/Fri Windows task **imported** as Mondays-only; and the agent's `TriggerBuilder` resolved only `DaysOfWeek[0]`, so it would have **registered** Monday-only even once the server sent all five. Fixed in all three with a real day parser (lists, ranges, `0`/`7` → one Sunday, unparseable → warned fallback rather than a guessed day), plus a lossy-conversion warning for minute/hour steps that don't divide 60/24 evenly (`*/7` now scores 0.7 and says why; `*/15` still 1.0). 9 new tests (6 TS + 3 C#), each confirmed failing before the fix. Verified end-to-end against the live agent: Windows registers `DaysOfWeek=62` (Mon–Fri) where the old build wrote `2` (Monday).
- **MCP server refuses to start on an unexpanded `TASKHUB_TOKEN`** (2026-07-14): when the variable is unset, Claude Code passes the **literal** `${TASKHUB_TOKEN}` through — documented behavior for an unset variable referenced from `.mcp.json`. The literal is non-empty, so it sailed past the emptiness guard and the API rejected it as `403 Invalid or expired token`, which reads as an expired JWT and sends you debugging auth instead of your environment. `configFromEnv()` now detects the unexpanded form and fails at startup naming the real cause. Tradeoff noted: a host drops a server that fails to boot, so the symptom becomes *absent tools* rather than an error — visible via the host's MCP status. Logged as troubleshooting **#8**, cross-linked from #2 so the shared 403 symptom stops routing to the secrets-mismatch cause. Also corrected `.env.example` and both MCP READMEs, which claimed the server reads a `.env.local` (it reads the process environment only) and that it is **not** wired into the root `.mcp.json` (it is — that stale claim actively deepened the debugging session).
- **Self-heal tasks no longer flash a PowerShell window + deduplicated** (P2 agent hygiene, 2026-07-13): the `\Task-Hub\` self-heal scheduled tasks popped a PowerShell/conhost window on every recurring fire — `powershell.exe -WindowStyle Hidden` still creates the console window and only *then* hides it (a flash), and two overlapping tasks (`TaskHubStack` every 5 min + `TaskHubAgent` every 10 min, both running the idempotent `taskhub.ps1 up`) doubled it. New `scripts/startup-task/run-hidden.vbs` launches PowerShell hidden-from-creation via `WScript.Shell.Run(cmd, 0, False)` (verified live — no window). `Register-TaskHubStack.ps1` now points `TaskHubStack` at the shim (the single recurring self-heal) and repairs any existing `TaskHubAgent` to a flash-free, logon-only bootstrap (removing its redundant recurring repetition). `Set-TaskHubRepetition.ps1` deprecated. Apply to a live install with one elevated re-run of `Register-TaskHubStack.ps1`.
- **Empty `\TaskHub\` scheduler folder auto-pruned** (P2 cleanup, 2026-07-13): TaskHub-created Windows tasks register under `\TaskHub\<name>`, and `task:delete` left the now-empty folder behind (Task Scheduler doesn't auto-prune empty folders). The agent's `Win32TaskScheduler.DeleteTask` now removes the `\TaskHub\` folder when deleting its last task (`TryPruneTaskHubFolder` — guarded to our folder only, never root or the hyphenated `\Task-Hub\` infra folder; only when it has no tasks/subfolders; best-effort so a folder-delete failure never fails the task delete). Verified live against real Task Scheduler. A full rename to remove the near-duplicate `\Task-Hub\` (infra) vs `\TaskHub\` (user) folder names is deferred (see ROADMAP — large blast radius / needs elevated re-registration); the auto-prune makes the overlap transient.

### Changed
- **The Active Only / Showing All filter now tells you which state it's in — and how much it's hiding** (2026-07-25): the two states differed only by a `bg-primary/10` tint and the eye icon's slash, and at 10% opacity on a dark surface they read as the same button — so you couldn't tell whether "Active Only" described the current state or the action a click would take. Both states now carry a deliberate treatment: **Active Only** = green tint/border + green `EyeOff` (the same green as the `ACTIVE` dot on every task card, so the association is already learned), **Showing All** = amber tint/border + amber `Eye`. Colour, icon, *and* a count — three redundant signals, so meaning never rests on colour alone — plus `aria-pressed` (previously absent) and honest tooltips. **The count is the substantive part, not the polish:** the filter keeps `status === 'ACTIVE'` only, so Active Only also hides `MISSING` and `UNKNOWN` tasks — a natively-deleted task flagged MISSING was invisible in the default view with nothing to indicate it. The button now reads e.g. `Active Only · 110 hidden`, because a dashboard that quietly withholds 110 rows is lying by omission. Verified in a real browser in both states. Deviation from the original suggestion (plain yellow): used the app's existing `amber-*` caution scale so it reads as intentional rather than as a new colour.
- **A task that vanishes from the platform is now shown as MISSING, not silently deleted (and never left claiming it's active)** (P3, 2026-07-16): sync reconciliation used to **hard-delete** any tracked task absent from the platform's snapshot — on the theory that absence means "deleted natively." But absence isn't proof: an offline agent, or a folder the agent can't read (ACL), looks **identical** from the backend to a real delete. Deleting a user's task (and its execution history) on that evidence is the confident-lie failure mode aimed at the user — and the symptom that surfaced it was the opposite tail: two DB rows (`TestingMCP`, `MCP Test - Webhook Ping`) sitting `ACTIVE` with a `nextRunTime`, **stating as fact** that a task was due while it no longer existed (deleted out-of-band when `\TaskHub` was pruned). `TaskService.removeStaleTasks` is now `reconcileMissingTasks`: an absent task is flipped to a **new `MISSING` status** with its `nextRunTime` cleared (so it stops claiming it's due), and the row + logs are **kept**. MISSING is **self-healing** — the next sync that sees the task upserts it back to ACTIVE/DISABLED (the sync upserts before it reconciles, so a task that reappeared this cycle is never re-marked) — and **honest**: a user who truly wants it gone deletes it explicitly (`DELETE /api/tasks/:id`), the only path that removes the row. The partial-snapshot guard is kept (a suspicious tiny snapshot flips nothing, avoiding a whole-dashboard flap). The dashboard shows MISSING with an amber dot (distinct from a user-`DISABLED` task), and the task modal replaces the Enable/Disable toggle with an honest note ("Not found on the platform. Re-sync if the agent was offline, or delete to remove it.") rather than offering a toggle that would 502 against a task that isn't there. Over MCP, `list_tasks` gains a `MISSING` status filter ("what did I lose?"); `set_task_status` still refuses it (system-derived, not user-settable). New migration `20260716120000_add_task_status_missing` (`ALTER TYPE … ADD VALUE`, verified applying against real Postgres 16). Tests: TaskService unit rewritten (mark-not-delete, idempotent re-mark, empty/partial guards), data-flow integration rewritten (kept-not-deleted + self-heal + cleared nextRunTime, run live against real Postgres), +1 MCP guard. Honest scope note: reconciliation only runs on a sync, so a vanished task shows MISSING on the **next** sync, not instantly; and a task that's genuinely gone stays MISSING until the user removes it (no auto-cleanup by design). [ROADMAP › "Reconcile tracked tasks that no longer exist on the platform".]
- **`convert_schedule` / the schedule preview now return a machine-readable `lossy` discriminator, so the two meanings of a `0.7` score are finally distinguishable** (P3, 2026-07-16): `convertCronToWindowsTrigger` returns **`0.7` for two different risks** — *approximated* (an uneven `*/N` step whose trigger **is** derived from your cron and merely drifts) and *replaced* (a cron the pattern list can't match, **discarded** for a fixed hourly trigger). Since 2026-07-15 the *wording* told them apart, but the **number** — the part a program reads — did not, so a caller thresholding on `>= 0.7` accepted both. `ConversionResult` now carries `lossy: 'approximated' | 'replaced' | undefined` (undefined on an exact conversion or a null-trigger refusal), surfaced on `POST /api/tasks/preview`, `POST /api/templates/:id/preview`, and both create responses. The MCP `convert_schedule` renders it as a distinct line (`Lossy: REPLACED — your cron was discarded …` vs `Lossy: approximated — … drifts …`), and `create_task` leads its warning with the actionable verb: *replaced* tells the model to delete and re-create, *approximated* tells it the schedule is honored but drifts. Backend-only discriminator + presentation in the wrapper — no new wrapper logic. Tests: backend + MCP, mutation-checked; **needs an `mcp-server` rebuild** (it runs `dist/`). The `0.7` *number* is deliberately unchanged (splitting it would move every caller's threshold semantics — a separate call), so `lossy` is the honest machine-readable half. [ROADMAP › "Split the `0.7` confidence score".]
- **`TEMPLATE_REGISTRY_URL` now defaults to unset — the dev catalog loads from `bundled.ts`, not the public CDN** (2026-07-15): `backend/.env.example` shipped `TEMPLATE_REGISTRY_URL="https://mikesailab.com/taskhub-registry"` as its default, so a fresh clone's backend synced its catalog **from the public registry**. That inverts the authoring loop: `registry/` is a *generated artifact* (nothing reads it at runtime — `buildCatalogSource()` offers exactly two sources, compiled-in `bundled.ts` or a remote URL), so with the hosted URL set, a local `bundled.ts` edit is **invisible until it has been published publicly** — you'd have to ship a broken template to the world to test its fix. Caught immediately after the webhook fix above: the corrected template was in source, in the local artifact, and green in 230 tests, yet the running app still served the broken command. Now commented out by default with the hosted URL documented as opt-in (set it to fetch a hosted catalog and to exercise the real fetch + sha256 + cache path). A local-first app should have a local-first dev loop. Verified: with it unset the backend logs `[catalog] synced 5 core templates from "bundled"` and the fixed command reaches the DB with no publish. Documented in [`references/templates.md`](../skills/cronsole/references/templates.md) (authoring loop) + `registry/README.md`. **No behavior change for anyone who sets the variable.**
- **`convert_schedule` now spells out the converted trigger in its text output** (MCP, 2026-07-15): the tool returned only `Convertible for WINDOWS_TASK_SCHEDULER (confidence 1).` — **character-for-character what the multi-day weekly bug would have printed**. That bug turned `0 9 * * 1-5` into a *Monday-only* trigger and still scored 1.0 with no warnings, so the one output an assistant sees carried no signal that anything was wrong. The trigger was always present in `structuredContent`, but a host that renders only text couldn't see the thing most worth checking. The text now appends a rendered trigger — `Trigger: Weekly at 09:00 on Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday, Friday` — so a wrong conversion is visible at a glance. This also surfaces the **fallback** case honestly: `0 9 30 2 *` (Feb 30) scores 0.7 with a vague "might not align 100%" warning, and now shows `Trigger: Time at 00:00 repeating every PT1H for P1D` — i.e. it silently becomes an **hourly** task, which the score alone never conveyed. Durations stay ISO-8601 because that's what Task Scheduler itself displays, keeping the line checkable against the real trigger. Presentation only — `describeTrigger` in `mcp-server/src/tools.ts` formats what `/api/tasks/preview` already returns, so the wrapper still owns no logic; no tool, param, or schema changed. **Needs an `mcp-server` rebuild** (it runs `dist/`). Verified over stdio against the running backend across weekday-range, explicit-list, daily, step-hour, invalid (score 0 → no trigger line), and impossible-date cases.
- **ROADMAP, CHANGELOG, and troubleshooting are now required updates on every change, not end-of-session chores** (CLAUDE.md §11a, 2026-07-14): the trigger table listed the mirror surfaces and the roadmap but **omitted CHANGELOG entirely** — and predictably, a full day of work reached `ROADMAP.md` while the changelog got nothing, until it was noticed by hand. If it isn't in the table, it gets forgotten; that is the whole reason the table exists. Added CHANGELOG, and lowered the troubleshooting bar from "cost real hours" to **"it cost you time"** — including *wrong turns*, which are worth logging even when the eventual fix was small, because the next person takes the same wrong turn. Step 7 of §11 is now a last check rather than the first time these get written.
- **The MCP server and the `taskhub` skill are now "mirror surfaces", updated in the same change that obsoletes them** (2026-07-14): an audit found the skill silent on the MCP server entirely — its only two "MCP" hits were a routing line and `context7` (dev tooling, a different thing) — and its `description`, which is the *activation trigger*, never mentioned MCP, so the skill would not have fired on MCP work at all. Both `mcp-server/` and `skills/taskhub/` **describe** TaskHub rather than implement it, so **neither breaks loudly when it drifts**: the suite stays green and the drift surfaces later as an agent confidently doing the wrong thing. Added a trigger table (CLAUDE.md §11a, mirrored in the skill) mapping each kind of change to the surfaces it obligates, plus the `/sync-surfaces` command that mechanizes it. Also recorded that `mcp-server/` runs `dist/`, making it a **third thing that runs stale** alongside the Docker backend and the published agent.
- **`.claude/` tracks only `commands/`, `skills/`, and `agents/`** (2026-07-14): the ignore rules only ever named the skill junction, so anything else dropped into `.claude/` was tracked unless someone noticed — which is how four duplicate `" copy"` skill trees (byte-identical to their originals, declaring the *same* `name:`, so Claude Code loaded both with no basis to choose) ended up one `git add -A` away from the repo. Switched to deny-by-default: ignore `/.claude/*`, re-include the three shared dirs.
- **Resilience QA stale-pruning guard** (P1 QA): `TaskService.removeStaleTasks` now refuses destructive pruning on empty snapshots and on suspicious partial snapshots from established platforms (20+ tracked rows where the connector returns less than 50% of IDs). This closes the main failure mode exposed by mock-agent soak work: a bad non-empty agent response could otherwise delete most tracked Windows rows. Added backend unit coverage and real-Postgres integration coverage for normal prune, empty-snapshot skip, and partial-snapshot skip. Added `artifacts/taskhub_resilience_2026-07-10.md` with the QA results; mock-agent repeated E2E passes in single-worker mode (`--repeat-each=3 --workers=1`), matching the current single-agent MVP.
- **Backend hygiene sweep** (P1): (1) **shared Prisma singleton** — 9 files each constructed their own `PrismaClient` (9 connection pools); everything now imports the one client from `backend/src/db.ts`, and an entirely unused client in `auth/auth.ts` was removed. (2) **Single error boundary + Zod validation at route boundaries** — new `middleware/errorHandler.ts` (`HttpError` + one Express error handler: `HttpError`→status, `TemplateParamError`→400, malformed JSON→400, unexpected→logged generic 500 so internals never leak) and `middleware/validate.ts` (`validateBody(schema)` → field-qualified 400, parsed body replaces `req.body`). The tasks/templates/auth routers dropped their per-route try/catch-500 boilerplate (Express 5 forwards rejected promises); intentional catches remain (per-connection sync/discover isolation, duplicate-email). Zod schemas cover every mutating body; auth register now enforces email format + 8-char password minimum; `POST /sync` with a non-array `categories` is now a 400 instead of silently ignored. (3) **Batched sync upserts** — `TaskService.upsertTasks` chunks upserts into `$transaction` batches of 100 (a full 284-task Windows sync: ~3 DB round trips instead of 284 sequential awaits; ~2.2s end-to-end verified live). Backend 117 tests (+1).

### Removed
- **Live-demo mode (`VITE_DEMO_MODE`) removed entirely**: the frontend-only demo — built-in `DEMO_TASKS`/`DEMO_TEMPLATES`/`DEMO_CONNECTIONS` fixtures, the "Demo data" banner, the per-modal "simulated" mutation paths, and the demo notices in Settings and the Run History tab — is gone. The dashboard now always talks to the backend. Dropped `VITE_DEMO_MODE` from `frontend/.env.example` and the demo-mode unit test in `ApplyTemplateModal.test.tsx`. Scrubbed the public demo (`taskhub.mikesailab.com`) from the README, docs, and roadmap; the Vercel deployment that hosted it is being retired. TaskHub runs locally only until the frontend + backend are hosted for real (ROADMAP › P3 / Go-public).

### Added
- **`taskhub` stack control script + self-healing data services** (`scripts/taskhub.ps1`): one command to run the whole local stack as an up-or-down unit — `taskhub status` prints a single table (Postgres, Redis, backend, frontend, agent + an API health check, with an ALL UP / PARTIAL / DOWN verdict), and `up` / `down` / `restart` / `logs` control it, replacing the "which service is down this time?" guesswork. `up` is idempotent (starts only what's missing). The Docker `db`/`redis` services now carry `restart: unless-stopped` so they recover from a crash or reboot on their own (applied to the running containers too). The logon auto-start launcher (`Start-TaskHub.ps1`) now delegates to `taskhub.ps1 up`, so boot and manual control share one code path. Added `scripts/startup-task/Register-TaskHubStack.ps1` to register a `\Task-Hub\TaskHubStack` self-heal watchdog (runs `taskhub.ps1 up` at logon + every 5 min — faster recovery than the 10-min launcher; run once as Administrator since the `\Task-Hub\` folder needs elevation). (The agent stays a host process — it needs Windows Task Scheduler access, so it can't be containerized.)
- **Browser live updates (push, not polling)**: the dashboard reflects task changes the instant they happen — an agent sync, a native scheduled run, or an edit in another tab — instead of waiting for the 45s poll. New JWT-authenticated, receive-only Socket.IO `/ui` namespace (`backend/src/ws/uiChannel.ts`), kept separate from the pairing-secret-guarded agent channel; the backend calls `notifyTasksChanged(userId)` on every task mutation and after each `NativeScheduler` tick, emitting `task:updated` to that user's room only. Frontend `useLiveTaskUpdates` hook connects with the user token, forces the WebSocket transport, backs off gently when the backend is down, and invalidates `['tasks']`/`['connections']` on each push (no-op in demo mode). Requires the frontend origin in `ALLOWED_ORIGINS`. Lighter than polling — one idle socket that only carries a message on a real change.
- **Task search on the dashboard**: free-text search box beside the category chips, matching name, category, platform path, command, and schedule (multi-term queries narrow). Includes a live match counter, clear button, `/` to focus, Esc to clear, and a "Clear search" action in the empty state. Composes with the category/platform/active filters across all four views. Matching logic in `frontend/src/utils/taskSearch.ts` with unit tests.
- **Platform selector in the New Task modal** (`CreateTaskModal`, replacing the native-only `CreateNativeTaskModal`): choose TaskHub-native (HTTP job) or **Windows** (command). The Windows path creates a real Task Scheduler task via the agent with the cron converted to a structured trigger, shows live conversion warnings (debounced `POST /api/tasks/preview`), stores the cron on the task row, and surfaces a clear error when the agent is offline.
- **`\TaskHub\` scheduler folder for created tasks**: the agent now registers all TaskHub-created Windows tasks under `\TaskHub\` (auto-created), so they're identifiable, category-extract as "TaskHub" on sync, and are cleanly removable.
- `POST /api/tasks/preview` — cron→trigger conversion preview for a platform (mirrors the template preview endpoint).
- **Last-run failure surfacing**: `GET /api/tasks` now includes `lastRunStatus`/`lastRunAt`/`lastRunDurationMs`; tasks whose most recent run failed show a red "Run failed" indicator on dashboard grid cards and in the list view.
- **Run durations**: new `ExecutionLog.durationMs` column (migration `add_execution_duration`), recorded for manual runs and native scheduler fires, displayed in the Run History tab (e.g. "· 44ms").
- **Task delete for TaskHub-native tasks**: `DELETE /api/tasks/:id` (native-only guard; synced tasks are rejected until the agent supports `task:delete`) + a confirmed Delete button in the task modal.
- **Run History tab** in the task detail modal: shows the last 20 `ExecutionLog` entries (manual runs + native scheduler fires) with SUCCESS/FAILURE/TIMEOUT status pills, timestamps, and log snippets. Backed by new `GET /api/tasks/:id/executions` endpoint. Demo mode shows a notice; empty/loading/error states handled. Works for all platforms — any task run from the dashboard now leaves a visible trail.
- **TaskHub-native tasks** (`TASKHUB_NATIVE` platform, design: `docs/specs/Native_Tasks.md`): tasks that exist only in TaskHub, scheduled and executed by the backend itself with no Windows Task Scheduler entry. Includes:
  - `NativeScheduler` (30s tick loop): fires due tasks, writes `ExecutionLog` rows, advances `nextRunTime` via `cron-parser` (UTC), skips runs missed by more than a 5-minute grace window.
  - `NativeTaskExecutor` with an HTTP job type (webhook pings, health checks; 15s timeout, non-2xx = FAILURE) stored in `Task.metadata.job`.
  - `TaskHubNativeConnector` in the registry so Run Now / enable / disable reuse existing routes; `POST /api/tasks/native` creation endpoint; auto-provisioned platform connection at boot.
  - UI: violet TaskHub badge identity, a dashboard **platform filter** (All / Windows / TaskHub with counts) for one-click isolation of native vs. synced tasks, and a New Task modal (name, category, cron presets, method + URL, optional body).
  - Shared `frontend/src/platform.ts` label/badge helpers — also fixes list/kanban/schedule views labeling every non-Windows platform as "Claude".
  - Tests: cron-next util (5) and NativeTaskExecutor (8, mocked axios). Verified end-to-end live: a per-minute native task fired on schedule twice with SUCCESS logs and correctly advancing nextRunTime.
- Real cron→Windows-trigger conversion in the template apply pipeline: the apply route converts the schedule via `convertCronToWindowsTrigger` and passes a structured `trigger` object through `PlatformConnector.createTask` into the `task:create` agent payload.
- Agent `TriggerBuilder` + `TriggerSpec`: registers native Daily/Weekly/interval Task Scheduler triggers from the server spec (UTC→local conversion, ISO-8601 repetition intervals), replacing the two-case cron parser. Interval schedules are registered as daily triggers with repetition so they recur beyond the first 24h window.
- `POST /api/templates/:id/preview` endpoint returning `{ score, warnings, trigger }` (template/OS compatibility + cron conversion confidence).
- Apply modal now shows live (debounced) conversion warnings and a "converts cleanly" confirmation under the schedule field.
- Tests: `TriggerBuilder` unit tests, agent `task:create` trigger-parsing tests, connector trigger-payload passthrough test.
- Proprietary `LICENSE` / rights notice for the private repository.
- Contributor guide in `CONTRIBUTING.md`.
- Repository contracts document in `docs/specs/CONTRACTS.md`.
- Explicit roadmap and phase-status sync across the planning docs.
- Basic GitHub Actions CI workflow in `.github/workflows/ci.yml`.
- Windows Agent automated startup registration script `setup-agent-startup.ps1` to compile and register the agent inside a dedicated `\Task-Hub\` Task Scheduler folder.
- Headless daemon configuration (`WinExe` output type) for the C# Agent to run silently on logon.
- Setup and troubleshooting manual in `docs/user-guides/Agent_Setup_Guide.md`.

### Changed
- **Launch posture set to local-first; cloud hosting dropped as the default** (2026-07-13): the roadmap's P3 "host the backend + frontend on a Hetzner VPS → hosted control plane" plan is dropped. TaskHub launches and stays a **local-first** app (each user runs the full stack on their own machine; no hosted/SaaS instance). **Remote access** — reaching your own local instance from other devices (phone/laptop) via Tailscale or Cloudflare Tunnel + Access, the [code-server](https://github.com/coder/code-server) model — is reframed as the **final, optional P3 enhancement** (docs + optional single-origin reverse-proxy tooling), explicitly sequenced last; go-live does not depend on it. Drafted the guide [`docs/user-guides/guides/Remote_Access_Guide.md`](user-guides/guides/Remote_Access_Guide.md) (leads with the security gate: TaskHub can run commands, so the network layer — VPN membership / Access login — must be the auth until the real login ships). Updated `ROADMAP.md` (P3 reorder, Go-public intro, Launch surface, the "Final hosting choice" open decision resolved as moot) and `CLAUDE.md` (§1 hosting note). No code change.
- **Planning docs restructured to a single living roadmap**: the phase lifecycle (0–6) is complete, so `docs/Project_Plan.md` and `docs/phases/` moved to `docs/archive/` (frozen), replaced by `docs/ROADMAP.md` — completed work plus prioritized open items (P0 security → P3 expansion) drawn from the 2026-07-07 project analysis. All doc links updated (`README`, `CLAUDE.md`, `CONTRIBUTING`, `docs/README.md`, `CONTRACTS`).
- Aligned `README.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, and planning docs with the current implementation state.
- Clarified that the public deployment is a frontend demo backed by sample data.
- Reframed Claude Code support as experimental rather than production-ready.
- Updated immediate action items to focus on Phase 3 exit criteria and Phase 4 QA.

### Changed
- **Task detail view shows recurring schedule times in your local timezone** (P1): the Schedule panel described recurring times in UTC ("Daily at 9:00 AM UTC"). `describeCron` now honors the Settings Local/UTC mode — in local mode (the default) it converts the recurring clock time to the viewer's zone and rolls the day-of-week across midnight (e.g. `0 2 * * 1` reads "Weekly on Sunday at 9:00 PM" in US Eastern). Anchored on the current date so it respects DST; interval schedules (every-N, hourly) are unchanged. Also fixed a latent `tsc -b` type-narrowing error in `TaskModal.actionInfo`.

### Fixed
- **Weekly schedules crossing midnight in UTC showed the wrong day** (found by the live schedule-normalization smoke test): the agent's `TriggerReader` converted a weekly trigger's start *time* to UTC but read its *day-of-week* without the same roll, so a late-evening local trigger reported the prior day (e.g. `Weekly-Analysis`, Sunday 23:45 PDT = Monday 06:45 UTC, normalized to `45 6 * * 0` instead of `45 6 * * 1` — and contradicted its own Monday next-run time). `TriggerReader` now rolls the day-of-week by the local→UTC day shift (matching the already-correct `TriggerBuilder` create path), via a unit-tested `ShiftDayName` helper. Re-verified end-to-end against a real 282-task sync. Agent tests +6 (52 total).
- **`POST /api/tasks` (clone / custom creation) skipped cron→trigger conversion**: Windows tasks created through it (e.g. the Clone modal) fell into the agent's legacy fallback and ran "daily at now+1h" instead of the requested schedule. The route now validates the cron, converts it like the template-apply path, and rejects unconvertible schedules with a 400.
- **Tasks deleted natively no longer linger in TaskHub**: `POST /api/tasks/sync` now prunes DB tasks (and their execution logs) that are missing from the connector's full task list via `TaskService.removeStaleTasks`. Pruning is based on the pre-filter list, so category selections in the Import modal don't affect it; `TASKHUB_NATIVE` and empty connector lists are guarded. Verified live: 22 stale Windows tasks purged.
- **Agent permanently disconnected after a backend outage**: SocketIOClient gives up after its default 10 reconnect attempts, so any backend restart longer than ~1 minute left the agent silently offline until manually restarted. The agent now exposes `ISocketClient.Connected` and runs a 30s watchdog loop that re-calls `ConnectAsync` whenever disconnected — this also covers the agent starting before the backend at boot. (Do **not** "fix" this with `ReconnectionAttempts = int.MaxValue`: the library's internal delay math overflows and every connect throws.)
- **Startup task killed the agent after 3 days**: `setup-agent-startup.ps1` previously registered `TaskHubAgent` with Task Scheduler's default 72-hour execution time limit, which force-stops the long-running agent (restart-on-failure does not apply to time-limit kills). The task is now registered with no execution time limit.
- **Setup script failed on re-run while the agent was running**: the script now stops the scheduled task and kills any running `TaskHub.Agent` process before publishing, so the locked `.exe` no longer breaks rebuilds.
- **Import modal rendered a blank body when discovery returned nothing** (e.g. agent offline), making "Sync Now" look like a no-op. It now shows an explicit empty state pointing at the likely cause.
- Template apply route ignored the Apply modal's edited cron: the modal sends `schedule` but the route only read `scheduleExpression`. Both keys are now accepted, and non-5-field crons are rejected with a 400.
- GitHub Actions CI checks by:
  - Switching from `npm ci` to `npm install` in frontend and backend jobs to resolve cross-platform native package installation issues.
  - Adding `npx prisma generate` in backend pipeline to build the Prisma Client before testing/compiling.
  - Quoting the numeric `ENCRYPTION_KEY` environment variable in the workflow YAML to prevent scientific notation conversion.
  - Adding a `TaskHub.Agent.slnx` solution file inside the `agent/` folder to allow root-level `.NET` CLI commands to execute successfully.

### Known gaps
- Recurring `\Task-Hub\` self-heal tasks flash a PowerShell console window every few minutes (`-WindowStyle Hidden` doesn't suppress the conhost flash under an Interactive principal); `TaskHubAgent` + `TaskHubStack` are also redundant. Tracked in ROADMAP.
- An empty `\TaskHub\` Task Scheduler folder lingers after a Windows task is deleted, and the `\Task-Hub\` (infra) vs `\TaskHub\` (created tasks) naming is confusing. Tracked in ROADMAP.
- No export of a user's actual tasks yet (Windows → XML / native → JSON). Tracked in ROADMAP.
- Failure notifications are backend-env driven in the first slice; UI configuration, email, retry/backoff, and dedup policy remain open.
- Claude connector behavior is still scaffold-level.
- Hosted backend, staging, public-account operations, and signed Windows agent installer are still pending.

## Repository baseline

Current repo baseline at the time this changelog was introduced:

- Functional Windows Task Scheduler MVP path via local .NET agent.
- React frontend with dashboard, templates, platforms/settings views, demo mode, selective import, and local task categorization.
- Node/Express/Prisma backend with auth routes, task routes, template routes, WebSocket agent bridge, and connector registry.
- Backend test coverage present for encryption, connectors, task service, and agent manager.

**1. Rename stages 3 and 4 — the public surface, then the checkout folder.** ✅ **COMPLETE 2026-07-31.** The product is now Cronsole everywhere it is publicly visible, and the local checkout moved to `…\Live_Apps\cronsole` (**stage 4**, added after stage 3 shipped — see the rename item in P3 for what it cost and what it taught). Stages 1 (in-repo) and 2 (this machine) shipped earlier the same day. Final state:

| Thing | State | Note |
|:---|:---|:---|
| `michaelschecht/cronsole` | ✅ renamed, private, `origin` repointed | was `taskhub` |
| `michaelschecht/cronsole-registry` | ✅ renamed, public | the repo name **is** the URL path |
| `michaelschecht/cronsole-site` | ✅ renamed, public, `CNAME` flipped | custom domain + HTTPS enforcement re-set |
| `https://mikesailab.com/cronsole-registry/index.json` | ✅ **live, 200, 55 templates + 6 packs** | old path now **404** — as predicted |
| `https://cronsole.mikesailab.com` | ✅ **live, 200** | the front door |
| `https://taskhub.mikesailab.com` | ✅ **retired — DNS record deleted** | now `NXDOMAIN`, confirmed on 8.8.8.8 + 1.1.1.1 |

**The old front-door subdomain is retired rather than redirected**, decided 2026-07-31. It was kept briefly on the expectation that GitHub would forward it, and it does not — Pages redirects only `<user>.github.io/<repo>` to the configured custom domain, never a second custom domain pointed at the same IPs ([#34](troubleshooting/README.md#34-the-old-custom-domain-404s-after-moving-a-pages-site-to-a-new-one)). The alternative — a minimal second repo holding the old `CNAME` with a `<meta refresh>` + `<link rel="canonical">` — buys nothing here, because the site predates public launch and the URL was never advertised. **A name that doesn't resolve reads as "gone"; one that resolves to a 404 reads as "broken"** — so deleting the record is the more honest of the two failure modes, not merely the cheaper one. The `cronsole-site` commit that made the wrong claim carries a follow-up commit correcting it, since the mistake is in that repo's public history.

- **The registry URL was moved, and that was a one-time-only opportunity.** `TEMPLATE_REGISTRY_URL` is commented out in both `backend/.env` and `.env.example`, so **zero installs were fetching it** — not even this machine, which runs the bundled catalog (deliberate, 2026-07-15). GitHub redirects renamed repos' git URLs indefinitely but **not** Pages paths, so the old path 404s immediately (verified). The same move after the first real install would be a silent outage on a schedule nobody watches. **Treat the URL as frozen from here on** — see CLAUDE.md §9 and `publish-frontdoor.ps1`, both of which previously argued for the freeze on grounds this move has now spent, and were rewritten rather than quietly left standing.
- **The front door moved to `cronsole.mikesailab.com`**, after the DNS record was confirmed resolving on two independent resolvers — flipping first takes the site down for however long propagation takes, which is why it was held rather than done with the repos. The 25 held references went with it, including the **Connect Pack** (version bumped **1.1 → 1.2**: `CONNECT_PACK_HOME` is where a reader looks for a newer copy, so changing it *is* a content change, and a downloaded pack can never update itself). **Verify a domain move by requesting the old URL and reading the status code**, not by confirming the record resolves — that assumption is what produced [#34](troubleshooting/README.md#34-the-old-custom-domain-404s-after-moving-a-pages-site-to-a-new-one).
- ~~**The local clone directory stays `…\Live_Apps\taskhub`, permanently**~~ — **superseded the same day by stage 4, which moved it to `…\Live_Apps\cronsole`.** The reasoning was that every scheduled task embeds the directory as an absolute path and the name is not user-visible branding. The second half still holds; the first half was an argument for **migrating** the holders, not for never moving — which is what `Migrate-RepoFolder.ps1` does. Kept struck through rather than deleted, because "permanently" was asserted twice here and in CLAUDE.md and someone will otherwise re-derive it.

**2. Loose ends from the 2026-07-31 session** ✅ **all cleared 2026-07-31** — and two of the three were not what they were logged as:
- **The Import modal's live browser check** happened, and **found a real bug the unit tests structurally could not**: the modal rendered the raw platform enum as **"TASKHUB NATIVE"**. Nothing in the tree contains that string — it is computed at render time — so a green suite proved nothing. Fixed + pinned; see CHANGELOG › Fixed. Everything the item was *meant* to verify also checks out: None/Non-system/All presets, remembered selection, `Uncategorized` excluded by default, and the count stated before the click (`All` → *"This will import 354 tasks across 14 folders"*, button `Import 354 tasks`). No console errors.
- **The 3 stale rows are gone.** Sync marked them MISSING, Clear Missing removed them (356 → 351). Verified from **both** sides rather than the UI: DB shows 0 MISSING and 0 old-name rows, and Windows still holds all three `\Cronsole-Stack\` tasks with the agent Running — the confirm dialog's promise that "nothing on your machine is touched" held.
- **The `.mcp.json` rekey was not harmless.** It still declared `TASKHUB_TOKEN`, a variable stage 2 **deleted from the machine** — the tools only kept working because this Claude Code process inherited the old value from a pre-migration terminal. On the next restart from a fresh shell the literal `${TASKHUB_TOKEN}` would have been forwarded and the server would have refused to start, taking the tools away silently ([#8](troubleshooting/README.md#8-every-mcp-tool-returns-403-invalid-or-expired-token)). Rekeyed to `cronsole` / `CRONSOLE_*` and **verified by driving the server over stdio with the machine's real token**: handshake as `cronsole`, 14 tools listed, `delete_task` correctly absent behind its gate.

  **The pattern across all three: an item logged as cosmetic was load-bearing in two cases out of three.** "Harmless — it points at `dist/`" and "worth one click-through" were both written from reading, not running.

  **Decided while closing this out: `\Cronsole-Stack\` stays untracked.** Clearing the stale rows left the launcher folder out of the dashboard, which is a change from before the rename (the three tasks *were* tracked as `\Task-Hub\*`), so it needed a call rather than a default. They are **infrastructure — the thing that runs Cronsole, not work Cronsole runs** — and tracking them puts the app in its own task list, where disabling `CronsoleAgent` from the dashboard takes the agent offline and thereby removes the dashboard's ability to re-enable it. It also keeps the `\Cronsole-Stack\` ↔ `\Cronsole\` separation true at the UI layer rather than relying on nobody clicking Delete. **Nothing enforces it** — Import discovers `Cronsole-Stack` as an ordinary category, ticked under *Non-system* — so it is a standing choice, written down in CLAUDE.md §9 and [`scripts/startup-task/README.md`](../scripts/startup-task/README.md) precisely because a future import would silently undo it. Status for these three comes from `cronsole.ps1 status` and Task Scheduler.

**3. Then, product work, in this order** — all naming-independent, so none of it waits on stage 3:
~~`Richer execution analytics`~~ ✅ **shipped 2026-07-31** (see P3) → ~~`Bulk task actions`~~ **first slice — bulk enable/disable — shipped 2026-07-31** (see the P2 item; the selection model landed with it and the remaining verbs now cost only their own logic) → ~~`Saved views`~~ ✅ **shipped 2026-07-31** (see the P2 item — and note the item's premise was wrong: four of its five named views needed filter dimensions that did not exist, so the filter model was widened to build them) → **`Versioning & releases`** ← *next* (which also unblocks the deliberately-skipped `version` fields in the package manifests).

*Note for whoever picks up the **rest of Bulk task actions**: selection, the shared row-action component, and the honest per-task reporting all shipped with enable/disable, so **bulk recategorize** (DB-only), **bulk untrack**, and **export selected** (the bulk export route with an id list) each need only their own route now. Reuse `POST /api/tools/tasks/status`'s shape — five outcomes, the halt-on-agent-down rule, the `notFound` list — rather than inventing a second reporting convention; a second one is how two mechanisms answering the same question drift.*

*Last updated: 2026-07-31 — **the product is now `Cronsole`, publicly** (rename stages 1 + 2 shipped, and stage 3 renamed all three GitHub repos and moved the template registry to `https://mikesailab.com/cronsole-registry/` — the one URL that only gets one free move, taken while `TEMPLATE_REGISTRY_URL` was commented out and nothing was fetching it. The `taskhub.mikesailab.com` front door is the sole piece still held, pending a DNS record). Also shipped that day: the four Go-public checklist items (`/auth/register` deleted, REST CORS aligned with `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` + a refusal that explains itself, Apache-2.0 in every manifest, `check_db` → `npm run db:check`), the **Schedule tester** on the Tools tab, and **Import defaults** (presets, remembered selection, the count before the click). New traps: [#31](troubleshooting/README.md#31-the-dashboard-loads-but-every-api-call-fails-with-a-cors-error) (CORS: curl works, the browser doesn't) and [#32](troubleshooting/README.md#33-a-rename-pass-silently-disables-the-back-compat-it-just-added--and-rewrites-the-tests-too) (a rename pass disables its own back-compat **and** the tests guarding it). The paragraph below is the 2026-07-13 snapshot, kept as written.*
