  - **Don't build a second distribution channel.** The template registry already solves *"ship content that updates without redeploying the app"* — versioned, sha256 integrity-checked, published to a public repo on GitHub Pages, synced on boot + interval. Skills and prompts are the same problem. Ride the registry or serve from the gallery site; a parallel mechanism is a second thing to keep in sync.
  - **A downloaded copy on a stranger's machine is a mirror surface you cannot update** — §11a's exact failure mode, at the one distance where you can never fix it. Every artifact ships **version-stamped** and pointing at a canonical hosted URL, so a stale copy can at least tell its reader it is stale.

- [ ] **Restore's plan doesn't check that a task's action points at anything that exists** *(logged 2026-07-31, found while checking a pre-rename backup)*: the plan validates `\Microsoft\`, folder existence, and whether the task is already there — it says nothing about the `<Exec>` inside the XML. So an archive whose tasks reference paths that have since moved previews as a set of clean `create`s, restores "successfully", and produces tasks that **fail silently**: `wscript.exe` launching a `.ps1` that isn't there opens no window and reports nothing. Found concretely — a 2026-07-28 export of `\Task-Hub\` still points at `Start-TaskHub.ps1` and `taskhub.ps1`, both renamed away by the Cronsole migration, so two of those three would restore as tasks that do nothing at logon. **This is not a bug in what restore promises**, and the fix is not to refuse: an executable missing *here* may exist on the machine being restored *to*, which is the migration case the feature exists for. What's wrong is that the preview looks more protective than it is. Wanted: the plan reports, per file, whether the action's executable and any file-looking arguments resolve **on this machine**, as an advisory column — not a refusal. Cheap: the agent already reads the machine, and the XML is already parsed to extract the target path. **The general lesson is worth more than the fix**: a backup records paths, and paths are machine state — so any rename or move silently ages an archive, and the archive cannot tell you that.

- [ ] **Tools tab — further candidate tools** *(logged 2026-07-28; **candidates only, none scheduled**)*: ideas that genuinely belong on the Tools tab, with the cost of each checked against the existing API. Listed so they aren't re-derived later; **not committed work**. Ranked roughly by value-per-cost, and separate from **Restore** below, which is the strongest and has its own item.

  - **Scheduled automatic backups.** The bulk export only runs when someone clicks it, so it protects you exactly as often as you remember to. A weekly export to a chosen folder would make the backup real. **The design point that decides this:** it needs something to write files unattended, and **the backend can do that where the agent must not**. The refusal to give the agent a file-write verb is because the agent runs **elevated** — the backend does not, so writing to a user-configured folder from the backend is an ordinary local process writing to a local directory. Don't let "we already refused file writes" kill this for the wrong reason. Also on-brand: TaskHub already dogfoods itself with self-heal scheduled tasks.
  - ~~**Schedule tester.**~~ ✅ **Shipped 2026-07-31** — a Tools-tab card over `POST /api/tasks/preview`, which now also returns the upcoming run times. **The design point that made it worth building rather than merely cheap:** it shows *two* lists — what you asked for and **what will actually run** — because the trap is not that a cron is hard to read, it is that on Windows the cron is **discarded**. `0 4 1 1 *` renders as one run next January beside an hourly list starting an hour from now, which is the same fact as the existing warning in the register people actually read. **What it deliberately does not do:** an `approximated` step (an uneven `*/N`) gets **no** effective dates at all. The reverse converter maps `PT7M` straight back to the same step expression, so a round-trip would report the two schedules as identical when the drift *is* the point — and simulating Windows repetition properly means writing a second scheduler, which is exactly the confident-lie risk this feature exists to remove. The dates are shown only where they can be **derived**, never inferred; the drift stays stated in words. Found by a failing test, not by review. *(Original note below.)*

    *(Original)* Type a cron, see the actual Windows trigger it produces, the `lossy` verdict, and the next five run times in **local and UTC**. Cheapest item here and it attacks the most expensive trap in the project: `0 4 1 1 *` (once a year) silently becoming an **hourly** trigger, 8,760 runs instead of 1 ([#14](troubleshooting/README.md#14-a-rare-cron-becomes-an-hourly-trigger)). `POST /api/tasks/preview` already returns `score` / `warnings` / `trigger` / `lossy`; it does **not** return next-run times, but `computeNextRun` (`utils/cron-next.ts`) already exists, so that is a small backend addition. **No agent involvement at all.** The trap is currently documented in three places and *visible* in none — making it visible before the task is created is worth more than the documentation.
  - **Snapshot diff — "what changed since your last backup."** Feed it a previous export, get *3 added, 1 deleted, 2 with changed commands*. A pure backend comparison of two XML sets — **almost free now that export exists**, no new agent work — and the natural answer to "did something change my scheduled tasks?"
  - **Diagnostics panel** — a user-facing `/doctor`: agent online, backend reachable, when the catalog last synced, when a sync last actually ran. TaskHub is local-first, so **every user is their own sysadmin**, and today the only health signal is a colored dot in the sidebar.
  - **Bulk enable/disable by folder.** *"Disable everything in `\Work` while I migrate this machine,"* then re-enable. Reuses the existing **signed** `task:set_status` path, so **no new agent verb**, and it is *reversible* — the same reason `set_task_status` ships ungated over MCP. One honest asymmetry to surface in the UI: unlike export, this reaches only **tracked** tasks, since it works from DB rows.
  - ~~**Run history export (CSV).**~~ ✅ **Shipped 2026-07-28** — see the *Run history export + automation health score* item above. `ExecutionLog` was readable only 20 rows at a time **per task**, so *"which tasks failed this month"* had no answer. One bulk query route, no agent work, and it turned out to be the shared data layer for the health score and the analytics item too.

  **What stays off the tab:** anything acting on a *single* task belongs on the Dashboard, where the task is. The tab earns its name only while everything in it is genuinely cross-cutting; the moment it collects one-offs it is a junk drawer, and that is the point to rename it rather than keep adding.

  *2026-07-28 — the outside analysis independently arrived at four of these six* (scheduled backups, schedule tester, diagnostics panel, run-history export), which is weak evidence they're the right ones. It also connects two of them upward: **Schedule tester** is the cheapest slice of the P3 *Dry run & validation lab* bet, and **Run history export** needs the same bulk `ExecutionLog` query as *Richer execution analytics* — build that route once and both fall out of it.

- [x] **Restore tasks from an export — the other half of the backup** *(logged 2026-07-28; **shipped 2026-07-28** — see CHANGELOG › Added)*: shipped as designed below, with the four decisions honored. **What the build settled beyond them:**
  - **The plan phase turned out to be the whole feature.** It was scoped as a nicety and is the reason restore is safe: because it is computed from the two *read-only* verbs, `dryRun` is a real preview rather than a rehearsal, it needs no agent republish, and an offline agent becomes a **502 instead of a blind restore**. The rule generalizes — *when a write path can be planned from reads you already have, the plan is not a feature, it is the honest form of the write.*
  - **The four-state outcome was forced by the data, not chosen.** `exists` is neither success nor failure, and every boolean shape tried collapsed it into a lie in one direction or the other. It survives from the agent's `AgentImportResult` through the connector and the route to the screen unchanged.
  - **`action` travels beside `outcome` in every result** — so a plan that said `create` and an agent that said `exists` are visibly in disagreement rather than quietly reconciled. That gap is the machine changing under us, which is worth seeing.
  - **New trap:** [#27](troubleshooting/README.md#27-a-route-that-takes-an-upload-413s--and-raising-the-global-body-limit-is-the-wrong-fix) — `express.json()` caps bodies at 100 kB, and the reflex fix (raise it in `createApp`) hands **every** endpoint a 32 MB budget. Scoped parser, mounted **before** the global one, because `body-parser` skips a request another parser already consumed — get the order wrong and it looks exactly like never having added it. Pinned by an integration test that posts >100 kB and requires a 502, not a 413.
  - **Live-verified against real Task Scheduler after an agent republish**, per the rule that the reporting layer cannot be its own witness — every check below read Windows, not TaskHub. A probe task was registered by hand in its own folder and exported; the plan **skipped** it while it existed; the task and folder were deleted; the plan then **refused** with the folder named (checkbox off) and **planned one folder** (checkbox on); the commit reported `created` + `foldersCreated: ['\TaskHubRestoreProbe']`, and **`Get-ScheduledTask` confirmed the round trip** — `Ready`, action, trigger, description, and critically the **principal intact** (`UserId=mikes LogonType=Interactive RunLevel=Limited`), which settles the one thing the unit tests could not: `TaskLogonType.None` does preserve the XML's own principal. Restoring again with overwrite off returned `exists` and left the registration date **unchanged**; with overwrite on it returned `replaced`. A real `\Microsoft\Windows\Defrag\ScheduledDefrag` export was **refused**, and Defrag was untouched.
  - **The live pass found the real cost of the folder carve-out, and it is sharper than the invariant assumed** ([#28](troubleshooting/README.md#28-a-restored-task-or-the-folder-it-landed-in-cant-be-deleted-access-is-denied)): because the **agent is elevated**, a task it restores — and any folder it creates — get an administrator ACE, so the *user who created the original unelevated could no longer delete the restored copy*. Cleanup had to go back through TaskHub (import → Delete from Windows, which routes through the same elevated agent), and **the empty folder needed an elevated prompt**, since TaskHub only ever prunes its own `\TaskHub`. The original rationale said a created folder is "a one-way door only the user can close"; in practice it is a door **only an administrator** can close. The design still stands — refusing would fail every task in an archive on a reinstalled machine — but it is now stated on the checkbox, in the plan, and in the docs, so it is a decision rather than a surprise.
  - Coverage beneath the agent: backend 361 unit + 8 integration against real Postgres, agent 151, frontend 145, with four load-bearing behaviors mutation-checked. Still not covered by an automated test: `Win32TaskScheduler.ImportTaskXml` itself, which talks to COM — hence the live pass.
  - Also fixed in passing: the Tools tab's subtitle, one of the *UI polish pass* items below (*"Backup, and setup for other AI tools."* → *"Back up and restore tasks, and connect AI tools."*).

  *(Original scoping notes retained below.)* bulk export shipped; **restore did not**, and a backup you cannot restore is a filing cabinet. Everything needed is in place: the exported XML is UTF-16 LE + BOM by construction, which is exactly what `Register-ScheduledTask -Xml` and `schtasks /create /xml` consume, and every archive carries a `_taskhub-export.json` manifest naming each file's original task path. The work is a **new agent verb** (`task:import` taking XML — and unlike export, this one **writes**, so it must be a *signed* command, must re-validate `\Microsoft\` independently in the agent as every write path does, and needs a decision on overwrite-vs-refuse when the task still exists) plus a restore UI that reads a folder or archive and reports per-task results. Sequenced after export deliberately: export is read-only and shipped safely in a day; restore registers elevated scheduled tasks from a file the user supplies, which deserves its own design pass rather than being tacked on.

  **Decided 2026-07-28, before implementation** (the three questions the item left open, plus one it didn't anticipate):

  - **Overwrite is refused by default and opted into explicitly.** A task already on the machine is reported `already exists — skipped`, not overwritten and not failed. `overwrite` is a checkbox, and it rides **inside the HMAC signature**: it is the field that decides blast radius, so an on-path attacker flipping it would turn a skip into a destroy — the same argument that put `folder` inside the `task:create` signature. Windows enforces it too (`TaskCreation.Create` refuses rather than our existence check racing), because the platform, not TaskHub, is the witness that matters.
  - **The XML is signed by hash, not by value.** The XML *is* the task — action, trigger, and principal (including `RunLevel Highest` and the run-as account) — so an unsigned XML would make the signature decorative. It enters the canonical message as a lowercase-hex **sha256 over its UTF-8 bytes**, mirroring how `canonicalizeAction` / `canonicalizeTrigger` reduce a structured field to a fixed, unambiguous string on both sides of the language boundary.
  - **Missing folders are recreated, opt-in and reported — a deliberate carve-out to "TaskHub creates exactly one folder".** That invariant exists because folder deletion needs elevation, so a folder TaskHub creates is a one-way door only the user can close; it was written after a template apply left three empty folders nobody asked for. Restore is the opposite consent situation: the user is asking for *their own* folder tree back, by name, from a file they chose. Refusing would be correct by the letter and useless in practice — a restore onto a reinstalled machine would refuse every task in the archive. So: a **Recreate missing folders** checkbox (signed, like `overwrite`), and the result **names every folder it created**. The invariant is amended rather than abandoned — TaskHub still creates nothing unasked, and `\TaskHub` is still the only folder it ever *removes*.
  - **The preview needs no agent write, which is why it can exist at all.** The route always computes a **plan** first — per file: `create` / `overwrite` / `skip` (exists) / `refuse` (bad XML, `\Microsoft\`, missing folder with the checkbox off) — from the two **read-only** verbs that already ship (`task:list`, `task:folders`). `dryRun: true` returns the plan and writes nothing, so the count arrives *before* the click rather than after it (the same principle the *Import defaults* item is filed for). It also means the preview works against an agent that has not been republished yet.
  - **Target path resolution is manifest › the XML's own `<URI>` › the relative file path**, in that order, and the resolved path is shown for every file in the plan. Our own archives carry the manifest, so they are exact; a hand-supplied file falls back to what Windows itself wrote into the XML; the filename is a guess and ranks last precisely because `safeSegment` sanitization made it lossy on the way out.
  - **Scope is Windows XML only.** The bulk export it pairs with is Windows-only, so a TaskHub-native JSON restore is *not* in this slice — stated rather than silently absent.

- [x] **Untrack — remove a task from TaskHub without deleting it from Windows** *(logged 2026-07-28 from the analysis; **shipped 2026-07-28** — see CHANGELOG › Added)*: shipped as scoped, with both design points honored. **What the build settled:**
  - **The exclusion memory was the right call** (the Open decision below is now resolved): a `TaskExclusion` table keyed on the platform-native `externalId`, **subtractive only** — it can remove a task from a sync, never add one, so the include-set still has exactly one definition and this doesn't become the second source of truth #20a punished.
  - **The way back is explicit re-import, and it announces itself in both directions** — the item said "until explicitly re-imported" without saying how the user would know. `/discover` now reports `excludedCount` per category **before** the click (an amber `+N removed` chip in the Import modal) and the sync toast says what came back after it. Sync Now (`scope: 'tracked'`) never clears exclusions; only an explicit `categories` import does, because a routine refresh undoing a deliberate removal is the invisible fence again.
  - **`TASKHUB_NATIVE` is refused, not accommodated.** A native task exists nowhere else, so "remove but keep it" cannot be true — doing a delete under a reversible label is precisely the mistake the feature exists to prevent.
  - **The MCP surface needed it too, and that was the sharper finding:** the only removal an agent had was the gated, irreversible `delete_task`, so "clean up my dashboard" had one tool and it destroyed real tasks. `untrack_task` ships **ungated** — gating deletion is only honest once a safe removal exists without the gate (surface 14 → 15).
  - **Verified from outside TaskHub**, per the rule that the reporting layer can't be its own witness: after untracking a throwaway Windows task, **Windows' own `Get-ScheduledTask` still reported it `Ready`**; a tracked sync then left it out and an explicit import brought it back.

  *(Original scoping notes retained below.)* `DELETE /api/tasks/:id` deletes the **real Task Scheduler entry** via a signed agent command, and that is the only removal path a user has. So there is currently **no way to undo an over-import**: import a folder you didn't mean to, and your choices are to keep 200 rows you don't want forever, or delete someone's actual scheduled tasks. The dashboard state observed live — 352 tracked tasks, **257 of them `\Microsoft\`** — is exactly the mess this creates, and it is reachable in **one click** from the Import modal's default selection. **The absent capability is the tell**: `DELETE /api/tasks/missing` already exists and already does the DB-only half (it makes **no platform call**, because MISSING means the platform entry is gone) — so the mechanism is built, it is just scoped to the one status where it happens to be safe. Untrack generalizes it to any status, and the row can be re-imported later because `(platform, externalId)` is the identity and the platform still has the task.
  - **The whole design is in the labeling, not the code.** Two removals that differ only in blast radius, one of which is irreversible, must not look alike: separate verbs (**Remove from TaskHub** vs **Delete from Windows**), separate placement, separate confirm copy naming *what survives*, and destructive styling on exactly one. A shared "Delete" with a checkbox is the version of this feature that eventually deletes a user's backup job.
  - **Untrack must not fight the next sync.** A task removed from TaskHub while still present on the machine will be re-imported by the next sync that includes its category — which is arguably correct (it *is* on the machine) but reads as "untrack didn't work". Needs a decision: either untrack is scoped to categories the user then stops importing, or an untracked task is remembered as excluded (a suppression list keyed on `externalId`) until explicitly re-imported. **Lean: remember the exclusion** — silently undoing the user's action on a timer is the invisible-fence failure again.
  - Pairs with the system-task filter below: filtering hides them from view; untracking gets them out of the counts, the category chips, and the sync payload.

- [x] **The dashboard is dominated by `\Microsoft\` system tasks once they're imported** *(logged 2026-07-28 from the analysis; **shipped 2026-07-28** alongside Untrack, its other half — see CHANGELOG › Added)*: `GET /api/tasks` now carries a **server-owned `isSystem`** verdict (`TaskService.isSystemTask` — the predicate `summarizeUntracked` already used, so there is still exactly one definition and the browser never re-derives it), and the dashboard has a persisted **Personal / Incl. System** toggle. It is a separate control from Active Only, not a shared mode — they answer different questions and the useful default is both — and it names what it hides (`257 system hidden`) with colour, icon and count. The two rules that make it correct (outermost lens; count over *all* tasks) were extracted to `applySystemLens` so tests pin them. Live split confirmed on the real machine: **257 system / 95 personal of 352**. *(Original finding below.)*

  *(Original)* Import excludes `\Microsoft\` **by default** — but that guard fires exactly once, at import time, and afterwards nothing distinguishes an OS-owned task from one the user wrote. Every headline number, every category chip, every view mode, and the un-imported-tasks signal all count 257 rows the user will never act on, so **the 95 tasks that matter are a rounding error in their own dashboard**. Wanted: a **persistent** system/personal split — a "Hide system tasks" toggle or a Personal-by-default mode — that is **independent of the active/disabled filter** (they compose: *personal + active* is the real default view) and, per the lesson from the active/all toggle, **says what it is hiding** (`Personal · 257 system hidden`) rather than silently trimming. Server-side the predicate already exists in spirit: `TaskService` deliberately counts `\Microsoft\` separately for `summarizeUntracked` (`TaskService.ts:31`), so "is this a system task" is a defined concept that just never reached the UI. Cheap and high-leverage; sequenced with Untrack, which is the same problem's other half.

- [x] **Import defaults are one click from the mess above** *(logged 2026-07-28; **shipped 2026-07-31** — see CHANGELOG › Changed)*: shipped as scoped — **None / Non-system / All** presets, the selection **remembered from the last import**, and the count stated **before** the click (*"This will import 214 tasks across 12 folders"*), with the primary button reading `Import 214 tasks` rather than `Sync 12 Categories`. The count was always free: `/discover` returns every category's total and the modal simply never showed it.

  **The distinction the item is really about is first-run vs. every-run-after**, and it is carried by `lastImportCategories` being **`null`** rather than empty. Null means "never imported" and takes the generous non-system default, because a first-run user who imports nothing sees an empty product and concludes it doesn't work. Any other value is the user's own last answer. Two things fall out that the item didn't name: a remembered category whose folder has since gone is **dropped** rather than shown ticked (otherwise the count promises tasks that aren't there), and the selection is persisted **only on commit** — ticking boxes and closing must change nothing. Both are mutation-tested.

  Also removed here: the two `console.log`s in `ImportModal` that printed the whole discovery payload — task names and native paths — into a console that gets screen-shared. That was part of the *Console noise* item below, which stays open for the remaining backend/Dashboard cases. **Honest gap:** the live browser check did not happen — the extension hung after the stack restart — so this rests on 11 unit tests (2 mutation-checked) plus build/lint. Worth a click-through next session. *(Original finding below.)*

  *(Original)* discovery correctly unticks `Microsoft` and `Uncategorized`, then **pre-selects the remaining 12 categories** and offers `Sync 12 Categories` as the primary action. That is right for a first run on a clean machine and wrong for every run after, when the user usually wants one new folder — and it is how a 352-row dashboard happens. Wanted: **Select none / Select all / All non-system** presets, selection persisted from the last import, and a **preview count before committing** (*"this will import 214 tasks across 12 folders"*), so the number arrives before the action rather than after it. The count is free — `/discover` already returns every task with its path. Deliberately **not** changing the default to "none": a first-run user who imports nothing sees an empty product. The fix is that the second run shouldn't inherit the first run's answer.

- [~] **Bulk task actions** — **bulk enable/disable shipped 2026-07-31; recategorize / untrack / export-selected remain.** The selection model, the shared row-action component, and the per-task reporting convention all landed with the first slice, so each remaining verb now costs only its own route. Two things the investigation below did **not** anticipate, both found while building it:

  - **The reporting, not the selection, was the real work.** At 350 tasks a bulk operation is partially successful as the *normal* case — an ACL'd Windows task refuses elevation, one went MISSING since the last sync, another is already in the state you asked for — so `POST /api/tools/tasks/status` reports **per task with five outcomes**, not a count. `unchanged` had to be its own state: counting a no-op as `updated` inflates the number people read, counting it as `failed` invents an error. Re-sending a completed request reads `0 disabled · 2 already disabled`, which is the whole point.
  - **A bulk operation needs a halt rule, and it is not the same as a failure rule.** Each Windows task is a round trip that costs its full ~15s timeout when the agent is gone, so a 50-task selection against an offline agent would be a twelve-minute HTTP request ending in fifty copies of one error. The run **stops at the first agent-down signal** and marks the rest `skipped` with the reason. A *per-task* refusal deliberately does not halt — it says nothing about the next task. Getting that discrimination wrong in either direction is a bug: too loose and one ACL'd task aborts the batch, too tight and you wait out fifty timeouts.

  Verified live against real Windows tasks and confirmed **from Windows** (`Get-ScheduledTask` → `Disabled`), not from Cronsole's own report — the standing rule that the reporting layer cannot be its own witness. Original entry follows.

  *(Original, logged 2026-07-28 from the analysis)*: everything on the dashboard is a per-task modal round-trip, which does not survive contact with hundreds of tasks — the same asymmetry that made *Bulk-clear MISSING* necessary (one sync flags 56 at once; clearing was 56 open→confirm→close cycles). Wanted, in rough value order: **bulk recategorize**, **bulk untrack** (above), **bulk enable/disable**, and **export selected**. Cost is already scoped: bulk enable/disable reuses the **signed** `task:set_status` path with **no new agent verb** and is reversible (the same argument that ships MCP `set_task_status` ungated); export-selected is the bulk export route with an id list; recategorize is DB-only. ~~**The one real design question is selection**~~ — **investigated 2026-07-31; the answer is yes, one model covers all four, and it is cheaper than this item assumed.** Findings, so nobody re-derives them:

  - **All four views already render from one array.** `filteredTasks` (`DashboardScreen.tsx:204`) is the single source: grid and list map it directly, **schedule is `[...filteredTasks].sort(...)`** — the same set in a different order, not a different query — and **kanban is that set partitioned by `status === 'ACTIVE'`** into two columns. So "what is on screen" already has one definition, and a `Set<string>` of task ids keyed off it works in every view without a per-view selection concept.
  - **One genuine asymmetry, and it is small.** `filteredTasks` *itself* branches on `viewMode` (lines 209–211): kanban skips the active-only filter because its second column *is* the disabled tasks. So grid→kanban **widens** the on-screen set and kanban→grid **narrows** it, which means a selection carried across a view switch can include tasks the user can no longer see. **Do not silently drop them** — keep the selection (it is keyed by id, so it survives) and state the gap, exactly as the active/all toggle already does with `Active Only · 110 hidden`. The toolbar chip reads *"12 selected · 3 not visible here"*.
  - **Every view has a natural checkbox slot**, so no view needs restructuring: grid/kanban cards have a `flex flex-col` header beside the platform badge; the list view gets a leading column (and is the only one that could carry a header select-all — put it in the **toolbar** instead, where it can name the number); the schedule card has a header row beside the task name.
  - **Every row's container already has an `onClick` that opens the task modal**, so a checkbox must `stopPropagation` — the established pattern here, already used by all four views' action clusters.
  - **The real cost is not the checkbox, it is that the action cluster is written out four times** (clone / toggle / run, at four different icon sizes). Adding selection touches all four. `DashboardScreen.tsx` is now **889 lines** and is named in *Split the files that have become fault lines* — so extract the row-action cluster and the checkbox as shared pieces **while** doing this, per that item's own rule of splitting along the seams only when next touching the area.
  - **Kanban has one wrinkle specific to bulk enable/disable:** its columns are defined by the very status the action changes, so rows jump columns mid-operation. Not a blocker (selection is id-keyed and survives), but the toast should say what moved rather than leaving the list to silently reshuffle.
  - **Precedent to reuse rather than reinvent:** `ImportModal` already implements this shape — presets (None / Non-system / All), a remembered selection, and the count stated **before** the click. Bulk actions want the same three. Overlaps the *Tools tab candidates* › **Bulk enable/disable by folder**: that item is the folder-scoped, cross-cutting version; this one is checkbox selection where the tasks are. Ship whichever answers a real need first, not both.

- [x] **Saved views** ✅ **shipped 2026-07-31** *(logged 2026-07-28 from the analysis)*: five built-ins — "My jobs", "Failures", "Due today", "Disabled", "System" — plus any number the user names and saves, each a chip with its own count, route-backed so a view is a bookmarkable URL and persisted in settings beside `templateView` / `taskView`. The honest UI answer to the problem the *Automation health score* bet attacks with scoring: **at 352 tasks the question is never "show me everything"**.

  **The item's own premise was wrong, and correcting it was most of the work.** It claimed the composing filters "all already exist"; in fact the dashboard had category, platform, search and two **binary** toggles, so only *My jobs* was expressible. Showing everything is not showing only the disabled; including system tasks is not showing only system tasks; and there was no run-outcome or next-run filter at all. **Four of the five named views needed dimensions that did not exist** — status widened to four values, the system lens to three, plus new run-outcome and due-window dimensions, all consolidated into one `TaskFilters` object (`frontend/src/utils/taskFilters.ts`).

  Two decisions worth carrying forward. **"Failures" consumes the server's health verdict and forms none of its own** — the obvious `lastRunStatus === 'FAILURE'` would be confidently wrong on Windows, where that value records the agent *accepting a start*; the real evidence is `lastTaskResult`, already reasoned about in `taskHealth.ts`, so the view reads `GET /api/tools/task-health` and shares its cache key with the Tools panel. And **absence of evidence reaches the UI intact**: an unscored task is `unknown`, so with no health data a Failures view is empty — which reads as "nothing is failing" — and the chip therefore shows `–` rather than `0` until the scan lands, with a banner saying why.

  Found while verifying it, and fixed in the same change: **the Task health card was counting three tiers over personal tasks and `Healthy` over all 352**, printing a row that summed to 256 under the label "across 352 tasks". See CHANGELOG › Fixed. Tests: frontend 305 (+59), six mutation checks; verified live on the 352-task machine.

- [ ] **Task-detail trust indicators — say how old the truth is** *(logged 2026-07-28 from the analysis)*: the task modal renders schedule/action/settings parsed from **synced metadata**, with no indication of *when* that snapshot was taken or whether the platform has confirmed it since. Given the project's own history — a `MISSING` feature that reported success for nine days, a `SUCCESS` that is also what a hung task reports ([#12](troubleshooting/README.md#12-a-template-passes-every-test-and-still-hangs-on-the-target)), a sync that can't see a renamed category's folder ([#20a](troubleshooting/README.md#20a-and-a-renamed-category-silently-stops-syncing-its-folder)) — a detail view that states metadata as timeless fact is the confident-lie register. Wanted: **last platform-confirmed sync** (timestamp, per task, not the global "synced N ago" chip), **last agent result**, and **Windows' own `LastTaskResult` where the agent can read it** — that last one matters most, because it is the only field in the modal that comes from **outside TaskHub**, and the rule this project has now learned three times is that the reporting layer cannot be its own witness.

- [ ] **Optional periodic Windows sync** *(logged 2026-07-28 from the analysis)*: there is **no automatic Windows sync at all** — no poll, no interval; `NativeScheduler` runs native jobs and `catalogSync` refreshes templates, neither touches Task Scheduler ([#20](troubleshooting/README.md#20-sync-now-never-brings-in-a-task-you-just-created-in-task-scheduler)). That is a defensible default (selective import means an automatic sync would make choices for you) but it is *undiscoverable*, and it is why a user pressing **Sync Now** repeatedly can watch nothing happen and conclude the agent is broken. Wanted: an **opt-in** interval sync with the interval stated, the last run shown, and the same `untracked` remainder surfaced that Sync Now now reports. Must stay `scope: 'tracked'` — an automatic sync that widened the include-set would import folders the user declined, which is worse than not syncing. Sequenced after the system-task filter, so the thing it refreshes is legible.

- [ ] **UI polish pass — the small things the browser review caught** *(logged 2026-07-28 from the analysis; one item, several fixes, none individually worth its own line)*:
  - ~~**Tools tab copy**~~ *(done 2026-07-28 with Restore)* — now *"Back up and restore tasks, and connect AI tools."*
  - **Truncated task paths on cards** — a native path is the task's identity and is routinely cut mid-string; add a copy affordance or full-path tooltip rather than widening the card.
  - **The onboarding banner outlives its usefulness** — it shows on every section until explicitly dismissed. Auto-retire it once the user has opened Help, completed an Import, or created a task; a first-run banner that survives the first run is an ad.
  - **Apply-template modal** — the sticky footer crowds the first parameter field at laptop heights; add bottom padding to the scroll area (or trim the explanatory text above it, which is doing less work than the parameters below).
  - **Help Center vertical tightness** — verify every guide card is reachable above the fixed footer at ~720px viewport height.
  - **Dev-mode Socket.IO console warnings** — two *"WebSocket closed before connection established"* per load, almost certainly React StrictMode double-mounting `useLiveTaskUpdates()`'s `/ui` socket and closing the first before it opens. Functionally harmless, which is the problem: **warnings that are always there train you to ignore the console**, and this project has now twice been saved by someone actually reading it.

- [ ] **Console noise in user-facing flows** *(logged 2026-07-28 from the analysis; **verified**)*: `ImportModal.tsx:26,28` logs the discovery request **and the full response**, and `Dashboard.tsx:198` logs connection setup; the backend has scattered `console.log` on connector paths. On a local-first tool these lines carry **task names, native paths, and command lines** to a console anyone screen-sharing or screenshotting can read, and they bury the warnings that matter (above). Move them behind a debug flag or a structured logger — the backend already needs the structured-logging work listed under *Production operations*, so do the two together rather than deleting the frontend lines and calling it done.

- [ ] **Reframe or grow the Platforms tab** *(logged 2026-07-28 from the analysis)*: Platforms currently reads as thin next to Dashboard/Templates/Tools because most of what it holds is **quick links to other products' native UIs** — which is honest (ChatGPT has no automations API, Jules and Open Claw have no connector) but under-labels itself as a *platform integration* surface. Two honest options: **rename** it to what it is (`Links` / `Launchers`), or **grow** it into a real per-platform status surface — connection state, capability matrix (can create / can run / can edit / read-only / links-only), last contact, and what each platform's connector actually supports today. **Prefer growing it**: the capability matrix is content the project already maintains in prose across CLAUDE.md, the skill, and `task-authoring.md` §5, and a user reading "Claude Code: experimental scaffold, cannot create" in the product is worth more than a tab renamed to be smaller. Related to the same instinct as the *Tools* naming caveat — a label earns its place only while the contents justify it.

- [x] **Windows task enable/disable from TaskHub** *(highest current priority; first slice)*: expose the already-existing signed `task:set_status` path as a real product feature. Add an owner-scoped REST route, make the agent/backend exchange return an explicit ack/error like `task:delete`, update the DB only after platform confirmation, refresh the task list, and add Enable/Disable controls in the task modal/card with honest offline, not-found, and access-denied errors. *(Shipped 2026-07-10)*
- [x] **Edit Windows task schedules from TaskHub** *(second slice; shipped 2026-07-10 — see Completed)*: the **Edit Schedule** button now opens a cron editor (presets + local/UTC preview + live conversion warnings) that changes a Windows task's trigger via a signed `task:update_schedule`; the agent rebuilds only the trigger (action/principal/settings preserved), the DB is updated only after platform confirmation, and non-cron triggers (boot/logon/event/on-demand) stay read-only. Live-dogfooded against real Task Scheduler.
- [x] **Edit Windows task actions/settings from TaskHub** *(third slice; shipped 2026-07-11 — see Completed)*: a signed agent `task:update` write path now edits a Windows task's action (executable + args + working dir) and selected settings (description, run level), reusing the structured no-shell `{ executable, args[] }` command model; the agent replaces the exec action + description/run-level while preserving triggers, principal identity, and other settings, DB metadata is refreshed only after platform confirmation, and elevation/ACL failures surface as an honest 502. Live-dogfooded against real Task Scheduler; covered by backend integration + agent unit + mock-agent E2E. *(This completes the three-slice Windows management pass: enable/disable → schedule edit → action/settings edit. Enabled toggling stays with the enable/disable feature; run-as user / logon type are deferred as riskier v2 edits.)*
- [x] **Full task detail view** *(shipped 2026-07-08 — see Completed)*: the task modal now parses synced metadata into Schedule / Action / Settings panels (with honest fallbacks) instead of a raw JSON dump, and the agent was extended to report actions + principal/registration settings. Recurring clock times now display in the viewer's local zone (P1 item done 2026-07-09). Remaining polish: surfacing last-result/next-run for native tasks.
- [x] **Failure notifications** *(first slice shipped 2026-07-10 — see Completed)*: server-side webhook delivery now covers failed manual runs and failed scheduled TaskHub-native runs via generic/Discord/ntfy webhooks. Remaining expansion: UI configuration, email, richer retry/backoff/dedup policy.
- [x] **Agent `task:delete`** *(shipped 2026-07-10 — see Completed)*: deleting a Windows task from TaskHub now removes the real scheduler entry via a signed agent command, with confirm-before-DB-delete ordering, idempotent stale-row cleanup, and an honest 400 for platforms without delete support.
- [x] **User resources & onboarding** *(shipped 2026-07-13 — see Completed)*: the in-app **Help Center** now leads with a 4-step **Getting Started** walkthrough (connect the agent → import & categorize → run & manage → use & grow templates) and a **Guides & Docs** section linking the real guides ([UI User Guide](user-guides/guides/UI_User_Guide.md), [Agent Setup Guide](user-guides/guides/Agent_Setup_Guide.md)), the [template catalog spec](reports/templates/Templates.md), and the public template registry. A dismissible **first-run banner** ("New to TaskHub? Take the tour") points new users at it, persisted via a new `onboardingSeen` setting. Content lives in `frontend/src/data/onboarding.ts`.
- [x] **Apply modal upgrades** *(shipped 2026-07-10 — see Completed)*: editable task-name field with a server-side duplicate guard (409 instead of silent overwrite), shared cron preset chips, and a human-readable local-time schedule preview; applied tasks are now tracked immediately instead of waiting for a sync.
- [x] **Windows folder selector on apply** *(decided + shipped 2026-07-14 — see Completed)*: a created Windows task always landed in `\TaskHub\` — the agent hardcoded `const string TaskHubFolder = "TaskHub"` and both create paths built `externalId` as `\TaskHub\<name>`. This was an **asymmetry the rest of the code already contradicted**: `TaskService.extractCategory` derives a Windows task's category from its **real root folder**, so an *imported* task categorizes as `Microsoft`/`Work`/whatever it actually lives under, while every *created* task categorized as `TaskHub` forever. For Windows, **category is a projection of the real folder** — so the Apply modal now selects a real folder, and non-Windows platforms keep the local category concept. (The Apply modal had no category field at all, so this was additive.) **Needs an agent republish** to take effect — the running agent has no folder handler and rejects the new signature ([troubleshooting #7](troubleshooting/README.md#7-new-agent-command-502-times-out-until-the-agent-is-republished)).
  - **Boundary — any existing folder except `\Microsoft\` and its descendants** (case-insensitive), plus creating new folders outside it; `\TaskHub\` stays the default. The hardcoded folder is a **safety property, not laziness**: `RegisterTaskDefinition` **silently overwrites** a same-named task in the same folder (already documented in `windowsTaskName.ts`), and the agent runs **elevated** — so a free-text folder would let the dashboard, or an AI via the MCP `create_task_from_template`, clobber a real system task under `\Microsoft\Windows\` with no error. That is the confident-lie failure mode with OS-level consequences. `\Microsoft\` is refused **in the agent as well as the backend** — the agent holds the elevation, so it cannot trust its caller.
  - **The duplicate guard must become folder-aware in the same change.** `windowsTaskName.ts` hardcodes `` target = `\\taskhub\\${name}` ``; with selectable folders it would silently stop matching (create `Backup` in `\Work\` and the tracked `\Work\Backup` is overwritten without a 409). The guard that exists *specifically* to prevent silent overwrite would quietly stop firing — worse than not having it, because the UI would still imply it was protecting you.
  - **Prune stays scoped to `\TaskHub\`.** `TryPruneTaskHubFolder` already refuses any other folder; creating tasks elsewhere must **not** teach it to delete a user's folder. Empty non-TaskHub folders are the user's to keep.
  - Delivered across four layers: agent read-only `task:folders` enumeration + folder-aware `CreateTask` + `TaskFolderPath` guard; backend `windowsTaskFolder.ts` (validate/normalize/join) + `folder` on `POST /api/tasks` and `/api/templates/:id/apply` + `GET /api/tasks/folders`; the Apply-modal selector (existing folders only, honest agent-offline fallback to the default); and per [CLAUDE.md §11a](../CLAUDE.md) the MCP `create_task_from_template` `folder` param + the skill/doc surfaces.
  - **TaskHub creates exactly one folder: its own `\TaskHub`** — the same one it prunes when the last task leaves. Every other folder must already exist; the agent refuses a create into a missing one. Decided 2026-07-14 after a dogfood left three empty folders (`\ManualTest`, `\MicrosoftEdgeBackups`, `\Work`) that had to be deleted by hand: **folder deletion needs elevation**, so a folder TaskHub creates is a one-way door only the user can close. Never create what you cannot remove. (A user's own folder persisting *is* correct — it's theirs to keep — so the fix is to stop creating them, not to start deleting them.)
  - Verified: 230 backend + 70 frontend + 107 agent unit tests, and 11 `task-name` integration tests against real Postgres covering `\Microsoft\` at three casings, traversal, a per-folder collision (409), and the same name in a *different* folder (allowed — `\TaskHub\Backup` and `\Work\Backup` are different Windows tasks). Golden HMAC vectors regenerated from the algorithm and self-checked by first reproducing the previously-committed ones.
  - Incidental fix: the folder `<select>` had a visual label but no accessible name, making it indistinguishable from the template's own parameter combobox — caught by a failing test, fixed with `aria-label`.
- [x] **Template library UI** *(complete 2026-07-10 — see Completed)*: search box, faceted OS + Tags(category) chips, Type toggle, and starter-vs-pattern grouping *(2026-07-08)*; the 4 Tier-B patterns are now parameterized like the starters, and the static **upvotes** signal was **removed** (no more fake vote counts — sort is now starters-then-name). *(Dormant `Template.upvotes` DB column left in place; drop in a future migration.)*
- [x] **Template view selector (Grid / List / Kanban)** *(shipped 2026-07-10 — see Completed)*: the Templates tab has a Grid/List/Kanban switch mirroring the dashboard's, persisted to `settings.templateView`. Kanban lanes are grouped by Category (Tag). Grid keeps the Starters/Use-case-patterns card layout; List is a compact row layout.
- [x] **Template Resources menu** *(shipped 2026-07-10 — see Completed)*: a Templates-tab **Resources** dropdown linking curated sites/repos/docs, backed by a maintainable data file (`frontend/src/data/templateResources.ts`) sourced from `docs/resources/` + the catalog spec — sections for TaskHub docs, scheduler references, AI/agent workflows, and template repositories.
- [x] **Favorite templates** *(shipped 2026-07-11 — see Completed)*: a signed-in user can star/unstar templates from the card, list row, and kanban card, then filter by a **Favorites** toggle (with a live count) and see favorites float to the top of each group. Stored per-user in a new `TemplateFavorite` join (the shared seeded catalog is never mutated) — the honest personalization signal that replaces the removed fake `upvotes`.
- [ ] **Template registry — decouple the catalog from the repo** (architecture direction, decided 2026-07-13): templates are fast-moving *content*; the app is stable *code* — so stop baking the catalog into `seed.ts` + a Postgres reseed and instead serve it from a **decoupled registry** the app fetches at runtime. Two decided pieces:
  - **Format — a versioned, target-agnostic JSON schema** (not XML; XML is only a Windows *compile output*). A template describes the automation abstractly along the **Trigger → Action → Execution Target** model (see the *Template & scheduler catalog* reference appendix at the end of this doc), carrying `schemaVersion`, the existing `{{placeholder}}` `parameters`, an abstract `trigger` (cron/interval/event) and `action` (`{executable, args[]}` / http job), `compatibleTargets`, and `execution` settings (timeout/retries/runLevel). TaskHub **compiles** the abstract template to native config per target via a small compiler-per-target; only the **Windows** compiler is real today (reuses `TriggerBuilder` / structured `ExecAction`), so a declared-but-uncompiled target renders as the honest "copy to set up manually" path (this is what makes the *cosmetic-targets* item real). A template is never "a Windows job" or "a cron job" — the target is chosen at apply time.
  - **Distribution — a static JSON registry first, API/admin later.** MVP is a separate content repo (`index.json` + `templates/*.json`) hosted as static files (GitHub Pages / CDN / the Hetzner VPS); the catalog updates by pushing to that repo, never touching the app. The app fetches the index on load, **caches it, and falls back to a bundled snapshot** so first-run/offline still works. Upgrade to a DB-backed API + admin/submission UI behind the same fetch contract only if/when community contributions or non-git editing are wanted. **Security:** fetched templates are *untrusted, executable* content — validated against the schema exactly like user-submitted input, integrity-checked (checksums/signing on the index), never shell-wrapped; the existing server-side no-shell `{executable, args[]}` structuring + per-parameter validation already caps a hostile template parameter at a malformed arg to the *intended* exe. **Guardrail-safe:** the registry lets the *catalog* scale to hundreds of templates without the *code* growing — execution targets still unlock only when reliable (per the reliability-control-plane guardrail). Sequenced before / underneath import-export, save-as-template, and the Developer/AI packs (they all target this schema). Progress: **(1) schema + ADR ✅** (`docs/reports/templates/Registry_Schema_v1.md`, `docs/adr/0001-…`, real templates compiled to Windows); **(2) catalog behind an interface ✅** (`backend/src/catalog/` — v1 Zod schema, bundled snapshot, normalizer, `TemplateCatalogSource`; `seed.ts` is now the materializer; no behavior change); **(3) static registry + remote source ✅** (`npm run registry:build` emits `registry/index.json` + `templates/*.json` with `sha256`; `RegistryCatalogSource` fetches + integrity-checks + caches, falls back to bundled; wired via `TEMPLATE_REGISTRY_URL`; drift test guards the artifact). **(4) hosted ✅** (2026-07-13): the artifact is mirrored to a separate public repo **[`michaelschecht/taskhub-registry`](https://github.com/michaelschecht/taskhub-registry)** served via GitHub Pages at **`https://mikesailab.com/taskhub-registry`** (set as `TEMPLATE_REGISTRY_URL`); `scripts/publish-registry.ps1` mirrors `registry/` → the public repo, and the live seed was verified sourcing from it (checksums matched over the CDN, no fallback). **Runtime refresh ✅** (2026-07-13): the backend syncs the catalog from the source into the DB on boot and, when pointed at a registry, on an interval (`catalog/catalogSync.ts`, default 30 min, `TEMPLATE_REGISTRY_SYNC_INTERVAL_MS`), so a registry change lands with no reseed/redeploy — upsert-by-id, favorites untouched; verified live (a modified registry propagated a template rename into the DB, then restored). **Prune-on-sync ✅** (2026-07-14): `catalogSync` now reconciles the DB to the current **core** set — auto-synced templates carry a `managed: true` flag (new `Template.managed` column, migration `20260714200000_add_template_managed` backfills existing rows), and after upserting core it `deleteMany`s managed rows outside the core set, so an existing install converges to the curated default (not just fresh ones) and a demoted/removed template disappears without a reseed. Import-safe: imported / saved-as-template rows are `managed: false` and never pruned; guarded against an empty core so a bad sync can't wipe the catalog. Verified live: the running instance's catalog dropped 55 → 5 on the next sync (`pruned 50 stale managed`). Remaining follow-up (optional): **index signing** (beyond per-file checksums); keep exactly one real compiler (Windows).
- [x] **Template import/export + agent authoring** *(shipped 2026-07-13 — see Completed)*: users (and agents) can now grow/edit the catalog without a reseed — `GET /api/templates/export` (whole catalog / `?ids=` subset / `?id=` single) lowers DB rows to Registry v1 JSON via a new `denormalize.ts` (inverse of `normalize.ts`), and `POST /api/templates/import` (bundle / array / single) schema-validates + runs the same Apply `{{placeholder}}` resolvability check + upserts-by-id, reporting bad entries per-item. Header Export/Import buttons in the Templates tab. Because import targets the v1 schema, an agent can be pointed at it to draft/bulk-edit templates. Pairs with **Save task as template** (below), the still-open way to grow the catalog *from a real task*.
- [x] **Export existing tasks** (Windows → XML, native → JSON) *(shipped 2026-07-13 — see Completed)*: per-task **Export** in the task modal via `GET /api/tasks/:id/export`. A **Windows** task exports as its native Task Scheduler **XML** — a new read-only `task:export` agent round-trip returns `Task.Xml` (identical to `Export-ScheduledTask`), delivered UTF-16 LE + BOM so it re-imports through every Windows path. A TaskHub-**native** task exports as TaskHub **JSON** (`{ taskhubTaskVersion, exportedAt, task: { name, platform, category, schedule, job } }`) straight from the DB. Granularity decided: **per-task** now; a whole-account/multi-select bulk action (needs a zip for mixed XML+JSON) is deferred. *(Asymmetry vs. the template registry, as noted: templates are JSON; a Windows task export is XML because that's the platform-native format.)*
- [x] **Cross-platform template targets are cosmetic** *(honesty — "no fake data" guardrail; approach (a) shipped 2026-07-10 — see Completed)*: template cards now frame `targetPlatforms` under a **"Compatible with"** label (creatable platforms highlighted, others muted with a `*`), and the Apply modal gates the platform picker to the platforms TaskHub can actually create on (`CREATABLE_PLATFORMS` = Windows + TaskHub-native) — uncreatable targets are disabled with an honest "no agent or API yet" note instead of a silent failure. Also removed the fake cross-platform target from the DB Backup pattern. Follow-up (b) — real **export** artifacts (cron line / launchd plist / Claude routine payload) — stays open, to land as connectors and the macOS agent do; the **template-registry** compiler-per-target model is the mechanism (a `compatibleTargets` entry becomes real when its compiler exists, honest "manual" until then).
- [x] **Tags model** *(shipped 2026-07-13 — the shared prerequisite for the two template packs below; see Completed)*: templates now carry **free-form `tags` (`String[]`)** persisted on the `Template` model — distinct from the single `category` enum, so they're discoverable without overloading `TemplateCategory`. Tags flow through the whole pipeline (registry → `normalize` → DB → `denormalize`/export, round-trip verified) and are surfaced in the Templates tab as a dedicated **Tags** facet + per-card chips (the old mislabeled "Tags" chip row is now correctly "Category").
- [x] **Developer Pack templates** *(shipped 2026-07-13 — see Completed)*: 9 development-focused use-case templates added to the bundled catalog + registry (git hygiene ×3, npm dependency-check/build/test ×3, .NET build, Docker prune + compose self-heal), every one tagged `dev` plus specifics (`git`, `build`, `test`, `docker`, `ci`, …) via the tags model. New entries use the plain-kebab registry id form (`dev-*`); a new whole-catalog resolvability test guards every bundled `commandTemplate` through the Apply substitution pipeline. Registry artifact regenerated and **published** to the public `taskhub-registry` repo (hosted index verified serving all 33 templates).
- [x] **AI / LLM / CLI agent templates** *(Claude Code slice shipped 2026-07-13; Codex slice shipped 2026-07-13 — see Completed)*: the gate ("a safe non-interactive execution path — no interactive prompts, scoped permissions, captured output") is now cleared for **Claude Code** and **Codex**. Claude Code uses headless print mode (`claude -p`), `--permission-mode dontAsk`, a user-scoped `--allowedTools` allowlist, `--bare`, and captured output. Codex uses `codex --ask-for-approval never exec`, explicit `--sandbox` (`read-only` by default; `workspace-write` only for the higher-trust autofix template), `--ephemeral`, `--color never`, `-C`, `-o`, and captured logs. Shipped as 7 real, creatable Windows templates total: 4 Claude Code + 3 Codex. Remaining future expansion: LLM report/digest jobs on other providers.
- [x] **Save task as template** *(shipped 2026-07-13 — see Completed)*: a **Save as template** button in the task modal turns a real Windows/native task into a catalog template via `POST /api/tasks/:id/save-as-template` (`templateFromTask.ts` derives a v1 template from the task's command + schedule, then runs it through the same import pipeline). The second non-reseed authoring path alongside import.
- [x] **Settings runtime backend-URL override** *(shipped 2026-07-13 — see Completed)*: Settings → About → API origin is editable; `VITE_API_URL` is the default, a browser-local override can point REST + `/ui` live updates at another backend without rebuild, and Reset returns to the env default.
- [x] **On-demand elevated agent republish** *(dev tooling, shipped 2026-07-14)*: the agent never hot-reloads and runs at RunLevel Highest, so every agent change needed a hand-opened Administrator prompt. That friction is the *cause* of [troubleshooting #7](troubleshooting/README.md#7-new-agent-command-502-times-out-until-the-agent-is-republished) — a republish you have to remember, elevate for, and run by hand is a republish that gets skipped, and then you debug a stale agent. Now a **no-trigger** `\Task-Hub\TaskHubRepublish` task at RunLevel Highest runs `scripts/Republish-Agent.ps1`, registered once via `scripts/startup-task/Register-RepublishTask.ps1`; afterwards `Start-ScheduledTask` republishes from any prompt with no elevation. Dev-only (no installer registers it), logged to `%TEMP%\taskhub-republish.log`, refuses honestly when unelevated, and warns when the dll timestamp doesn't advance. Security tradeoff documented in-script and in #7: it *is* an elevation entry point, but it runs one fixed repo script and `\Task-Hub\TaskHubStack` already runs `taskhub.ps1` elevated on a recurring trigger — so it adds no capability that writing to this repo didn't already grant.
- [ ] **Settings agent-pairing panel**: deferred until the Go-public account system and per-user pairing-code flow exist; today's single shared `AGENT_PAIRING_SECRET` is intentionally not exposed as a casual UI setting.
- [ ] Native tasks follow-ups: `CLAUDE_PROMPT` job type and Redis lock before multi-instance. *(Edit schedule shipped 2026-07-13.)*
- [x] **PowerShell console flashes on screen every few minutes** *(fixed 2026-07-13 — see Completed)*: the flash came from `powershell.exe` (conhost created then hidden) firing on both overlapping self-heal tasks. Fix: a new `scripts/startup-task/run-hidden.vbs` launches PowerShell hidden-from-creation via `WScript.Shell.Run(cmd, 0, False)` (verified live — no window), and `Register-TaskHubStack.ps1` points both tasks at it while deduping them (TaskHubStack = the one recurring self-heal; TaskHubAgent = flash-free logon-only bootstrap). `Set-TaskHubRepetition.ps1` deprecated. Apply to a live install with one elevated re-run of `Register-TaskHubStack.ps1`.
- [x] **Empty `\TaskHub\` scheduler folder auto-pruned** *(fixed 2026-07-13 — see Completed)*: (1) The lingering empty `\TaskHub\` folder is now removed by the agent when its last task is deleted (`Win32TaskScheduler.TryPruneTaskHubFolder`, guarded to our folder + only-when-empty + best-effort), verified live against real Task Scheduler; the folder stays lazily created. (2) The near-duplicate `\Task-Hub\` (infra) vs `\TaskHub\` (user tasks) folder-name consolidation is **deferred** — a rename in either direction needs either a large backend/test/frontend blast radius (the user-tasks folder is referenced across `externalId` matching, the name guard, tests, and UI copy) or coordinated elevated re-registration of the live infra tasks. The auto-prune makes the overlap transient (`\TaskHub\` only exists while user tasks do), and the two folders' distinct roles are documented in the agent code + scripts README.

#### 🟢 P3 — Expansion

> **Launch posture (decided 2026-07-13): TaskHub ships and stays a local-first application.** Cloud/SaaS hosting is **not** a launch requirement — the earlier "host the backend + frontend on a Hetzner VPS → a hosted control plane" plan is **dropped as the default direction**. Remote access (reaching your own local instance from other devices) is delivered as *documentation + optional tooling* the user self-hosts, and is sequenced as the **final, optional enhancement** at the bottom of this list. Go-live does not depend on it.

- [x] **Bulk-clear MISSING tasks** *(logged **and shipped** 2026-07-25 — see CHANGELOG › Added)*: `DELETE /api/tasks/missing` + a "Clear N Missing" button that only exists when N > 0. Notes worth keeping: the route is registered **above `DELETE /:id`** because Express matches in declaration order and the parameterised route would otherwise swallow `missing` as a task id and 404 — a route that silently never runs; it deliberately makes **no platform call**, since MISSING means the platform already reported the task gone; and the `where` is guarded against a stale generated client, because `TaskStatus.MISSING === undefined` in a **`where`** clause is *ignored* by Prisma and would widen to every task the user owns — the inverse of [#22](troubleshooting/README.md#22-deleted-a-windows-task-synced-and-cronsole-still-shows-it--while-reporting-missing-n), which under-wrote a field where this would over-delete rows. 5 integration tests against real Postgres, mutation-tested against both a dropped status filter and a dropped owner scope. Original rationale: a single sync can flag **56 tasks MISSING at once** (delete a Task Scheduler folder and every task under it goes at once — the normal case, not an edge case), but clearing them is **per-task only**: 55 modal open → confirm → close cycles. The asymmetry is the bug — the feature that creates the mess in bulk offers no way to resolve it in bulk. `MISSING` is also the one status where a delete is unambiguously safe, since the platform entry is already gone (verified: `DELETE /api/tasks/:id` on a MISSING row returns 200 and the agent's `task:delete` doesn't object to an absent task), so this needs no new confirmation semantics — just a "clear all MISSING" action scoped to `status: MISSING`, plus ideally a filter/section so they can be reviewed before clearing. Pairs naturally with the un-imported-tasks signal below: both are about the dashboard telling you what state it's actually in.
- [x] **Surface that un-imported tasks exist** *(logged 2026-07-25; **shipped 2026-07-27** — see CHANGELOG › Added)*: `POST /api/tasks/sync` now returns `untracked: { count, folders, systemCount }` per platform (new `TaskService.summarizeUntracked`), and the Sync Now toast names the remainder — *"Synced. 26 tasks in 2 folders aren't imported — use Import to add them."* Built exactly as scoped: **no new machinery and no extra agent round-trip**, since it reads the same full pre-filter enumeration reconciliation already computes. **The one decision the logged item didn't anticipate: OS-owned tasks must be counted separately.** Live on a real machine the split is **95 user tasks across 13 folders vs 257 `\Microsoft\` tasks** — folding those in would pin the message at "352" permanently, and **a warning that never changes is one you stop reading**, which would have re-created the very invisibility this fixes. Excluding them *silently* would be the opposite failure, so `systemCount` is surfaced, just not counted. Also deliberately **not gated behind `toastOnSuccess`**: that setting suppresses routine "it worked" noise, and this is its inverse — the one thing the sync did *not* do. **Verified twice against the real thing**: arithmetically (empty include-set on the real 352-task machine — 95 + 257 = 352 exactly accounts for the enumeration, probe mutated nothing), then by **reproducing #20 for real** — a natively-created `\Testing-Import\` folder with one task, which `/discover` saw, Sync Now still correctly refused to import (`count: 352`, unchanged), and which the response now names (`untracked: {count: 1, folders: ["Testing-Import"]}`) where it previously returned only `{count: 352, missing: 0}`. The toast was then **watched rendering in a real browser** — *"Synced. 1 task in 1 folder isn't imported — use Import to add it."*, the singular grammar path, identical to the test that pins it. 6 backend + 7 frontend tests, all mutation-tested. *(Original rationale below.)* The dashboard gave **no signal at all** that tasks exist in folders you haven't imported. A user created tasks in `\IAM\` and `\Edge-Radar-MikesAILab\`, pressed **Sync Now** repeatedly, and got a cheerful `Tasks synced.` each time while 26 tasks sat one fence away — `/discover` had been reporting them the whole time. The fence is correct (selective import is the design); its **invisibility** is the defect, and it cost a full debugging session to establish that nothing was broken. Cheapest honest fix: after a `scope: 'tracked'` sync, compare the agent's full enumeration against the tracked set and, if the remainder is non-zero, surface it — *"N tasks in 2 folders you haven't imported — open Import"*. The data is already in hand (`allExternalIds` is computed unfiltered for reconciliation), so this is a response field + a toast/banner, not new machinery. Sequenced P3 because the workaround (press Import) is one click **once you know**; the whole cost is not knowing.
- [x] **MCP server** *(shipped 2026-07-13 — see Completed)*: `list_tasks` / `run_task` / `list_templates` / `create_task_from_template` / `convert_schedule` as a thin stdio wrapper over the REST API (`mcp-server/`), so an MCP host (Claude Code / Claude Desktop / Codex / Cursor) can list, run, and create tasks in natural language. Remaining optional expansion is now tracked as its own item below (**MCP surface expansion**); per-user auth still waits on the Go-public pairing flow.

- [x] **MCP surface expansion — direct task creation + management, paired with the skill** *(logged 2026-07-15; **complete 2026-07-15** — `create_task` + the skill guide, then `list_folders`, then the seven management verbs; surface is now **14 tools**. The three open questions are settled — see below.)*: today the MCP surface can only **create from a template**, which makes the catalog a gate on a capability the app itself doesn't gate — the dashboard has had a free-form Create Task since the MVP. An agent asked to "schedule this script nightly" has to either find a template that happens to fit or give up, and `create_task_from_template` with `tpl_starter_powershell_script` is a workaround, not an answer. **Demonstrated, not theorized** (2026-07-15): asked over MCP for a task that "just launches a PowerShell app", the only available path was to route through `tpl_starter_powershell_script` — whose `commandTemplate` is `-File "{{scriptPath}}"` — and therefore to *invent a `.ps1` for it to point at*, because the template cannot express "run this command" without a script file. The task registered and ran correctly (`LastTaskResult: 0`), but the shape of the request had to be bent to fit the catalog. That is the gap in one sentence: **the template is the wrong unit when the user already knows the command.** Two halves, deliberately paired:

  **(a) The tools.** The decisive finding from the route survey: **every tool below wraps a route that already exists**, so this is wrapper + schema + docs work with **no new backend logic** — which is exactly what §11a demands, since a tool that needs new behavior needs a *backend route*, not cleverness in `mcp-server/`.

  | Proposed tool | Existing route | Notes |
  |:---|:---|:---|
  | ✅ `create_task` | `POST /api/tasks` (`tasks.ts:348`) | **Shipped 2026-07-15.** Free-form task from `{name, command, schedule, platform, category, folder}`; no-shell structuring stays server-side. Verified live: direct exec (no `cmd.exe /c`), correct UTC→local trigger, ran to completion per Windows' `LastTaskResult` + a real side effect. Shipped with a `folder` param that had no honest way to be filled until `list_folders` landed the same day. |
  | ✅ `create_native_task` | `POST /api/tasks/native` (`tasks.ts:454`) | **Shipped 2026-07-15.** Kept **separate** rather than folded into `create_task` behind `platform`: this route takes a full job spec (method/headers/body) where `POST /api/tasks` takes a command *string* and the connector only builds an HTTP GET from it. Folding them would mean either a `create_task` that silently ignores half its inputs on one platform, or wrapper logic choosing routes — additive tool, no new logic. |
  | ✅ `update_task_schedule` | `PATCH /api/tasks/:id/schedule` (`:143`) | **Shipped 2026-07-15.** Re-schedule without delete+recreate. Description carries the #14 warning: read the returned trigger, not the score. |
  | ✅ `update_task_action` | `PATCH /api/tasks/:id/actions` (`:235`) | **Shipped 2026-07-15.** Command / working dir / run level. **Replaces** rather than patches (the route's zod schema makes `command` + `runLevel` required), so the tool says so — omitting `runLevel` would silently reset a task's elevation. |
  | ✅ `set_task_status` | `PATCH /api/tasks/:id/status` (`:88`) | **Shipped 2026-07-15, ungated on purpose.** Enable / disable — **the honest way to park a task** (see the #14 trap: don't encode "don't run" in the cron). Gating this would make the safe path harder than the unsafe one. |
  | ✅ `delete_task` | `DELETE /api/tasks/:id` (`:683`) | **Shipped 2026-07-15, gated** behind `TASKHUB_MCP_ALLOW_DESTRUCTIVE`; **absent** from `tools/list` when off. Already platform-confirmed before the DB row goes. |
  | ✅ `list_folders` | `GET /api/tasks/folders` (`:504`) | **Shipped 2026-07-15** — sequenced first of the remaining tools because it's **read-only**, so it raises none of the gating question below. Was arguably a prerequisite for `create_task` rather than a follow-up: without it, `folder` was a parameter an agent had no honest way to fill. Preserves the route's two honesty behaviors instead of re-deciding them in the wrapper — unwritable folders are **listed** (`\Microsoft\…` as *NOT writable*; an omission reads as "doesn't exist"), and `defaultFolder` is reported **separately** because `\TaskHub` is lazily created and **pruned when empty**, so it's routinely absent from a real listing (found by driving it live, not by reasoning). |
  | ✅ `get_task_history` | `GET /api/tasks/:id/executions` (`:722`) | **Shipped 2026-07-15.** "Did last night's job work?" Capped at the route's 20. Says an empty history means *TaskHub has no record*, **not** "it never ran" (a Windows task on its own trigger is recorded by Windows), and warns that `SUCCESS` is what a hung task reports too (#12). |
  | ✅ `export_task` | `GET /api/tasks/:id/export` (`:783`) | **Shipped 2026-07-15.** Windows → native XML; native → JSON. Needed a **raw-bytes client path**: the route delivers UTF-16 LE + BOM (the only encoding Windows re-imports), which axios would have decoded as UTF-8 into mojibake. The tool decodes it and tells the caller it must be *saved back* as UTF-16. |

  **The three open questions are now settled** *(decided 2026-07-15)*. They were the real work; the wrappers are mechanical.

  **(1) Destructive-op gating → tier it, and gate only the irreversible verb.** Read-only (`get_task_history`, `export_task`) and *reversible* verbs (`set_task_status`, `update_task_schedule`, `update_task_action`) register unconditionally. **`delete_task` registers only when `TASKHUB_MCP_ALLOW_DESTRUCTIVE=true`** — and when it's off the tool is **absent from `tools/list`**, not present-and-erroring, so the model never plans around a capability it doesn't have.

  The reasoning that picked env over the alternatives: **a `confirm: true` parameter is not a gate**, because the model fills it in itself — it's the caller asserting to itself that it's sure, which is exactly the deliberation that's missing. An env var is **out-of-band**: the human sets it, and no amount of agent reasoning reaches it. That's the same posture the agent already takes toward *its* caller (`\Microsoft\` is refused **in the agent** as well as the backend, because the agent holds the elevation and cannot trust who's asking) — the MCP server is one more untrusted caller, so the gate belongs somewhere the caller can't touch.

  Gating *every* mutating verb was rejected for conflating **irreversible** with **reversible**: `set_task_status` is the honest way to park a task, and gating it pushes an agent toward the worse workaround of encoding "don't run" in the cron — [#14](troubleshooting/README.md#14-a-rare-cron-becomes-an-hourly-trigger) exactly. A gate that makes the safe path harder than the unsafe one is worse than no gate.

  **(2) Tool-count creep → one tool per verb** (7 → 14; `create_native_task` is additive, not a fold-in of `create_task`, since `POST /tasks/native` takes a full job spec where `POST /tasks` takes a command string). Collapsing the three `update_*` into one mode-flagged tool saves ~2 tools and costs the thing that makes these tools *safe*: a mode-flagged tool needs one description covering every mode, so the per-verb warnings dilute precisely where they matter most. **The descriptions are the safety mechanism** — an agent reads them, not this roadmap. Revisit if host context pressure ever makes the count a real problem.

  **(3) Low-confidence schedules → fix the warning, don't refuse in the wrapper.** Refusing below a threshold would put a policy decision in `mcp-server/`, which §11a forbids and which would protect only MCP callers while the UI and Apply kept the same silent replacement. The **backend fix** (the *fallback warning names the frequency* item below) fixes it once for every caller. `create_task`'s description already tells the model to read the returned trigger rather than the score.

  **(b) The skill must teach it. ✅ Shipped 2026-07-15** — [`skills/cronsole/references/task-authoring.md`](../skills/cronsole/references/task-authoring.md), in the same change as `create_task`, per the mirror-surface rule. The two AI surfaces are complements, not duplicates: the MCP server is the *hands*, the skill is the *judgment*. Shipping (a) without (b) gives an agent a `create_task` tool and no idea that schedules are **5-field cron in UTC**, that `\Microsoft\` is refused, that a folder must **already exist**, that a `0.7` confidence means the schedule was *replaced* rather than nudged, or that a green suite is not evidence a command runs — it would confidently create wrong tasks, the exact failure mode §11a exists to prevent. The guide covers the six creation paths and when each is right, command recipes per kind of work (script / direct exe / explicit shell opt-in / HTTP / npm / Python / Docker / unattended CLI agent), quoting, folders, the disable-don't-cron rule, an MCP-vs-REST capability table, and how to verify from **outside** TaskHub. **Extend it as the tools below land** — the table in §5 is the honest record of what MCP can't do yet and must not go stale.

- [x] **`convert_schedule` fallback warning names the resulting frequency** *(logged 2026-07-15; **shipped 2026-07-15** — see Completed)*: an unrecognized cron falls back to a **hard-coded** `PT1H`/`P1D` trigger (`scheduler-conversion.ts`) **not derived from the input at all** — discarded, not approximated. The old warning ("execution times might not align 100%") described *drift*, so `0 4 1 1 *` → 1 run/year becoming **8,760 runs/year** read as a rounding error at `confidence: 0.7`. Decided (of the three options logged): **say it plainly**, and do it in the **backend** so it protects every caller — UI, Apply, and MCP — rather than special-casing the MCP wrapper (which §11a forbids: a wrapper that patches over a backend message has started owning logic). The warning now says **REPLACED — not approximated**, names the frequency (~8,760 runs/year), states the never-less asymmetry, and points at disable-don't-cron. Verified live through the running backend and out through `convert_schedule`.
  - **Residual, deliberately left and now documented:** the score stays `0.7` — the *same* value as a genuinely-approximate `*/7` step, whose trigger *is* derived from the input. Wording distinguishes them; the number doesn't, so `>= 0.7` still accepts a replacement. Splitting the score is a separate call (it would change every existing caller's threshold semantics); a test now pins that the step path keeps its **drift** language and the fallback keeps **REPLACED**, so a future edit can't quietly merge the two registers. **Tracked as its own open item below — *Split the `0.7` confidence score*.** [#14](troubleshooting/README.md#14-a-rare-cron-becomes-an-hourly-trigger).

- [x] **Signed agent commands carry a nonce — a legitimate re-send inside one second is no longer dropped as a replay** *(found 2026-07-15 while verifying the MCP management verbs; **shipped 2026-07-15** — see Completed; [troubleshooting #16](troubleshooting/README.md#16-a-second-identical-agent-command-within-one-second-is-dropped))*: `ts` is **second-granular** and the signature covers `(message, ts)`, so two identical commands to the same task inside one second are **byte-identical** — indistinguishable from a captured packet replayed. The agent's replay guard drops the second, silently and *correctly*; the backend then times out after 15s and reports `Agent trigger timeout`, blaming the transport. Reproduced cleanly: run #1 39ms OK, run #2 (+0.3s) 15,032ms timeout, run #3 (+1.5s) 21ms OK — same task, only the clock changed.
  - **User-visible today**: double-clicking **Run Now**, or any script/agent driving two runs quickly, eats a 15s hang and a misleading error.
  - **The fix is uniqueness, not chattiness.** Add a **nonce** to the signed message so a legitimate re-send is never byte-identical, while a true replay (same nonce ⇒ same signature) is still caught. **Do not "fix" it by making the agent answer a rejected command** — the silence on an unverifiable command is a deliberate security property; the bug is that a legitimate command can *look* unverifiable.
  - Scope: a **protocol change across both languages** — backend `emitSignedCommand`, the C# `AgentAuthenticator` message builders, and the **cross-language golden HMAC vectors** move together, plus an agent republish.
