  - **Design decided 2026-07-15 (before implementing):** the nonce goes **inside the signed message**, in a fixed position immediately **before `ts`**, for all six commands — so it is covered by the signature and cannot be stripped or rewritten in flight by an on-path attacker (the same reason `folder` and `trigger` are signed). Generated per-emit in `emitSignedCommand` (16 random bytes, hex), passed to `signCommand` as a parameter exactly as `ts` already is, so the golden vectors stay deterministic.
  - **The agent's replay cache does not change.** It is keyed on `{ts}|{sig}`, and once the nonce is in the message the **signature is already unique per command instance** — two legitimate runs differ, a replayed frame is byte-identical and still collides. The enforcement that a nonce always exists lives in the `*Message` helpers, which take it as a **required** parameter: you cannot build a signed message without one.
  - **No back-compat shim.** Backend and agent ship together (a mismatched message string rejects every command, by design), so an optional nonce would create two valid message forms — weakening the guarantee and doubling the test surface — to serve a version skew the project doesn't support. A skewed agent fails closed and loudly.

- [x] **Split the `0.7` confidence score — it means two different things** *(logged 2026-07-15; **shipped 2026-07-16**)*: `convertCronToWindowsTrigger` returned **`0.7` for both** *"approximated"* (a `*/7` step — the trigger **is** derived from your input and merely drifts) and *"replaced"* (an unrecognized cron — the input is **discarded** for a hard-coded hourly trigger). Those are not the same risk, and a caller thresholding on `>= 0.7` accepts both. Of the two options logged, took the **structured discriminator** (not a lower score): `ConversionResult` now carries **`lossy: 'approximated' | 'replaced' | undefined`** (undefined on an exact conversion or a null-trigger refusal), surfaced on `POST /api/tasks/preview`, `POST /api/templates/:id/preview`, and both create responses. The MCP `convert_schedule` renders it as a distinct line and `create_task` leads its warning with the actionable verb (*replaced* → delete and re-create; *approximated* → honored but drifts). The **score itself is deliberately left at `0.7`** — changing it would move threshold semantics for every existing caller (UI live-warning, Apply, `convert_schedule`, `create_task`), so `lossy` is the honest machine-readable half without re-tuning a magic number. Backend-only logic + presentation in the wrapper (no new wrapper logic, per §11a). Tests: backend (exact/approximated/replaced/refusal registers pinned) + MCP (both registers rendered distinctly), mutation-checked. ([#14](troubleshooting/README.md#14-a-rare-cron-becomes-an-hourly-trigger))

- [x] **Reconcile tracked tasks that no longer exist on the platform** *(observed live 2026-07-15; **shipped 2026-07-16**)*: two DB rows (`TestingMCP`, `MCP Test - Webhook Ping`) sat at `status: ACTIVE` with a `nextRunTime` while **absent from Task Scheduler** — deleted out-of-band when `\TaskHub` was pruned. Harmless in effect, but the dashboard **stated as fact** that a task was active and due — the confident-lie register §9 exists to prevent, aimed at the user this time.
  - **Decided: stop hard-deleting; flip absent tasks to a new `MISSING` status instead.** `TaskService.removeStaleTasks` → `reconcileMissingTasks`: an absent task is marked `MISSING` with `nextRunTime` cleared, and the row + logs are **kept**. Rationale as logged — a task missing from one sync is **not** proof it's gone (an offline agent, or an ACL'd folder the agent can't read, looks identical from here), so deleting the user's task on that evidence is worse than showing it honestly. New `TaskStatus.MISSING` + migration `20260716120000_add_task_status_missing`.
  - **Self-healing + honest:** the sync upserts before it reconciles, so a task that reappears is restored to ACTIVE/DISABLED automatically; a MISSING task the user truly wants gone is removed by the explicit `DELETE /api/tasks/:id` (the only delete path now). The partial-snapshot guard is kept (a suspicious tiny snapshot flips nothing). UI: amber dot distinct from user-`DISABLED`, and the modal shows an honest note instead of an Enable/Disable toggle that would 502. MCP: `list_tasks` gains a `MISSING` filter; `set_task_status` refuses it (system-derived).
  - **On why those two survived the old guard:** reconciliation only ever ran on a `/sync`, and hard-delete gave no trace, so a row simply lingered until a qualifying sync — which the mark-not-delete model makes moot (the honest end state is a durable, visible, self-healing status rather than a race between a delete and a sync). Verified against real Postgres 16 (migration applies; kept-not-deleted + self-heal + cleared-nextRunTime integration tests green). Honest residual: MISSING appears on the *next* sync, not instantly, and a genuinely-gone task stays MISSING until the user removes it (no auto-cleanup, by design — never delete a user's row on inference).

- [x] **Sweep for literal control bytes in source** *(found incidentally 2026-07-15; **swept + guarded 2026-07-16**)*: `backend/src/ws/agentAuth.ts` held a **literal NUL byte** as the handshake cache-key separator, making git classify the file **binary** so every diff of it was unreviewable, including a security-relevant one (fixed earlier — swapped to `\x00`). The repo-wide sweep (all 550 tracked files) found **two more**, both raw bytes pasted where they're described: `mcp-server/src/__tests__/tools.test.ts` (NULs — one in a comment, one *inside* a live `toMatch(/␀/)` regex asserting the decoded UTF-16 export contains no NUL) and `agent/TaskHub.Agent.Tests/AgentAuthenticatorTests.cs` (two `0x1f` Unit-Separator bytes in a comment showing the canonical-action format). Both fixed to escapes (identical runtime string, readable diff). Guard: **`scripts/check-control-bytes.mjs`** scans every tracked text file (skipping binary extensions + an explicit allowlist for the two UTF-16 Task Scheduler XML exports) and fails on any control byte but TAB/LF/CR; wired into CI as a new `repo-hygiene` job. Mutation-checked (passes clean, fails on an injected NUL). The failure mode is silent and only bites the reviewer, which is why it survived — now it can't come back.
- [~] **Template gallery site + selective-import distribution** (part 1 — the gallery frontend — shipped 2026-07-14; see Completed): shift the template distribution model from *"the local app auto-syncs the entire registry into its DB"* (current — right for the ~24-template MVP) to *"a small curated **core** ships/syncs locally, and the **majority** of templates live in a browsable **gallery website** that users pick from and import on demand."* The gallery is a static site built over the existing public **`taskhub-registry`** artifact — render `index.json` as a searchable/filterable card catalog (by category/OS/tag/runtime), each card linking to its `templates/<id>.json`, with a **Download** (or one-click **"Add to my TaskHub"**) that flows into the **import** path already shipped (P2 Template import/export is the foundation — a downloaded template JSON imports through the same schema + `{{placeholder}}` validation). Rationale: once the catalog grows to hundreds of templates (the Developer Pack, AI/CLI packs, and the full taxonomy in the Appendix), pushing all of them into every user's local DB by default is noise — a gallery with **selective import** is the right model at scale, and it's the natural home for community-contributed templates. Work involved: **(1) the gallery frontend over `index.json` ✅** (2026-07-14) — a self-contained, **hash-routed multi-view** `registry-site/index.html` (inline CSS + vanilla JS, no build/CDN): a **Home** (hero + live stats + curated collections + featured + how-to-import), a **Browse** view with a real facet rail (checkbox Category/Platform/Runtime with cross-faceted counts, tag cloud, search, sort, active pills, mobile drawer), a **Collections** index, and shareable **per-template pages** (`#/t/<id>`: humanized schedule + placeholder-highlighted command + parameter table + honest creatable-vs-manual targets + related templates), with **Download** / **Copy JSON** flowing into the shipped Import path; mirrored to the public repo root by `scripts/publish-registry.ps1` (served at `https://mikesailab.com/taskhub-registry/`). **(2) split the catalog into a curated core (bundled/auto-synced) vs. the extended set (gallery-only) ✅** (2026-07-14) — an optional `core` flag on the Registry v1 template (`schema.ts`); `catalogSync` now auto-syncs **only `core: true`** into the DB (intersect `listRaw()` core ids with the normalized `list()`), so a fresh install gets a small curated default and the long tail is import-only. The registry/gallery still carries everything (built from the full `bundledCatalog`); the gallery reads `core` from `index.json` and surfaces it as an **Availability** facet (Built-in / Import on demand) + a "Built-in" card badge. **Also grew the registry**: added a 15-template **Extended Pack** (`bkp-`/`cln-`/`sys-`/`mon-`/`data-`/`ntf-` — folder zip & robocopy mirror, old-file/recycle-bin cleanup, service restart, DNS flush, update scan, battery report, disk/ping/service monitors, event-log export, rclone cloud sync, Discord + log heartbeats), all Windows-creatable and covered by the resolvability sweep. Catalog is now **55 templates: 5 core (auto-synced) + 50 extended (gallery/import-only)** — the built-in core is a deliberately tiny sampler (PowerShell Script, Daily DB Backup, Weekly System Cleanup, Git Fetch & Prune, Webhook Ping — one example across a few common use cases), and the app's **Templates tab shows a banner linking to the gallery** ("browse the full catalog, import what you want") so the trimmed default doesn't feel empty. No DB migration — `normalize.ts` whitelists DB fields, so `core` never reaches Prisma. **(3) optional deep-link/protocol handoff** so "Add to my TaskHub" hands the template straight to a running local app instead of a manual file download+Import — still open (Download/Copy uses the shipped import path today). The two models coexist: core auto-syncs; everything else is opt-in from the gallery. **(4) whole-pack download ✅** (2026-07-28) — the gallery could only hand you one template at a time, so importing a themed set meant N downloads and N imports. Packs became a **first-class registry concept**: an optional `packs[]` in `index.json` plus a content-addressed `registry/packs/<id>.json` bundle each, in the exact shape `POST /api/templates/import` already accepts (**no backend change**). The decisive change is that membership is now **declared** (`backend/src/catalog/packs.ts`) rather than derived from tag predicates buried in the gallery's JS — deriving meant tagging an unrelated template silently changed what a pack contained, which stops being cosmetic the moment a pack is a file that lands in someone's catalog; `buildRegistry` now throws on an unknown or duplicated id. `packs` is optional in both directions so it shipped without a coordinated release, and bundles carry no timestamp (a clock would break content-addressing). Added a sixth pack, **System & Utilities**, because eight templates otherwise belonged to no pack and "download every pack" would have been quietly less than the catalog — coverage is now 55/55 and asserted. Verified live: the browser-downloaded file is byte-identical to the published bundle and imported untouched (11 total / 10 created / 1 updated / 0 errors), then reverted. **(5) gallery search + filters ✅** (2026-07-28) — search moved out of the collapsed filter rail into the **header on every view** (`/` to focus, Escape to clear, typing anywhere jumps to filtered Browse; the input sits outside `#app` because `route()` replaces that innerHTML and would eat focus mid-keystroke); **Pack became a filter dimension** with cross-faceted counts, pills, a `#/browse?pack=<id>` deep link and a hand-off button from each pack page; and the **filter rail became searchable** (40+ tags were truncated to 14, so finding the filter was its own search problem). Fixed a **pre-existing** routing bug this exposed: `parseHash()` split the raw hash before stripping the query, so *every* query-string deep link — including the older `?tag=` — silently fell through to Home. Honest gap: mobile was not visually verified (the browser would not resize), so the <620px nav-wrap rule is reasoned from measured widths, not observed.
- [x] **Frontend refactor** *(shipped 2026-07-13 — see Completed)*: delivered as five slices — (1) an accessible `Modal` primitive + promise-based `useConfirm` replacing both native `confirm()` gates (run + delete); (2) that shell adopted across all 8 modals (Escape/focus-trap/ARIA + a modal stack so nested dialogs close top-first); (3) a mobile sidebar drawer meeting the `<375px` requirement; (4) react-router with bookmarkable sections + deep links (`/tasks/:id`, `/templates/:id`); (5) `Dashboard.tsx` split 1,830 → 248 lines into `src/screens/` (DashboardScreen / TemplatesScreen / PlatformsScreen). All `alert()` were already on toasts (2026-07-08). Every slice browser-verified; 69 frontend unit tests + build + lint green. Optional follow-up: route-level code-splitting to trim the bundle (now trips Vite's 500 kB hint — easy now that the router + screen modules exist).
- [ ] **Product bets — from "a better Task Scheduler UI" to "a local automation control plane"** *(logged 2026-07-28 from the analysis; **directions, not scheduled work** — each needs its own design pass before it becomes an item)*: fifteen were proposed; they are kept here because the good ones share a premise the dashboard has now demonstrated at 352 tasks — **TaskHub already holds more truth about a machine's automation than anything else on it, and currently spends all of it on rendering cards**. Listed in the order they should be *considered*, with the four the analysis recommended first marked ★. The honest constraint on all of them: this project's guardrail is **reliability first**, and every bet below is a feature that can be confidently wrong.

  - ★ **Automation health score.** Score each task from signals TaskHub *already stores* — last success age, recent failure runs, `MISSING`/disabled state, a schedule that hasn't fired, no run history at all, duration drift — and add a **"Needs attention"** view. Highest value-per-effort in the batch because it invents no new data: `ExecutionLog` has `triggeredAt` / `status` / `durationMs`, and the status model already distinguishes MISSING from user-DISABLED. **The trap to design against first:** a score is a summary, and a summary is a claim — a task scored *healthy* because it reports `SUCCESS` is exactly the hung-webhook case ([#12](troubleshooting/README.md#12-a-template-passes-every-test-and-still-hangs-on-the-target)). Score what can be *evidenced*, and show the signals next to the number, never the number alone.
  - ★ **Runbooks attached to tasks.** A per-task operational note: purpose, expected output, how to verify it worked, what to do when it fails, where the secrets live (locations, never values). Lowest architectural risk here — one nullable text/JSON field, one panel in the task modal, and it flows through the existing export path. Turns a forgotten 3 AM job into a documented operation, which is the actual problem with a machine that has 95 user tasks on it.
  - ★ **Dry run & validation lab.** Validate a task *before* it is registered: executable resolves on PATH, working directory exists, every `{{placeholder}}` is filled, the cron converts **non-lossily** (or says `replaced` vs `approximated`, which the `lossy` discriminator already distinguishes), and the **final command is shown exactly as the platform will receive it**. Directly serves the reliability promise, and its cheapest slice is already logged as the Tools-tab **Schedule tester** — build that first (`POST /api/tasks/preview` already returns `score`/`warnings`/`trigger`/`lossy`; `computeNextRun` already exists), then extend outward to the filesystem/PATH checks, which need the agent since only it can see the target machine.
  - ★ **Task collections / playlists.** Manually-runnable ordered sequences — "Morning startup", "Repo maintenance", "Shutdown cleanup" — with stop-on-failure and per-step results. Unlocks new daily workflows **without a single new connector**, which is rare in this list. Needs a real decision on where sequencing lives: `NativeScheduler` runs backend-owned jobs and could orchestrate, but a Windows task's *completion* is only observable through the agent, so "step 2 after step 1 finishes" is a protocol question, not a UI one.
  - **Task dependency graph.** Upstream/downstream relationships — backup before upload, digest after scrape — declared manually first, *suggested* later from shared paths, schedules, and naming. The most differentiating bet and the most dangerous: **inferred edges are fabricated relationships**, and this project's rule is that a guess must never render as a fact. Manual-only for v1, inference strictly as a suggestion the user confirms.
  - **Watchdog tasks.** One task monitors another: alert if it hasn't succeeded in N hours, flag it when a run takes 3× normal, run a recovery task after failure. Notable because it is the one bet that makes TaskHub useful for tasks **created outside** it — which is most of them on a real machine. Builds on the shipped failure-webhook path.
  - **Task definition versioning.** A timeline of definition changes (schedule, command, folder, run level, category, enable/disable) with diff + rollback. TaskHub is now the thing *making* those changes (three signed write verbs), so it is the only place that can record them — and "what changed my scheduled task?" currently has no answer anywhere on Windows.
  - **Schedule conflict & load map.** *"12 jobs fire at 03:00; 8 touch `D:\AI_Agents`; 4 invoke Docker."* Pure analysis over data already held; the honest version of the "everything runs at 3 AM" problem every scheduled-task estate has.
  - **Intent labels & purpose-driven views.** First-class intent metadata (backup / sync / cleanup / monitoring / reporting / ai-agent / security / deploy) so views can answer *"what protects my data?"* instead of only *"what lives in `\Work`?"*. Note the overlap: `Template.tags` already exists and templates already carry semantic tags — the gap is that **tasks** have only a single `category`, which for Windows is a projection of the real folder and therefore not free to mean anything else.
  - **Failure triage assistant.** On a failed run, assemble definition + recent edits + exit code + log snippet + platform state + the matching troubleshooting entry into one diagnosis. Explain/assist only — **autonomous repair is explicitly out**, for the same reason `delete_task` is gated: the elevated agent's caller is untrusted by design.
  - **Approval gates.** Mark high-risk tasks as requiring approval before the next run or before a definition change takes effect. The concept already exists in miniature (`TASKHUB_MCP_ALLOW_DESTRUCTIVE`), and the same reasoning applies — the gate must be reachable only **out-of-band**, or an agent simply approves itself.
  - **Environment profile manager.** A task declares which env vars it needs; TaskHub reports present/missing **without ever storing values**. Small, and it addresses a real class of 3 AM failure (the token expired, the task kept "succeeding").
  - **Calendar & quiet hours.** Suppress or defer non-critical tasks during meetings, on battery, or in a manual quiet window; local rules first, `.ics` later. Attractive and *not* cheap — deferral means TaskHub is rewriting triggers on the machine, which is a write path with a rollback problem.
  - **Restore & migrate wizard.** The bigger sibling of the logged **Restore tasks from an export** item: take a bundle onto a *new* machine and warn about missing paths, missing users, absent executables, and folder collisions before registering anything. Do restore first; migration is restore plus a diff against reality.
  - **Automation inventory report.** One generated document: what runs, when, why, last success, what's disabled or missing, risky command shapes, system-vs-personal split, what has no documentation. Mostly a rendering of the other bets' data, and the most demo-able artifact in the list.

- [x] **Run history export + automation health score** *(**shipped 2026-07-28** — see CHANGELOG › Added)*: shipped as designed below, plus two things only live data taught:
  - **Live testing found what the tests could not, twice.** Against the real 354-task machine, four of the five worst-scoring tasks were `\Microsoft\` system tasks — **re-creating the exact burial the Personal lens was built to stop on this same day**. The panel now carries the server-owned `isSystem` verdict, hides them by default, and names the count (37 personal vs 99 system). And `1073807364` (`DBG_TERMINATE_PROCESS`) was being read as an exit code, reporting *"the last run failed"* for tasks merely stopped at shutdown; it now reads *"stopped before it finished"*, moving three tasks out of critical. **A scorer can only be validated against a real population** — a fixture has no 257 system tasks in it.
  - **Verified live end to end:** all 354 Windows tasks now report `lastTaskResult` after the republish, the score surfaced genuine failures (a OneDrive backup that failed *that day*, a weekly job failing since 2026-07-13, an updater hitting DNS failure `12007`), and the CSV was parsed back by a real CSV reader with its BOM, CRLF endings, and a quoted comma-bearing log intact.
  - **Health moved from the Dashboard to the Tools tab** *(same day, on feedback)*: it first shipped as a panel above the task list, where it **took over the main content area on the one screen used for actual work**. It belongs on Tools by the tab's own rule — *"anything acting on a single task belongs on the Dashboard"* — and health reads across every task and acts on none. Two things changed with the move: the card is **self-contained** (it deep-links to `/tasks/:id` instead of taking Dashboard props), and it **always renders**, including an explicit "nothing needs attention". That inverts the earlier no-permanent-banner reasoning **on purpose**: a banner you didn't ask for should stay quiet, but a *tool* that vanished when all was well would read as a missing feature — you opened the tab to ask the question.
  - **What is still open from *Richer execution analytics*:** its data layer is now built (this route), but the analytics *views* — failure trend, duration trend, stale-task list — are not. That item stays open and is now cheap.
  - Tests: backend 406 unit + 120 integration, frontend 158, agent 151; two mutation checks. New traps [#29](troubleshooting/README.md#29-prisma-migrate-refuses-to-run-migration-was-modified-after-it-was-applied--and-offers-to-drop-your-database) and [#30](troubleshooting/README.md#30-a-route-500s-on-real-data-while-tsc-is-green--a-cast-on-a-query-result). **Needs an agent republish** for run results to appear; until then tasks honestly score `unknown` and say so.

  *(Design record, written before implementation.)* taken together deliberately — the *Run history export (CSV)* Tools candidate, the *Richer execution analytics* item below, and the ★ *Automation health score* bet all need the same thing first: **one bulk `ExecutionLog` query**, since today it is readable only 20 rows at a time per task.

  **The finding that reshaped the score, found before writing any of it:** `ExecutionLog` rows are created in exactly **two** places — `POST /api/tasks/:id/run` and `NativeScheduler`. So it records runs **TaskHub performed**, not runs that *happened*. A Windows task firing on its own schedule writes nothing at all, and a manual Windows run logs `SUCCESS` meaning *"the agent successfully triggered it"* (fire-and-forget; `durationMs` measures the trigger, not the work). Scoring Windows tasks on `ExecutionLog` would therefore have reported nearly every task on a real machine as never-run — **a confident lie at dashboard scale**, which is precisely what this item was already warned about ("a task scored *healthy* because it reports `SUCCESS` is exactly the hung-webhook case, [#12](troubleshooting/README.md#12-a-template-passes-every-test-and-still-hangs-on-the-target)"). The warning was about trusting `SUCCESS`; the sharper version is that for Windows tasks **there is usually no record at all**, and absence reads as failure to any scorer that doesn't know the difference.

  Decisions:
  - **The evidence for a Windows task's health comes from Windows.** The agent gains `lastTaskResult` (the exit code — `0` is success) and `numberOfMissedRuns` on the **read-only** `task:list` payload: two extra properties on a `Task` object it already has open, under the existing access-denied guard. No new verb, no signature, no protocol change — but it **needs an agent republish**. This also delivers most of the *Task-detail trust indicators* item below, which asks for exactly this field and notes it "matters most, because it is the only field in the modal that comes from **outside** TaskHub".
  - **Two platforms, two sources of truth, and the difference stays visible.** Windows tasks score from the platform snapshot (`lastTaskResult`, `lastRunTime`, missed runs, `MISSING`/disabled); TaskHub-native tasks score from `ExecutionLog`, which genuinely *is* their run history. Signals that cannot exist for a platform (duration drift for a fire-and-forget Windows trigger) are **absent**, never defaulted to healthy.
  - **`unknown` is a first-class outcome.** A task TaskHub holds no evidence about — never synced, agent offline, an unreadable ACL'd definition — scores `unknown`, not `ok`. Silence is not health, and that mistake is the whole failure mode of this feature.
  - **The number ranks; it does not grade.** Each signal carries a fixed weight and the score is `100` minus their sum, so it is explainable rather than tuned — its job is to sort 352 tasks worst-first, which needs a scalar. The UI **always renders the signals with it** and never the number alone. Disabled is deliberately *not* unhealthy: parking a task is the recommended safe action (it is why `set_task_status` ships ungated over MCP), so it is informational and explains why "hasn't run" doesn't apply.
  - **Both routes live on `/api/tools`** — they are cross-task, and `GET /api/tasks/health` already means *per-platform connector health*. `GET /api/tools/task-health` and `GET /api/tools/history` avoid a collision that would otherwise read as the same concept.
  - **The CSV states what it contains.** It exports TaskHub-performed runs, and says so in the file and in the UI — otherwise an empty month reads as "nothing ran" when it means "TaskHub triggered nothing". It also **neutralizes formula injection**: TaskHub stores command lines, and a cell beginning `=`, `+`, `-` or `@` is executed by Excel on open, so an exported task named `=cmd|'/c calc'!A1` would be a live payload. UTF-8 **with a BOM**, so non-ASCII task names survive Excel.
  - **A new index is required, not optional.** `ExecutionLog`'s only index is `[taskId, triggeredAt]` — per-task, and useless for the cross-task time range both features run. Per CLAUDE.md §10 that gets an index in the same change.

- [x] **Richer execution analytics** *(logged 2026-07-28 from the analysis; **shipped 2026-07-31** — see CHANGELOG › Added)*: all three views shipped — failure trend, duration trend, and an idle-task list — as `GET /api/tools/analytics` over a new `services/runAnalytics.ts`, plus an **Execution analytics** card on the Tools tab. As predicted it was cheap: the bulk read landed with the health score, so this was analysis and presentation with **no agent work and no new data**. Four things the logged item didn't anticipate, all forced by the same fact:
  - **The three questions need three different sources, and the item's own wording ("what hasn't run in 30 days?") named the trap.** `ExecutionLog` holds runs *Cronsole performed*, so answering "hasn't run" from it would have flagged **every Windows task on the machine** — the exact failure the health score was redesigned around three days earlier. Windows tasks are judged from **Windows' own `lastRunTime`** in the sync snapshot; native tasks from `ExecutionLog`, which genuinely is their history.
  - **The duration trend is native-only, and says so.** For a Windows row `durationMs` times the agent accepting a start request, so a slowest-first list would be ranking WebSocket round trips. `taskHealth.ts` already restricted its own duration signal for this reason; this is that rule applied across tasks. The excluded row count is stated, because on a Windows-only machine the list is legitimately empty and **an empty list with no explanation reads as a broken feature**.
  - **"Idle" needed four outcomes, not two** — idle, never-run, no-run-evidence, and disabled/on-demand. The last is not a defect (parking a task is the recommended safe action, the same reason `set_task_status` ships ungated), and the third is the unmeasured case that must never be reported as a verdict. Everything unassessed is counted out loud so "nothing is idle" can't be read as "everything is fine".
  - **A partial chart narrows its span rather than thinning its data.** Totals come from an exact aggregate and never depend on the row ceiling; if the ceiling is hit the trend covers a *shorter, complete* period and says so. Empty days are drawn as empty columns — a trend plotted only from days that have rows draws a straight line across an outage. Days are cut in the **reader's** timezone (an unknown zone is a 400, not a silent UTC fallback), and the chart never rests identity on hue: emerald/red separate by only ΔE 8.1 under deuteranopia, so segments carry gaps, the legend is always present, and hover **or tab-focus** prints the numbers into a live region.

  Tests: backend 457 unit (+23, three mutation checks) + 132 integration against real Postgres (+12), frontend 193 (+12, three mutation checks); frontend build + lint clean. **Honest gap:** not yet driven in a live browser against the real machine — the data path is proven by the integration suite against real Postgres, but the two things only live data has ever taught this feature family (a real 354-task population, and how the panel reads at that scale) are unproven here. Worth a click-through next session.

- [ ] **Split the files that have become fault lines** *(logged 2026-07-28 from the analysis; **re-measured 2026-07-28** — all six have grown since the review)*: `mcp-server/src/tools.ts` **1,041**, `backend/src/routes/tasks.ts` **993**, `frontend/src/screens/DashboardScreen.tsx` **823**, `frontend/src/screens/TemplatesScreen.tsx` **768**, `agent/TaskHub.Agent/AgentService.cs` **694**, `frontend/src/components/TaskModal.tsx` **684**. Size alone is not a defect — but `tasks.ts` is the file where **declaration order is load-bearing** (`DELETE /missing` must precede `DELETE /:id`, and it is the reason cross-task routes went to `/api/tools` at all), and a 993-line router is where that kind of rule goes unnoticed. **Do not schedule a refactor sprint**; split along the seams the analysis named — task creation / task mutation / export+history, dashboard filters vs. views, template cards vs. facets, MCP tool groups — **only when next touching that area**, which is how the 1,830-line `Dashboard.tsx` came down to 248 without a rewrite.

- [ ] **macOS agent** (launchd) — 7 catalog templates already wait on it; `ITaskScheduler` abstraction ports cleanly.
- [ ] **Installer packages (agent only)**: standard signed Windows Installer (`.msi` via WiX) for the agent, replacing the PowerShell setup script; matching packages for other OSes as their agents land (macOS `.pkg`/Homebrew once the launchd agent exists). Matters once there are users beyond Mike. **Scope note:** this item is the *agent* package. Packaging the **whole stack** (Postgres + backend + frontend + agent) as one installer is the separate, larger item directly below.

- [ ] **Ship the whole application as a Windows installer (`.exe`)** *(logged 2026-07-28 — feasibility assessed, **not started**)*: today an install means clone the repo, `docker compose up` for Postgres + Redis, two host Node dev servers, and a published agent, orchestrated by `scripts/taskhub.ps1`. That is a **developer's** install — and the local-first launch posture (see the P3 header) means **every user performs it**. A signed installer that lays the full stack down and registers it to start with Windows is the natural endpoint of local-first. **Feasible — the app is already all-Windows and all-local — but it is four jobs, not a packaging step.**

  **(1) Postgres is the fork in the road.** `docker-compose.yml` runs `postgres:16-alpine`, and Docker Desktop cannot be a dependency of a consumer installer. Two honest paths:
  - **Bundle Postgres binaries** — EnterpriseDB portable zip, `initdb` into `%ProgramData%\TaskHub\pgdata` on first run, registered as a service on a non-default port. **Zero code change; dev/install parity preserved.** Costs ~250 MB and a real support surface: port conflicts, failed `initdb`, orphaned data directories on uninstall.
  - **Migrate to SQLite** — one file, no service, dramatically better install UX. **Not a provider flip:** `Template.tags String[]` (`backend/prisma/schema.prisma:123`) is a Postgres scalar list with no SQLite equivalent, so it needs a schema change plus a migration — and `npm run test:integration` is written against real Postgres, so the test infra either forks or runs both providers.
  - **Lean: bundle Postgres for v1.** It ships without touching the data model or the test suite, and SQLite stays available if install weight ever becomes an actual complaint. Tracked in **Open decisions**.

  **(2) Redis just goes.** It is already optional for single-instance; cut it from the installed stack entirely.

  **(3) The frontend must stop being a dev server.** Today it is `vite` on `:7373` with `VITE_API_URL` pointing at `:3000`, and the backend serves **no static files at all** (no `express.static` in `backend/src/index.ts`). Installed, it should be `vite build` → static assets served by Express on **one** port, same-origin — which also removes the `ALLOWED_ORIGINS`/CORS dance from the installed config. Small, but genuinely new code.

  **(4) Runtimes.** Ship `node.exe` + `backend/dist` + `node_modules` (pin Prisma `binaryTargets` so the Windows query engine is present). The agent is the easy half — already a `WinExe` with a startup script (`agent/setup-agent-startup.ps1`) — but `TaskHub.Agent.csproj` declares no `RuntimeIdentifier`/`SelfContained`, so it is **framework-dependent**: the installer either chains the .NET 10 runtime or the agent publishes self-contained (+~70 MB).

  **Two things that must be designed in, not bolted on:**
  - **Secrets are generated per machine, at install time.** `JWT_SECRET`, `ENCRYPTION_KEY`, and `AGENT_PAIRING_SECRET` carry dev defaults in compose. Baking fixed values into an installer would give **every install on earth the same JWT signing key and the same pairing secret** — anyone could mint a valid token for anyone else's instance, and pair an agent to it. Generate into an ACL'd `%ProgramData%\TaskHub\.env` on first run.
  - **Uninstall must sweep Task Scheduler.** TaskHub creates real OS state under `\TaskHub\`. Removing the app while leaving live scheduled tasks running is the worst outcome available — that is a `task:delete` sweep at uninstall time, and it needs elevation.

  **Packaging choice:** the agent item above specifies WiX MSI; for a *multi-payload app* installer, **Inno Setup** or **Velopack** reaches a working signed `.exe` far faster than WiX Burn, and Velopack brings the update channel *Agent distribution & trust* already wants. The outer `.exe` can chain the agent MSI rather than replace it.

  **Expect SmartScreen.** An unsigned installer that registers a service and writes to Task Scheduler is precisely the flagged profile, so the OV/EV Authenticode certificate under **Agent distribution & trust** is a hard prerequisite here, not a polish item.

  **Rough scope:** ~1 week to something installable on a clean machine; ~1 more to make it *trustworthy* (signing, uninstall correctness, a first-run wizard for the owner account + agent pairing). Largest unknown: embedded-Postgres lifecycle on machines you don't control.
- [ ] Claude Code connector: promote from experimental scaffold to production-ready.
- [ ] ChatGPT stays quick-links-only unless a public automations API appears.
- [ ] **Remote access (self-hosted) — the final, optional enhancement** *(the last action item; do everything else first)*: instead of hosting TaskHub as a SaaS, let a user reach their **own** local instance from other devices (phone, laptop) — the [code-server](https://github.com/coder/code-server) model. The frontend, backend, Postgres, and agent all stay on the user's machine; **only the dashboard view is exposed remotely**, over a private, access-controlled channel — never nakedly on the public internet. Delivered primarily as **documentation** (drafted: [`docs/user-guides/guides/Remote_Access_Guide.md`](user-guides/guides/Remote_Access_Guide.md)) covering two paths: **Tailscale** (private mesh VPN — recommended, zero public exposure) and **Cloudflare Tunnel + Access** (a real HTTPS URL, login-gated, outbound-only). Two enablers already shipped make this low-effort: the **runtime API-origin override** (Settings → About, so a remote dashboard points at the backend without a rebuild) and the app's existing `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` CORS/Socket.IO gating. Optional bundled tooling: a small **single-origin reverse proxy** (Caddy Compose profile) so a public tunnel has one URL to expose (like code-server's single port) instead of two origins. **Security gate (hard requirement):** TaskHub can create/run commands (RCE by design) and today auths with a dev token, so the network layer (Tailscale membership / Cloudflare Access login) **is** the authentication until the Go-public real-login system lands — the guide leads with this. This is an *enhancement for the future*, explicitly after the app is otherwise complete and running; it is **not** part of the local-first launch.

#### 🚀 Go-public checklist — public repo + advertisable product

Everything required before the repo flips public and TaskHub is promoted beyond personal use. All of **P0 is a hard prerequisite**; P1 correctness and the P2 onboarding/resources item are strongly recommended first. **TaskHub launches as a local-first app** (each user runs the full stack on their own machine) — there is **no hosted TaskHub instance** at launch, so the items below cover the repo/product going public, not standing up a multi-tenant cloud service. Remote access to a user's own instance is the optional P3 enhancement above.

##### Repo goes public

- [x] **License decision** *(resolved 2026-07-13 — Apache-2.0)*: the repo `LICENSE` is now **Apache-2.0** (permissive open source + explicit patent grant), replacing the proprietary all-rights-reserved notice; README license badge + footer updated. Chosen over MIT (wanted the patent grant) and source-available/closed.
- [~] **Secret & history audit** *(audit done 2026-07-13; history scrub deferred to publish time)*: manually scanned all 155 commits + the working tree. **Clean on the critical items**: no `.env` was ever committed, and the real `JWT_SECRET` / `ENCRYPTION_KEY` / `AGENT_PAIRING_SECRET` values never entered history. The committed **dev JWT** (in `frontend/src/api.ts` + `Dashboard.tsx` across many commits) is already removed + rotated (invalidated) — it only lingers in history. Personal email is not tracked; the only non-example emails are OFL font-license attributions. **Decided:** squash to a **fresh public root** at publish time (drops the rotated-JWT-in-history + early-dev mess). Personal machine paths in internal docs (CLAUDE.md, registry/README.md) still to scrub at publish.
- [~] **Repo hygiene for outsiders** *(done 2026-07-13, except branch protection)*: removed the stray `fix_connection.sql`; `.env.example` exists for backend + frontend + mcp-server; the other artifacts the analysis flagged (`coverage/`, stray `src/`, `Gemini_Agent/`) are **not** tracked. Remaining (a GitHub setting, do at publish): make `main` the clean default with branch protection.
- [x] **Community scaffolding** *(shipped 2026-07-13; two GitHub toggles remain)*: added **`SECURITY.md`** (private vulnerability reporting + the security model + the "don't expose publicly until real login" limitation), **`CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md`** (Contributor Covenant 2.1), **issue templates** + `config.yml` (security routed to private reporting, blank issues off), and a **PR template**, plus a **CI status badge** on the README. Remaining (GitHub settings): enable **Discussions** and add branch protection when the repo flips public.
- [~] **Final README/docs pass for a stranger audience** (github-readme skill): quick-start that works on a machine that isn't Mike's, screenshots/GIF of the dashboard, honest feature-status table. **Add (2026-07-28, from the analysis):** sweep the docs for text that still implies a **cloud/AWS production deployment** — CLAUDE.md §3's tech-stack table still lists *"Hosting (prod): AWS ECS Fargate or Render; static frontend on S3 + CloudFront or Vercel"*, which was true before the 2026-07-13 local-first decision and now contradicts both the P3 header and the launch posture a stranger will read first. ✅ **That one row is fixed (2026-07-31)** — it now says local-first and names the retired claim, so a reader who saw the old text knows which is current. The rest of this item (quick-start on a foreign machine, screenshots, feature-status table, the wider sweep) is still open.

- [x] **Package metadata says ISC while the repo says Apache-2.0** *(logged 2026-07-28; **fixed 2026-07-31** — see CHANGELOG › Changed)*: both manifests now say `Apache-2.0`, and the backend gained a real `description`. `frontend/package.json` stays `private: true` with no license field, as scoped. **Not** done in the same change: real `version` fields, which wait on the *Versioning & releases* item — a version number that isn't cut from a tagged release is just another claim nothing checks. *(Original finding below.)*

  *(Original)* `backend/package.json:18` and `mcp-server/package.json:28` both carry `"license": "ISC"` — the `npm init` default, never updated when the license decision was made on 2026-07-13. `frontend/package.json` is `"private": true` with no license field, which is fine and can stay. Trivial to fix and worth doing before the repo flips public: a package manifest is a **machine-readable license claim**, and two of them currently disagree with the `LICENSE` file, the README badge, and each other's parent repo. Same change should set real `version` fields if the *Versioning & releases* item lands first, since these are two of the four independently-versioned components.

- [x] **Remove or relocate the one-off `check_db` diagnostics** *(logged 2026-07-28; **fixed 2026-07-31** — see CHANGELOG › Changed)*: taken as the *fix and move* option, because the item's own aside was right — "what does the DB actually contain?" is the question that diagnosed [#22](troubleshooting/README.md#22-deleted-a-windows-task-synced-and-cronsole-still-shows-it--while-reporting-missing-n). Both files are gone; `backend/scripts/check-db.ts` replaces them behind `npm run db:check`, documented in the scripts README. **It leads with the generated-Prisma-client currency check**, since a stale client silently drops enum writes and would make every count below it a claim rather than a fact. **Why the malformed one survived at all, which is the reusable part:** `backend/tsconfig.json` includes only `src/**/*`, so a `.ts` file in the package root is never typechecked, never built, and never run — it can be syntactically broken indefinitely and nothing says so. *(Original finding below.)*

  *(Original)* `backend/check_db.js` and `backend/check_db.ts` are ad-hoc DB pokes sitting in the backend package root, outside `src/`, outside `scripts/`, and outside every test suite — the analysis flagged the `.ts` one as looking malformed. They are exactly the kind of thing a stranger opens first and draws conclusions from. Either fix and move them under `scripts/` with a line in the scripts README (there is a real case for a "what does the DB actually contain" helper — [#22](troubleshooting/README.md#22-deleted-a-windows-task-synced-and-cronsole-still-shows-it--while-reporting-missing-n) was diagnosed by exactly that question), or delete them.

- [x] **Close the unauthenticated `POST /auth/register` primitive** *(logged 2026-07-28; **fixed 2026-07-31** — see CHANGELOG › Security)*: taken as the first of the three listed options — **deleted**, not flag-gated. A dev-only env flag is one misconfiguration away from open, and it would have kept alive a route the product has no use for: `/setup` creates the one owner (409 afterwards) and `/login` authenticates. **The find worth recording is why it survived:** the integration suite drove `/register` for its own convenience, so the route had a constituency that wasn't a user — which is exactly how a primitive nobody wants stays mounted. Those tests now run through `/setup`, and one **pins the 404** as a tripwire. `SECURITY.md` now states the account model positively (single-user, `/setup` only, no open registration) instead of describing a security model in which `/register` merely goes unmentioned; while there, its "authenticates with a development token" limitation — stale since login shipped 2026-07-16 — was corrected to name what is actually missing (no reset, no refresh, no per-user pairing). Verified live: `POST /api/auth/register` → 404 and `needsSetup` unchanged. One casualty, replaced rather than dropped: the manual IDOR runbook created its second user through `/register`, and now signs a token for a second identity directly — which tests owner scoping *more* sharply, since scoping is enforced on the `userId` inside the token, not on which accounts exist. *(Original finding below.)*

  *(Original)* (**verified — `backend/src/routes/auth.ts:89`, no auth middleware**) the single-user login shipped with `/setup` as the real first-run path, but the raw `/register` route was left live for tests and possible future multi-user work. On a local-first install that is a **public account-creation endpoint on a service that can create and run elevated commands** — and the P3 remote-access work is specifically about making that service reachable from other devices. It is already listed as a decision inside the deferred *Multi-user / hosted account system* item; promoting it to its own line because it is a **launch blocker**, not a multi-user design question. Options, in preference order: delete it and let the tests use `/setup`; keep it behind an explicit dev-only env flag that fails closed; or construct it only in the test app factory. Whichever ships, the choice belongs in `SECURITY.md`, which currently documents the security model as if `/setup` were the only way in.

- [x] **Align REST CORS with `ALLOWED_ORIGINS`** *(logged 2026-07-28; **fixed 2026-07-31** — see CHANGELOG › Security)*: shipped as scoped — both halves of the API now read **one** parser (`backend/src/config/origins.ts`), so REST and Socket.IO cannot drift apart again, which was the actual defect: two mechanisms answering the same question. Two decisions the item didn't settle: **a request with no `Origin` header is always allowed** (every non-browser caller — MCP server, `curl`, the integration suite — and refusing them would break real clients while stopping nothing, since a non-browser caller simply omits the header; authentication is what guards those paths), and the **loopback condition was dropped** — there is no bind config to read (`server.listen(PORT)` binds every interface, and the container must), so a condition on it would have been invented rather than observed. Instead: empty list stays permissive, **and says so at boot**, because "unset" and "deliberately open" are indistinguishable from inside the process and only one is a decision. Both `.env.example` and compose ship the variable set. The tests assert **response headers, not status codes** — a refused origin still gets a 200 body; what it doesn't get is `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`. **Follow-up shipped the same day: a refusal now explains itself in the backend log**, once per distinct origin, naming the rejected origin *and* the allowed list. The refusal was already correct; what was missing was the **diagnosis** — the failure shows up in the browser as a generic CORS error while `curl` keeps working and confirms the wrong theory ([#31](troubleshooting/README.md#31-the-dashboard-loads-but-every-api-call-fails-with-a-cors-error)), and the server is the only party that knows both halves. Deduped rather than per-request, because a dashboard that can't reach the API retries and this project already has a logged item about warnings you learn to ignore; capped at 20 origins, and the origin is sanitized on its way into the log since it is attacker-controlled header content. Still true that the P3 whole-stack installer would remove the problem entirely by **serving the frontend same-origin from Express** — that is the structural fix, and it stays with the installer item rather than being pulled forward. *(Original finding below.)*

  *(Original)* (**verified — `backend/src/app.ts:21` is a bare `app.use(cors())`**) Socket.IO's origin list is restricted (`backend/src/index.ts:24`) but Express reflects **any** origin, so the REST API is open to cross-origin reads from any page the user visits while the app is running — with the browser sending no cookies but the token living in the app's own storage, the practical exposure is limited today and **stops being limited the moment remote access lands**, which is the final P3 item and specifically puts a browser origin in front of this API. Already named in the P1 QA item's list of deferred public-hosting risks; broken out because it has a concrete fix (pass the same parsed `allowedOrigins` array Socket.IO uses, defaulting to permissive **only** when the list is empty *and* the bind is loopback, so local dev is unchanged) and because the whole-stack installer (P3) would remove the problem entirely by serving the frontend same-origin from Express.
- [ ] **Rename TaskHub → Cronsole** *(decided 2026-07-31 — see Open decisions; staged deliberately, because the stages have very different blast radii)*:

  **Stage 1 — in-repo brand + code identity** ✅ **shipped 2026-07-31**: 183 files, 1,513 occurrences, applied by a scripted pass with an explicit protection list rather than by hand, so it is reviewable and repeatable. Verified by every suite plus a real browser pass (the app now reads **Cronsole** end to end; 356 tasks load, both platforms online, clean console).

  **Two boundary corrections the dry run and the suites forced, both worth keeping:**
  - **`agent/` was pulled out of Stage 1 entirely.** The agent's identity is bound to *this machine* — the published exe path, the `\Task-Hub\` task that launches it at logon, its appsettings beside the binary — so renaming the project would have left the machine starting a path that no longer exists. That is an external effect, which is the one thing Stage 1 is defined not to have. Same reasoning protected `taskhub.ps1`, `TaskHubAgent`, `TaskHubRepublish` and `TaskHubStack`: **code may only be renamed where the machine is not pointing at the old name.**
  - **The suites caught three half-renames the protection list created**, which is exactly what they are for: a test asserting `TaskHub/Backup.xml` from a protected `\TaskHub\` input, a case-insensitivity test whose *lowercase* `\\taskhub\\ping` fixture fell through the exact-case protection, and a search test matching a protected `externalId`. Each was a real inconsistency, not a test being fussy.
  - **`CRONSOLE_*` env vars fall back to `TASKHUB_*`, and say so once.** The variables live in the *user's* environment, not this repo, and on Windows need a fresh terminal to re-read ([#8a](troubleshooting/README.md#8a-and-restart-from-a-fresh-terminal-does-nothing-under-vs-code)) — renaming them in code alone would have taken the MCP tools away from a working setup and silently stopped failure webhooks. The MCP suite found this too: a test deleting only `CRONSOLE_TOKEN` stopped throwing because the real `TASKHUB_TOKEN` was still exported, which is a suite quietly depending on the developer's shell.

  **Stage 2 — this machine** ✅ **shipped 2026-07-31**: 105 files / 712 occurrences plus the machine itself. The agent project, its published exe, the launcher scripts, the three scheduled tasks and the Task Scheduler folder all moved together, because code and machine must move together or logon start points at a path that is gone. `\Cronsole-Stack\` holds the launcher tasks and is kept **separate** from the app's own `\Cronsole`, so the app's prune-its-own-folder logic can never reach them. Verified from Windows, not from the migration's own report: all three tasks registered and pointing at existing files, `\Task-Hub\` removed, `CRONSOLE_TOKEN` carrying the JWT and the legacy variable gone, `cronsole.ps1 status` → **ALL UP**, and the app still logged in with its theme intact.

  **Four things this stage taught, each of which nearly shipped as a silent break:**
  - **A rename pass will eat its own back-compat layer.** Stage 1's `CRONSOLE_* ?? TASKHUB_*` fallbacks were rewritten by Stage 2 into `CRONSOLE_* ?? CRONSOLE_*` — a tautology that compiles, reads correctly, and quietly drops the compatibility it exists to provide. **It rewrote the guarding tests to match**, so they still passed. Every legacy prefix is now assembled from parts (`['TASK','HUB'].join('')`) precisely so a future pass cannot helpfully rewrite it, and each fallback is mutation-checked.
  - **`\TaskHub\` was empty**, because the app prunes its own folder when the last task goes — so the "move the user's tasks" plan had nothing to move. Worth checking the machine before designing a migration for it.
  - **A script ending in `Stop-Process -Id $PID` takes its caller with it.** `setup-agent-startup.ps1` closes its own window as a convenience for double-clicking; called in-process it killed the migration after one of three tasks, and the run merely *looked* like it stopped early. Registrars now run as child processes, and success is verified by asking Task Scheduler rather than by an exit code a self-killing script cannot produce.
  - **The golden cross-language HMAC vector moved, and that was correct.** The signed `folder` field is part of the message, so renaming it changed the signature. Both implementations recomputed **the same** new value independently — the disagreement was with the frozen constant, not between the languages, which is the distinction the vector exists to make. The comment now says that updating it is legitimate *only* in that circumstance.

  *(Original scope note.)* (needs elevation, and it is where a mistake is felt): the `TASKHUB_*` User env vars, the `\TaskHub\` app folder and `\Task-Hub\` agent folder in Task Scheduler, **the `agent/` project + published exe** (deferred here from Stage 1 for exactly this reason), the Docker database/container names, and the `taskhub.*` `localStorage` keys. **Decided 2026-07-31: existing tasks are MOVED to `\Cronsole\`** — created, re-registered, old folder deleted — rather than orphaned in a folder the renamed app no longer treats as its own. Take a Tools-tab export first, so a failed move is recoverable. **The scheduler folders are the sharp edge** — TaskHub only ever prunes its own `\TaskHub`, and the restore work already proved ([#28](troubleshooting/README.md#28-a-restored-task-or-the-folder-it-landed-in-cant-be-deleted-access-is-denied)) that a folder the elevated agent created can need elevation to remove. Existing tasks must be **moved, not orphaned**, and `localStorage` keys should be *read-with-fallback* rather than switched, or the rename silently logs you out and resets your theme.

  **Stage 3 — the public surface** ✅ **repos + registry URL shipped 2026-07-31**; the `taskhub.mikesailab.com` front door is **held pending DNS** (see Next up). All three repos renamed, descriptions corrected (all still advertised TaskHub; the site's still called itself the landing page retired on 2026-07-28), all three local remotes repointed and proven by a live fetch, and the two mirror clone directories under `Repos\Tools\` renamed with the publish scripts' `-WorkDir` defaults following them. 95 occurrences across 25 files, scripted with an explicit protection list.

  **What the stage actually taught:**
  - **The registry URL move was a single non-renewable opportunity, and the argument for the freeze had to be rewritten rather than deleted.** Both `CLAUDE.md` §9 and `publish-frontdoor.ps1` carried a reasoned case that the URL must not move *because* moving it breaks catalog sync for every install. That case was right, and this move spent it — it was safe only because `TEMPLATE_REGISTRY_URL` is commented out everywhere, so nothing was fetching. Leaving the old text standing would have left two documents arguing against a thing that had just been done; both now state what happened and that there is no second free move. **When you take the one exception a rule allows, the rule's own text is part of the change.**
  - **The pass tried to eat stage 2's protection list.** `scripts/rename-stage2.mjs` holds `'taskhub-registry', 'taskhub-site', 'michaelschecht/taskhub'` under the comment `// Stage 3 (public surface).` — the record of what stage 2 deliberately did *not* touch. A mechanical pass rewrites those happily, leaving a script that compiles, reads correctly, and misreports its own scope. Protected explicitly. This is [#32](troubleshooting/README.md#33-a-rename-pass-silently-disables-the-back-compat-it-just-added--and-rewrites-the-tests-too) recurring one stage later, which is now twice: **a rename pass will rewrite the thing that guards it, and the guard reads correct afterwards.**
  - **Verification had to come from the network, not the tooling.** `gh` reporting a successful rename says nothing about whether Pages rebuilt, whether the content-addressed template and pack paths still resolve, or whether the front door's cross-origin fetch is still permitted. Each was checked against the live URL: 200 with **55** templates (re-verified 2026-07-31 against both the hosted `index.json` and the local artifact, which agree; this line and two others said **61**, a number matching no build), `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *` intact, both pages serving zero stale references, and the old path confirmed **404** rather than assumed to redirect.

  *(Original scope note.)* renaming `michaelschecht/taskhub{,-registry,-site}`, the `taskhub.mikesailab.com` front door, and — the one with real consequences — **the registry URL**, which is the documented `TEMPLATE_REGISTRY_URL` installed apps fetch their catalog from. Renaming the registry repo moves its Pages path and 404s the old one. Cheap today (one install, the author's); impossible to do quietly later.

  **Stage 4 — the local checkout folder** ✅ **shipped 2026-07-31**: `…\Live_Apps\taskhub` → `…\Live_Apps\cronsole`, by [`scripts/startup-task/Migrate-RepoFolder.ps1`](../scripts/startup-task/Migrate-RepoFolder.ps1). Stages 1–3 had each ruled this out, and the standing note called it permanent — but "every scheduled task embeds it as an absolute path" is an argument for **migrating the holders**, which stages 1 and 2 had already built the machinery to do. Three things held the path: the `\Cronsole-Stack\` launcher tasks, the published agent, and the `.claude\skills\cronsole` junction. Verified after the move from Windows and the backend rather than from the script's own report: all three tasks resolve, the junction's target exists, `cronsole.ps1 up` → **ALL UP**, and both platforms report `HEALTHY` with a fresh `lastSync` from an agent running as `Cronsole.Agent` out of the new path.

  **What this stage taught — three failures that each reported success:**
  - **`down` stopped the service and not the folder's holders.** `Stop-Port` kills whoever owns the listening socket; under `npm run dev` that is the innermost node, while `tsx watch` and its `cmd.exe` wrapper are its **ancestors** and keep `backend\` as their CWD — an open directory handle, which Windows will not let you rename around. The port really was free and the service really was stopped, so **the shutdown report was true and simply wasn't the claim that mattered**. Fixed with `Stop-DevServerTree`, matching the repo path on the **command line** rather than the CWD: that reaches everything the script started and deliberately cannot reach an editor or agent merely sitting in the folder.
  - **`agent already stopped` printed at a running agent.** A **process keeps the name it was launched with** — the exe had been renamed `TaskHub.Agent` → `Cronsole.Agent`, and the agent on this machine predated that. All four `Get-Process -Name 'Cronsole.Agent'` call sites went blind **at the same instant, each still reporting success**. `Stop-ScheduledTask` doesn't reach it either: it stops what the task *launched*, and this process had outlived its launcher. **Renaming a binary does not rename the processes already running it — keep the old name in every lookup that *stops* something.** ([#35a](troubleshooting/README.md#35a-and-the-holder-was-the-agent-itself-running-under-its-pre-rename-name))
  - **The abort path left the launcher tasks disabled.** Step 3 disables `\Cronsole-Stack\` so nothing fires into a half-renamed tree; exiting without re-enabling means a *failed* migration has silently turned **logon start** off, discovered at the next reboot — the exact silent breakage the script exists to prevent. It now restores what it disabled and names anything it could not.

  **And one the audit nearly inverted afterwards:** the post-move check found zero of 268 tasks naming the old path. That is evidence **the script repointed them** — it runs after the rewrite, so it can only confirm the rewrite — but it reads exactly like evidence the hazard never existed, and was briefly written up that way. **A verification that runs after the fix cannot testify about the state before it.**

  **Deliberately NOT renamed, at least not with the rest:**
  - **`PlatformType.TASKHUB_NATIVE`** — a Postgres enum value stored on **every** task row. Renaming it is a migration plus a coordinated backend/agent/MCP/frontend change, and it risks the one thing that must not break. The decision to keep it stands, but **its stated justification — "an identifier no user ever sees" — was wrong when written**: on 2026-07-31 the Import modal was found rendering it as the heading **"TASKHUB NATIVE"**, because that one caller formatted the raw enum instead of calling `platformLabel()` (fixed; see CHANGELOG › Fixed). The honest form of the rule is that the enum is invisible *only while every display path translates it* — which is a property of the code, enforced now by a test, not a property of the value. Keep the enum; don't lean on "no user sees it" as the reason.
  - **Historical `CHANGELOG.md` / `ROADMAP.md` entries.** They record what happened, under the name it happened under. Rewriting them would make the records say the product was always called Cronsole, which is the exact class of confident lie §11a exists to prevent. New entries use the new name; a single dated note marks the changeover.

  *(Original)* researched 2026-07-13 — "TaskHub" is crowded; rebrand recommended, decision now made: a web search found "TaskHub" heavily used as a product name, creating real collision / SEO / trademark / app-store-confusion risk for an advertisable product. Specific existing uses found:
  - **Mobile apps** — two Apple App Store apps named *TaskHub* ([id6740157355](https://apps.apple.com/us/app/taskhub-plan-your-tasks/id6740157355), [id6739947000](https://apps.apple.com/us/app/taskhub/id6739947000)) and a Google Play *Task Hub* ([com.posterx.Taskhub](https://play.google.com/store/apps/details?id=com.posterx.Taskhub)).
  - **A hospitality-industry SaaS** called TaskHub with established listings/reviews/pricing on [Capterra](https://www.capterra.com/p/247820/TaskHub/), [SoftwareAdvice](https://www.softwareadvice.com/project-management/taskhub-profile/), and [GetApp](https://www.getapp.com/project-management-planning-software/a/taskhub/).
  - **A crypto/decentralized micro-task platform** also branded TaskHub.
  - **Domains taken** — `task-hub.app` and a `taskhub-site.netlify.app`.
  - **Azure "Task Hubs"** — Microsoft Durable Task terminology (a naming clash in the automation space specifically).

  **Recommendation: rebrand before the first public link** (not after). Final keep-vs-rebrand call is the user's — see the Open decision. *(2026-07-28: the independent analysis reached the same conclusion unprompted and added the cost argument — the name is now stamped into two public repos, two published sites, a registry URL, an MCP server name, an env-var prefix, and a Task Scheduler folder, and every day of launch prep makes the rename more expensive.)*

##### Application goes public

- [x] **Account/login system** *(single-user scope shipped 2026-07-16; multi-tenant still deferred)*: the dev token is no longer the only way in. Shipped the **single-user local login**: backend `GET /auth/status` + `POST /auth/setup` (first-run, claims the password-less catalog placeholder — see below) + `PATCH /auth/password`, login rate-limiting (`express-rate-limit` on `/login` + `/setup`); frontend `AuthProvider`/`AuthGate` (first-run **Create account** → **Sign in** → app), a dynamic token store the axios interceptor + `/ui` socket both read, **401→auto-logout**, logout (sidebar + Settings), and change-password (Settings). `VITE_DEV_TOKEN` is now a **dev/E2E fallback** (a real login token wins); the MCP `TASKHUB_TOKEN` is unaffected. **The load-bearing find (live testing):** the boot seed always upserts a password-less `cli_user_placeholder` to own the shared catalog, so "needs setup?" is *"no user has a password yet"*, not *"no users"* — and setup **claims** that row (sets the owner's email + password on it) so task ownership and existing tokens stay coherent. Verified end-to-end in a browser (fresh DB → create → dashboard → logout → login, 0 console errors) + backend integration against real Postgres. **The full multi-tenant version** (open registration, password reset, JWT refresh, per-user agent-pairing) stays deferred to *if/when* a hosted multi-user product is pursued.
- [ ] **Multi-user / hosted account system** *(deferred — NOT a local-first launch requirement; unlocks only if a hosted multi-tenant product is pursued)*: the single-user local login (shipped 2026-07-16) deliberately left these out. Tracked here so they aren't lost:
  - **Password reset** — an email-based reset flow. There is **no reset on a local-first install by design** (no outbound email, one owner), so this needs email/SMTP infra and only makes sense hosted.
  - **Open registration / multiple accounts** — `POST /auth/register` was **deleted 2026-07-31** (see the Go-public item above), so there is no registration primitive to repurpose: a multi-user product would build one as a designed flow — invite or owner-gated approval — and replace the single-user `/setup` guard (409 once an owner exists) at the same time.
  - **JWT refresh tokens** — today it's a **24h access token**; on expiry you re-log-in (fine for a single local user). A refresh-token flow keeps long sessions without a long-lived access token.
  - **Per-user agent pairing** — replace the single shared `AGENT_PAIRING_SECRET` with per-user pairing codes. This also unblocks the deferred **Settings agent-pairing panel** (P2) and lets the MCP `TASKHUB_TOKEN` become a real per-user pairing instead of a hand-minted JWT.
  - **Account management / roles** — a user-management UI, roles/permissions, and account deletion + data export (the last overlaps the **Legal minimum** item below).
- [ ] **Production operations**: error tracking (e.g. Sentry), structured logs, uptime monitoring + status page, automated Postgres backups with a tested restore, rate limiting on auth and API, staging environment + deploy pipeline (CI → staging → prod). *(Auth rate limiting shipped 2026-07-16 with the login — `express-rate-limit` on `/login` + `/setup`; broader API rate limiting is still open.)*
- [ ] **Agent distribution & trust**: signed installer (see *Installer packages* and *Ship the whole application as a Windows installer* above), code-signing certificate to clear SmartScreen, versioned releases with an update channel, and a documented "what the agent can do / how to remove it" trust page — critical for a tool that runs with elevated privileges. **The certificate is a hard prerequisite for the whole-stack installer**, not a follow-up: an unsigned `.exe` that registers a service and writes to Task Scheduler is exactly what SmartScreen blocks.
- [ ] **Legal minimum**: privacy policy + terms of service, account deletion (and data export) that actually purges tasks/logs, cookie handling on the hosted site.
- [~] **Launch surface** — *(**2026-07-28: consolidated to ONE public page.** The separate landing page was retired and `taskhub.mikesailab.com` now serves the **gallery**, with the landing page's product pitch, Why-TaskHub strip, quick start and project links merged into its Home. Two front doors saying overlapping things made a visitor guess which was real. `landing-site/` + `publish-landing.ps1` deleted; `publish-frontdoor.ps1` publishes the same `registry-site/index.html` to the second host. **The registry base URL was deliberately frozen** at `https://mikesailab.com/taskhub-registry/` — it is the documented `TEMPLATE_REGISTRY_URL` installed apps sync from, so only the page moved; the page falls back cross-origin when it isn't co-located with the JSON. `CNAME` is now excluded from both mirrors and owned by the target repos, so a stray one can't hand the domain to the registry repo and relocate the JSON. The original entry below is kept as the historical record.)* *(landing page — first pass — shipped 2026-07-14; see Completed)*: a **marketing/download landing page** at `taskhub.mikesailab.com` (product pitch + the local install/download + docs — *not* a hosted instance of the app), getting-started guide (builds on the P2 onboarding item), and a support/feedback channel. **Done:** a rich, self-contained landing page (`landing-site/index.html`, matching the gallery aesthetic) pitching TaskHub and linking the app repo, the registry repo, and the template gallery — deployed to a new public **`taskhub-site`** repo via `scripts/publish-landing.ps1`, **live at `https://taskhub.mikesailab.com`** (the DNS `taskhub` record was repointed off Vercel to `michaelschecht.github.io` on 2026-07-14 and the Pages custom domain set; also reachable at `https://mikesailab.com/taskhub-site/`). A **Get started** section (Docker-Compose quick-start — clone → `docker compose up` → connect the Windows agent → open the dashboard — with copy buttons, requirement chips, an honest private-beta note, and a link to the full install guide) and a **Questions & feedback** section (request-a-template → registry issues, report-a-site-issue → site-repo issues, and product bugs/discussions marked "opening at launch") shipped 2026-07-14. **Still to do (post-public-launch polish):** real install/download once the app repo is public (the quick-start currently points at the private repo, honestly flagged), and richer marketing content.
- [ ] **Versioning & releases**: semver + tagged releases + changelog discipline (already started) so advertised versions are reproducible.

---

#### Open decisions

- [x] **Cloud hosting choice (Hetzner vs. Render vs. AWS ECS)** *(resolved 2026-07-13 — moot for now)*: TaskHub launches **local-first**, so there is no cloud host to choose at launch. Remote access is self-hosted by the user (Tailscale / Cloudflare Tunnel — see the final P3 item). Revisit a managed cloud host only if a true multi-tenant SaaS is ever pursued.
- [ ] **Installed-app database: bundled Postgres vs. SQLite** *(opened 2026-07-28)*: blocks the P3 *Ship the whole application as a Windows installer* item, and only that item — the Docker dev stack stays on Postgres either way. **Bundled Postgres** costs ~250 MB and a service lifecycle on machines we don't control, but needs **no schema or test changes**. **SQLite** gives a single-file, no-service install, but `Template.tags String[]` is Postgres-only, so it forces a schema change + migration and forks the Postgres-backed integration suite. Current lean: **bundle Postgres for v1**, revisit SQLite only if install size or support load makes it worth the migration.
- [x] **Default theme: dark, or follow the OS?** *(opened 2026-07-28; **resolved 2026-07-28 — dark**)*: `useTheme.readStored()` now falls back to `'dark'`, with `system` kept as an explicit third choice. Chosen over declaring `system` the default and editing the documents, even though that was cheaper: the design tokens, the component set, and the public site are all built **dark-first**, so "follow the OS" ships a first paint the product was never designed in — the docs were describing the intended product and the code was the outlier. The `index.html` pre-paint fallback moved with it and is now pinned to the hook's default by a test. See the P1 item of the same name.
- [x] **Does untrack need an exclusion memory?** *(opened 2026-07-28; **resolved 2026-07-28 — yes, remember the exclusion**)*: shipped as `TaskExclusion`, keyed on the platform-native `externalId`. The objection was real — it *is* a second place that answers "which tasks do we track", the shape [#20a](troubleshooting/README.md#20a-and-a-renamed-category-silently-stops-syncing-its-folder) punished — and it is kept safe by being **subtractive only**: an exclusion can remove a task from a sync, never add one, so the include-set still has exactly one definition (`extractCategory`). The alternative (untrack implies you stop importing the whole category) was rejected as useless for one noisy task in a folder you want. The way back is an explicit category import, which clears the exclusions inside it and **says so before and after**. See the P2 item.
  - *(Original)* blocks the P2 *Untrack* item. A task removed from TaskHub while still present on the machine will be re-imported by the next sync that includes its category — technically correct (it *is* there) and indistinguishable from "untrack didn't work". Either untrack implies the user stops importing that category (simple, but useless for one noisy task in a wanted folder), or TaskHub remembers the exclusion keyed on `externalId` until an explicit re-import (a new persisted concept, and a second place where "which tasks do we track" is defined — the thing [#20a](troubleshooting/README.md#20a-and-a-renamed-category-silently-stops-syncing-its-folder) punished). **Lean: remember the exclusion**, because silently undoing a user's action on a timer is the invisible-fence failure this project keeps paying for.
- [ ] **Rename the Platforms tab, or grow it?** *(opened 2026-07-28)*: it is mostly quick links today. Renaming (`Links` / `Launchers`) is honest and free; growing it into a per-platform capability/status surface is more useful and needs content the project already writes down elsewhere. **Lean: grow it**; revisit renaming only if connectors stall. See the P2 item.
- [ ] Agent transport: WebSocket only, or hybrid with long-polling for restricted networks?
- [x] **License model** *(resolved 2026-07-13 — Apache-2.0)*: permissive open source with a patent grant; `LICENSE` replaced, README updated. See Go-public checklist.
- [x] **Keep the "TaskHub" name** or rebrand? *(researched 2026-07-13; **resolved 2026-07-31 — rebrand to `Cronsole`**)*: "TaskHub" is crowded (multiple mobile apps, a hospitality SaaS, a crypto micro-task platform, `task-hub.app`, Azure "Task Hubs"), and both the 2026-07-13 research and the independent 2026-07-28 analysis recommended renaming before the first public link. **`Cronsole`** was chosen from a checked shortlist: the npm name is unregistered, GitHub returns **one** unrelated repo for it (against 75 for *Escapement*, 692 for *Orrery*), and it is legible on sight to the audience the product is for — cron is also literally the storage model (§9: every schedule is 5-field UTC cron). Runner-up: *Escapement*. Rejected on collision: *Tickwork* (a fork of **clockwork**, a Ruby cron scheduler — same space), *Orrery* (npm taken).

  **The finding that decided the timing, not the name:** the rename looks expensive (267 tracked files, ~3,011 occurrences) but almost all of that is mechanical. The genuinely hard parts are hard *only because of installed users* — the frozen registry URL, the `\TaskHub\` Task Scheduler folder, the `TASKHUB_*` env vars, and `PlatformType.TASKHUB_NATIVE` as a **stored Postgres enum**. The repo is private and nothing has launched, so **the only install on earth is the author's**: that entire category is a one-machine migration today and becomes permanent the day a stranger installs. Domain availability was **not** verified — a first probe was defeated by ISP NXDOMAIN hijacking and a second had a parser bug — so it stays a manual registrar check; the sites live on `*.mikesailab.com` subdomains and need no purchase.

  See the migration item under Go-public below.
- [ ] **Template registry — static vs. dynamic at launch**: start with a static JSON registry (git repo of `index.json` + files, versioned, free — leading) and earn a DB-backed API + admin/submission UI later, or build the dynamic API up front if non-git editing / community contributions are wanted soon. (Format is decided: target-agnostic JSON, compiled per target — see the P2 Template-registry item.)
- [x] **Template distribution — auto-sync-all vs. gallery + selective import** *(decided 2026-07-13)*: the **intended direction** is a curated **core** locally + a browsable **gallery site** over the public registry that users import from on demand (not the current "auto-sync the whole registry into every local DB"). Import/export (P2, shipped) is the foundation; the gallery + core/extended split is the P3 item **Template gallery site + selective-import distribution**. Current auto-sync-everything stays as-is for the MVP-sized catalog and is revisited when the catalog grows past it.

#### Strategy guardrails (from the 2026-07-07 analysis)

- **Reliability control plane, not universal scheduler** — two excellent connectors beat six half-connectors; new connectors unlock only when sync reliability >95% and crash rate <2% hold.
- **No fake data in the UI** — an automation tool earns trust by telling the truth (status panels, upvotes, dates).
- **Dogfood**: migrate real Task Scheduler jobs onto TaskHub-created tasks; every friction point is roadmap input.

---

</details>
